WORKING FOR THE WEEKEND
Book One
A Buffy the Vampire Slayer Fan Fiction

Disclaimer: I do not own Buffy the Vampire Slayer or any associated characters, settings, or properties. This is a not-for-profit work of fan fiction written for personal enjoyment only. No copyright infringement is intended.



Chapter 13: Chapter 13



The jump always felt the same way, which Xander had decided a long time ago was either the least or the most reassuring thing about it, depending on his mood — one second standing inside the cone-marked square behind the football field lacing up his boots, the next standing in the exact same spot except the cones were gone, and Sunnydale was gone, and the only sound anywhere was wind moving through grass that had never once been mowed by anybody. Same ten-foot patch of ground the Mayor had marked off years before any of this started, security detail rotating through in shifts nobody talked about openly, everybody at school having long since learned not to ask too many questions about the cones that lived permanently behind the bleachers. Buffy made a point of being there for the return whenever her schedule allowed it, which by now was most weeks — the timing predictable enough, even if the destination never was, that she could plan around it the same way she'd plan around anything else that mattered to her.

"Okay," he said out loud, mostly out of habit, because talking to himself was the closest thing to company he got for the next three days. "Let's see what we've got this time."

This time turned out to be grassland, rolling out flat and gold in every direction, a tree line so far off it looked more like a rumor than an actual landmark. No roads. No fences. No contrails overhead, which still got him every single time — a whole sky with nothing man-made in it, not even the memory of a plane.

He checked the pack out of habit before he did anything else — the Mayor's pack, technically, though four years in it had stopped feeling like borrowed gear and started feeling like just his: bigger inside than any bag had a right to be, deep enough that he'd really stopped being surprised by what turned up in it when he actually needed something specific, like it understood the assignment better than he did some days. Water purification tablets, the machete that had technically started life as Buffy's before quietly becoming his, three days of compressed food, the satellite-phone-shaped brick that didn't actually get signal here but made him feel better to carry anyway, his own personal water reservoir for drinking, the collapsible fish tank Alan had insisted on after the second fish delivery went badly enough that nobody wanted to repeat the experience, the tranquilizer pistol the Mayor had insisted on ever since a very memorable afternoon with a twelve-foot crocodile and an unattended backpack, the bear mace that had been Joyce's one non-negotiable addition after the incident with the bear he still refused to fully describe to anyone at the dinner table, the air horn that had followed a week later once Alan pointed out mace only worked at a distance he'd apparently gotten uncomfortably good at closing, and — folded down small enough to forget it was even in there until he needed it — the collapsible shed, packed flat since it was a solo trip this time and he didn't feel like hauling it out for one guy and a sleeping bag. Everything present. Everything exactly where he'd packed it eighteen hours ago in a bedroom that currently didn't exist as far as he was concerned.

The grass came up to his knees, warm from a sun that looked the same as his own sun, because as far as anyone had ever been able to figure out, this was his own Earth — just a version of it with the "people" setting switched off sometime a very long while back, judging by how thoroughly nature had reclaimed everything. No ruins. No roads under the grass. Whatever this world's history had been, it hadn't included humans doing much of anything to it, which was either a comforting thought or a deeply unsettling one, and Xander had long since stopped trying to decide which.

He walked for two hours before he saw anything worth walking toward — a smudge of trees that turned out, on approach, to be the edge of a much larger woodland than the grassland had let on, and it was in the transition zone between grass and trees that he found the tracks.

"Okay, that's a new one."

The prints were deep, four-toed, each one bigger than his own boot laid flat, and they were fresh enough that the grass hadn't fully sprung back up around the edges. He crouched down, running a finger along the impression without quite touching it, doing the math on stride length the way four years of this had taught him to.

"Big," he said, to nobody. "Big and not in a hurry, based on the spacing." He glanced up at the tree line, suddenly a great deal more interested in what might be standing just inside it, watching him do geometry homework in the dirt. "Cool. Cool cool cool. Big mystery animal, zero backup, day one. Very normal start to the weekend."

He followed the tracks for another twenty minutes, more out of professional curiosity than good judgment, before the trees finally thinned enough to show him what had made them.

It was enormous — bigger than any sloth he'd ever seen in a zoo textbook, easily the size of a small car, moving through the underbrush with a slow, deliberate patience that suggested nothing in this entire empty world had ever once given it a reason to hurry. Shaggy brown fur, claws long enough to tear open a tree trunk like wet cardboard, which it was, in fact, currently doing, peeling bark off a fallen log in search of something underneath.

"Oh, you're real," Xander breathed. "You're actually real. I've been telling people about you for weeks and I wasn't sure you were real."

The sloth — because that was clearly what it was, some giant prehistoric cousin of the little rainforest kind, extinct on his own Earth for ten thousand years and apparently just fine here — didn't so much as glance up at him. It kept working the log, completely unbothered, the same unhurried confidence of something that had never once needed to worry about predators.

He got the camera out with hands that weren't quite steady, snapped four photos before he trusted himself to breathe normally again, and spent a long moment just watching it work, feeling the same quiet awe that never really wore off no matter how many weekends he'd done this.

'Nobody's gonna believe this without the photos,' he thought. 'Not even with the photos, probably. Willow's gonna want measurements. Willow always wants measurements.'

He got those too, pacing off the length of the log it had partially destroyed, estimating height against a nearby sapling, filling half a notebook page with the kind of amateur field notes that would've gotten him laughed out of any actual biology program and would absolutely make Willow's entire week regardless.

The sloth finally looked up around the twenty-minute mark, small dark eyes finding him without any specific alarm, sniffed once in his general direction, and went back to the log, apparently deciding he wasn't worth the effort of caring about.

"Yeah, that's fair," Xander said. "I wouldn't be scared of me either."

He didn't try for it today. Even wanting to — and some small, ambitious part of him absolutely did — a creature that size startled by a dart fired from too far off, or hit with a guess instead of an actual read on its size, was more than one guy alone in an empty world wanted to gamble on, claws that could open a log like wet cardboard doing far worse to a person standing too close when an undersedated animal that big realized something was wrong. Photos and measurements were the safe win today, and the measurements mattered for more than Willow's spreadsheet — no vet on his own Earth had ever dosed anything this size, and guessing wrong in either direction was its own kind of dangerous. He made camp that night close enough to keep an eye on the tree line, and lay awake longer than usual turning the actual math of it over.

'Random jumps,' he thought, the old fact sitting heavier tonight than it usually did. 'No pattern. Willow said it herself, years ago — I don't land in the same place twice. Whatever this world is, wherever it sits in however many worlds are actually out there, I've got no guarantee I ever find it again. Might be tonight's the only shot I ever get.' He turned that over a long while, the sloth's slow, unhurried shape settled somewhere in the dark beyond the firelight, entirely unbothered by his presence the same way everything here always was. 'Small birds, I can grab and go, no thinking required. This is different. This actually needs deciding.'

He spent the next two days doing exactly what his own checklist told him to — getting closer, patient and unhurried the same way the sloth itself moved through the world, letting it get so thoroughly used to him being nearby that its idle, disinterested sniff in his direction stopped happening altogether, like he'd simply become one more unremarkable feature of its territory. It wasn't about getting close enough to touch — it was about getting close enough for a shot he actually trusted, and about the two full days of watching it move, feeding, breathing, stretching that shaggy bulk against the log, that let him talk himself into an actual number for the dart instead of a guess. By the third morning he could sit an arm's length away while it worked a fallen log, close enough to be sure of the shot and sure enough of its size to commit to a dose, and by early afternoon, watching the light shift toward the hour he knew, from four years of practice, meant the pull was close, he made the actual call.

The first dart went in clean, high on the shoulder where the fur was thickest, and got him a startled bellow and a lumbering half-turn that had his heart in his throat for a full ten seconds before the sloth decided, with the same unbothered patience it brought to everything else, that whatever had just happened wasn't worth the effort of investigating. It took a second dart twenty minutes later, his hands much less steady reloading than they'd been the first time, before its movements finally started to slow, blinking heavier, legs folding under it in stages until it eased into the underbrush like something deflating, breathing slow and even and thoroughly, blessedly unconscious.

He gave it another ten minutes before he trusted it enough to actually approach, one hand finally resting flat against its shaggy flank, feeling the slow rhythm of drugged breathing under his palm. Timing was going to have to be exact now — close enough to touch when the pull came, and no margin left to wait it out if he'd read the clock wrong. 'It's not going to panic. It's not going to do anything except keep sleeping, however this goes.' Worst case, still a lot of very confused waking up to do on the other side, and he'd have Buffy and half a house full of people who could handle that notably better than he could handle never finding this place again.

The pull took him without warning, the way it always did, and this time it took the sloth too — one disoriented, half-conscious groan and shifted ground, claws twitching uselessly against grass that had, in the space between one heartbeat and the next, become the cone-marked square behind the football field, and then very much became a problem for several people who had absolutely not been expecting one.

***

13.5. Faith's Entrance

The Sunnyview Motel sat on the wrong end of the highway, the kind of place that rented rooms by the week to people who didn't want anyone asking questions about why, and Joyce Summers had never once had a reason to know it existed until Giles gave her the address with a carefulness that told her exactly how bad the situation actually was.

"Her Watcher is dead," he'd said, over the phone, voice tight in a way Joyce had learned to take seriously. "Killed three weeks ago. The girl ran. The Council's tracking system finally caught up with her here, and I'm told she's refusing all contact with anyone official."

"And you're telling me this because?"

"Because I don't think official is what she needs right now, Joyce. I think she needs somebody who isn't going to hand her a clipboard and a briefing packet."

Joyce had weighed that a long moment before agreeing to go, mostly because she couldn't think of a single good reason to say no, and several bad ones for saying yes anyway.

The room, when the manager finally let her up, smelled like old carpet and cheap disinfectant, one window with the blinds bent permanently askew, a bed that had clearly seen better decades. The girl on it barely looked up when the door opened — dark hair, sharper cheekbones than a girl her age should have, the kind of stillness that came from someone who'd trained themselves not to react first and ask questions later.

"Room's paid through Thursday," the girl said, flat, not quite a warning. "Whatever you're selling, I'm not buying."

"I'm not selling anything." Joyce stayed in the doorway, deliberately not crowding the space. "My name's Joyce. I know your Watcher passed. I'm sorry."

Something flickered across the girl's face, gone fast. "Everybody's sorry. Sorry doesn't fix dead."

"No. It doesn't." Joyce considered her next words carefully. "I'm not here on behalf of anybody official. I'm here because a friend of mine was worried about you, and because nobody should be doing this alone in a room like this."

"I've done plenty alone. I'm good at it."

"I believe you. Doesn't mean you have to keep proving it to strangers in doorways." Joyce glanced at the room properly for the first time — a single duffel bag, half-unpacked like the girl hadn't decided whether she was staying, a stake resting within easy reach on the nightstand. "What's your name?"

A long pause. "Faith."

"Faith. I have a spare room, Faith. Actual walls that don't have a highway on the other side of them. No strings, no clipboard, no Council paperwork waiting on the kitchen table."

"Why would you do that? You don't know me."

"I know you're seventeen, alone, and grieving in a motel that charges by the week. That's enough to know for now." Joyce kept her voice even, patient. "You don't have to decide tonight. But the offer's there, and it'll still be there tomorrow if you need a day to think about it."

Faith studied her for a long moment, the same flat, assessing look she'd probably learned to survive with. "You're serious."

"Completely."

"People don't just do that. Not for nobody."

"I'm not most people, apparently." Joyce allowed herself a small smile. "Also, for what it's worth, I make excellent chili, and I've been told my spare room has considerably better blinds."

Something that might have been the ghost of a laugh crossed Faith's face before she caught it and put it away again. "This some kinda test?"

"No test. Just an offer, open as long as you need it open."

Faith looked at the duffel bag, half-packed, half a life crammed into it, and then back at Joyce, something calculating and exhausted both at once behind her eyes. "I'll think about it."

"You can think about it in the car." Joyce said it lightly, but there was no give in it at all, the same tone she used on Buffy when curfew truly wasn't negotiable.

Faith's whole posture shifted, wariness sharpening into something closer to alarm. "Excuse me?"

"I'm not leaving you in this room. I've seen the manager downstairs. I've seen this hallway. I'm not driving home and spending the next three days wondering if you're still breathing." Joyce kept her voice even, but she didn't move from the doorway either, and Faith clearly clocked that the exit was, for the moment, not really an exit. "You don't have to unpack anything tonight. You don't have to stay past breakfast if you hate it. But you're not staying in this room another night, and I'd rather you walk out under your own power than make this harder than it needs to be for both of us."

"You don't get to just decide that."

"I'm aware it's not really my call to make for you. I'm making it anyway, because somebody has to, and right now that somebody's me." Joyce didn't raise her voice. She didn't need to. "You can be furious with me the whole drive over. I'll survive it."

"This is insane. You don't even know me."

"I know enough. Grab the bag, Faith."

Faith stood there a long moment, jaw tight, every instinct clearly screaming at her to dig in — and something else, quieter, apparently losing that particular argument, because she grabbed the bag off the bed with more force than it required and shouldered past Joyce into the hallway without another word, radiating fury the entire way down the stairs.

She didn't speak in the car. Joyce didn't push her to. The silence held all the way to the house, Faith staring out the window with her arms crossed, jaw set, looking for all the world like a girl being kidnapped rather than rescued.

"For the record," Joyce said, pulling into the driveway, "I know you're angry. You're allowed to be. I made a decision for you that wasn't mine to make, and I did it anyway, and I'd do it again."

"Then why bother apologizing."

"I'm not apologizing. I'm acknowledging it. Different thing." Joyce cut the engine. "There's a spare room upstairs. Door locks from the inside, if that matters to you. Bathroom's down the hall. I'll be up for a while if you need anything, and I won't be offended if you don't."

Faith didn't answer, just got out of the car, bag over one shoulder, and followed her inside — stiff, furious, clearly still undecided about whether this counted as a defeat.

It took her the better part of a week to stop looking at every closed door like it might lock behind her. It took significantly longer than that to stop being angry about the ride over, though the anger gradually thinned into something closer, eventually, to a grudging, unspoken gratitude she'd never once said out loud, not directly, not even months later.

13.6. The Sloth Situation

Buffy was between him and the sloth before Xander had even finished stumbling from the landing, Slayer reflexes doing what the two security guards stationed at the cones hadn't quite managed yet, both of them still fumbling their radios instead of their rifles, though the sloth itself wasn't going anywhere — a dead weight sprawled half across the marked square, sides rising and falling in the same slow, drugged rhythm it had carried since the second dart, totally unbothered by its sudden change of universe the same way it had been unbothered by everything else in its life.

"Xander." Buffy's voice stayed admirably level, all things considered, stepping carefully around a car-sized animal that had, until several seconds ago, not been part of her Tuesday. "Why is there an unconscious car-sized prehistoric animal on the football field."

"In my defense, it's very sedated," Xander said, already dialing before he'd finished the sentence. "Two darts, should be out for a while yet, but I want Alan's actual guess on the timeline before anybody gets close. Also give me a second, I'm calling Alan."

Alan Finch answered on the second ring, calm and immediate, like a man who had, apparently, spent four years quietly expecting a call exactly like this one.

"How large," he said, no preamble.

"Car-sized. Small car. Two darts of the standard mix, guessed the weight off three days of watching it move — it's down, but I don't actually know how long down."

"Understood. Don't approach it further, don't let anyone corner it if it starts to stir, and give me twenty minutes." A pause, papers shuffling audibly on his end. "Small mercy, son — I've had a protocol drafted for this exact scenario since roughly the second year of this arrangement. Mayor Wilkins insisted. Said your luck couldn't possibly hold indefinitely against something proportionally larger than the birds."

Willow arrived at a dead sprint across the field, having been camped out at the edge of the cones same as most weeks lately, notebook still clutched in one hand, and skidded to a stop at the sight of it with an expression somewhere between alarm and pure, unfiltered scientific joy.

"That's the sloth," she said. "That's THE sloth. You actually brought back the sloth."

"I actually brought back the sloth."

"Why didn't you warn anybody?"

"Because I decided about four seconds before the jump actually happened, and there wasn't exactly a window for a heads-up text." Xander kept his distance same as Alan had said, watching the sloth's ribs rise and fall with zero apparent awareness that it had just crossed into an utterly different universe. "Also I wasn't sure it was gonna work. Random jumps, remember? Wasn't sure I'd ever see that thing again if I didn't just — go for it."

"You could've been hurt. Guessing a dose blind on something nobody's ever darted before."

"Could've. Wasn't, though. Took two days of just watching it before I trusted the number enough to actually pull the trigger. It's honestly the chillest animal I've ever met, which given some of the stuff I've met, is really saying something — didn't even startle much off the first dart."

Alan arrived far faster than twenty minutes, tranquilizer rifle riding in the truck bed same as it had for years now — standard equipment for the job, the same way a first aid kit lived in most people's cars — a transport trailer and two handlers following close behind him, the whole convoy pulling straight onto school grounds through a service gate that had clearly seen this exact routine before. The rifle went unused, Xander's own two darts having already done the job; Alan spent most of the visit checking breathing and reflexes instead, professionally impressed despite himself that the dosage had landed as close to right as it had.

Buffy studied the whole efficient operation with grudging, reluctant awe. "Okay. That's either extremely reassuring or extremely unsettling, that you people had an actual plan ready for this."

"Both," Alan said, cheerfully. "Mostly both."

The eventual transfer became, within the week, the single proudest addition the zoo had ever received, a plaque already being drafted before the enclosure was even finished.

"Four years," Mayor Wilkins said, beaming at the sloth like a man who considered this the highlight of his entire tenure, "and this is, without question, the crown jewel of the whole operation. Well done, son. Genuinely well done. Alan, tell me the contingency protocol worked as smoothly as I'm hearing."

"Smoother, actually. I may have overbuilt it."

"There's no such thing, with this boy's track record."

Xander, watching the sloth settle into an enclosure much larger than anything else the zoo had ever built, felt something warm and satisfied settle into place that had nothing to do with the danger of the day and everything to do with finally, properly finishing a conversation he'd started weeks ago.

"Worth it," he said, to nobody in certain.

"Definitely worth it," Willow agreed, already three pages deep into notes she'd be revising for months.

***



Chapter 14: Chapter 14



By the next afternoon, once Alan's crew had come and gone and the yard had finally stopped being a small-scale zoological event, there was actually time to sit down and do the thing properly — Xander considerably more sunburned than he'd left three days earlier, grinning like a man who'd been sitting on good news with nobody but a very calm, very unconscious sloth to tell it to.

"Okay, NOW show me the actual photos," Willow said, materializing at his side before he'd even finished stretching out a cramped shoulder. "Yesterday was all sedation logistics and Alan yelling measurements at a clipboard. I want the field notes. Properly."

"Patience was never your strong suit." Xander dug the camera out of his bag with the reverence of a man handling something sacred anyway. "Fine. Witnessed standing up, like it deserves."

Willow's whole face changed the second the first photo came up. "Show me. Show me all of them."

He pulled up the four photos on the little screen, and Willow made a sound that wasn't quite a word, more of a general noise of scientific joy, as she scrolled through them twice.

"This is huge. Xander, this is genuinely huge. Look at the claw length relative to the forelimb, that's — okay, I need the actual measurements, please tell me you wrote down actual measurements and not just 'big.'"

"Half a notebook page. Stride length, height against a sapling, log damage radius, the works." He handed it over like a man passing off a trophy. "Took two full days of just sitting near it before I trusted a dosage number enough to actually pull the trigger. It looked at me the whole time like it couldn't quite be bothered to care."

"That's so validating," Faith said with a grin, dropping into the conversation from wherever she'd been lurking near the fridge. "Even the prehistoric wildlife's unimpressed by you."

"I choose to interpret it as the sloth respecting my personal space."

"Sure. Very respectful. Very mutual respect between man and ten-thousand-year-extinct megafauna."

"Wait, so you actually caught it?" Jesse asked, appearing out of nowhere the second the word 'sloth' hit the air, the way he always did — clearly hearing the full version for the first time. "Nobody told me you caught it. I heard 'sloth' and 'Xander's yard' secondhand and just assumed disaster."

"Not just tried," Xander said. "Two tranq darts, and it's getting the biggest enclosure the zoo's ever built once Alan's crew finishes it — should be done within the week, knowing him. I want it clearly on record that I thought about NOT trying it, given it's the size of a car and I have exactly one working spine, and did it anyway."

"Show-off."

"Realist who got lucky on the dosage. There's a difference, and the difference is whether or not my ribcage survived the weekend."

Willow was still scrolling through the notebook page, lips moving slightly as she did some quick math. "If these measurements are even close to accurate, this isn't just a big sloth. This might be bigger than any ground sloth species we have fossil records for. Like, actually new-to-science bigger."

"New to science, old to that world," Xander said. "It's been chilling out there for ten thousand years with nothing to bother it. Probably the most well-adjusted creature in either universe."

"I want to see it," Willow said, already mentally packing a field kit.

"Absolutely not," Xander and Buffy said, at the exact same time, from opposite sides of the kitchen, Buffy crossing over to loop an arm around Willow's shoulders as a kind of physical veto, the gesture equal parts affectionate and immovable.

"I wasn't actually going to ask to go."

"You were about thirty seconds from asking to go."

"I was going to ease into it," Willow admitted. "Build a case first."

"There's no case. It's an empty world with zero cell service and apparently at least one thing bigger than the sloth moving around in the dark, because something walked past my campsite the second night and I never did find out what."

The kitchen went briefly quiet at that.

"You didn't mention the mystery night thing," Faith said.

"I'm mentioning it now. It was probably nothing. Probably some other big, unbothered herbivore doing its own thing at three in the morning." Xander shrugged, though the shrug came a half-second slower than his usual ones. "Anyway. Good weekend. Great weekend, honestly. Bag's got the usual haul too — grabbed a few ground-nesting birds on the way back through the grassland, figured the zoo people would want something they can actually put on display without needing a bigger enclosure budget than the entire city has."

"You're an entire pipeline," Jesse said with a laugh. "A one-man wildlife pipeline. I bet you could smuggle anything through customs at this point, given the practice."

"I don't go through customs, Jesse, I materialize behind the football field."

"Even better. No paper trail. You're basically a criminal mastermind who happens to only steal animals nobody's ever heard of."

"I contain multitudes," Xander said, fully serious, already heading for the stairs with his notebook and his bag, clearly intent on sleeping for approximately fourteen hours before doing anything else productive with his life.

"Wait, what kind of birds," Willow called after him.

"Ask me tomorrow. I've been awake since a sun that isn't the same sun as this one and I would like eight of my fourteen hours before anybody asks me anything with the word 'species' in it."

Nobody argued with that, mostly because it seemed, on reflection, like an entirely reasonable request from a man who'd just spent three days alone with a car-sized sloth and something unidentified in the dark.

***

14.5. Leaving Town

Angel found Giles in the library the following evening, patrol notes spread across the table between them same as most evenings lately, the quiet, practiced rhythm of two people who'd worked together long enough not to need much conversation to do it well.

"I'm leaving Sunnydale," he said, without preamble, because there wasn't really a graceful way to lead into it and he'd never been much good at pretending otherwise.

Giles set down his pen. "I see. May I ask why?"

"Not because of anything here. Nothing's wrong." Angel shifted, restless in the way he got when a feeling needed more room than his usual stillness allowed. "It's just time. I've been in one place longer than I let myself stay most years, and staying too long in comfortable stops mattering to me if it stops meaning something. There's still a lot of ground I haven't covered. People I haven't found yet who need finding."

"The atonement," Giles said, not a question.

"The atonement. Doesn't stop just because Sunnydale's been good to me." A pause, something almost rueful crossing his face. "Might be part of the problem, honestly. It's been good to me. Easy to forget why I started walking in the first place when a place starts feeling like it could be home."

Giles studied him a long moment, the kind of look that weighed more than it said outright. "You've been really useful here. Backup Faith and Buffy could rely on. I confess I'll be sorry to lose that."

"They don't need me the way they used to. Faith's got a whole house behind her now. Buffy's got Xander and Willow and whatever this whole strange, extended family's turned into. I was useful when things were thinner around here. They're not thin anymore." Angel's mouth curved, brief and genuine. "That's a good problem. Best kind, honestly."

"When do you leave?"

"Tomorrow, I think. No sense drawing it out." He rose, restless energy finally finding somewhere to go, and crossed toward the stacks, pausing at the door long enough to glance back. "Tell Faith and Buffy for me? I'm not much for goodbyes. I'll say it badly if I try to say it in person, and I'd rather they remember something better than that."

"I'll tell them," Giles said, and meant it, watching him go quietly, understanding better than most the pull of unfinished penance and the cost of trying to talk someone out of it. "Angel. Wherever the road takes you — I hope you find what you're looking for."

"Yeah," Angel said, already half into the shadows of the stacks, voice carrying back soft. "Me too."

Faith took it about as well as Giles expected, which was to say she didn't say much of anything for a full day afterward, and threw herself into patrol with a focus that had less to do with vampires and notably more to do with not sitting still long enough to feel the shape of one more person leaving. Buffy took it better, or at least louder, a long, indignant phone-tag session with Giles that ended in grudging acceptance rather than actual peace with it. Neither of them saw him off. He'd asked Giles to make sure of that too, and Giles, understanding exactly why, made sure.

***



Chapter 15: Chapter 15



Cordelia found out about the sloth secondhand, which she considered a personal failure, since she generally prided herself on knowing everything worth knowing at Sunnydale High a full four hours before anyone else did.

"There's an actual car-sized sloth. At the zoo. Right now." She cornered Willow at her locker, practically vibrating at having been scooped on a story this good. "Someone said Xander brought it home himself. Right onto the football field, security and everything."

"He tranq'd it and everything. It's real. It's got its own enclosure now, they broke ground on it within the week."

"That is not better, Willow, that is somehow worse. Why does Xander Harris keep bringing home bigger and bigger animals every time he disappears for the weekend?"

"I think it's just what was there. It's not like he's escalating on purpose."

"It feels like escalating on purpose. Last month it was birds. Now it's an actual sedated prehistoric sloth in my zoo. What's next month, a dinosaur?"

"Statistically unlikely, dinosaurs have been gone a lot longer than ground sloths, but I can't actually rule it out."

Cordelia stared at her. "That was supposed to be a joke."

"I know. I just got excited about the taxonomy." Willow shrugged, unbothered. "It happens."

"Separate topic," Cordelia said, regrouping. "Is it true Faith's been staying at Buffy's?"

"For a while now, yeah."

"Since when does Buffy's mom just adopt people?"

"Since Joyce doesn't really take no for an answer once she's decided to help somebody, and Faith needed somewhere to stay." Willow closed her locker. "It's not really a whole story. She just kind of lives there now."

"Everything in this friend group is 'not really a whole story' until it turns out to be an enormous story two weeks later," Cordelia said. "I've learned not to trust the phrase."

"Fair, honestly."

Faith arrived at that exact moment, sliding into the space beside Willow like she'd been listening from one row of lockers over the whole time, which she probably had.

"Somebody talking about me?" Faith asked.

"Cordelia wanted to know about the living situation."

"It's not a whole story," Faith said, flat, daring anyone at the table to push further. "Needed a place. Joyce had one. Not everything's gotta come with a plot."

"Everything about you people is gossip-worthy," Cordelia said. "That's sort of the problem."

"Then find better gossip," Faith said, "because you're not getting mine."

The bell rang before the conversation could go anywhere else, and the hallway filled with the ordinary noise of a school day continuing to happen — lockers slamming, somebody's radio playing too loud two hallways over, the general low hum of four hundred teenagers pretending they'd done the reading. Faith fell into step beside Willow toward the cafeteria, in no visible hurry despite the bell, and had to steer them both around a senior couple stretched hand-to-hand across the width of the hall, walking slow enough to block traffic on principle, followed immediately by a second couple doing the four-legged-beast thing — his arm locked around her waist from behind, her hands over his, both of them steering like a single ungainly organism straight down the center of the hallway.

"Every single day," Faith muttered, once they'd cleared both of them. "That's the third one this week. And Denise and Marcus have been doing their goodbye thing by the trophy case again, which means we've got maybe ninety more seconds before the bell rings on them too and everybody in a four-locker radius has to watch the whole production."

"I think it's sweet."

"It's an obstruction. There's a difference. Also I've heard their whole 'no, I love you more' bit through a closed door. Twice. It does not improve with repetition."

"She's gonna tell everybody about the sloth by lunch," Faith said. "You know that, right?"

"Oh, absolutely. I give it forty minutes, tops."

"You could've asked her not to."

"Wouldn't have mattered. Also, honestly, it's not really a secret. Xander tells everyone anyway. I think he wants people to know he actually caught it."

"He definitely wants people to know he caught it. He's been rehearsing the story since it happened. I caught him practicing the two-dart part in the mirror this morning."

"He does not practice in the mirror."

"I watched him do it, Red. Full gestures and everything. Really committed to the part where his hands were shaking on the second reload."

They reached the cafeteria to find Xander already holding court at their usual table, one hand gesturing wide to indicate some dimension of the sloth story that apparently required physical demonstration, Jesse leaning in with rapt attention despite having presumably heard some version of this already that morning.

"—and it just went down slow, like watching a building deflate," Xander was saying, "which honestly, after two days of convincing myself I actually trusted that dosage number, felt like the longest ten minutes of my entire life."

"Told you," Faith murmured to Willow, and the two of them slid into the remaining seats just in time to catch the part of the story where the second dart came up, which Xander delivered with the reverence most people reserved for actual combat.

***



Chapter 16: Chapter 16



The next jump dropped all three of them into rain.

Not a light rain either — a steady, cold downpour that started the second they materialized and showed no signs of planning to stop, turning the ground beneath their boots to something between mud and soup within the first ten minutes of just standing there getting their bearings.

"Great," Xander said, to the rain, which didn't respond, because it was rain. "Love this. Love getting drenched immediately, day one, hour one, in front of witnesses this time."

"In front of witnesses who are equally drenched," Buffy pointed out, wringing out her sleeve to approximately zero effect.

"Small comfort. I'll take it anyway."

They found tree cover within twenty minutes, a dense stand of conifers that cut the worst of it, and spent the better part of an hour watching the rain hammer down beyond the canopy, waiting to see if it would ease. It didn't. If anything it got heavier, the kind of rain that turned every low spot into a stream and every stream into something with actual current behind it.

"This is fine," Willow said, forcing herself to sound optimistic about it. "Rainy weekend. Character building."

"Character building," Xander repeated. "That's one word for it." He was already unstrapping the shed from where it rode flattened against his pack frame, the one piece of gear he never left behind on a multi-person trip anymore, not since the first time three of them had tried to share a tarp built for one and learned better the hard way. "This, on the other hand, is the actual character-saving part."

It took him and Buffy both, working fast in the downpour, to get it staked and upright, canvas snapping taut against the wind before either of them was fully soaked through, and the three of them piled inside a minute later, significantly drier than the alternative would've left them.

"Okay, I take back every joke I've ever made about you hauling this thing around," Willow said, wringing out her sleeves onto the floor rather than the sleeping bags this time, lesson well and truly learned. "This is honestly the best decision anyone's made all day."

They settled in to wait the storm out, canvas walls holding steady against wind that would've flattened a tarp inside the hour.

The second day, the rain finally broke around midday, restlessness after eighteen straight hours crammed into a shed built for cozy, not comfortable — Buffy wedged against Xander's side the whole stretch, his arm looped loose around her shoulders more out of necessity than romance at first, then very much not just necessity somewhere around hour six, Willow's head on his other shoulder while she read old field notes out loud just to fill the silence — and the restlessness got the better of all three of them at once, pushing further than any of them had really planned. The ground was treacherous — soaked through, every slope a potential slide, every root slick enough to turn an ankle if nobody was watching their footing.

They were watching their footing, carefully, right up until the ground simply wasn't there anymore.

The slope gave out under Xander first, mud and loose rock sliding together in a rush that took his feet out from under him, and in the process of grabbing for anything solid, his flailing hand caught Buffy's ankle, which took her down too, which in turn meant Willow — reaching to catch Buffy — went down a half-second after that, all three of them sliding down a hillside none of them had clocked as a hillside until they were already most of the way down it in an undignified, mud-covered tangle of limbs.

"Okay!" Xander shouted, somewhere in the chaos. "Okay, this is a moment, this is a whole moment happening to all of us right now!"

"You grabbed my ANKLE!" Buffy managed, sliding past him.

"I was FALLING, grabbing things is a REFLEX!"

"Why am I involved in this," Willow wailed, arms windmilling uselessly, "I was STANDING PERFECTLY STILL—"

They came to rest in a heap at the bottom, tangled together in mud up to their ears, nobody quite sure whose elbow was in whose ribs, and for a long moment nobody moved, just breathing hard, coated head to toe, profoundly, gratefully, ridiculously alive.

"Ow," Buffy said, into what turned out to be Xander's shoulder. "Ow, but also, everyone still has all their limbs, so, silver lining."

"I lost a fight with a mudslide," Xander announced, flat on his back, staring up at the canopy. "Round one, mudslide. Round two, also mudslide, technically, since I didn't really get a round two so much as a controlled surrender."

"That's not how boxing works," Willow said, spitting mud out of her own mouth with far less grace than the observation deserved.

"It's exactly how boxing works when the other guy's an entire hillside."

They extracted themselves slowly, checking for actual injuries amid the mud — nothing broken, a scrape along Xander's forearm, Buffy favoring one ankle slightly, Willow somehow the least damaged of the three of them despite her protests to the contrary.

"For the record," Willow said, once they'd found drier ground and started scraping the worst of it off, "next time somebody starts sliding, the correct move is NOT to grab the nearest person."

"Noted," Xander said. "Next time I'll just fall with dignity, alone."

"That's not what I said either."

"I don't know what the right answer is anymore. I've been humbled by a hillside."

They made it to actually dry ground before dark, got a fire going with hands that had finally stopped shaking, and Buffy pressed a mud-streaked kiss to Xander's cheek once they'd both stopped shivering, leaving a visible smear behind that Willow refused to let either of them wipe off, insisting it was "documentation." Once the worst of the shivering had burned off and the fire had done its job, they hauled the shed the rest of the way down to drier ground and dragged themselves back inside it for the night rather than trusting three exhausted, mud-caked bodies to a campfire and open sky, and spent what was left of the evening picking mud out of increasingly unlikely places while trading blame for whose reflexes had actually started the chain reaction, a debate that would resurface, unresolved, at lunch tables for weeks afterward.

***



Chapter 17: Chapter 17



"You look like you lost a fight with a mudslide," Faith said with a grin, the second Xander walked through the door, duffel bag squelching slightly with every step.

Buffy got to him before Faith finished the sentence, arms around his neck despite the mud, completely unbothered by what it was about to do to her shirt. "You're disgusting," she informed him, not letting go. "Also, hi. Also, you're never doing three days in a rainstorm without me again, I don't care whose weekend it technically was."

"Missed you too," he said into her hair, mud and all. "Also you're gonna smell like a swamp in about ten seconds, just so you know."

"Worth it."

"I lost a fight with a mudslide," Xander said, once Buffy had finally released him, still grinning despite the state of him. "Round one, mudslide. Round two, also mudslide, technically, since I didn't really get a round two so much as a controlled surrender."

"You're bleeding."

"Scraped, not bleeding. There's a difference, and the difference is whether Joyce makes me sit still for antiseptic."

"I'm making you sit still for antiseptic," Joyce called from the kitchen, without looking up from whatever she was cooking, in the tone of a woman who had apparently developed supernatural hearing specifically for the phrase not bleeding.

"See, this is what I get for coming home honest."

He sat still for the antiseptic, mostly, wincing through Joyce's brisk and totally unsympathetic application of it, and told the story of the hillside twice more before dinner — once for Buffy, once for Willow, who wanted the exact sequence of hand placements on the roots for reasons Xander chose not to examine too closely.

"You could've died," Buffy said, somewhere in the second telling, sliding closer to inspect the scrape on his arm herself despite Joyce having already handled it, pressing a kiss to his temple once she'd satisfied herself he was actually fine.

"I did not almost die. I almost had a very embarrassing conversation with a hillside. There's a difference."

"That's the same thing you said about the mudslide."

"Everything out there is a difference from the thing it looks exactly like. It's the whole job."

The zoo delivery happened two days later, once the birds he'd brought back had cleared whatever quarantine process Alan Finch had cobbled together for exactly this purpose, and Xander insisted on coming along to see them settled, mostly because he liked watching the moment a creature that had spent its entire existence assuming it was the last of its kind discovered it very much wasn't.

Alan Finch met them at the zoo's back entrance with a clipboard, genuinely loving his job in a way most civil servants managed to lose within the first year.

"Four new arrivals," he said, checking them off as the crate came through. "Ground-nesting species, previously presumed extinct — the paperwork on this gets more creative every time, by the way, I'm running out of plausible cover stories for where these keep coming from."

"Tell them a very generous anonymous donor with excellent luck," Xander suggested.

"I have used a version of that phrase in four separate official documents this year."

"And it's working?"

"Nobody's asked a follow-up question yet, which either means it's working or means nobody at the state level actually reads these forms closely, and I've decided not to investigate which."

Mayor Wilkins appeared partway through the unloading, drawn by whatever internal radar he apparently had for anything happening on zoo grounds, beaming at the crate like wildlife paperwork brought him more joy than most people got out of an actual vacation.

"Look at that," he said, peering in at the birds. "Aren't they handsome. Alan, make sure they get the good enclosure, not the one with the drainage issue."

"Already on it, sir."

"Wonderful." He turned to Xander with the same warmth, entirely undiminished by the fact that he was, by any reasonable definition, congratulating a teenager on smuggling extinct wildlife across dimensional lines. "And how was the trip, son? You look a bit worse for wear."

"Fell down a hill. Very embarrassing hill. Won the fight eventually."

"Good man. Persistence is the whole game, in my experience." Mayor Wilkins clapped him once on the shoulder, warm and fatherly in a way that always sat slightly strange against everything else Xander knew about him. "You're doing good work here. I mean that. This zoo's become something honestly special because of you."

"Just trying to help the birds out," Xander said with a shrug, a little thrown by the sincerity of it.

"That's exactly the right attitude." The Mayor beamed once more at the crate, then checked his watch. "I've got a council meeting in ten minutes that I am dreading with my whole heart, so I'll leave you to it. Alan — the drainage issue, please."

"On it, sir."

He swept off with the same energy he'd arrived with, and Alan let out a breath once he was out of earshot.

"He means it, for the record," Alan said. "The sincerity. It's real. He's got some actually strange priorities for a man who also runs half the demon economy in this town, but the zoo thing isn't an act."

"Yeah," Xander said, watching the birds settle warily into their new enclosure, already looking less like refugees and more like residents. "I know. It's honestly one of the better things about this whole arrangement."

"Better than the extinct animals?"

"Tied, maybe. Real close tie."

***



Chapter 18: Chapter 18



The thing about the sewer system under Sunnydale, Xander had decided a long time ago, was that nobody who designed it had ever considered that a teenage girl with superhuman strength might one day need to crawl through it at two in the morning chasing a demon that smelled like wet dog and old pennies.

"I hate this part of town," Faith announced, ducking under a pipe that dripped something she chose not to examine too closely. "Every damn time."

"You could stay topside," Buffy offered. "Xander and I can handle a runaway Fyarl."

"And miss you two bickering the whole way there? Not a chance."

"It only threw one person into a wall," Xander said, from slightly behind them. "And I'd appreciate it if we all stopped bringing that up like it's a running bit."

"It's a great running bit."

The demon had wandered into a downtown pawnshop two hours earlier, terrified a night clerk into early retirement, and fled into the storm drains with a haul of costume jewelry it apparently mistook for something valuable.

"Question," Xander said, as they rounded a bend. "Purely academic. If the Fyarl hasn't actually hurt anyone since the wall incident, do we have to fight it, or can we negotiate? Offer it a nice necklace and call it a night?"

"You want to negotiate with a Fyarl."

"I want to not get thrown into a second wall in one week."

"Giles says Fyarls respond to loud noises and confidence," Buffy said. "Not negotiation. I don't think it speaks English."

The demon announced itself a moment later, lumbering out of a side passage with a fistful of stolen bracelets clutched against its chest, roaring loud enough that all three of them flinched.

"Negotiations are closed," Faith said, already moving.

It was less a fight than a very damp, very loud disagreement about personal space, the Fyarl swinging wide and clumsy while all three ducked and darted around it, Buffy finally landing a hit that staggered it long enough for Faith to finish it with a length of rebar she'd apparently been carrying the whole time without mentioning it.

"Where did you even get that," Xander asked, as the Fyarl slumped, unconscious rather than dead.

"Found it at the junction. Figured it might come in handy." Faith tossed it aside. "You good?"

"Nobody threw me into a wall. Calling that a win."

They left the demon for Giles' contact at the county to handle in the morning, and hauled the recovered jewelry back up to street level.

"For the record," Xander said, "very normal Tuesday. Regular demon, regular crime, nothing weird about it at all. Compared to some weekends I've had lately, this was basically a vacation."

"The mudslide weekend?" Faith asked.

"The mudslide weekend."

"Yeah, that one still wins for worst recent Tuesday-equivalent."

"By a lot," Xander agreed. "A sewer Fyarl's got nothing on a hillside with personal vendetta issues."

"Don't jinx it," Faith said. "You say something's not that bad, universe usually takes it as a personal challenge."

"I'm not jinxing it. I'm ranking it. There's a difference."

"There's really not."

***



Chapter 19: Chapter 19



The Bronze on a Friday night ran at a volume that made actual conversation a negotiation, which suited Xander fine, since most of what he wanted to say to Buffy across their little table didn't strictly require words. It was the same booth they always ended up in without ever quite deciding to — back corner, good sightline to the door, close enough to the speakers that nobody bothered eavesdropping — and Jesse had taken to calling it theirs with enough consistency that other regulars had really started leaving it open most Fridays.

"You're staring," Buffy said, not looking up from her soda.

"I'm appreciating. There's a difference." He reached over and laced his fingers through hers on the table, thumb tracing idle circles against her knuckles.

She smiled at that, leaning across the small table to press a quick kiss to his cheek before settling back into her seat, still holding his hand, feet already hooked over his ankle under the table the way they ended up most nights without either of them tracking when it happened. "Smooth."

"Years of dedicated practice."

Faith arrived a few minutes later with Jesse in tow, mid-argument about something that had started three blocks away and showed no signs of resolving.

"Tell her," Jesse said, dropping into the booth, "that a hot dog is not, under any definition, a sandwich."

"We already had this fight," Willow said.

"We're having it again. New audience." Jesse turned to Buffy. "Back me up."

"I've never had a strong opinion on this, and I'm starting to think that was the right call."

"Coward," Faith said with a shrug, stealing Xander's fries.

Jesse's attention had already drifted across the room toward Cordelia, holding court near the bar with her usual crowd, tracking her with the unsubtle, hopeful focus he'd been deploying at her, unsuccessfully, since roughly the ninth grade. "Okay, actual important question — is three years of working up to actually saying something a normal amount of working up to something, or is that its own diagnosis."

"It's a little concerning, Jesse," Willow said.

"Noted. Persisting anyway." He still hadn't moved, still watching from the safety of the booth. "She'll come around. I contain hidden depths. She just hasn't discovered them yet."

"Unrelated," Xander said, "and considerably more important — anybody want to hear the full, unedited version of the hillside incident? Because I've been workshopping it, and I think I finally nailed the pacing."

"We've heard it four times," Willow said.

"You've heard the short version four times. This is the director's cut."

"Absolutely not," Faith said. "I lived through hearing about it in real time. I don't need the theatrical release too."

"Your loss. It's got a great third act."

The rest of the night dissolved into the ordinary noise of people who'd long since stopped being surprised by each other — the sandwich debate resurfacing twice more, Jesse trying and failing to get Xander to commit to a firm date for the sloth rematch, Willow quietly sketching what she insisted was just a rough anatomical guess at ground sloth proportions and definitely not something she planned to submit anywhere official.

Faith caught Buffy's eye at one point, tipping her head toward the door.

"Walk and talk?" she asked. "Getting a little loud in here."

They ended up on the curb outside, the Bronze's noise dulled to a bassline through the walls, the night cool enough to actually feel good after the crowd inside.

"You good?" Buffy asked. "You've seemed a little quiet tonight."

"Yeah, I'm good. Just thinking." Faith leaned back against the brick. "Two months in this town and I've seen extinct birds, a demon with a jewelry fetish, and a hillside that apparently tried to kill Xander. I keep waiting for it to stop being weird and it just doesn't."

"Welcome to Sunnydale. It's always gonna be a little weird."

"I'm not complaining. Just noticing." Faith looked up at the sky, mostly obscured by the Bronze's sign and streetlight glare. "Beats the alternative, anyway."

"What alternative?"

"Wherever I would've ended up if I hadn't landed here." Faith said it light, the way she said most things that were actually a little heavier than they sounded. "This is better. Just saying it out loud so it's officially on record."

"Noted," Buffy said, bumping her shoulder against Faith's. "Glad you landed here too, by the way."

"Yeah, yeah. Don't get sappy on me, B, we're supposed to be having fun."

"I'm allowed one sappy sentence a week. I'm using it now."

"Fine. One sentence. Don't make it a habit."

They stood there another minute, comfortable in the quiet, before the muffled thump of the Bronze's music pulled them both back toward the door, Jesse's voice already audible through the wall, still apparently litigating the sandwich question to anyone who'd listen.

***



Chapter 20: Chapter 20



The third jump in a row dropped all three of them somewhere warm, which after the rainy weekend and the penguin chaos felt less like a location and more like a personal favor from whatever cosmic system decided these things.

"Okay, I'll take it," Xander said, surveying the scrubland stretching out in every direction, dry grass and scattered acacia-looking trees under a sky doing an aggressive impression of a postcard. "Warm and dry. Low bar, currently clearing it easily."

"I'll take warm and dry over the shed and the penguins," Willow said, already pulling out her notebook. "Not that the penguins weren't incredible. Just, you know. Cold."

"Understatement of the century," Buffy said, shading her eyes against the sun. "So what's the game plan? Water first?"

"Water first, always," Xander confirmed. "Then camp, then whatever this place decides to throw at us."

The first two days went about as smoothly as any trip out here ever went — water source found by midday, decent camp spot by evening, an actual sighting of something that looked like a small, slightly wrong-shaped antelope that bolted before Willow could get a clean photo, much to her audible disappointment.

"I almost had it," she complained. "So close. So, so close."

"There'll be more," Buffy said. "There's always more. This place seems to specialize in more."

The trouble started on day three, with the bees.

Nobody had seen the hive. That was the honest, if embarrassing, truth of it — a low mound near the base of a tree that looked exactly like every other low mound near the base of every other tree, right up until Xander put his hand down on it to steady himself climbing over a fallen branch and the entire mound came alive at once.

"Okay!" he shouted, already moving. "Okay, that's bees, that's a lot of bees, RUN, just run, discuss later!"

Nobody needed to be told twice. All three of them ran, a furious cloud trailing at a distance that felt simultaneously too close and, thankfully, not quite close enough, Buffy outpacing both of them easily, Willow shrieking something that might have been a scientific observation and might have just been pure panic, until they hit water — an actual stream, shallow but wide — and went in without slowing down to check depth first.

The bees, apparently unwilling to follow anyone into the water, gave up somewhere around the bank.

They surfaced together, spitting stream water, soaked through, and lay there for a solid minute just breathing, comparing stings, Buffy's head finding Xander's shoulder without either of them deciding it, Willow's legs tangled loosely over both of theirs where the current had washed her up against them.

"I have four," Buffy reported. "You?"

"Six," Xander said. "I found the hive, I get the most stings. Feels statistically fair, honestly."

"I have zero," Willow said, sounding almost apologetic about it. "I think I outran the actual bee cloud utterly."

"Of course you did," Xander said. "Of course the one time it matters, you're the fast one."

"I panic efficiently. It's a skill."

"Worth it, though," Buffy said, grinning despite the stings, despite being soaked through for the second time that trip. "Genuinely worth it. I'd do it again." She pushed herself up onto one elbow, dripping, and kissed him properly, longer than the quick hello-goodbye kind, one hand coming up to rest against his jaw, fully unbothered by the general chaos of the moment or by Willow lying right there watching. "Come here, you earned that one for taking most of the stings."

"I did earn that. I'm claiming it. Officially, on record, in front of Willow as witness."

"Noted and witnessed," Willow said, still catching her breath, grinning at both of them, and reached over to pull Xander down for one of her own, unhurried about it, apparently unwilling to let Buffy have the last word on the subject of earned kisses. "There. Now it's on the record twice."

"Too late," Xander said. "She's already jinxed us. I felt it happen."

They treated the stings that evening with what supplies they had, added bee encounter, group edition to the running list of incidents this trip had produced, and spent the rest of the visit giving every low mound within sight a very healthy amount of berth.

By the time the jump pulled them home, three days folding into one, they'd added the bee story to the penguin story and the growing collection of near-misses that Willow had, by now, genuinely started tracking in an actual notebook, much to everyone's mixed amusement and mild concern.

***



Chapter 21: Chapter 21



Gerald the emu had, by all accounts, been settling into zoo life just fine right up until the morning he decided the drainage ditch along the edge of his enclosure looked like something worth investigating personally.

"He's stuck," Alan Finch reported over the phone, sounding like a man who'd had a very different morning planned. "Not hurt. Just — stuck. Wedged. He got about halfway into the ditch and apparently decided that was far enough."

"How does an emu get stuck in a ditch," Xander asked, already grabbing his shoes.

"With great commitment, apparently. I've got two keepers out there now, but Gerald's taken a liking to you specifically, and I'm hoping that counts for something, because right now he's not liking anybody who isn't you."

Xander arrived to find exactly what Alan had described — Gerald, wedged at an undignified angle in a drainage ditch, radiating pure outrage, utterly convinced his current predicament was everyone else's fault but his own.

"Gerald," Xander said, crouching down at ditch level. "Buddy. What are we doing here."

Gerald made a noise that conveyed, with impressive clarity for a bird, exactly zero interest in explaining himself.

"Okay. Fair. Not gonna ask again." Xander assessed the angle, the mud, the general structural situation. "This is gonna require some finesse, and also probably some mud, and I want it on record that I already did the mud thing this week, universe, I don't need a second helping."

It took twenty minutes, two keepers, one very patient zoo veterinarian, and a truly heroic amount of mud before Gerald finally came free, indignant but unhurt, immediately shaking himself off and stalking away like a creature already pretending this had never happened.

"He's gonna act like that didn't happen," Alan said, watching Gerald reclaim his enclosure with visible authority. "Give it an hour, he'll be back to acting like he owns the place."

"He does own the place. I just paid the deposit."

"Fair point." Alan made a note on his clipboard. "I'll get the ditch filled in properly before he finds another way to embarrass himself. And you—" he gestured at Xander's current state, "—might want a shower before anyone sees you like that."

"Like what?"

"Like you went three rounds with the actual ditch and the ditch won on points."

"Rude, but accurate." Xander looked down at himself, mud from collar to boots, and decided against arguing the point further. "For the record, this is a full month of mud incidents now. Hillside, bees, and now Gerald. I'm starting to think the universe has a theme going."

"Could be worse," Alan offered. "At least Gerald's fine."

"Gerald's better than fine. Gerald's got main character energy. I respect it, even covered in his ditch."

He told the story at lunch two days later, once the worst of the mud had washed out of everything he owned, and Willow burst out laughing hard enough at his "controlled surrender" recap that she nearly choked on her own lunch, which Jesse considered the highlight of an otherwise ordinary Tuesday.

"You're basically a full-time emu rescue service now," Buffy said with a grin, once Willow had recovered. "On top of everything else."

"I contain multitudes," Xander said, and this time nobody argued with him, mostly because between the sloth, the bees, and Gerald, the claim had started to feel less like a joke and more like an actual job description.

***



Chapter 22: Chapter 22



Willow's notebook had stopped being a notebook sometime around the third species and become, in her own words, "a preliminary field taxonomy," which Giles found charming and Xander found slightly alarming, mostly because she'd started asking increasingly specific questions before he even left for a jump.

"Okay, if you see anything that looks like a big cat," she said, cornering him at his locker Monday morning, "I need you to note ear shape specifically. Rounded versus pointed changes the whole family tree."

"I'll be sure to ask it to hold still for ear measurements."

"I'm serious, Xander. This matters."

"I know it matters. I'm just also aware that most things out there don't love being closely observed by a guy with a notebook, and I'd rather come back with vague ear notes than not come back at all."

"Fair. Vague ear notes accepted." Willow flipped back through her notebook, clearly pleased with herself. "You know this could actually be huge, right? Like, honestly important. An entire parallel ecosystem, isolated from human contact for who knows how long — that's not just cool animal facts, that's actual evolutionary data nobody's ever had access to before."

"I mostly think about it as 'place with weird animals and also apparently bees now.'"

"That's because you're not a scientist."

"I'm an adventurer. Very different skill set. Mine involves not dying, yours involves knowing what almost killed me afterward."

"That's a good division of labor, actually."

"I thought so."

She'd started keeping a second notebook specifically for what she'd begun calling "incident correlation" — cross-referencing which biomes produced which hazards, on the theory that if there was any pattern to which jump landed him where, understanding it might eventually help him prepare better.

"I don't think it's random," she told Giles one afternoon, spreading both notebooks across the research table with a level of organization that made even Giles blink. "Or it might be random, but even random has patterns if you track enough data points. Four years of jumps is actually a pretty solid sample size."

"You believe there's a system to which world he lands in."

"I believe there might be. I haven't found it yet. But I've got desert, grassland, rainforest, and now scrubland, and I keep thinking there's gotta be something underneath the randomness, some kind of actual mechanism instead of just cosmic dice rolls."

"And if there is a system?"

"Then maybe eventually we could predict it. Or at least prep him better for what's coming instead of him finding out the hard way every single time." Willow tapped the incident notebook. "Bee attack, hillside slide, near-miss with something in the dark on night two of the sloth trip — that's three genuine scares in under a month. I don't love that trend line."

"Unrelated theory, while I've got you," she added, flipping to a page with a rough tally scrawled down one side. "It's always three days out there, no matter how long he's actually gone. Doesn't matter if the jump pulls him back after what feels like an afternoon here or a full week — three days, every time, on his end. I think it's not the trips that are inconsistent. I think it's us. Like our world just runs slower than basically everywhere else."

Giles considered that, unhurried. "That is, admittedly, consistent with what he's reported. I'd want considerably more data before calling it established, but it isn't an unreasonable theory."

"Obviously. It's a page-two theory, not a published paper." Willow tapped the tally once more. "But it'd explain a lot, if it holds up."

Giles considered the notebooks for a long moment. "It's admirable work. I'd only caution you against becoming so focused on the pattern that you lose sight of the fact that some risk is simply inherent to what he does. Not every hazard has a predictable cause."

"I know. I just — I don't love not being able to help more than I currently can. Even on the trips I do get to go on, there's still so much he handles solo, just by nature of the job, and all I can do from here the rest of the time is take notes after the fact."

"The notes matter," Giles said with a small smile. "Understanding a system, even partially, is rarely wasted effort. And I suspect he takes considerable comfort in knowing someone's tracking it this closely, even if he teases you about the ear measurements."

Willow managed a small smile at that. "He really does tease me about the ear measurements."

"Extensively, I imagine."

"Constantly. It's very rude, given how useful the ear measurements would actually be."

"Scientists are rarely appreciated in their own time," Giles said, dry enough that it took Willow a second to catch that he was joking, and once she did, she laughed for real, closing the incident notebook with slightly less weight in her shoulders than she'd been carrying it with all afternoon.

***



Chapter 23: Chapter 23



Joyce had started keeping a running list, taped inside a kitchen cabinet where she could consult it without anyone asking too many questions, titled simply Things I Now Accept Are Real. It had grown much since Buffy's arrival in Sunnydale, and Sunday dinner gave her a fresh entry roughly every other week.

"So this month we've had a sloth, a bee incident, and an emu rescue," she said, passing the salad down the table. "Did I miss anything?"

"Hillside," Xander said. "Don't forget the hillside. The hillside deserves its own entry."

"The hillside gets its own entry," Joyce agreed, adding it to her mental tally with the ease of a woman who'd long since stopped being surprised by anything involving this particular teenager's weekends. "How's your arm, by the way? The bee one."

"Mostly healed. Willow wanted photos of the swelling for her notebook, which, side note, is an actually unsettling request to get from a friend." Xander reached over and laced his fingers through Willow's under the table, mostly out of habit, the gesture small enough that Joyce pretended not to notice, the way she'd trained herself to over the past several months, while Buffy's chair had migrated close enough on his other side that she was more or less leaning against him for the second half of dinner, her head tucked against his shoulder between bites.

"It's for science," Willow said, entirely unbothered, squeezing his hand back without looking away from her plate.

"Everything's for science with you lately."

"Everything's interesting if you look closely enough."

"Gerald would disagree," Faith said with a grin. "Gerald just wanted to sit in a ditch in peace."

"Gerald's fine," Xander said. "Gerald's better than fine. Gerald's currently terrorizing his enclosure like nothing happened, which honestly, I respect the recovery time."

Joyce shook her head, smiling despite herself. "I remember when the strangest thing at this table was whether Buffy made curfew."

"Those were simpler times," Buffy agreed, stealing a piece of Xander's roll without asking, which he let happen without comment, the way he let most things happen where Buffy and food were concerned.

"I don't miss them," Faith said. "No offense to curfew, but a sloth's a way better dinner topic."

"None taken," Buffy said. "I don't miss them either. Actually, since when do you have opinions about my old curfew drama?"

"Since Xander told me the whole saga during patrol last week. Very dramatic. I give it a solid seven out of ten as far as teen drama goes."

"It was not a seven. It was way more dramatic than a seven."

"You weren't there for the delivery, B. Trust me, it played as a solid seven."

"Can I get a ranking too?" Willow asked. "I feel like my curfew drama never gets properly ranked, and I had a whole thing in ninth grade."

"Submit it for review," Faith said. "I take submissions."

"You're not an actual ranking authority, Faith."

"I've self-appointed. It's basically the same thing."

The table dissolved into good-natured argument after that, Xander weighing in with his own ranking of the curfew incident, Jesse — who'd been invited on the strength of having heard most of the recent chaos secondhand and therefore qualifying, by Summers-family standards, as extended family for the evening — trying to negotiate an actual scoring rubric, Willow quietly noting something in her ever-present notebook that had nothing to do with wildlife and everything to do with cataloguing this specific evening for reasons she didn't fully explain to anyone.

"I think that's everything," Buffy said eventually, once the argument had run its course and dessert had appeared without anyone quite noticing Joyce leave to get it.

"For now," Joyce said with a tired smile, and reached for the pie server, settling in for whatever the following Sunday would inevitably bring to add to the list.

***



Chapter 24: Chapter 24



Buffy had patrol commitments that weekend that couldn't move, which left this one to Xander and Willow, and the fourth jump in the recent stretch dropped them somewhere quiet — rolling hills, scattered woodland, a small lake catching the afternoon light about a mile off from where they'd materialized.

"Okay," Xander said, turning in a slow circle, waiting for the catch. "This seems nice. Suspiciously nice. What's the catch."

"Maybe there isn't one," Willow said, already scanning the tree line, hopeful after a rough few weeks of near-misses to compare against. "Maybe this one's just nice."

There didn't turn out to be a catch, at least not right away, and they spent the first day doing something that almost never happened out here — actually relaxing, fishing in the shallows of the lake with a line rigged from Xander's pack, catching nothing but enjoying the quiet enough that it didn't especially matter.

"This is what people probably think this job is like," Willow said, lying back against a sun-warmed rock, watching clouds drift over a sky with no planes in it, Xander's arm looped under her head as a pillow, her legs draped loosely over his. "Peaceful. Scenic."

"Don't jinx it." He pressed a kiss to the top of her head without breaking his own lazy study of the clouds.

"I'm not jinxing it, I'm just observing that it's nice for once."

"That's exactly what jinxing sounds like."

The second day brought the deer.

Not deer exactly — close enough in shape that they both filed them there automatically, but bigger, with a strange, branching pattern to horns that curled forward instead of back, a small herd of maybe eight grazing along the tree line as they approached with the careful, unhurried pace that worked best for skittish things.

They didn't bolt. That was the strange part — the herd watched them with mild, unbothered curiosity, the same total absence of learned fear Xander had seen in nearly everything on nearly every world he'd visited, animals that had simply never needed to know humans meant danger.

"This is incredible," Willow whispered, already sketching furiously. "Look at the curl direction. That's not like anything in any field guide I've ever seen."

"Told you it'd be a good one."

They got twenty good photos before the herd finally wandered off, unhurried, and spent that evening comparing notes, Willow filling page after page with the kind of careful, delighted attention that made the whole trip feel like exactly the win it was.

"This is going in the good column," she said. "No injuries, no near-misses, just genuinely great data."

"I'll take a good column trip. I've had my fill of bad column ones lately."

They spent the last day mostly resting, restocking water, letting the peaceful stretch run its course, and when the jump finally pulled them home, they came back with clean clothes, a full notebook of horn measurements, and not a single new scrape to explain to Joyce.

"No injuries?" Buffy asked, checking them both over with visible suspicion when they walked through the door. "Genuinely none?"

"Genuinely none," Xander confirmed. "Quiet trip. Nice lake. Weird deer with forward horns. Willow got actual good data for once instead of just chasing me around documenting disasters."

"That feels like tempting fate a little," Buffy said.

"Don't say that. Don't you dare say that."

"I'm just saying, statistically—"

"Statistics can wait until after we've had one clean weekend to enjoy in peace," Xander said, already heading for the stairs, notebook held like something precious. "Let us have this one, B. Just this one."

***



Chapter 25: Chapter 25



Cordelia cornered Willow at her locker for what had become, by unofficial count, the fourth time this month, which Willow had started to suspect meant she'd been unofficially promoted to Cordelia's primary source on all things weird, whether she wanted the job or not.

"Okay, deer with forward horns," Cordelia said, without preamble. "Real, or are you people exaggerating again to make your weekends sound more interesting than they are?"

"Real. I was there, I've got actual measurements. The curl pattern's really unusual."

"And no injuries this time? Shocking. I was starting to think Xander was contractually required to bleed at least once a trip."

"First injury-free one in about a month, yeah."

"I've been keeping track," Cordelia said, like she was confessing to something mildly criminal. "Not because I care what you people get up to. Because it's truly the only interesting thing at this school that isn't prom committee drama or the debate team's ongoing personality crisis, and my standards, unlike some people's, remain high."

"That's fair. It has been a busy month."

"Sloth, bees, an emu incident, a hillside with apparent personal vendetta issues, and now peaceful forward-horn deer. Honestly? Feels like a step down. Where's the chaos, Rosenberg. I was promised chaos."

"I don't control the wildlife's dramatic pacing."

"Clearly somebody should. I want to see the actual notebook at some point. Purely so I can judge your handwriting properly."

"I can't show you the actual notebook."

"Obviously you can. You just don't want to."

"Because it's got Faith's handwriting in the margins making fun of half my entries, and some of it's honestly funny, but a lot of it's aimed at me specifically."

"Now I definitely want to see it."

"That tracks, unfortunately."

Faith arrived mid-conversation, sliding in with a tray she hadn't finished eating from yet. "Somebody talking about my margin notes?"

"Cordelia wants to see the notebook."

"Absolutely not. My best material's in there. I'm not giving away the good stuff for free." Faith stole a fry off Cordelia's tray without asking, which drew a flat, unimpressed look.

"Do that again and I stab you with this fork," Cordelia said, completely conversational, not even glancing up.
