"Noted. Threat received." Faith didn't look remotely deterred. "Also, unrelated, I need a second opinion. Cordelia. Hot dog. Sandwich or not sandwich."

Cordelia stared at her, weary, like someone who'd already decided this conversation was beneath her but couldn't quite resist answering anyway. "It's a hot dog. Obviously not a sandwich. I genuinely can't believe this is a debate that's lasted long enough to require my input."

"See, thank you," Jesse said, arriving in time to catch the tail end of it, dropping his tray with enough force to make his soda slosh. "Finally, a voice of reason at this table."

"I've been saying this for weeks," Willow said. "Nobody listens to me either."

"You don't count, Red, you agree with him." Faith gestured at Jesse. "I need actual outside perspective."

"I don't want to be involved in this," Cordelia said, already backing half a step away, though she made no actual move to leave. "I answered one question because it was an easy point to score, not because I'm interested in your ongoing personality disorder of a debate."

"Too late," Faith said with a grin. "You're in it now."

"I am extremely not in it."

"You've checked on the notebook four times this month, Cordelia," Willow pointed out.

"That's research. There's a difference between research and being 'in it.'"

"Sure there is," Faith said, unconvinced.

Cordelia opened her mouth, found she didn't have a rebuttal sharp enough to satisfy her own standards, and settled instead for a look that suggested the whole table had personally disappointed her. "Whatever. If any of you tell people I willingly sat here for this conversation, I'll deny it under oath."

"Wouldn't dream of it," Faith said, totally unconvincingly.

Jesse, meanwhile, had already moved on to needling Xander's empty seat like the man himself was present to defend it. "Where is our fearless leader, by the way? Still recovering from the emu incident emotionally, or is he just late."

"Emotionally recovering," Willow said. "He's very sensitive about the whole thing."

"He should be. Man got out-maneuvered by a bird with the wingspan of a Buick." Jesse shook his head with theatrical solemnity. "I'm gonna bring it up at his funeral. 'Here lies Xander. Bested by poultry.'"

"Emus aren't poultry."

"They're bird-shaped and rude. Close enough."

The lunch table settled into its usual noise after that — the sandwich debate reopening in full, Willow's notebook getting passed exactly one page deep before Faith snatched it back, Jesse narrating the whole exchange like a sports commentator, Cordelia lingering notably longer than her stated indifference should have technically allowed — ordinary, loud, and entirely unconcerned with anything more dangerous than an argument about hot dogs.

***



Chapter 26: Chapter 26



Faith noticed the pattern before anyone else did, mostly because she was the only one actually walking the east side every single night instead of splitting patrol routes with Buffy the way the rulebook technically suggested.

Three nests in two weeks, all within four blocks of each other, all freshly established rather than long-standing — new vampires, recently turned, moving into the same stretch of the old industrial district like something was actively recruiting there instead of just happening to settle.

She didn't mention it right away. Partly because she wasn't sure yet it was actually a pattern and not just a statistical blip, and partly because some stubborn, familiar part of her wanted to be sure before she brought it to anyone, wanted one thing in this town that was hers to figure out instead of something she'd been looped into after the fact.

"You've been quiet on patrol," Buffy said, the fourth night in, both of them walking the same stretch for once instead of splitting up.

"Just thinking."

"About?"

"Nothing yet. Ask me again in a few days." Faith kept her eyes on the tree line, the old warehouse silhouettes along the block, cataloguing without quite deciding to. "If it's actually something, I'll tell you. If it's nothing, no point wasting your night on my hunch."

"You sure? I can help you think out loud."

"I'm sure. Let me have this one, B."

Buffy let it go, which Faith appreciated more than she said, and the pattern kept building over the following week — a fourth nest, then a fifth, all clustered, all recent, and one thing common to every single one that took her three separate visits to actually notice: a symbol, small and half-hidden, scratched into brick or spray-painted crude and fast near each entrance, the same shape every time, a rough circle with three lines crossing through it.

She sketched it from memory onto a napkin at the Bronze, turning it over in her head without any real theory yet, just the itch of a pattern that hadn't explained itself.

"What's that," Jesse asked, leaning over to look.

"Nothing. Doodle."

"That's a really specific doodle."

"I have a specific imagination." Faith folded the napkin before he could look closer, mostly out of instinct rather than any real secrecy. "Forget it. Not important yet."

It became important four nights later, when she found a sixth mark — same symbol, same crude style — on a building that hadn't had a nest inside it at all, just the symbol alone, fresh, like someone was marking territory ahead of whatever came next rather than after the fact.

That was the night she finally went to Giles, notebook page torn out and folded in her jacket pocket, because six was too many to keep calling a hunch.

"I've been tracking something," she said, setting the sketch down on the research table. "Five nests, one empty mark, all within a few weeks, all the same symbol. I didn't say anything sooner because I wasn't sure it was real yet."

Giles studied the sketch for a long moment, turning it under the lamp, his expression sliding into a stillness that meant he'd recognized something he didn't like.

"You've done good work noticing this," he said finally. "This isn't a pattern I recognize immediately, which concerns me somewhat more than if it were something familiar. I'd like to keep this sketch, if I may, and cross-reference it properly."

"Yeah. Course." Faith hesitated. "It's actually something, then. Not just me seeing shapes in nothing."

"It's very much something," Giles said with a nod. "And I'd like you to know — you were right to trust your own read on this before bringing it to anyone. That instinct matters. I'd rather you develop confidence in it than defer to Buffy or myself out of habit."

Faith didn't have an immediate comeback for that, which was rare enough that Giles noticed, glancing up from the sketch with something like quiet approval.

"I'm gonna keep watching it," she said, finally. "Whatever this turns out to be. I found it. I want to be the one who figures out what it means."

"I wouldn't have it any other way," Giles said, and meant it.

***



Chapter 27: Chapter 27



Buffy and Willow both had midterms that week, which left the desert to Xander alone — the trip that tested patience instead of reflexes, and patience had never been his strongest event even with company along to share it.

Flat red rock stretched out under a sky bleached nearly white with heat, no shade anywhere he could see from the drop point, and he had the shed up and staked within the first ten minutes, canvas throwing enough shadow to make the difference between bearable and actually dangerous while he waited out the worst of the afternoon sun before doing anything productive at all.

"Great," he said, to the empty horizon. "Love this. Love a landscape actively trying to cook me."

He found an overhang by early evening, wide enough to sit under, and spent the cooling hours doing the slow, careful work of scanning for water — dry creek beds that might hold something below the surface, low points where morning dew might collect, anything that suggested this particular stretch of desert wasn't utterly without resources, his own reservoir already down to a level that made the search feel significantly more urgent than academic.

He found a seep spring on the second morning, barely a trickle emerging from a crack in the rock, and treated it like the small miracle it actually was, filling his reservoir and testing it twice before trusting it.

'Willow's gonna want the coordinates logged,' he thought, marking the spot carefully on his map. 'She's building that whole resource chart. This'll make her week.' It was, he'd come to realize somewhere around the second year of doing this, one of the genuine comforts of the job — knowing that even the smallest useful thing he found out here got turned into something useful back home, filed and cataloged and never wasted.

The lizard showed up on the third day, sunning itself on a flat rock near his camp with a boldness that suggested it had never once needed to worry about being someone's dinner — bigger than anything he'd have expected, easily three feet from nose to tail, scaled in a mottled pattern that blended almost perfectly against the red rock around it.

"Hey there," Xander said, keeping his voice low and his movements slower. "You're a big one, aren't you."

The lizard regarded him with the flat, unbothered patience of something that had survived considerably longer than he had, tongue flicking once, and made no move to leave.

He got his photos carefully, keeping a respectful distance, and spent a solid twenty minutes just watching it exist, the same quiet fascination that never quite wore off no matter how many strange, patient creatures this job put in front of him.

"Willow's gonna ask if it's dangerous," he said, to the lizard, which didn't respond, because it was a lizard. "I'm gonna tell her I have no idea and I didn't test it, and she's gonna be disappointed in my scientific rigor, and I'm gonna be fine with that, because I like having all my fingers."

The lizard eventually wandered off on its own schedule, unhurried, leaving him alone with the desert quiet and a genuine sense of having witnessed something worth the discomfort of getting there.

The pull came right on schedule, sunburned, dehydrated in a mild and manageable way, and carrying a full water container's worth of proof that even the hardest world to survive out here still had something worth finding if he waited long enough to see it.

***



Chapter 28: Chapter 28



"You're extra crispy this time," Faith said with a grin, the second Xander walked in, skin several shades darker than it had any right to be after three days.

"Desert trip. Very sunny. Very lacking in shade options." He dropped his pack with a groan, joints protesting the whole trek back through the jump. "Found water though. And a lizard the size of a small dog. Willow's gonna love the lizard."

"Willow already loves the lizard," Willow said, appearing with her notebook already open and pressing a quick kiss to his cheek, careful of the sunburn. "I just don't say it out loud that I have favorites, because it feels unprofessional."

"What's your favorite? Out of everything so far?"

"The sloth. Obviously the sloth. Nothing's touching the sloth's numbers." Willow flipped through her notes with the reverence most people reserved for photo albums. "Show me the lizard properly, though. I need details."

He pulled up the photos, and Willow made the same small, delighted noise she made every single time, scrolling through carefully, clearly building something much bigger than a hobby scrapbook.

"This is really huge for the resource map," she said. "A seep spring, marked and confirmed drinkable, that's exactly the kind of data point I need if we're ever gonna actually understand the water table out there instead of just hoping you find something every trip."

"Every trip I find something. I'm very reliable that way."

"You're reliable in the sense that you keep almost dying and then finding something cool anyway," Faith said. "Different kind of reliable."

"I prefer my version."

"Your version's more flattering. Doesn't make it accurate."

Joyce appeared in the doorway with a plate of something that smelled far better than three days of dehydrated rations, setting it down in front of Xander without comment, the same quiet, practiced kindness she'd extended to every single homecoming since this whole arrangement started.

"Eat something that isn't from a foil packet," she said with a small smile. "Then shower. You smell like a desert."

"I was in a desert."

"Doesn't make the smell better."

He ate, gratefully, while Willow continued cross-referencing the seep spring against her growing resource map, and Faith stole exactly one bite off his plate on principle, and for a while the kitchen fell into the ordinary, comfortable noise of a household that had long since adjusted to weekends that ended in sunburn and lizard photographs instead of anything resembling normal.

"For the record," Xander said, around a mouthful of actual food, "next trip better be somewhere with shade. I've used up my desert tolerance for at least a month."

"You don't get to pick," Buffy pointed out.

"I know. I'm just putting it out into the universe. Manifesting a nice, shady rainforest."

"Didn't the rainforest one have the thing with the ants?"

Xander closed his eyes briefly. "I'd truly forgotten about the ants until you just said that."

"Sorry."

"No, you're not."

"No," Faith agreed, grinning, "she's really not."

***



Chapter 29: Chapter 29



Giles called Faith into the Magic Box on a Tuesday afternoon, which she'd learned over the past couple weeks meant either good news or nothing at all, since bad news tended to come by phone at inconvenient hours instead.

"I cross-referenced the symbol," he said, sliding a photocopy across the counter. "It took longer than I'd hoped. I still don't have a full answer, but I have a partial one, which is more than I had a week ago."

Faith leaned over the page. "Partial how?"

"The symbol appears, in a much older and much more elaborate form, in a handful of texts related to territorial marking among certain organized demon factions. Not random vandalism. A deliberate claim, meant to be recognized by anyone who knows what they're looking at."

"So somebody's claiming that stretch of the industrial district."

"That would be my working theory, yes. Which raises the obvious question of why, and by whom, and what they intend to do with newly turned vampires once the claim's established." Giles removed his glasses, cleaning them slowly, buying himself a moment to think. "I don't have those answers yet."

"But it's real. It's not nothing."

"It's very much not nothing." Giles met her eyes. "I'd like you to keep watching it, if you're willing. Carefully. I don't want you engaging directly with whatever's behind this until we understand it better, but continued observation would be valuable."

"Yeah. Course I'm willing." Faith took the photocopy, folding it carefully into her jacket the same way she had the original sketch. "This is mine to figure out. Already told you that."

"You did." Something that might have been the beginning of a smile crossed Giles' face. "And I'll admit, I've come to trust your read on it more than I might have a month ago."

"High praise, coming from you."

"Accurate praise," Giles said with a small smile. "There's a difference."

She left the Magic Box turning the new information over, the shape of an actual theory finally starting to take form after weeks of just watching and cataloguing — somebody was building something in that stretch of the district, deliberately, patiently, and she intended to be the one who figured out what before it finished building.

Patrol that night took her back through the same four blocks, symbol-marked walls catching moonlight at the same familiar spots, and she walked the perimeter slower than usual, watching for anything new, any change in the pattern that might tell her more than the pattern alone already had.

Nothing new tonight. Just the marks, quiet and waiting, same as they'd been for weeks.

'Patience,' she told herself, the way Giles probably would have if he were here instead of at home with his books. 'Whatever this is, it's not finished yet. Keep watching. It'll show itself eventually.' She believed it too, mostly — enough to keep walking the same four blocks every single night without complaint, because this one, unlike most things in her life so far, was actually hers to see through to the end.

***



Chapter 30: Chapter 30



The sandwich debate had, over the course of several weeks, evolved from a running joke into something closer to an actual institution, complete with its own history, its own casualties, and its own increasingly elaborate rules of engagement.

"New evidence," Jesse announced, sliding into the booth with a printout he'd clearly gone to some effort to produce, immediately shoving Xander further into the corner with one shoulder to make room. "I looked it up. Actual dictionary definition of sandwich."

"You did research," Faith said with a grin. "For the bit. I respect the commitment."

"It says 'two or more slices of bread with a filling between them.' Key word, slices. Plural, flat. A hot dog bun is not slices."

"It's a sliced roll," Willow pointed out. "Technically."

"Don't help him."

"I'm not helping him, I'm being accurate. There's a difference."

"There's really not, not right now, not when I'm losing."

"You're not losing," Buffy said, leaning back against Xander's shoulder without really thinking about it, the way she'd started doing whenever they shared a booth. "There's no scoring system. Nobody's losing."

"I'm losing," Jesse said. "I can feel it happening."

Xander, who'd been quietly eating fries through most of this, one arm settled loosely around Buffy without breaking his focus on the argument, finally looked up. "Can I propose a third category? Not sandwich, not not-sandwich. Just its own thing. Hot dog. Singular classification. Ends the debate, everybody wins."

The table went silent for a moment, considering this with more seriousness than the suggestion probably deserved.

"That's actually not terrible," Faith said.

"It's a cop-out," Jesse said, flicking a fry across the table that bounced off Xander's forehead. "It's an answer designed to make everybody comfortable instead of actually resolving anything."

"Rude," Xander said, unbothered, eating the fry anyway. "Also, most good answers are cop-outs. I contain multitudes of diplomatic solutions nobody asked for."

"I liked it better when you were just tired from getting attacked by a lizard."

"The lizard didn't attack me. The lizard and I had a respectful, distant relationship."

"Every animal you meet has a respectful, distant relationship with you, according to you."

"Because it's true. I'm very good at respecting personal space. Ask the sloth."

"The sloth didn't have a choice, Xander, it's a sloth."

"Consent's complicated in the animal kingdom. I do my best."

"You'd know all about complicated consent conversations, wouldn't you," Jesse said, waggling his eyebrows in Buffy's general direction, which earned him a swift kick under the table from Willow.

"Do NOT," Willow said.

"I said nothing incriminating!"

"You implied plenty. Xander's face is doing a thing."

"My face isn't doing a thing," Xander said, his face very much doing a thing.

Willow snorted into her drink, hard enough that Faith had to thump her on the back, and the whole table dissolved into the kind of loose, easy laughter that came from nothing in certain being especially funny except that they were all tired and it was Friday and nobody had anywhere urgent to be for the next few hours. Buffy tipped her head back against Xander's shoulder, laughing too, and he pressed a quick, unthinking kiss to the top of her hair without breaking stride in the conversation, the gesture so automatic by now that nobody at the table even bothered commenting on it anymore.

"Okay," Jesse said, once he'd recovered, still grinning. "New rule. No more animal consent jokes at the sandwich table. Different table for that."

"There's no different table, Jesse, it's the same table."

"Then no more animal consent jokes, period. I'm instituting a rule."

"You don't get to institute rules," Faith said. "You lost the sandwich debate. Rule-making privileges revoked."

"That's not how any of this works."

"It's exactly how this works," Faith said with a grin. "Welcome to the table."

"I hate it here," Jesse said, without any real conviction, already reaching to steal a fry back off Xander's plate in retaliation. He caught sight of Cordelia passing the booth a moment later, sitting up a little straighter on reflex, same as always. "Okay, unrelated — think today's the day she finally notices me?"

"No," the entire table said, in near-perfect unison, without even looking up.

"Rude, but fair. Statistically consistent with every other day."

***



Chapter 31: Chapter 31



The universe, all three of them decided within the first hour, had apparently taken Xander's manifesting seriously and then decided to be funny about it, twice over, by dropping company into the joke too.

It was shade, technically. Dense rainforest canopy, green in every direction, humidity thick enough to chew, and absolutely, categorically, the rainforest with the ants.

"Okay," Xander said, standing very still the second he recognized the terrain. "Okay, I know exactly what this is, and I would like to formally request a redo."

"What is it," Buffy asked, already wary.

"Supercolony. Ants. Miles of them, moving in columns. Learned this the hard way, solo, more than once."

"How do we avoid it?" Willow asked, notebook already out despite the warning.

"Very, very carefully. Check the ground before every single step. I mean it."

They checked the ground. They checked it religiously, in fact, for a solid day and a half, right up until the trouble found them anyway on day two, in the form of a column none of them saw until Buffy was already three steps into the middle of it.

"Nope," Buffy said, already moving, "nope, nope, NOPE—"

"RUN," Xander confirmed, unnecessarily, since everyone was already running.

They ran together, not fast so much as steady and purposeful, aimed at the nearest water Xander remembered from his initial scan, and reached the shallow stream in a scrambling, undignified rush, all three of them going in without slowing down to check the depth.

"I have ants in my BOOT," Willow announced, mid-splash, her scientific composure completely, finally failing her. "There are ants INSIDE my BOOT."

"We all have ants somewhere," Buffy said, sitting hip-deep in the stream, checking a handful of bites along one ankle. "This is a group ant experience now."

"I didn't consent to a group ant experience."

"None of us did," Xander said. "That's kind of the whole theme of today."

They sat there a solid ten minutes, cursing insects collectively, loud enough to startle a bird out of a nearby tree, before finally dragging themselves out to assess the damage, Xander pulling Willow into a brief, fully unhelpful hug that just got both of them wetter, and pressing a kiss to Buffy's temple for good measure once she'd finished counting her own bites.

"Okay," Willow said, once she'd emptied her boot with visible ceremony. "For the record, I've read papers about supercolonies. Reading about it and personally being invaded by one are very different experiences."

"Welcome to fieldwork," Xander said. "It's rarely as dignified as the papers make it sound."

"I hate that you're right about that."

They spent the rest of the trip walking with the deliberate, ground-scanning caution of three people who had, collectively, finally learned something, and got their actual reward on the final afternoon — a bird none of them had seen before, brilliant blue-green, perched low enough for a genuinely good photo, entirely unbothered by their presence the way nearly everything out here seemed to be.

"Worth it," Buffy declared, watching Willow get the shot. "Barely. But worth it."

"I'm still mad about the ants," Xander said. "For the record. Officially, collectively mad, on behalf of all three of us." He held a hand out slowly toward the branch, and the bird hopped onto his fingers without any visible concern at all, weighing next to nothing. "Also, hey, small and cooperative. Alan's getting a new arrival."

"Seconded," Willow said.

"Seconded," Buffy agreed, and the bird, completely unaware of the diplomatic consensus reached in its honor, simply kept perching, unbothered, while all three of them counted the trip a qualified success by the time the jump pulled them home — ant bites all around, one honestly beautiful photo, and a newly shared, thoroughly earned respect for watching where they put their feet.

***



Chapter 32: Chapter 32



"The ants got you again," Alan Finch said, not quite managing to hide his amusement as Xander recounted the trip over the newest addition's paperwork.

"The ants got me again. I want it on record that I saw it coming, tried to prevent it, and lost anyway. This is not a story about carelessness. This is a story about an actually well-organized enemy."

"I believe you. I've heard worse origin stories for a bird acquisition, honestly." Alan peered into the crate at the blue-green bird, currently regarding both of them with the same unbothered patience as everything else that came through this pipeline. "This is a stunning specimen, by the way. Genuinely one of the nicer ones you've brought back."

"She helped make the ant thing worth it. Least she could do."

"Does she have a name yet?"

"Not officially. Willow's got some Latin-sounding placeholder in her notebook, but that's more of a filing system than a name."

"Might I suggest something," Alan said, already reaching for his own clipboard, clearly taking naming privileges more seriously than the moment strictly called for. "I've always thought this species — well, this genus, at minimum — deserved something a bit more dignified than a filing number."

"Go for it. You've earned naming rights on at least a few of these by now."

Mayor Wilkins wandered by partway through the debate over names, drawn by the same internal radar that always seemed to find him wherever new arrivals were being processed, and peered into the crate with open delight.

"Oh, isn't she lovely," he said with a warm smile. "Alan, make sure she gets a perch with good sun exposure. I remember reading these species prefer it."

"Already planned for, sir."

"Wonderful." He turned to Xander, the same paternal warmth as always sitting slightly strange against everything else Xander knew he was capable of. "Ant troubles again, I hear?"

"Word travels fast."

"Alan mentioned it. I do hope you're taking better precautions these days."

"I check the ground now. Genuinely. It's a whole ritual."

"Good man." Mayor Wilkins clapped him once on the shoulder. "Council meeting again. The things I do for this town." He gave the bird one last admiring look, checked his watch, and sighed like a man with somewhere notably less pleasant to be, humming something under his breath that sounded suspiciously cheerful for someone walking toward a budget meeting.

"He really does love this stuff," Xander said, once he was out of earshot.

"Completely genuine," Alan agreed. "It's honestly one of the more grounding things about working for him. Most days I have absolutely no idea what else is happening in this town, but I know he means every word about the birds."

"Small mercies."

"The smallest, and somehow still the ones that matter most." Alan finished his notes and closed the clipboard with a satisfied nod. "I'll get her settled properly. Go home, Xander. You look like you fought a war with insects and lost most of the battles."

"I fought a war with insects and won the war, technically. Just lost a few individual skirmishes."

"Sure," Alan said with a grin. "Tell yourself that."

***

32.5. Chapter 32.5

It was Faith who found it, which felt right in a way she couldn't have explained to anyone, stepping out onto the Summers' back porch before dawn on a morning she couldn't sleep through and nearly tripping over something that had absolutely not been there when she'd come in from patrol six hours earlier.

It was planted upright in the flowerbed, head-down, like someone had driven it into the soil on purpose and then simply left — a hammer, heavy-headed, handle sticking straight up and worn in a way that didn't match its otherwise pristine condition, sitting there in Joyce Summers' pansies as though it had always belonged among them.

"Okay," Faith said, out loud, to nobody. "That's new."

She didn't touch it right away. Four months in this house had taught her that things which showed up overnight in Sunnydale usually earned a second look before a first touch, and something about the stillness of it — the *weight* of it, radiating off the metal even without her hand anywhere near the grip — made the back of her neck prickle in a way she'd learned not to argue with.

"Buffy," she called, not loud, just enough to carry through the screen door. "You up? You're gonna wanna see this."

Buffy appeared a minute later in sleep shorts and one of Xander's old shirts, hair a mess, blinking against the early light, and stopped dead at the top of the porch steps.

"Is that in Mom's pansies?"

"Dead center. Very inconsiderate of it."

"Where did it even—" Buffy crouched down at the edge of the flowerbed, studying it without touching. "There's no footprints. No drag marks. It's just — there."

"Yeah, I noticed that too. Kinda my whole reason for waking you up at an unholy hour."

Buffy reached for it slowly, the way you'd reach for something that might bite, and closed her hand around the grip mostly just to test the weight of it, the way you pick up anything unfamiliar sitting somewhere it shouldn't be — and found it lighter than it looked, comfortable in her palm in a way that made her glance down at it a second time, some part of her brain flagging the sensation as worth noting even though nothing about it seemed obviously wrong.

"Huh," she said. "That's — huh."

"What?"

"Nothing. It's just lighter than I expected." She lifted it clean out of the dirt with almost no effort at all, turned it over once, testing the balance, and held it out toward Faith without further thought. "Here. Tell me I'm not imagining that."

Faith took it the same casual way, mirroring the same easy motion — and it didn't move.

Not stuck, not caught on anything — she could feel her own grip tight around the handle, could feel herself actually pulling — but the hammer simply stayed exactly where Buffy's hand had left it, planted in midair for a fraction of a second before gravity won and it dropped straight back into the flowerbed, refusing every ounce of effort Faith put into it as cleanly as if her hands weren't there at all.

"Okay, that's not lighter than expected," Faith said, forcing a laugh, trying again with both hands and getting the exact same nothing for her effort. "That's just heavy. Really heavy. How is a random garden hammer doing this."

"You're not even lifting it," Buffy said, watching. "Faith, your hands aren't even moving."

"I'm aware, B, thank you, I have the same eyes you do." Faith let go, flexing her fingers, something flickering across her face too fast to properly name before she smoothed it back into her usual easy nonchalance. "Whatever. Weird garden ornament. Not everything's gotta mean something."

Buffy picked it back up — easily, the same effortless lightness as before — and stood there a moment just looking at it, something uneasy and curious both sitting behind her eyes.

"I'm calling Giles," she said finally.

"Good plan. This feels like a Giles problem."

Neither of them touched breakfast before Giles arrived, and neither of them mentioned, in the time it took him to get there, the strange easy lightness of something that had apparently refused, just as easily, to move for somebody else.

***



Chapter 33: Chapter 33



The vampire nest turned out to be smaller than expected and considerably more argumentative, which Buffy hadn't realized was possible until she was standing in the middle of it listening to two vampires debate feeding order instead of actually attacking.

"You went first last time," the taller one said.

"That's not even true, I let you go first because you whined about it."

"I did not whine, I made a reasonable case."

"Can we focus," Buffy said, staking the taller one mid-sentence before he could finish his reasonable case. "This is a fight. You're supposed to be fighting."

The second vampire looked really offended on his friend's behalf, which lasted right up until Buffy staked him too, and the whole thing was over inside two minutes, significantly less dramatic than the argument leading up to it.

"That's it?" Faith asked, arriving a beat too late to actually help, stake still in hand. "I heard yelling. I was expecting more."

"They were arguing about who got to bite the mannequin they apparently found in an alley."

"A mannequin."

"A mannequin. I don't think either of them had actually killed anyone. I think they just found something vaguely person-shaped and got excited about it."

Faith snorted. "That's genuinely the saddest vampire story I've heard all year."

"It's up there."

They cleared the rest of the block without incident, the ordinary rhythm of a patrol night settling in once the mannequin situation resolved itself, and ended up sitting on a low wall near the cemetery entrance for a few minutes afterward, the kind of comfortable silence that came after a fight that hadn't actually needed much effort.

"How's the symbol thing going?" Buffy asked, eventually.

"Slow. Giles thinks it's some kind of territory claim. Still don't know whose." Faith turned her stake over in her hands, not quite fidgeting, more just keeping her hands busy. "It's weird, having something that's actually mine to work on instead of just tagging along on whatever you and Giles already figured out."

"You're not tagging along. You never have been."

"Feels different this time. Like I found it, not like I got looped in after the fact." Faith shrugged, a little self-conscious about the admission. "I don't know. Feels good, is all. Wanted to say that."

"I'm glad," Buffy said with a smile. "You should have more of those. Things that are actually yours."

"Don't get sappy on me, B."

"Too late. I already did it."

"Fine. One sentence. I'll allow it." Faith stood, stretching out a shoulder that had taken a glancing hit earlier in the fight. "C'mon. Still got the west side to cover before either of us calls it a night."

"After you."

"Age before beauty," Faith said with a grin, already walking.

"We're the same age."

"Details."

***

33.5. Chapter 33.5

Giles examined the hammer for the better part of an hour, still planted in the Summers' flowerbed exactly where it had appeared, crouched at the edge of the dirt with the particular stillness he brought to anything he suspected might matter far more than it first appeared to.

"No footprints," he confirmed, echoing Buffy's own observation from earlier. "No disturbance to the soil beyond the hammer's own placement. Nothing dragged, nothing dropped from height — the flowers immediately surrounding it aren't even crushed, which they very much should be if this had simply fallen."

"So it didn't fall. It just — arrived," Buffy said.

"That would be my working assumption, yes, unsettling as it is." Giles reached out, hesitated, then touched the haft carefully with one finger, testing nothing in specific beyond his own nerve. "The head is wrong for a modern reproduction — too heavy at the balance point, the engraving worn in patterns that don't match any decorative style I can immediately place. I believe I may have seen something like it before, though it will take me some time to confirm where."

"You gonna take it? Study it properly?"

"I'd rather not move it more than necessary, given how little we understand about why it's here or what conditions brought it. I'll want to research first, examine second." Giles straightened up, brushing dirt from his knees, glancing between the two girls with real gravity. "In the meantime, I would ask that neither of you attempt to lift it again until I've had a chance to understand what we're actually dealing with."

"Kind of already did that part," Faith admitted. "This morning. Before I called Giles."

"And?"

"Buffy picked it up like it was nothing. I couldn't budge it with both hands and everything I had."

Giles went very still at that, something crossing his face that neither girl had quite seen from him before — not alarm exactly, more the look of a man quietly filing away a detail he suspected he'd need later.

"That's..." he started, then stopped himself. "I'd like some time to turn that information over before I say anything more definitive. Buffy — would you mind lifting it once more, for my own observation?"

Buffy did, easily, the same effortless lightness as before, and Giles watched the whole motion with the intensity of someone memorizing it for later recall.

"Extraordinary," he said, quietly, mostly to himself. "Set it back down, if you would. Precisely where it stood."

She did, and Giles spent a long moment simply looking at it, planted once more in the pansies like it had never moved at all.

"I'll be in touch," he said finally. "This may take some time to properly understand. I'd ask you both to be patient, and to say nothing of it beyond this porch until I have more to offer than speculation."

"Yeah," Buffy said, still looking at the hammer with something unresolved behind her eyes. "Take your time. It's not exactly going anywhere."

She thought about it, on and off, for the rest of the day, the strange easy lightness of something that had apparently refused, just as easily, to move for somebody else.

Faith didn't think about it at all, or told herself she didn't, and if some small, old, familiar feeling sat a little heavier in her chest than usual that evening, she didn't examine it closely enough to give it a name.

***



Chapter 34: Chapter 34



The complication showed up on a Thursday, in the form of a seventh mark that wasn't just a mark this time — a full mural, crude but clearly deliberate, covering most of a warehouse wall that hadn't had anything on it three nights earlier.

Faith stood in front of it for a long time before she called anyone, flashlight tracing the shape of it slowly: the same three-lined circle from the smaller marks, but bigger, surrounded now by additional symbols she didn't recognize at all, radiating outward like the original mark had grown roots.

'This isn't waiting anymore,' she thought, taking photos with the disposable camera she'd started carrying specifically for this. 'This is building toward something. Whatever this was gonna be, it's closer now than it was a week ago.'

She called Giles from the payphone on the corner rather than waiting until morning, which told him something about the situation before she'd said a word.

"There's a mural now," she said, once he picked up. "Big one. New symbols I don't recognize, all connected to the original mark. It went from small tags to this in less than a week."

"Describe the new symbols," Giles said, already sounding more alert than a person had any right to at this hour.

She did her best, working from the photos she'd already taken, and heard him go quiet on the other end in a way that wasn't reassuring.

"I don't like the escalation," he said finally. "A mark that stays small and hidden suggests patience. A mural this size, this visible, suggests either confidence or urgency, and neither of those is a comfortable option."

"You think something's about to happen."

"I think something is being prepared for, which may amount to the same thing. I'd like you to stay away from that block until I've had a chance to examine what you've found properly. Not out of doubt in your judgment — truly, out of caution given how quickly this has changed."

"Yeah. Okay." Faith looked back at the mural once more before turning away, the shapes of it already burned into her memory whether she wanted them there or not. "I found this. I'm not gonna just hand it off now that it's actually getting somewhere."

"I'm not asking you to hand it off. I'm asking you to be careful with it, which is a different thing totally." Giles' voice softened slightly. "You did exactly the right thing calling immediately rather than waiting until morning. That instinct is exactly what I'd hope for from someone tracking something like this."

"Doesn't feel like enough. Feels like I should be doing more than just watching and calling you."

"Watching and calling me at the right moment is doing something, Faith. It's arguably the most important something in a situation you don't yet fully understand." Giles paused. "I'll meet you at the Magic Box in twenty minutes. Bring the photographs. We'll look at this properly, together, and figure out the next step from there."

"Together," Faith repeated, testing the word.

"Together," Giles confirmed. "This is still yours to see through. I'm simply not willing to let you see it through alone, not with the pace this has just taken."

"Fine. But I'm getting a hot chocolate on the way. Non-negotiable. I don't do ominous murals on an empty stomach."

"That seems like a reasonable condition."

"Damn right it is."

She hung up the payphone and looked back at the mural one last time, the shapes of it stark under her flashlight beam, and felt something shift in her chest that was equal parts unease and a strange, stubborn satisfaction — whatever this was building toward, she'd been the one who noticed it changing, and that still counted for something even with Giles walking alongside her now instead of hearing about it after the fact.

***

34.5. Giles Confirms

Joyce, ever practical, agreed the flowerbed wasn't a long-term arrangement for something that apparently answered to nobody's schedule but its own, and between the three of them it ended up moved indoors instead — propped in the corner of Buffy's room at first, then eventually just accepted as a fixture of the house the way anything unexplainable in Sunnydale eventually got absorbed into the ordinary furniture of a life already full of stranger things. Buffy and Faith kept it close, mostly because neither of them could think of a good reason to hand something like this off to anyone else, and Giles, once he understood that, didn't push for it to go anywhere at all.

The confirmation, when it finally came, arrived not from the Council archive at all but from a footnote in an entirely unrelated text — a survey of "extra-planar royal houses" that Giles had requested months earlier for reasons that had nothing to do with the hammer, back before he'd had any reason to connect the two.

The footnote referenced a realm called Asgard. A house of warrior-gods, or something near enough to the concept that translation hardly mattered. A weapon, tied to worthiness through some mechanism the text admitted it didn't fully understand, occasionally passing between realities during moments of crisis on its home plane.

Giles read the passage four times before allowing himself to believe what it was telling him.

He didn't call anyone. Didn't rush over to confirm it with his own eyes, not immediately — some part of him needed to work through the shape of the thing first, alone, before he trusted himself to react to it in front of anyone else.

It explained everything, once he let himself see it plainly. The weight that responded to something other than muscle. Buffy lifting it without thought, Faith unable to shift it at all despite genuine effort. A hammer belonging to a realm actual gods apparently called home, having chosen — deliberately, he now believed, however impossible that still sounded — to land in a teenage girl's flowerbed and stay, quite deliberately, in that same girl's keeping ever since, waiting for something Giles didn't yet understand well enough to name.

'I should tell someone,' he thought, more than once, in the weeks that followed. 'Buffy, at minimum. Perhaps the Council, though God knows what they'd do with the information.' He didn't, each time, for reasons that felt less like caution and more like a private, stubborn instinct that this particular truth wasn't his to hand off carelessly — not until he understood much more about why it had come here, and what, if anything, was still looking for it.

He kept the footnoted text on his own private shelf, alongside the earlier volume, and found himself returning to both more often than he'd have liked to admit, turning the same unanswered questions over and over without arriving anywhere closer to certainty.

Somewhere out there — another reality, another sky utterly — something knew this hammer existed. Something had, perhaps, put it here on purpose, for reasons Giles couldn't yet guess at.

He hoped, quietly, that whatever came looking for it eventually would be patient. He suspected, having learned not to trust his own hopes too far, that patience wasn't likely to be what showed up instead.

***



Chapter 35: Chapter 35



The coastline stretched in both directions further than any of them could see, gray-blue water meeting gray-blue sky at a horizon so clean it looked drawn with a ruler, and for the first few minutes all three of them just stood there, letting the sound of waves replace the sound of Sunnydale in their heads the way it always took a moment to do.

"Okay," Xander said, breathing in air that tasted like salt and nothing else. "This is new. Never had ocean before."

"This is exactly what we needed," Buffy said, already pulling her boots off to feel the sand properly. "After the ants. After the mud. We needed a nice one."

"Don't jinx it," Willow warned, though she was already crouching by the first tide pool, delighted.

They spent the first day walking the shoreline together, cataloguing tide pools with the careful attention Willow brought to everything — strange crustaceans in colors none of them had seen on anything back home, shells in shapes that belonged to no family anyone recognized, a whole ecosystem going about its business in six inches of water without a single human footprint disturbing any of it before theirs. Xander filled a corner of the fish tank with a few of the more vividly colored ones before they moved on, muscle memory from three years of Alan's requests kicking in before he'd even finished deciding to do it.

Buffy walked between them for a stretch, one hand in Xander's and the other looped through Willow's arm, all three of them content to just exist in the quiet together, no urgency to the pace at all.

The seals showed up on day two, hauled out on a rocky outcrop a few hundred yards down the beach, easily twice the size of anything they'd expect to see back home, mottled gray and utterly unbothered by their approach.

"They're enormous," Buffy whispered. "Genuinely enormous. I did not expect enormous today."

One of them lifted its head, regarded all three of them with the same flat, patient curiosity as everything else out here, and went back to sunning itself, apparently deciding none of them were worth the effort of actual alarm.

They watched a long while from the rocks, Buffy settled sideways across Xander's lap with her legs over Willow's, none of them in any hurry to move, the three of them tangled together in the unhurried, unselfconscious way that had stopped needing comment months ago.

"This is going in its own section," Willow said, already sketching furiously despite the awkward angle. "Ocean ecosystem. That's a whole different resource category than anything we've cataloged so far."

"Told you it'd be worth bringing you both this time," Xander said.

They spent the rest of the trip exploring the tide line further out than any of them usually wandered, buoyed by how gentle this certain world seemed to be — no ants, no bees, no hillsides with apparent personal vendettas, just quiet coastline and creatures too unbothered by their presence to cause any trouble.

It was, by the time the jump pulled them home, one of the easiest trips any of them had had in months, all three of them coming back smelling like salt water instead of mud or sweat or antiseptic, a full memory card of seal photos, a fish tank considerably more crowded than it had been three days earlier, and a quiet satisfaction that came from a weekend that hadn't once required anyone to run from anything.

"No stories this time?" Joyce asked, checking all three of them over out of habit the second they walked through the door.

"No stories. Genuinely peaceful," Xander said. "There's an ocean out there, completely untouched, full of seals the size of couches."

"That sounds nice."

"It was extremely nice," Willow said. "I almost don't know what to do with a nice weekend. I think we've all forgotten how they work."

"You'll adjust," Joyce said with a grin. "Give it a few days."

"Please don't say it like that," Buffy said. "That sounded like a threat."

"It wasn't a threat. It was an observation."

"Feels like a threat wearing an observation's clothes," Xander said, and Joyce just smiled, already heading for the kitchen, unbothered by any of it, the way she'd long since learned to be.

***

35.5. Harry: Nine Years

Gib called at 6:14 in the morning, which told me before he'd said a single word that it wasn't nothing. Gib doesn't call at 6:14 for nothing. Gib barely calls at all anymore, not since the trail went cold the third time and we all quietly stopped pretending we still had momentum.

"Say it fast," I told him, already sitting up, already reaching for the pen I keep on the nightstand for exactly this kind of call.

"Facial match. Ninety-four percent confidence, small-town traffic camera, place called Sunnydale, California. Girl, seventeen, eighteen. Goes by Faith now, apparently, not the name we've been running." A pause, the kind Gib only does when he's bracing me for something. "Harry, it's her. I'd stake nine years of my own credibility on it. It's her."

I didn't say anything for a second. Couldn't, actually. Nine years builds a particular kind of scar tissue over hope, the kind where you learn not to let yourself feel the first jolt of it too hard, because you've felt that jolt collapse under you before, more than once, and each time it collapsed it took a little more of you down with it.

"You're sure," I said anyway, because some habits don't care what your scar tissue's telling you to do.

"As sure as ninety-four percent gets. I've got school enrollment records, Harry. I've got a library card. I've got a girl who's been living an entire ordinary life in an entire ordinary town for the better part of a year, and nobody who's looked for her ever thought to check because nobody expected her to just — settle. Live somewhere. Have a library card."

Helen was already awake beside me by then, some instinct of her own picking up on the shape of the call before I'd said a word to her directly, sitting up slow and careful like she already half-knew and was afraid to let herself know the rest of it.

"Harry."

"It's her," I said, and watched nine years leave her face all at once, replaced by something I hadn't seen on her in so long I'd honestly started to forget what it looked like.

"Where," she said, already moving, already reaching for the go-bag we've both kept packed by the closet door for longer than either of us liked to admit out loud.

"Sunnydale, California. Small town. Nothing about it should mean anything, and I've got a horrible feeling it's gonna mean everything anyway, because that's how our luck's always run."

"I don't care how it runs. I care that we're in the air within the hour." Helen was already dialing before she'd finished the sentence, that specific voice she uses for people who work for us but don't know they should be scared of her yet. "Marcus. Yes. I need the plane fueled and a flight plan filed for California in the next forty minutes, and I don't want to hear a single word about how that's not enough notice."

I called Gib back while she worked. "Anything else. Anything at all. Who's she with, is she safe, is there any read on danger before we walk into whatever this is."

"Working on it. Give me two hours and I'll have you a full picture before you land." A pause, and I could already hear it coming before he said it, same as I always could. "And don't even think about telling me I'm sitting this one out."

"Wasn't gonna say a word."

"Good, because you'd have been wasting your breath. Nine years I've watched you two carry this. I'm not finding out how it ends from a phone call in my kitchen." Something shifted in his voice, dropped the usual bluster down to something quieter underneath it. "She's alive, Harry. Whatever else this turns out to be, she's alive, and she's been alive this whole time. Start there. I'll meet you at the hangar in thirty minutes, and I swear if that plane leaves without me I will never let either of you hear the end of it."

I let myself have that for exactly one full breath, standing in a bedroom I'd shared with Helen through nine years of a hole neither of us had ever managed to properly fill, one full breath of just — alive — before the rest of the machinery kicked back in, because that's what we do, that's what we've always done, feel it for one breath and then move, because moving is the only thing that's ever actually gotten either of us anywhere.

"Pack light," Helen said, already three drawers deep into exactly that. "We can buy whatever else we need once we're there. I am not losing an hour to a suitcase."

"Wouldn't dream of slowing you down."

"You'd better not." She stopped, just for a second, hand braced against the dresser, something raw crossing her face before she visibly, deliberately packed it back down where she could use it later instead of right now. "Nine years, Harry."

"Nine years," I agreed. "And we're done waiting on the tenth."

We were in the air in fifty-eight minutes, Gib making the hangar with four minutes to spare and a travel mug of coffee he hadn't had time to actually drink, dropping into the seat across from us already scrolling through whatever fresh intel had come in on his phone during the drive over. Helen had the pilot doing math on headwinds before we'd even finished the safety briefing, and I sat there running Gib's ninety-four percent over and over in my head, trying to build myself a version of California I could actually walk into steady, instead of the version currently threatening to come apart at the seams the second we touched down.

Nine years. A library card. A whole ordinary life we knew absolutely nothing about.

I'd never wanted anything less like combat prep in my entire career, and never worked harder at holding myself together for one anyway.

***



Chapter 36: Chapter 36



Joyce's list had a new entry by Sunday dinner: seals the size of couches, apparently, and an ocean nobody back home would ever get to see.

"That's the nicest entry you've given me in weeks," she said, passing the salad down the table. "No injuries at all this time?"

"None. Genuinely peaceful trip. I think I forgot how those work."

"Enjoy it while it lasts," Faith said with a grin. "Statistically you're due for something ridiculous next time."

"I already had this argument with Buffy. I'm not litigating it twice in one week."

"Fine, I'll just be right about it quietly, off to the side, and you can deal with that instead." Faith stole a roll off his plate. "Sloth, bees, hillside, mud, ants, and now one peaceful ocean trip. Odds say the universe owes you another disaster."

"The universe doesn't work on a schedule."

"The universe absolutely works on a schedule. It's just a schedule nobody's cracked yet." Faith reached for his fries this time, since the roll was already gone. "Willow's probably got the data somewhere. Right, Red?"

"I'm not building a disaster-prediction model," Willow said, though the slight pause before she said it suggested she'd at least considered it. "That's not what the incident notebook is for."

"You've absolutely considered it."

"I've considered a lot of things. Doesn't mean I'm building all of them."

Joyce shook her head, smiling despite herself, and reached for the bread basket. "I remember when Sunday dinner conversation was about homework and curfew."

"Those were simpler times," Buffy said.

"I don't miss them," Faith said. "A disaster-prediction model's a way better dinner topic than curfew ever was."

"None taken," Buffy said. "Wait, I don't think that made sense."

"It made perfect sense to me."

"That's concerning, actually."

The table dissolved into its usual comfortable noise after that, Jesse arriving fashionably late to catch the tail end of the disaster-model debate and immediately taking Faith's side purely on principle, dropping into the one open chair and stealing a breadstick before he'd even fully sat down.

"What'd I miss?"

"Disaster-prediction model debate," Faith said. "I'm right, everyone else is wrong."

"Sounds accurate. I'm in." Jesse pointed his breadstick at Xander accusingly. "Also, unrelated, you've got Buffy's lip gloss on your neck, so either you two need a signal for when to actually check a mirror before family dinner, or you're doing it on purpose to freak out Joyce."

"I am absolutely not doing it on purpose," Xander said, hand flying to his neck, mortified.

"He is absolutely doing it on purpose," Faith said. "Look at his face. That's a guilty face."

"That's not a guilty face, that's a horrified face, there's a difference—"

Buffy, fully unbothered, reached over and wiped at the spot herself with her thumb, easy and unhurried, the way she'd done it a hundred times before without either of them thinking twice about it in front of company. "There. Crisis averted."

"I hate that this happens regularly enough that you have a technique," Xander muttered.

"I hate that you keep forgetting to check," Buffy said, entirely unrepentant, and kissed his cheek for good measure, right there at the table, apparently just to make his point for him.

Willow quietly sketched what was almost certainly the beginning of an actual chart despite her protests otherwise, Joyce refilling glasses without a word, long since resigned to simply enjoying the noise instead of keeping up with the specifics of any given evening's chaos.

"I think that's everything," Buffy said eventually.

"For now," Joyce said with a tired smile, and reached for dessert, settling in for whatever the following week would inevitably bring to add to an already very long list.

***

36.5. Faith: The Door

I was drying dishes when the doorbell rang, which is such a stupid, ordinary detail to remember clearly out of the whole night, but I do. Dish towel still in my hand. Water still running behind me. Some part of my brain apparently decided that was the detail worth keeping, out of everything that happened after.

Joyce went to get it. I heard the door open, heard her say something I didn't catch, and then heard her voice do a thing I'd never once heard it do in the months I'd been living under her roof — go careful, go quiet, the way you'd move around something fragile you didn't want to spook.

"Faith," she called back. "Can you come here a second?"

I figured it was Council business. Figured it was Giles, or some new Watcher rep come to make my life difficult, or honestly I didn't figure much of anything, because you don't brace for the actual thing. You can't. There's no bracing for it.

I came around the corner still holding the dish towel, and there was a man standing in the doorway who looked like he'd aged a decade since I'd last seen him and also, somehow, hadn't changed at all, and next to him a woman with her hand pressed flat over her own mouth like she was physically holding something in, and I stood there for what felt like an actual minute just — not processing. Not able to process. Because I knew that face. I knew both those faces, down in whatever part of my brain never stopped keeping them, and the part of my brain that ran the rest of my life had spent years telling me those faces were gone. Actually gone. Not gone like moved away, not gone like lost touch. Gone like a house fire I wasn't in the room for. Gone like a story I'd had to build for myself out of silence, because nobody ever told me different, and eventually silence starts sounding a lot like an answer if you sit in it long enough.

"Dad?" My voice came out smaller than I meant it to, cracked right down the middle.

"Hey, baby girl." Harry's voice cracked too, which undid something in my chest I hadn't braced for at all. "I know. I know this doesn't make any sense right now. I know you thought—"

"I thought you were dead." I heard myself say it flat, too flat, the words arriving before I'd decided to let them out. "I thought you were both dead. I mourned you. I actually — I built a whole life around you being dead, I made peace with it, I—"

"We weren't dead." Helen's hand came off her mouth, reaching for me without quite touching, like she wasn't sure she'd earned that yet. "God, Faith, we weren't dead, we were — we were taken. Held. Almost seven years of it, some of that time not even in the same country as each other, and by the time we got out, by the time we could even start looking—"

"Taken." I said the word like it belonged to somebody else's life. "You were taken. Not dead. Taken."

"Taken," Harry confirmed, and something in his face when he said it told me exactly how much that word was covering, how much of it he wasn't going to say standing in a doorway in front of an audience. "We've been looking for almost two years since we got free. Every lead, every dead end, every false match that turned out to be some other kid who wasn't you. And then three days ago a traffic camera in a town called Sunnydale flagged a face, and I have never in my life moved faster than I moved once I saw that flag come through."

I don't remember deciding to cross the distance between us. I just remember being in my father's arms, the dish towel still somehow in my fist, crying in a way I hadn't let myself cry in years, not since I'd stopped letting myself cry about them at all, because what was the point of crying over dead people, you just had to get up the next day either way. Except they weren't dead. They'd been alive this whole time, somewhere, held by someone, while I'd been building an entire life on the assumption I'd never see either of them again.

"I hated you a little," I admitted into his shoulder, muffled and wet and true. "For dying. I know that's stupid, I know you didn't choose it, but I hated you anyway, for a while, because it felt easier than just being sad."

"That's not stupid," Helen said, close now too, one hand finding my hair the way she used to when I was small enough that gesture still fixed things. "That's just grief. Grief doesn't care what's fair."

There was a third person on the porch I hadn't fully clocked until then, a big guy hanging back with a respectful kind of distance, arms crossed, doing an actually terrible job of pretending he wasn't wiping at his own eyes.

"Gib," I said, because some old, buried part of my memory supplied the name before I'd consciously reached for it.

"Hey, kid." His voice came out rougher than I bet he wanted it to. "Look at you. Look at you, all grown up and apparently living some whole life I know nothing about yet. I want the full download. Every bit of it. But not tonight. Tonight I'm just real glad to see your face."

Buffy had appeared somewhere behind me at some point, close enough to be a steady presence without crowding the actual moment, and Joyce stood a little further back, hand pressed to her own chest, clearly trying not to intrude on something this big while also clearly not about to leave a teenager alone with two strangers claiming to be her resurrected parents, whatever the resemblance said.

"I need — I need everybody to sit down," I said, finally pulling back enough to actually look at both of them properly, taking in the real, physical fact of them standing in Joyce's doorway. "I need the whole thing. I need every single detail, because I have spent years building a version of this where you were just gone, and I don't know how to hold both versions at once yet."

"We'll tell you everything," Harry said. "Everything. Take as long as you need to hear it."

"Okay." I wiped my face with the back of my hand, dish towel finally, absurdly, dropping to the porch floor. "Okay. Come in, I guess. Both of you. All three of you."

Behind me, I heard Joyce quietly telling Buffy to go find more chairs, her voice already shifting into the same practiced calm she always found for exactly this kind of impossible thing, and some small, still-processing part of my brain thought, distantly, that I was about to find out my life could still get stranger, even after everything it had already thrown at me.

***



Chapter 37: Chapter 37



Willow's disaster-prediction model existed, despite her repeated denials, and Giles found the first draft of it spread across the research table on a Tuesday afternoon, color-coded in a system that had grown notably more elaborate since the last time he'd checked in on her progress.

"This is remarkably thorough," he said, examining what appeared to be a chart cross-referencing biome type against injury severity. "I thought you weren't building a disaster-prediction model."

"I'm not. This is a hazard-correlation chart. Completely different thing."

"I see. And the practical distinction between those two things is?"

"One of them sounds like I'm treating Xander's life as a science experiment, and the other one sounds responsible." Willow tapped the chart. "Also, for the record, it's actually working. Desert and rainforest biomes correlate with the highest incident rates. Grassland and coastal are consistently the calmest. If the pattern holds, I might actually be able to warn him what to prep for before he even leaves, based on nothing but which supplies he happens to grab that morning."

"That would be remarkably useful, assuming the correlation holds beyond a handful of data points."

"I know it's not a huge sample size yet. But it's not nothing either, and every trip adds more data." Willow looked up at him. "I just — I want to actually help. Not just take notes after the fact. If there's a real pattern here, even a partial one, that's something I can hand him before he leaves instead of just cataloging what happened once he's already back."

Giles considered the chart a moment longer. "I admire the ambition. I'd only remind you, gently, that some risk in his work may never fully resolve into a predictable pattern, however carefully you track it."

"I know. I'm not trying to make it risk-free. I just want it to be a little less blind every time." Willow flipped to a fresh page, already sketching out the next iteration of the chart. "Also, Faith's mural thing has me thinking about patterns generally. If she can spot something building over weeks just from watching close enough, I feel like I should be able to do something similar with actual data instead of instinct."

"They're not completely different skills," Giles said. "Instinct built on careful observation over time tends to look very similar to a well-constructed model, in my experience. You may simply be arriving at the same kind of understanding from a different direction."

Willow considered that, clearly pleased by the comparison. "I like that. Instinct and data, same destination, different roads."

"Something like that, yes."

"I'm putting that on the chart somewhere. Feels like it deserves a place on the chart."

"I wouldn't dare argue with your filing system," Giles said with a small smile, and left her to it, the chart growing another color-coded column before he'd even made it to the door.

***



Chapter 38: Chapter 38



Xander left for the next jump on schedule, same as always, and this time Willow handed him a folded page before he vanished, tucked into his jacket pocket like a good luck charm disguised as a spreadsheet.

"What's this?"

"Hazard notes. Based on the chart. Nothing fancy, just — if you land somewhere that looks like X, watch for Y. It's not perfect. It's not even close to perfect. But it's something."

"You made me a cheat sheet."

"I made you a cheat sheet," Willow confirmed, a little defensive about it. "Don't lose it. And don't laugh at the handwriting, I did it fast."

"I would never laugh at your handwriting." Xander tucked it in carefully, then leaned down to kiss her properly before the pull could take him. "This is genuinely really thoughtful, Willow. Thank you."

"Just don't get eaten by anything the chart didn't warn you about. That's all I ask."

"No promises, but I'll do my best."

He vanished on schedule, same flicker of nothing-to-something that never stopped being strange no matter how many hundred times it happened, and the kitchen settled back into its ordinary rhythm without him — Faith already halfway out the door for patrol, notebook of her own tucked under one arm, mural photographs paper-clipped inside; Willow immediately starting a second copy of the hazard sheet, because apparently one wasn't enough insurance against her own nerves; Joyce humming something off-key while she did the dishes, unbothered by any of it after four years of practice.

Buffy caught Faith at the door before she left.

"Anything new on the mural thing?"

"Nothing since Thursday. Giles thinks whatever's building isn't ready yet. Which is either good news or just means it's got more time to get worse before it shows itself." Faith shrugged, stake already in hand. "Either way, I'm watching. Same as always."

"You'll tell me if it gets serious?"

"You'll be the first call. Promise." Faith paused at the door. "For what it's worth — this whole stretch, lately, sloths and seals and ominous murals and Willow's disaster spreadsheet — it's a good stretch. As far as Sunnydale stretches go."

"Yeah," Buffy said with a smile. "It really is."

"Say it quieter next time. This town's got ears."

"That's not a real thing."

"Neither was a car-sized sloth, and here we are."

"Fair," Buffy admitted, and watched Faith head off into the evening, patrol notebook under one arm and a mystery all her own tucked inside it, while somewhere on the other side of existence totally, Xander was three hours into a fresh weekend with a hand-drawn hazard sheet in his pocket and, for once, at least a fighting chance of seeing trouble coming before it found him.

***

38.5. Vetting the Situation

Harry found Giles at the Magic Box on the third day, no appointment, no warning, just walked in during a lull between customers and set a folder down on the counter with the particular economy of motion of a man who'd done this exact maneuver a thousand times in rooms significantly more hostile than a bookstore.

"Rupert Giles," he said. Not a question. "I did some reading on you. Watchers' Council, former field capacity, current advisor and, from what I've pieced together, informal guardian to a fair number of teenagers who apparently spend their nights fighting vampires."

Giles set down the book he'd been shelving, unhurried, and looked at the folder without touching it. "You've had two days in town and already assembled a file on me."

"Two days and a set of skills I've spent twenty years building. Habit, mostly. Hard to turn off." Harry didn't sit, didn't ask permission, just held Giles' gaze steady. "My daughter's been living under a level of danger I didn't sanction and can't undo, and I've spent nine years unable to do a single thing about any threat to her life. I'd like the actual truth about what she's involved in now, not the version that makes it sound manageable."

"That's a fair request." Giles removed his glasses, cleaning them in the way that meant he was deciding how much honesty the moment could bear. "The truth is far worse than 'manageable,' and considerably better handled than you might expect, given the people around her. Faith is what's called a Slayer. One girl in all the world, called to fight vampires and demons, given strength and instinct to match the task. She did not choose this. Neither did the girl before her, nor will the girl after. It is, without exaggeration, one of the most dangerous callings that exists."

Harry absorbed that with visible stillness, filing the information away rather than reacting to it. "And you're the one responsible for training her for it."

"I'm the one responsible for supporting her through it, which is a distinction I've come to care about a great deal. The Council I formerly answered to treats Slayers as assets to be managed. I no longer operate that way, and I no longer answer to them in any capacity that matters." Giles met his eyes without flinching. "I understand precisely what it looks like, a man my age with unsupervised authority over your daughter's life. I would want the same answers you're asking for, in your position."

"I already ran a check on the Council. Thin file, evasive front, exactly the kind of institutional shell that makes career intelligence people nervous." Harry finally sat, some of the coiled readiness easing out of his shoulders, though not all of it. "I also talked to the Summers woman for twenty minutes this morning. She didn't dress anything up. Told me flatly that your daughter's died once already, doing this job, and came back, and that you were the one who sat with her through the worst of the aftermath."

"That's accurate. I won't pretend otherwise to make myself sound better."

"I appreciate that." Harry studied him a long moment. "I've spent my career reading people who lie professionally for a living. You're not one of them. Which either means you're better at it than anyone I've ever met, or you're exactly what you appear to be, and I've decided to bet on the second one, because the alternative means I've already lost, and I'm not built to operate that way."

"I'd like to earn that trust properly rather than simply receive it," Giles said. "Perhaps you'd allow me to show you the training regimen, introduce you to the resources we actually have, and answer any question you have as thoroughly as I'm able. I suspect you'll find our operation much less reckless than 'unsupervised teenagers fighting monsters' suggests."

"I'd like that. And one more thing—" Harry picked the folder back up, tucking it away rather than leaving it on the counter, some small concession in the gesture. "Whatever resources my organization can quietly offer — equipment, training, intelligence — consider them available. I don't get to be the parent who was there for the last nine years. I can at least be useful for whatever's left."

Two blocks away, in Joyce's kitchen, Helen was learning notably more about her daughter's new life over coffee than any government file could have told her.

"She talks about you," Joyce said, refilling both their cups. "Faith, I mean. Not often, and never with details, but I could tell there was a — a shape missing. Something she'd built her whole guard around."

"I know exactly what that shape looks like from the inside," Helen said quietly. "Nine years of it." She turned the coffee cup slowly between her hands, not quite looking up. "I want to thank you. Genuinely. Whatever this house did for her while we couldn't, I don't have the words for how grateful I am that it existed."

"She did most of it herself, honestly. I just kept the porch light on and made sure there was always enough dinner for one more person." Joyce smiled, warm and a little tired in the way of someone who'd learned to expect the extraordinary at her own table. "Though I'll admit, learning your family's actual line of work has been quite the week, even by this house's standards."

"I imagine you've had a fairly unusual few years yourself, from what Faith's told me." Helen's mouth curved, something sharp and assessing underneath the warmth, the same instinct that had kept her alive through nine years of a life she hadn't asked for either. "I'd like to help, if you'll let me. Not take over. I know how this works when someone barges into a household that's already found its rhythm without them. But I have skills that might be really useful to a house full of teenagers running toward danger instead of away from it, and I'd like the chance to use them for something that actually matters again."

"I think," Joyce said, considering her carefully, "that this house has never once turned away someone who wanted to help for the right reasons. You'll fit right in. Whether that's a comfort or a warning, I truly can't say yet."

"Knowing this family so far," Helen said, "probably both."

***



Chapter 39: Chapter 39



Buffy was down with a bad cold and Willow had a paper due Monday, which left the mountain to Xander alone — honestly for the best, given how close this one would turn out to cut things.

The mountain jump dropped him somewhere cold enough that his first breath actually hurt, thin air biting at his lungs in a way that told him altitude before his eyes had even finished adjusting to the glare off the snowpack.

"Okay," Xander said, breath fogging immediately. "Cold. Very cold. Noted, appreciated, would maybe have preferred a warning."

He layered up fast, four years having taught him not to waste time being surprised by weather before doing something about it, and spent the first day just finding a defensible camp spot below the tree line, somewhere the wind couldn't reach him quite as directly, staking the shed out on the flattest ground he could find and anchoring it hard against gusts that seemed determined to test the guy lines.

The goats showed up on the second morning, a small herd picking their way along a rock face that looked, to Xander's eye, entirely unclimbable, moving with a casual confidence that made the whole thing look easy in a way he knew for a fact it absolutely was not.

"Show-offs," he muttered, watching one of them navigate a ledge barely wider than its own hooves without so much as slowing down. "Real show-offs, all of you."

He got his photos from a safe, flat stretch of ground, mindful of his own significantly less impressive balance, and spent a good hour just watching them work their way across the rock face, filing away details for Willow's notebook — horn shape, coat pattern, the way they moved in a loose formation that seemed to always keep at least one animal watching the surrounding terrain.

'She's gonna want to know if they're related to actual mountain goats or something weirder,' he thought, sketching the horn curve as best he could from memory. 'I'm gonna tell her they're really good at not falling off cliffs and let her sort out the taxonomy.' It felt like a fair division of labor, all things considered.

The cold got worse overnight despite the shed, thin air at this elevation cutting through layers and canvas alike in a way no amount of gear seemed able to fully answer, and he barely slept, teeth chattering hard enough to actually hurt his jaw, breath coming shorter and thinner than it should have even accounting for the effort, a persistent headache building behind his eyes that had nothing to do with cold and everything to do with air his lungs clearly weren't built to make full use of up here.

'Okay, this is genuinely bad,' he thought, flexing fingers that had stopped fully cooperating somewhere around midnight. 'Not a gear problem. Not a me problem.
