This is just what altitude does, this high, this fast, no matter how prepared you show up.' He kept talking to himself in his own head for another hour, mostly to stay awake, mostly because the alternative was silence and silence, up here, felt considerably more dangerous than his own dumb internal commentary ever could.

By morning he'd made the call to abandon the higher elevation utterly rather than push his luck against a mountain that clearly didn't care one way or another whether he made it back down in one piece.

The descent took most of the day, careful and unhurried, shed packed back down and slung over his shoulder, and he made it to warmer ground by evening with his fingers finally starting to feel like fingers again instead of numb, distant afterthoughts.

'Note to self,' he thought, working feeling back into his hands by the fire. 'Altitude's just actually dangerous. No prep fixes that fully, only makes it survivable instead of not.' It wasn't a comfortable lesson, exactly, but it was an honest one, and honest was better than pretending next time would somehow be different.

He made it home with goat photos, frostbite-adjacent fingers that thawed out fine by the time the jump pulled him back, and a genuine, hard-won respect for cold that no amount of desert heat had ever quite prepared him for.

"You're doing the thing where you hold your hands funny," Buffy said, the second he walked through the door.

"Mountain trip. Cold trip. Very cold trip. My hands have complaints."

"Are they gonna be okay?"

"They're gonna be okay. They just want it on record that they suffered." Xander flexed his fingers experimentally. "Everybody's got a complaint after this one. The goats complained about nothing, though. The goats had a great time."

"Show-offs," Buffy said.

"That's exactly what I said."

"I already called Alan," Buffy added. "Told him what happened. He wants an actual cold-weather kit added to the pack before you go anywhere that cold again — real insulation, not just extra layers you happened to grab that morning. Also something about supplemental oxygen and altitude sickness, once I mentioned the headache and the breathing thing. He used the word 'non-negotiable' twice."

"Makes sense. Turns out 'cold' and 'high-altitude cold' are not, in fact, the same problem with the same solution. Who knew. Besides everyone, apparently, except me until now."

***



Chapter 40: Chapter 40



"Mountain," Willow said, adding it to the chart, really serious about it. "That's a new biome category. I didn't even have a column for mountain."

"Glad my near-frostbite could expand your spreadsheet," Xander said, leaning over to press a kiss to the top of her head on his way past to grab a chair, the gesture automatic enough that he barely broke stride doing it.

"It's not a spreadsheet, it's a chart."

"It's got columns and everything. That's a spreadsheet."

"The distinction matters to me. Let me have this one thing." Willow tipped her face up for a proper kiss instead, unwilling to let the first one count, which he obliged without complaint.

Faith leaned over to look, stealing a glance at Xander's hands, which had mostly returned to their normal color by now. "You look significantly less frostbitten than I expected, given the way you walked in here."

"I'm a fast healer. Also, threshold for frostbite drama was maybe lower than the actual medical reality. I'm fine. Slightly traumatized by goats, but fine."

"The goats didn't do anything to you."

"The goats made it look easy. That's its own kind of psychological damage."

"That's not a real injury category."

"It should be."

Jesse arrived partway through, dropping into his seat with the timing of someone who'd learned exactly which conversations were worth showing up for. "Did I miss the mountain story? Also did I miss the kissing, because that's twice now I've walked in mid-smooch and frankly I deserve a warning system." He caught sight of one of Faith's patrol contacts leaving as he came in — a girl he'd apparently never seen before, cutting across the yard toward the driveway — and tracked her the entire way to her car with an expression of pure, undisguised appreciation. "Also, unrelated, who was that, and is she single, and is now a bad time to ask."

"Now is an extremely bad time to ask," Faith said, without looking up from her plate. "Also she'd eat you alive, and not in the fun way."

"Noted. Filing that under 'aspirational' rather than 'immediate plan.'"

"We don't run a warning system for that, Jesse."

"You should. Public health and safety. I could walk in on something far more traumatic one of these days."

"You could not possibly be that unlucky."

"I contain surprisingly bad luck specifically around this table's romantic timing. It's a documented pattern."

"You missed the mountain story, but the short version is cold, goats, and Xander complaining about goats specifically."

"The goats were showing off. I stand by the complaint."

"I need the long version," Jesse said. "The short version never has enough suffering in it."

"There was plenty of suffering. My hands hurt for a full day."

"That's not suffering, that's mild discomfort."

"It felt like suffering at the time."

Willow, still absorbed in her chart, added a note in the margin that nobody else could read from across the table, then looked up, clearly pleased with herself for connecting two data points.

"Okay, this is actually interesting," she said. "Cold biomes and hot biomes both produce higher incident rates than temperate ones. It's not just extreme heat that's dangerous, it's extremes generally. That's actually a useful pattern."

"Fascinating," Xander said. "Truly riveting information, delivered approximately six hours after my hands stopped hurting."

"I can't control when the data comes together, Xander, science takes the time it takes."

"I'm just saying, next time, faster science. Preemptive science. Science before the trip, not during my recovery."

"I'll get right on inventing predictive biome science," Willow said with a grin. "Give me a week."

"A week's fair. I appreciate the effort either way."

The table settled into its usual noise after that, Faith stealing fries with her usual lack of shame, Jesse still lobbying for a more detailed retelling of the mountain trip, Willow's chart growing one more careful annotation in handwriting small enough that it would probably need its own key eventually, just to stay readable.

***

40.3. The Rest of the Family

Harry, Helen, and Gib showed up for dinner that Sunday, official enough that Joyce had actually ironed a tablecloth, and Xander found himself shaking hands with Faith's father for the first time with the distinct sense of being quietly, thoroughly evaluated in the three seconds the handshake lasted.

"Xander," Harry said, in a tone that gave away absolutely nothing about whatever conclusion he'd just reached. "Faith's mentioned you. Good things, mostly."

"Mostly good is honestly better than I expected, given how often I've probably embarrassed myself in front of her."

"That tracks with what she described," Harry said, and there might have been the smallest curve of a smile underneath it, gone before Xander could be sure.

Gib, by contrast, gave up any pretense of subtlety about thirty seconds after sitting down, leaning across the table with open, immediate warmth, like a man who'd apparently decided liking someone was more efficient than working up to it.

"So you're the wildlife guy," Gib said. "The one Faith says brought back an actual sloth. A car-sized sloth. In this yard. Is that a true thing that actually happened or is that Faith messing with her old man for fun?"

"Extremely true. I've got the plaque receipts to prove it."

"I need the whole story. All of it. Don't skip the boring parts, I like the boring parts, the boring parts are usually where the real story's hiding." Gib was already settling in, elbows on the table, treating a good story like its own kind of meal. "Also, I've been in a van outside probably forty different dangerous situations over the years, so trust me, I appreciate a guy whose job is technically 'go somewhere unsupervised and hope for the best.' That's basically my whole career too, minus the sloth."

By the time dinner actually finished, Gib had heard the sloth story twice, the penguin story once, and had started, unprompted, calling Xander "kid" — easy about it, like the designation had already been earned as far as he was concerned.

Helen, meanwhile, had gone very quiet partway through the sloth retelling, something clicking into place behind her eyes.

"Wait." She set her fork down. "Wait, that's — I read about that. Months ago. There was a small piece in a trade newsletter my old office still forwards me out of habit, about an unusually well-documented new species acquisition at some private zoological collection in California. I remember thinking it was strange nobody could explain the provenance." She looked at Xander with something between disbelief and delight. "That was you. That's been you this whole time. I've been idly wondering about a mystery zoo acquisition for months and it turns out my own daughter's — " she paused, glancing at Faith, working out the actual word she wanted, " — the young man in her life was the source the entire time."

"In my defense, it's not really a mystery I was trying to keep. It's just also not something that comes up naturally in conversation with people outside the family."

"No, I imagine not." Helen shook her head, still faintly delighted. "I spent nine years reading intelligence briefings for a living, and I did not see 'my daughter's closest friend is responsible for half the cryptozoology chatter in three states' coming. That's a truly wonderful thing to not have seen coming, for once."

Harry had been quieter through most of it, watching like a man doing careful math, and finally spoke up once the sloth and penguin stories had both been thoroughly exhausted.

"These trips of yours. The random-world jumps." He leaned forward slightly. "I've spent two decades assessing operational risk for a living. I'd like to actually look at how you prepare for one, if you'd let me. Not to take over — I understand this is yours, and I'm not looking to change that. But I've got twenty years of threat assessment built into instincts you might find genuinely useful, and it would mean something to me, being able to offer you that."

Xander considered it, surprised by how little resistance he actually felt to the offer. "Yeah. Actually, yeah, I'd like that. I've been doing this on pure trial and error for four years. Wouldn't hurt to have someone who does this professionally take an actual look at my system."

"I'll be gentle," Harry said, with the dry, level humor Xander was starting to suspect was the closest thing the man had to a default setting. "Mostly."

"Somehow I don't fully believe that."

"Smart," Harry said. "You shouldn't."

***

40.5. Chapter 40.5

Dr. Marcus Webb, the Mayor's original lead biologist before Elena Vasquez ever took over the role, made his one and only trip that spring, after months of quietly lobbying Alan Finch for the chance.

"I understand the risks," he'd said, in the meeting Xander sat through mostly out of politeness. "I've spent three years processing specimens secondhand. I'd like, just once, to actually see the source firsthand."

The world they landed in was temperate forest, unremarkable at first glance, though Marcus's reaction to it suggested otherwise entirely.

"This is extraordinary," he said, within the first hour, already taking soil samples with a rigor Xander had never quite managed alone. "An entire forest ecosystem, completely undisturbed by human activity, going back who knows how many centuries."

"I mean, yeah, that's kind of the whole deal with these places."

"Understanding it intellectually and witnessing it firsthand are very different experiences, Xander." Marcus knelt to examine a cluster of fungi with genuine reverence. "I want to document everything properly. Soil composition, canopy structure, the works."

They spent three days in careful, methodical documentation, Marcus working with a thoroughness that made Xander's usual notebook look almost embarrassingly casual by comparison, and by the final evening, the older man looked honestly moved by what he'd witnessed.

"Thank you," he said, simply, packing away equipment that had clearly seen much more use than expected. "For letting me finally see it properly. This will inform my work for years."

"Anytime. Genuinely. Feels like more people should probably get this chance."

"Perhaps. Though I suspect the logistics rarely make it simple." Marcus managed a tired, satisfied smile. "Regardless. I'm deeply grateful."

He retired two years later, the demands of the position and his own age catching up with him, and the practice of bringing researchers along quietly lapsed in the transition — not forgotten exactly, just set aside, until a new lead biologist, notably more insistent than her predecessor had been about actually seeing things herself, eventually revived it.

"Dr. Webb went once," Alan Finch told Elena, the day she finally secured clearance for her own trip, years later. "Best research he ever produced, by his own account. Never quite understood why nobody picked the practice back up after he retired."

"Well," Elena said, already packing her own field kit with visible determination. "Consider it picked back up."

***

40.7. The Prep Session

Harry showed up two days later with a legal pad and an unhurried focus that made it clear he intended to do this properly, and spent the first twenty minutes just watching Xander lay out his usual pre-jump routine without saying a word.

"Walk me through your actual decision tree," he said finally. "Not what you do. Why you do it, in that order."

Xander talked him through it — the pack check, the mental inventory, the four-years-deep habit of listening for wrongness in a landscape before doing anything else — and Harry listened the whole way through without interrupting once, pen moving steadily against the legal pad.

"Okay. Two things." Harry set the pen down. "First, your threat assessment on arrival is actually solid. Better than solid, actually, for someone who built it completely through trial and error. You listen before you move, you catalog escape routes without being told to, and you don't commit to investigating anything until you've confirmed it's not actively hunting you. That's real tradecraft. Nobody taught you that, you just survived your way into it."

"That's oddly the nicest thing anyone's ever said about my process."

"I don't hand out compliments I don't mean. Second thing." Harry tapped the pad. "Your contingency planning's thin. You've got a good in-the-moment read, but almost nothing for 'what happens if the jump timer runs out while I'm mid-crisis.' No fallback protocol, no dead-man's check-in, nothing that triggers if you go quiet for longer than expected on this end."

Xander turned that over a moment. "I've really never had a plan for that. I just — figured if it happened, it happened."

"That's the gap I'd like to help close, if you'll let me. Simple stuff. A check-in with Willow before you go and the second you're back, defined enough that a missed check-in actually means something instead of just being background noise. And a triage framework for the landing itself — since you can't know what you're walking into until you're already standing in it, the useful thing isn't deciding whether to go, it's having a clean set of criteria for what to do in the first sixty seconds once you're there. When to observe only. When it's safe to approach. When the answer is dig in, stay hidden, and just survive until the pull takes you home regardless of what you came for. None of it changes how you operate out there. It just gives you a framework to fall back on when you don't have time to think it through fresh, and gives the people back here an actual protocol instead of just hoping."

"Yeah." Xander nodded slowly, something settling into place that he hadn't realized had been missing until it was named. "Yeah, I'd genuinely like that. Willow's gonna love having an actual system instead of just vibes and a countdown."

"She strikes me as someone who'd appreciate a well-built system," Harry agreed, already sketching a rough check-in framework onto the legal pad, economical and precise, twenty years of field discipline condensed into a few clean lines. "We'll refine it together. You know the actual conditions out there better than I ever will. I just know what it looks like when a plan's missing a piece nobody noticed until it mattered."

"Thanks. I mean it." Xander watched him work for a moment. "This is — I don't know, it's a weirdly nice thing, having someone treat this like it's worth actually protecting properly, instead of just something I got lucky surviving four years of."

Harry looked up at that, something briefly unguarded crossing his face. "You survived four years of it with nothing but instinct and improvised gear. That's not luck, son. That's competence nobody ever bothered to formalize. I'm just here to formalize it."

***



Chapter 41: Chapter 41



Faith saw someone at the mural for the first time on a Tuesday night, and spent a full ten seconds deciding whether to trust her own eyes before deciding it didn't matter either way — someone was there, hooded, working on the wall by flashlight, and that was true whether she trusted it immediately or not.

She stayed back, further than instinct wanted her to, watching from behind a stack of shipping containers while the figure added something to the mural's edge, slow and careful, like precision mattered more than speed.

'Don't move yet,' she told herself, hand tight around the stake she wasn't planning to use unless she had to. 'Watch. That's the job right now. Watching, not fighting.' It took real effort to hold still, four years of training pulling hard in the opposite direction, toward action instead of patience.

The figure finished after another few minutes, stepped back to examine the work, and walked off into the dark without ever once looking her way — human-shaped, human-sized, moving with none of the wrongness that would've told her vampire on sight, which somehow made the whole thing more unsettling instead of less.

She waited a full five minutes before approaching the wall herself, and found the addition immediately: a new symbol, smaller than the others, positioned at the exact center of the mural like a keystone finally dropped into place.

She called Giles from the same payphone as before.

"Someone's actually working on it," she said, once he picked up. "In person. I saw them add something tonight. Human, not a vamp, far as I could tell."

"Human," Giles repeated, and she could hear him sit up straighter through the phone somehow. "That's a significant detail. Did you get a look at them?"

"Hooded. Careful. Didn't see a face. But they moved like somebody who does this a lot — no hesitation, no checking around, like they weren't even worried about being seen."

"Confident, or simply well-informed that nobody's watching this closely except you." Giles' voice carried an edge she hadn't heard from him before. "I don't like this development."

"Yeah. Me neither." Faith looked back toward the wall, the new symbol at its center catching moonlight faintly. "New mark's centered. Feels finished, almost. Like whatever this was building toward is close now."

"I'd like you to stop patrolling that block alone starting tonight. Not as a restriction — as a precaution I should have suggested sooner." Giles paused. "I should have realized weeks ago that a project this deliberate would eventually require someone showing up in person to tend it."

"You couldn't have known that."

"I could have anticipated it as a possibility. I didn't. That's a failure of imagination on my part, not yours." He exhaled audibly. "I'll come look at the new symbol myself, tomorrow, in daylight. Tonight, please, go home."

"Giles—"

"Please, Faith. You found this. You've tracked it carefully and well. I'm not asking you to stop caring about it. I'm asking you not to face whatever's building here alone, now that it clearly has a person attached to it instead of just a mystery."

She didn't argue further, mostly because some quiet, practical part of her agreed with him even as the rest of her chafed at being told to step back from something she'd spent weeks making hers.

"Fine," she said. "Tomorrow. Daylight. Together. And for the record, I'm still getting hot chocolate on the way. That's not up for negotiation either."

"I wouldn't dream of negotiating it," Giles said, and she could hear, even through the phone, that he meant the bigger word too, not just the small one.

***



Chapter 42: Chapter 42



Cordelia found the group at their usual table with the determination of someone who'd been saving up gossip all week and had finally found an appropriately captive audience.

"Okay, mountain goats," she said, sliding into a seat nobody had offered her, which had never once stopped her before. "Real, or is Xander exaggerating again? I need to know how much of my day I should spend being impressed."

"Real," Willow confirmed. "I've seen the sketches. Very accurate horn curvature, from what I can tell."

"And he almost got frostbite?"

"Almost is generous," Xander said. "My hands were cold. Cold isn't the same as almost losing fingers."

"That's not what I heard."

"Then you heard wrong, because I was there, and I still have all ten."

"I'm just relaying what I was told. Don't shoot the messenger, shoot whichever idiot actually started the rumor."

"By who?"

"Jesse. Who apparently told three separate people before lunch that you nearly lost a hand to a mountain."

Jesse, caught, had the decency to look at least mildly sheepish. "I may have embellished slightly. For narrative purposes."

"There's no narrative purpose that requires amputee rumors, Jesse. That's just you being dramatic because your own life is boring."

"That's harsh."

"It's accurate. Those aren't mutually exclusive either, apparently, since that seems to be everyone's excuse this week."

Faith, who'd been quiet through most of this, finally looked up from her food. "Can we talk about something that isn't Xander's near-death experiences for five minutes? I've got a personal record to maintain here of not thinking about mountains."

"Fine. New topic." Cordelia considered her options with visible, slightly bored deliberation, examining her nails like the whole table's conversational output had truly disappointed her. "Sandwich debate. Where does it stand currently. And it better be entertaining, because my alternative right now is actual homework."

The table groaned in near-unison.

"It's not resolved," Willow said. "It's never gonna be resolved. It's achieved a kind of permanent stalemate at this point."

"I still think it's a sandwich," Faith said.

"I still think it's not," Jesse said.

"See, this is exactly what I mean," Cordelia said, unimpressed. "Permanent stalemate. I've watched this argument happen at least six times now and nobody's ever budged an inch. It's like watching two rocks argue."

"For the record," Jesse said, "you staying to watch it happen a seventh time is kind of flattering."

"I'm not staying for you, Jesse. I'm staying because the alternative is being alone with my own thoughts, and frankly this is the lesser evil."

"I didn't say you were staying for me. I said it was flattering. Two different statements."

"Everything you say near me is somehow both a compliment to yourself and something I immediately regret hearing."

"That's a skill, actually. Very few people can pull off both at once."

Cordelia gave him a look that could have stripped paint, sharp and totally unamused, and Jesse, entirely undeterred, simply grinned wider, the exact same grin he'd been deploying at her, unsuccessfully, since roughly the ninth grade.

"That's the appeal," Xander said, before Cordelia's expression could escalate further. "If we ever actually resolved it, we'd have to find something else to argue about, and honestly, this one's low stakes and reliably entertaining. I don't want to lose that."

"You're defending an unresolvable argument on the grounds that it's fun. That's honestly the saddest thing I've heard all week, and I sat through Harmony's breakup story this morning."

"I'm defending an unresolvable argument on the grounds that it's the best unresolvable argument we've got, and replacing it feels risky."

Cordelia opened her mouth, closed it, and shook her head slowly, disgusted the way someone gets when they'd hoped for better and, once again, received exactly what they should have expected. "I don't understand any of you, and honestly, I've stopped trying. It's easier this way."

The debate reopened in full a moment later, Jesse's embellished frostbite story getting good-naturedly torn apart from three directions at once, and Cordelia, despite every stated intention of leaving, stayed at the table for the rest of lunch anyway, sharp comments still landing with regularity, apparently unable to resist the material even while insisting she was above it.

***

42.5. Chapter 42.5

Buffy came along this time, the two of them dropping into a world of towering red rock spires that looked, at first glance, almost too improbable to be natural.

"Okay, this one's dramatic," Buffy said, craning her neck up at formations easily ten stories tall. "Very alien-landscape energy."

"Very alien-landscape energy that's apparently also home to actual wildlife," Xander said, spotting movement along one spire's base. "Look."

Goat-like creatures picked their way along ledges barely wide enough for a single hoof, moving with the same casual, gravity-defying confidence Xander had seen before on actual mountains, except here the terrain seemed determined to make the whole endeavor look even more impossible.

"How do they not just fall," Buffy asked, actually baffled, watching one navigate a gap that looked, from their vantage point, utterly unbridgeable.

"No idea. I've asked myself the same question about basically every mountain creature I've ever met. They just don't. It's very unfair, honestly."

They spent two days working their way through the spire field, cataloguing not just the goats but a whole ecosystem tucked into the shadowed gaps between formations — moss, strange insects, a small stream running clear and cold through a canyon barely wide enough for both of them to walk single file.

"This is genuinely one of the coolest places you've taken me," Buffy said, on the second evening, curled into his side with her back against sun-warmed rock, his arm settled around her shoulders. "I get why you never wanted to stop doing this."

"Yeah. Even after everything else going on lately, this part still feels like the actual point. Just finding stuff nobody's ever seen, getting to be the first." He tipped her chin up and kissed her, unhurried, nobody around for a hundred worlds to interrupt them.

"Good point to have," she said, once she'd caught her breath, settling back against him.

"Yeah. I think so too."

The trip passed without incident, rare enough lately that both of them commented on it more than once, and by the time the jump pulled them home, they had photos of the strangest goats either of them had ever seen and a shared sense that the job had, once again, delivered exactly what it always seemed to when it mattered.

Willow had the door open before they even reached it, already reaching for the camera bag. "Tell me everything. Also, hi. Also, everything."

"Alien rock spires. Goats that defy basic physics." Buffy held up the camera like evidence. "I don't even know how they don't fall."

"I need every photo immediately."

"You've got them," Xander said, already handing it over.

***

42.6. Show, Don't Just Tell

"I want to see it," Harry said, over dinner, in the same flat, decided tone he probably used for briefing rooms. "Not hear about it secondhand, not read your protocol notes. See it happen. If I'm going to help build contingencies for this, I need to actually understand what I'm building them for."

"That's fair," Xander said. "Fair warning, though — it's not exactly something I can schedule. I don't pick the world. The world picks me."

"Understood. I'm not asking for control over it. I'm asking to be there when it happens." Harry glanced at Helen, some silent conversation passing between them in half a second. "Both of us, if you'll have us."

"Grab hands right when the pull starts, and you're along for the ride. That's really the whole mechanism. People have been coming with me for a while now." Xander shrugged. "Never actually brought parents along before, but I don't see why the rule would be different."

The pull came two evenings later, all three of them waiting it out at the cones together, Harry having insisted on arriving early enough to properly study the setup — the marked square, the rotating security detail, the whole quiet, practiced routine of it — and Xander had just enough presence of mind to grab Helen's hand and shout "NOW" the second he felt it start before the world folded away fully, Harry's hand finding his other wrist a half-second later, quick enough that all three of them landed together, stumbling slightly on ground that hadn't existed for any of them one breath earlier.

Rolling grassland stretched out in every direction, warm and open under an afternoon sun, a tree line breaking the horizon far off to the east.

Harry's reaction was immediate and entirely professional — a full three-hundred-and-sixty-degree visual sweep before he'd even fully found his footing, cataloguing terrain, sightlines, and cover with the practiced speed of someone who'd run exactly this kind of assessment in considerably worse circumstances.

"Clear," he said, mostly to himself, then looked back at Xander with something that might have been genuine respect. "You do this alone. Every time. No backup confirming your read."

"Every time," Xander confirmed. "You get used to it. Or, I don't know, maybe you don't get used to it exactly, but you get efficient at it."

Helen, meanwhile, had already crouched down, running a hand through grass that had never once been touched by anyone, something unguarded crossing her face. "It's real," she said quietly. "I believed you both, obviously, but there's a difference between believing something and standing in it." She looked up at Xander. "Thank you for this. Genuinely. I don't think I understood what my daughter's closest friend's actual life looked like until right now."

They spent the afternoon shadowing Xander's usual routine, Harry asking sharp, economical questions at every step — how he judged terrain before committing to a direction, how he decided when curiosity outweighed caution, what his actual criteria were for approaching an animal versus simply photographing it from a distance — and by the time evening began to settle in, he'd filled several pages of a small notebook he'd apparently had the foresight to bring along.

"Your instincts are better than most junior field agents I've trained," Harry said, watching Xander set up camp with an efficiency that came from four years of repetition. "I mean that as a genuine compliment, not a casual one."

"That might be the best thing anyone's ever said to me. Possibly ever. I'm gonna need a second to fully let that sink in."

"Take your time. You've earned it." Harry looked out at the grassland, gold and endless in the fading light, something almost peaceful settling into his usually watchful posture. "I've spent my whole career in places built for danger. This is the first job I've ever seen that was built completely for wonder instead, and somehow just as demanding. I didn't expect that."

"Yeah," Xander said, following his gaze out toward the tree line. "That's honestly the whole appeal, most days."

They made it home safely the next afternoon, no incidents beyond a curious herd of antelope-adjacent creatures that kept a respectful distance the entire trip, and Helen spent most of the drive back from the football field already composing, out loud, an totally new appreciation for exactly what her daughter's found family had actually built around itself.

"I owe Joyce Summers an even bigger thank-you than I already gave her," she said, mostly to herself. "This whole operation runs on more competence than half the actual government offices I used to work in."

***

42.7. Faith: The Phone Call

They told me over coffee, the morning after the grassland trip, and I could tell before either of them said a word that it wasn't gonna be the kind of news I wanted to hear, just from the careful way Dad set his cup down first.

"We have to go back," he said. "There's an operation Gib and I have been quietly running point on for the better part of a year, back before any of this, and it just went active in a way that doesn't leave room for anyone else to take it over cleanly. I wouldn't leave if there was any real alternative, and I sure as hell wouldn't leave this soon after finally finding you again, if I had any choice in the timing. I need you to know that."

I felt the old thing try to climb up my throat, the specific, familiar shape of watching people I loved walk out a door with no guarantee about the other side of it, and made myself breathe through it instead of just reacting on instinct the way nine years of practice wanted me to.

"How long."

"I don't know yet. Weeks, probably. Could stretch longer, depending how it unfolds." He reached across the table, steady, not flinching from the question. "This isn't nine years, Faith. It isn't even close to nine years. I need you to understand the difference, because I can see you doing the math against the wrong number right now."

"I know it's not the same." I did know that, actually, somewhere underneath the old reflex. Knowing it and feeling it easy were two different things, same as everything else about this whole strange, complicated month had turned out to be. "I guess some part of me figured you two would just — be around now. Living here. I didn't actually think that through, did I."

"No, and that's on us for not being clearer sooner," Helen said. "We were never going to be able to just relocate to Sunnydale and disappear from our actual work, sweetheart. That was never realistic, however much we both wish it was. What's realistic is this."

"You're never going to lose us to silence again," she went on, cutting in before I could spiral further, her voice carrying the certain flat certainty she used for things she'd already decided weren't up for debate. "That's not a hope, that's a rule we're setting, right now, out loud. You get a call every single week, no exceptions, no missed check-ins without an explanation the same day. You get a number that goes directly to me, not through anyone, not through any office, straight to my actual phone, day or night. If nine years of silence taught me anything, it's that silence is the enemy here, not distance."

"Every week."

"Every week. And if an op ever runs long enough that a week's truly impossible, Gib checks in on my behalf, because Gib's better at remembering logistics than either of us most days anyway." Helen's mouth twitched, something warm cutting through the seriousness. "You are never again going to wonder whether we're alive. I don't care what it costs to guarantee that. It's already decided."

"Gib's staying?"

"Gib's staying a few more days to make sure the handoff's smooth, then he's coming with us," Harry said. "He already tried to argue for staying behind entirely, actually, on the grounds that someone competent should keep an eye on you kids. I told him that's not remotely his call to make, and Giles pointed out, correctly, that this house has survived significantly worse than a temporary Gib absence."

That actually got a laugh out of me, small and real, cutting through some of the tightness still sitting in my chest. "He's gonna miss the sloth stories."

"He will never stop asking about the sloth stories, is what he'll do. You'll get updates. Possibly unsolicited ones, at length, via whatever number Helen gives you." Harry's face softened, some of the careful professional composure finally cracking into something plainer. "I know the timing's brutal, right after we only just got you back. If I could hand this off to someone else and stay, I would, without a second thought. I can't, and I hate that I can't, and I need you to know both of those things are true at once."

"I know." I did know it, actually, watching his face. Nine years of reading people for a living apparently ran in the family, because I could see exactly how much this was costing him to say. "I'm not — I'm not the scared kid who thought you were dead anymore, Dad. I know the difference between you leaving because you have to and you leaving because you're gone. I can hold that. I've got a whole house full of people here who'll help me hold it, if it gets hard."

"That's the best thing I've heard all week," Helen said, something fierce and proud crossing her face. "That right there. That's exactly the thing I needed to hear."

"Every week," I said again, needing to hear it confirmed one more time.

"Every week," Helen repeated. "I promise you that the same way I promised myself I'd find you, and look how that one turned out."

We stayed there a while, coffee going cold between us, and it wasn't the goodbye I'd wanted, not really, not this soon, but it also wasn't the kind of goodbye that used to live in my worst imaginings — no vanishing, no silence, no nine more years of not knowing. Just an ordinary, difficult thing, the kind normal families dealt with all the time, and something in that ordinariness felt, strangely, like its own kind of gift.

They left three days later, Gib's van packed with far more sloth-related merchandise than any grown man reasonably needed, and the house felt quieter for a while after — not empty, never empty, just missing a particular register of noise it had only just gotten used to having around. The calls came every week, exactly as promised, and slowly, over the weeks that followed, that steady rhythm became just one more thread in the whole strange, sturdy weave of a life that had never once stopped finding new ways to hold more people than it had any right to.

***



Chapter 43: Chapter 43



Willow had a debate tournament and Buffy had promised Giles a full weekend of extra training, which left this one solo, same as plenty still were.

The swamp jump announced itself with smell before anything else — thick, green, standing water everywhere he looked, humidity so heavy it felt like walking through soup.

"Okay," Xander said, already regretting several life choices that had led to this exact moment. "Okay, this is a whole thing. This is a lot of swamp."

He picked his way carefully along what passed for dry ground, testing each step before committing his full weight, four years having taught him that swamps had a way of looking solid right up until they weren't. The water moved sluggishly between cypress-looking trees, dark enough that he couldn't see more than a few inches below the surface, which bothered him much more than open water ever did.

'Anything could be in there,' he thought, giving a wide berth to a particularly still stretch of water. 'Anything at all. I'm choosing not to find out what.' It felt like the correct call, the kind of caution that had kept him alive through several less careful weekends.

He found the alligator — or the thing that filled the same ecological role an alligator would, close enough in shape that his brain sorted it there immediately — sunning itself on a fallen log late in the afternoon, bigger than any alligator he'd ever seen in a nature documentary, easily fifteen feet from snout to tail.

"Nope," he said, immediately backing up several steps. "Nope, that's a big one, that's a very big example of whatever that is, keeping my distance, thank you."

The creature didn't so much as open an eye, apparently as unbothered by his presence as everything else on every other world he'd ever visited, and he got his photos from a distance that felt honestly, professionally respectful rather than merely adequate.

He spent the rest of the trip giving all standing water a wide, generous berth, camping on the highest, driest ground he could find each night, and counted himself lucky that nothing more aggressive than mosquitoes had taken any real interest in him by the time the jump pulled him home.

"You're all bitten up," Buffy said, the second he walked through the door, examining the constellation of mosquito welts along both arms.

"Swamp trip. Very buggy. Also there was an alligator the size of a small boat, so I'm choosing to focus on the fact that it didn't eat me instead of the bug situation."

"An alligator."

"A very large alligator-adjacent thing. Willow's gonna need a new page just for reptiles at this point."

"She's gonna love that."

"She's gonna love that so much. I could see her whole face lighting up before I even finished the sentence, and I haven't even shown her the photos yet."

"Go shower," Buffy said with a grin. "You smell like a swamp."

"I was in a swamp."

"Doesn't make the smell better."

"That's exactly what you said about the desert. And the ants. And the mountain."

"Because it's true every single time," Buffy said. "Go. Shower. Bug bites can wait ten more minutes."

***



Chapter 44: Chapter 44



The mural looked different in daylight, which Faith hadn't expected — smaller, somehow, the crude lines less ominous under an ordinary morning sun than they'd ever looked under flashlight and moonlight.

"The new symbol's here," she said, pointing it out to Giles, who'd brought an actual camera this time instead of relying on her sketches. "Center of the whole thing. Wasn't there a week ago."

Giles photographed it from several angles before saying anything, kneeling close enough to examine the linework without touching the wall itself. "This is considerably more sophisticated than the earlier marks. Whoever added this understood what they were drawing, not merely copying a shape."

"Meaning the person I saw last night knew what they were doing."

"Meaning I'd very much like to know who trained them to do it." Giles straightened up, expression grim in the daylight in a way it hadn't quite managed over the phone the night before. "This isn't casual vandalism, Faith. This is someone executing a plan with actual expertise behind it."

"So what's the plan?"

"I don't fully know yet. But symbols of this nature, built in stages, centered on a specific point — historically, this pattern is associated with summoning circles. Not always. But often enough that I'd be irresponsible not to consider it."

Faith felt something cold settle in her stomach that had nothing to do with the morning chill. "Summoning what?"

"I don't know that either. The style doesn't match anything I've catalogued specifically, which could mean it's old, or foreign, or simply outside my specific expertise." Giles took a few more photographs, methodical despite the obvious tension in his shoulders. "I'll need to research this properly. Today, if possible. This isn't something I want sitting unanswered any longer than necessary."

"I want to help. Actually help, not just watch from behind a shipping container."

"You will help. Cross-referencing this properly will take considerable time, and I intend to use every set of capable hands available." Giles glanced at her. "You found this, Faith. That hasn't stopped being true just because it's grown more complicated than either of us expected."

"Feels like it should've stopped being just mine, though. Feels like a whole town problem now."

"It likely is a whole town problem. That doesn't make it any less yours to have found, or any less important that you keep contributing to solving it." Giles packed up his camera carefully. "Some of the most significant discoveries in Watcher history began with someone noticing a pattern nobody else had bothered to track. You did exactly that. I won't have you diminish it now simply because it's turned out to matter more than either of us initially realized."

Faith didn't have an immediate response to that, which had become rare enough around Giles specifically that she'd started to notice the pattern in herself as clearly as she'd noticed the one on the wall.

"Okay," she said finally. "Whole town problem. Still mine to help solve. I can work with that."

"Good." Giles headed toward the car, then paused, glancing back at her with something that might have been genuine warmth breaking through the morning's grimness. "I'll say this much — I'm glad it was you who noticed this, and not someone with less patience or less care about getting the details right."

"High praise, Watcher."

"I don't hand it out carelessly." Giles allowed himself a small smile despite the grimness of the morning. "Now come along. We have notably more reading to do than either of us would like."

***



Chapter 45: Chapter 45



The vampire hiding in the mausoleum had, by all appearances, been waiting there for hours, which Buffy only found out after she'd already walked past twice without noticing him.

"You're bad at this," she told him, once he finally lunged and she sidestepped without much effort. "Genuinely bad. I walked past you twice."

"I was being patient."

"You were being invisible in a way that only works if the person you're ambushing is also blind."

He swung again, wide and easy to read, and Faith caught his arm mid-swing from where she'd been leaning against a headstone the whole time, apparently having clocked him even before Buffy had.

"Rookie," Faith said, staking him with almost bored efficiency. "Didn't even check his surroundings before picking a spot."

"Some of them just don't get proper training," Buffy said. "It's honestly a little sad."

"It's a little sad that we're feeling sympathy for vampires now."

"I didn't say sympathy. I said sad. Different thing."

They cleared the rest of the cemetery without further incident, the night settling into the easy rhythm of a patrol that had turned up exactly one target and dispatched him without breaking much of a sweat, and ended up sitting on the low wall by the entrance afterward, the way they usually did when the night wound down early enough to actually enjoy the quiet.

"Giles thinks it might be a summoning circle," Faith said, without preamble, the words clearly having been sitting in her for a while before she let them out. "The mural thing. That's the current working theory."

"Summoning what?"

"Doesn't know yet. Says the style's not familiar." Faith picked at a loose thread on her jacket sleeve. "I keep running through what that could even mean. Demon. Something bigger than a demon. I don't love any of the options my brain keeps generating."

"You want backup on the research side? I can help dig through books, even if I'm not exactly Giles-level with the old texts."

"Appreciate it. Right now it's mostly a lot of waiting on Giles to find the right reference. Not much for extra hands to do yet." Faith looked over. "Thanks for asking, though. Means something, that you did."

"Anytime. It's still your thing to run. I'm just saying the offer's there if it turns into an all-hands situation."

"Noted. Filed under 'good friend, occasionally sappy.'"

"I'll take that filing."

They sat a while longer, comfortable in the quiet, before Faith finally stood and stretched, patrol clothes settling back into their usual loose, ready posture.

"West side's still uncovered tonight," she said. "You want it, or should I?"

"I've got it. You look like you could use an early night, given everything currently living rent-free in your head."

"Rude, but accurate." Faith managed a real grin despite everything. "Night, B."

"Night. Don't let the summoning-circle theory keep you up."

"No promises."

She didn't go straight to bed once she got in, either — paused instead in the doorway of Buffy's room, dark and quiet, moonlight catching along the hammer's handle where it still leaned against the closet door same as always. She crossed over without turning on the light, wrapped her hand around the grip, and pulled, gently at first and then with everything she actually had.

Nothing. Not so much as a shift.

"Yeah," she murmured, letting go, and blew out a breath through her nose, short and sharp, the kind she usually saved for a punch that didn't land clean. "Figured." She left before Buffy woke up and caught her at it, same as she always did, jaw tight the whole walk back to her own room, and didn't loosen it again until she was under the covers with the light off, staring at a ceiling that didn't have any answers either.

***

45.5. Chapter 45.5

Willow came along this time, glad for the chance after missing the last couple of trips to midterms, with absolutely no idea what she was walking into until the fog actually closed in around both of them.

The fog turned out to be a permanent feature, not weather passing through — a low mist that clung to the ground no matter the hour, thick enough to swallow anything more than a few yards off.

"Okay, this is atmospheric in a genuinely unsettling way," Willow said, peering into the gray. "Very haunted-moor energy."

"Very haunted-moor energy that's apparently also got wildlife in it," Xander said, catching movement at the edge of visibility. "Stay close. Don't want to lose you in this stuff."

They found the source of the strange calls they'd been hearing since arrival on the second day — creatures that moved like deer but called like something significantly stranger, voices carrying eerie and multiplied through the fog in a way that made both of them jump the first time they heard it up close.

"That is deeply unsettling," Willow said, notebook forgotten for a moment in favor of just listening. "In the best possible research way."

"Glad you're getting something out of my minor heart attack."

"I'm getting a lot out of it, actually. This is actually fascinating."

They spent the rest of the trip working carefully through the fog, cataloguing what they could, relying more on sound and touch than sight, and by the final day had developed an odd, comfortable rhythm — Willow listening for calls, Xander checking footing, both of them trusting the other in a way four years of solo work had never quite prepared him to expect from a research partner.

"This was a really weird one," Willow said, once the fog finally thinned enough that the last hours felt survivable instead of just strange. "Good weird, though."

"Yeah. Good weird's kind of the whole brand at this point."

They made it home with recordings Willow was already itching to analyze properly, tired in the good way that comes from a trip that asked something truly different of both of them and paid it back in full.

Buffy kissed them both hello in turn, then held Willow out at arm's length, studying her. "Okay, you've got a look. What happened."

"Deeply unsettling. Incredible. I loved every second of it," Willow said, already digging for her recorder to prove it.

"That tracks," Buffy said, grinning, and let herself be pulled toward the kitchen table to hear the whole thing properly.

***



Chapter 46: Chapter 46



Buffy had a family thing with Joyce that weekend, which left the island to Xander and Willow, and it turned out to be small enough to walk the entire coastline in a single day — something Willow discovered by accident on the first afternoon when she set out in one direction expecting hours of exploring and found herself back at the starting point well before sunset.

"Okay," she said, looking back at her own footprints curving around the whole visible shoreline. "That's a small island. That's a genuinely small island."

"Told you," Xander said. "I called it small the second we landed. Nobody believed me."

"In my defense, 'small' from you has meant several different sizes of disaster before."

Small didn't mean empty, though — a colony of birds occupied nearly every available ledge on the rocky north end, nesting in numbers that turned the whole cliff face into a single, constantly shifting mass of white and gray, noise loud enough to hear from well before they actually reached it.

"This is incredible," Willow said, already reaching for her own camera, notebook forgotten in her excitement. "This is an unreasonable number of birds for one small cliff, and I mean that as the highest possible compliment."

They spent hours there together, careful not to disturb the colony more than their mere presence already did, cataloguing what looked like at least three distinct species sharing the same real estate with the easy, established truce of creatures that had been doing this arrangement for longer than either of them could measure.

Willow caught herself watching Xander instead of the birds at one point, sleeves rolled up, hair windblown, utterly too pleased with himself over a photo he'd just gotten of a bird mid-dive, and had to physically shake herself back into focus. 'Nope,' she thought, firmly, redirecting her own attention to the actual cliff face in front of her. 'We are cataloguing seabirds right now. Seabirds. Not — that. You've had four years to get used to him being like this. Get it together, Rosenberg.' It didn't fully work, but she managed a reasonable approximation of scientific focus for at least another ten minutes before catching herself again.

"This is the kind of thing you actually get excited about," Xander said, watching her work, something fond in his expression that made her stomach do a small, entirely predictable flip. "Not just the weird ones. The ordinary stuff too, when there's enough of it in one place."

"It's not ordinary, it's an entire functioning ecosystem nobody's ever documented. There's a difference." She glanced over and caught him still watching her rather than the birds himself, and reached over to bump his shoulder with hers, grinning. "You're doing it too, by the way. Staring instead of observing."

"I'm allowed. It's a free island." He caught her hand before she could pull it back, tugging her in and kissing her properly, salt air and windblown hair and all, until she laughed against his mouth and had to physically push him back toward the actual cliff face.

"You're supposed to be helping me document seabirds."

"I am documenting. You're a bird. A very distracting bird."

"That doesn't even make sense."

"Fair. I stand corrected," he said, though he didn't look remotely sorry about it.

The rest of the trip passed easily, tide pools on the south end yielding another handful of strange, colorful creatures for the collection, a corner of the fish tank filling up by habit as much as intent, no predators large enough to worry about, no weather aggressive enough to threaten their gear, just two quiet days on a small island that seemed, for once, completely uninterested in testing either of them.

The pull took them home with a full memory card, a fish tank far livelier than when they'd left, both of them mildly sunburned in the forgettable way that came from sitting near open water too long, and a quiet, easy satisfaction that came from a trip that had asked nothing difficult of them in return for everything it gave.

"Another clean one?" Buffy asked, checking them both over out of habit when they walked through the door.

"Completely clean," Willow said. "Small island, lots of birds, no drama whatsoever. I almost don't know what to do with myself."

"You two keep saying that like it's a problem."

"It kind of is," Xander said. "I've built my whole identity around surviving things lately. A clean trip messes with my sense of self."

"You'll recover," Buffy said with a grin. "Give it a week. I'm sure the universe has something waiting."

"Don't say that. Don't put that out there."

"I'm just being realistic."

"Realistic feels a lot like tempting fate from where I'm standing."

"Maybe," Buffy said. "But you'll survive it either way. You always do. Both of you, apparently, now."

***



Chapter 47: Chapter 47



Buffy and Willow were both out with the flu that week, which meant the volcano trip was Xander's alone — maybe for the best, given how this one would end up shaking him.

Ash crunched under his boots before he'd even taken ten steps, which Xander clocked as a bad sign well before he spotted the source — a volcanic cone rising in the distance, thin smoke trailing lazily from its peak, close enough to worry about and far enough to make the worrying feel almost theoretical.

"Okay," he said, checking the wind direction out of pure instinct. "Okay, active volcano, noted, staying upwind, staying far away, very reasonable plan."

He kept his distance for the first two days, exploring outward from his camp in the opposite direction of the cone, cataloguing a stretch of scrubland that turned out to host a family of long-legged, ostrich-like birds too fast for him to get more than a distant photo of no matter how patiently he approached.

The ground trembled on the third morning, a low rumble that traveled up through his boots before he consciously registered the sound, and he was on his feet and moving away from the cone before he'd fully processed what was happening.

"Nope," he said, already putting distance between himself and the mountain. "Nope, that's a whole geological event, not sticking around to see how big."

The tremor passed without anything worse following it, no eruption, no ash fall, just a single unsettling reminder that the ground here wasn't as solid a promise as it usually was, and he spent the rest of that day considerably more alert than he'd been since arriving.

'Willow's gonna want the exact timing logged,' he thought, marking it in his notebook with genuine care. 'Frequency, intensity, anything that might tell her whether this world's more active than most.' It was, he'd learned, one of the few honestly useful things fear was good for out here — it made him a more careful observer, if nothing else.

He found the hot springs on the final day, tucked in a low valley well clear of the volcano itself, steam rising off water clear enough to see straight to a rocky bottom, warm enough to actually be pleasant rather than dangerous.

He sat at the edge for a long while, feet in the warm water, watching steam drift up against a sky gone orange with sunset, and let himself have a moment that had nothing to do with cataloguing or surviving — just quiet, earned rest after three days of being more careful than usual.

The jump pulled him home with volcano photos, tremor notes, and a genuine, hard-won appreciation for ground that stayed put.

Buffy was waiting when he walked through the door, the way she usually was, and for once he beat her to the first line.

"Don't ask," he said. "It's a whole thing. Active volcano. I'm fine. Ground shook once. I'm still fine."

"I wasn't gonna ask about your smell this time," Buffy said. "I was gonna ask if you're actually okay, because you've got a look on your face I haven't seen after a trip before."

He considered that, a little thrown that she'd noticed something before he'd even said anything. "Yeah. I'm okay. Just — back home, when the ground moves, I know what it means. Duck, cover, wait it out, it's over in ten seconds. Out there, staring at an actual volcano when it happened? No drills for that one. No idea if ten seconds was the whole thing or just the start of something a lot worse. Made the whole thing feel a little more real than usual, is all."

"Made what feel real?"

"That it's not actually a safe hobby. I know that, obviously, I've known that for years. But most of the time it feels manageable. Today it didn't, for about ten seconds." He shrugged, working to make it lighter than it had felt in the moment. "I'm fine now. Just wanted to say the quiet part out loud once, I guess."

"You can say it more than once, if you need to." Buffy pulled him into a proper hug, holding on longer than a normal homecoming usually warranted, and he let himself lean into it fully, some of the tension he'd been carrying since the volcano finally easing out of his shoulders.

"I know. I appreciate that." He managed a real smile against her hair. "For now, though, I think I just want food that isn't from a packet, and maybe an hour of not thinking about geology."

"I can arrange both of those."

***



Chapter 48: Chapter 48



Willow noticed something was different before Xander said a word about it, which didn't totally surprise him anymore after four years of her paying closer attention to his moods than he usually gave her credit for.

"You're quieter than usual," she said, sliding into the seat across from him at lunch. "Volcano trip get to you?"

"A little. Ground shook. Made the whole thing feel less like an adventure and more like an actual risk for about ten seconds." He picked at his food, less enthusiastic about it than usual. "I'm fine. Just needed a beat longer than I normally do."

"That's allowed, you know. You don't have to treat every trip like it's just material for my chart."

"I know. I don't always successfully remember that in the moment, though."

Faith arrived mid-conversation, dropping into her usual seat with her usual lack of ceremony. "Heard there was a scary moment. B mentioned it."

"There was a moment. Ground shook. I ran away from a volcano. Very reasonable reaction, I stand by it."

"Nobody's saying it wasn't reasonable." Faith studied him for a second, the teasing edge she usually led with noticeably absent. "You good, though? Actually good, not just saying it to end the conversation faster?"

"Actually good. Just had a genuinely scary ten seconds, and it's still sitting with me a little today. Not a whole crisis. Just a real moment I haven't fully shaken yet."

"That tracks. You've had a lot of scary moments lately that you mostly just turn into jokes right away. Maybe it's fine that one of them didn't immediately become a bit."

"Since when do you dispense emotional wisdom at lunch?"

"Since I've got my own stuff going on that's taught me you don't gotta turn every hard thing into a punchline the second it happens." Faith shrugged, a little self-conscious about the admission. "Sometimes you just gotta let it land a minute first. Then joke about it later, once it's actually settled."

Xander considered that, really, longer than he usually gave anything at this table. "That's actually good advice."

"I have my moments. Rare ones, but they happen."

"I'm gonna hold you to giving more of them."

"Don't push your luck."

Willow, who'd been quietly listening through most of this, finally spoke up, reaching over to rest her hand on his arm. "The chart's not going anywhere, and it can wait a day if you need it to. I'd rather you actually process a scary thing than rush straight into giving me data about it."

"I appreciate that. Genuinely." Xander managed a real smile, the first one since he'd sat down, turning his hand over to properly hold hers instead. "Ask me again tomorrow. I'll probably have the volcano timing all logged and ready by then."

"No rush."

"I know. But I think I'm actually ready now anyway. Talking about it helped more than I expected."

The table settled into its usual noise after that, gentler than usual for a few minutes before sliding back into its familiar rhythm — Jesse arriving to ask what he'd missed, the sandwich debate getting a halfhearted mention before everyone silently agreed to let it rest for one day, Faith stealing exactly one fry off Xander's tray on principle, same as always, the ordinary comfort of a group that knew when to push and when to just sit quietly together instead.

***

48.5. Chapter 48.5

Faith asked to come along on a whim, mostly out of curiosity about a job she'd heard described secondhand more times than she could count, and mostly because Giles' research had gone quiet for the week and she had nothing pressing to fill the gap.

"Figured I should actually see it," she said, shrugging into a borrowed pack, testing the weight of it with visible unfamiliarity. "Instead of just hearing about it after the fact. Just this once, though. Not making it a thing."

"No pressure either way," Xander said. "Grab my shoulder whenever you're ready. It's not exactly a countdown situation."

She grabbed it like she was defusing something, hand landing careful and precise, and the world went sideways and empty and strange all at once, the way it apparently did for everyone the first time, judging by the way Faith's whole body went rigid the instant the pull actually took hold.

The world turned out to be scrubland, dry and golden, dotted with rock formations worn smooth by wind and time into shapes that looked, from certain angles, almost deliberately sculpted. Faith stood very still for a long moment after they landed, taking it in with an expression Xander had never quite seen on her before — not fear exactly, more like someone whose entire understanding of how the world worked had just quietly, permanently shifted.

"Okay," she said, finally, voice a little unsteady. "Okay, that's — that actually happened. That's a whole different reality standing between me and Sunnydale right now."

"Yeah. Takes some getting used to. Everybody gets that same look, first time."

"I don't doubt it." She turned in a slow circle, taking in the rock formations, the endless gold grass, the sky doing nothing more dramatic than being an ordinary, cloudless blue. "This is nice. Quiet. Real quiet. Different kind of quiet than patrol quiet."

"Yeah, this kind doesn't usually come with the risk of getting jumped by something undead."

"Fair point. I'll take this kind."

They spent the first day working through the scrubland, Faith's patrol instincts proving genuinely useful in spotting hazards Xander might have otherwise missed — loose rock that shifted underfoot before he'd even registered the risk, a den entrance tucked into shadow that she clocked from thirty feet out, a stretch of ground that looked solid but sounded wrong when she tested it with a thrown stone first.

"You're good at this," Xander said, truly impressed, watching her navigate terrain with the same wary competence she brought to a graveyard at midnight. "Didn't realize how much patrol skills would actually transfer."

"Same instincts, different monsters. Or, well, no monsters here, but you get what I mean. Watching for what's wrong instead of what's right. Same muscle, different target."

They made camp that evening in the shelter of one of the larger rock formations, the shed staked out fast and easy on solid ground for once, and Faith spent a long while just sitting outside it, looking up at a sky much darker and notably more thickly starred than anything Sunnydale's light pollution ever allowed.

"You do this every week," she said, not quite a question.

"Every week. Four years now."

"Alone, this whole time. Before any of us started coming along."

"Yeah."

She didn't say anything else about it, but something in her expression shifted, some private reassessment happening behind her eyes that she clearly wasn't ready to voice yet.

They found the actual wildlife on the second day — a pack of small, fox-like creatures denning in the rock formations, wary but curious, watching the two of them from a cautious distance that gradually, over hours of patient stillness, closed into something almost like acceptance. One grew bold enough to approach within a dozen feet, sniffing at the air with obvious curiosity before retreating again, tail low but not frightened.

"They're kind of cute," Faith admitted, watching one investigate a discarded water bottle with visible suspicion, batting at it with one careful paw before deciding it wasn't worth further attention. "Didn't expect cute. Expected something scarier."

"Most of it's not scary, honestly. That's kind of the secret nobody believes until they see it. Mostly it's just quiet and weird and beautiful."

"Yeah. I'm seeing that now." Faith looked out at the scrubland stretching gold in every direction, something in her posture easing that had been tight since the moment they'd landed. "Glad I came. Needed something that wasn't patrol or research or waiting on Giles to find the next lead."

"Anytime. Seriously. I like the company."

They spent the third day documenting what they could, Faith proving a quick study at the actual mechanics of careful observation despite having no formal reason to have ever learned them, and by the time the jump pulled them home, both of them wore the good kind of tired that comes from a trip that asked something real of you and gave something real back.

"Good first trip?" Buffy asked, catching them at the door.

"Good trip," Faith said, considering the words carefully before settling on them. "Not sure I'm cut out to make it a habit, but glad I saw it once. Glad I understand now, even a little, what you've actually been carrying this whole time."

"Door's always open if you change your mind," Xander said, and meant it, filing the thought away without pushing further, the way he'd learned to with most things where Faith was concerned.

***



Chapter 49: Chapter 49



Giles found the reference four days later, buried in a text he'd nearly given up checking, and called Faith to the Magic Box the moment he'd confirmed it wasn't a false match.

"Cambion summoning," he said, sliding the open book across the counter before she'd even finished sitting down. "The symbol style matches a specific regional variant, used historically to call forth entities that are neither fully demon nor fully human. Dangerous. Not catastrophic, typically, but dangerous."

Faith stared at the page. "Neither fully demon nor fully human. That's a great category. Really specific. Really not vague at all."

"I didn't invent the terminology."

"I'm not blaming you, I'm just saying whoever did invent it really phoned in the naming convention."

Faith leaned over the page, studying the illustration beside the text — a symbol close enough to the mural's central mark that her stomach dropped a little just recognizing it. "That's it. That's the same shape."

"I believe so, yes. Which gives us significantly more to work with than we had yesterday." Giles removed his glasses, cleaning them slowly. "Cambion summonings require significant preparation — the mural itself, the accumulating marks, all of it consistent with someone building toward a specific ritual date, likely tied to a particular lunar or calendar alignment."

"So we've got time. Maybe."

"Maybe. I'd like to narrow the timeline further before we assume anything comfortable." Giles flipped forward several pages. "The text suggests these rituals traditionally align with specific astronomical events. If I can determine which one applies here, we may have an actual date to work against, rather than simply waiting for the mural to finish itself."

"What do we do once we've got a date?"

"That depends heavily on what we're dealing with. Some cambion entities are relatively benign once summoned, bound loosely to their summoner's will. Others are considerably less cooperative." Giles met her eyes. "I won't pretend I know which version we're facing yet. I'd rather tell you honestly that I don't know than offer false comfort."

"Appreciate the honesty. Genuinely." Faith looked back at the symbol, turning it over in her memory against everything she'd already catalogued from the mural. "So next step's finding the date."

"Next step is finding the date, yes. I'll need a few more days with the text. In the meantime, I'd like you to keep a closer eye on the mural than you have been — not engaging, simply observing, but documenting anything that suggests the timeline's accelerating."

"Already planned on it." Faith straightened up, notebook already coming out of her jacket. "This is actually starting to feel like something. Not just a hunch anymore. An actual thing with an actual name."

"It's very much an actual thing," Giles agreed. "And I want you to know — finding this reference took far longer than I'd have liked, largely because the sketch you provided was detailed enough that I kept second-guessing my own less precise memory of the relevant texts. Your work made this discovery possible, not merely convenient."

"You're just saying that."

"I generally don't say things I don't mean, Faith. You've likely noticed that about me by now."

She had, actually, which was part of what made the compliment land harder than it might have from anyone else in her life up to this point. "Yeah. Noticed."

"Good." Giles closed the book carefully, marking the page. "Go home. Get some rest. Tomorrow we start narrowing down a date, and I suspect neither of us is going to enjoy how close it might turn out to be."

***



Chapter 50: Chapter 50



Willow had a family obligation and Buffy was covering a double patrol shift, which left the canyon to Xander alone that week.

The canyon walls rose on either side of him almost immediately, deep red rock striped with pale bands that spoke of more time than Xander could properly wrap his head around, and he spent his first few minutes just craning his neck up at them rather than paying attention to anything at ground level.

"Okay, this is honestly beautiful," he admitted, to nobody, since nobody was around to appreciate the observation with him. "Willow's gonna be so mad she can't see this in person."

He followed the canyon floor for most of the first day, a dry riverbed underfoot that suggested water ran through here at least occasionally, and found the paintings on a sheltered rock face late in the afternoon — handprints, mostly, dozens of them overlapping in ochre and red, alongside simpler shapes that might have been animals if he squinted.

He stood in front of them for a long time, camera forgotten in his hand.

'Somebody made these,' he thought, the realization landing slower than it probably should have. 'Not recently. Not this world's current empty version. Somebody was here once, and now they're not, and this is all that's left of them.' It was a different kind of discovery than the animals — quieter, heavier, the first real evidence he'd found on any of these worlds that people had existed here before whatever had emptied them out.

He photographed the paintings carefully, respectfully, more mindful of his own presence than he usually bothered to be around wildlife, and made careful notes about position and condition for reasons that had less to do with Willow's biome chart and more to do with a feeling he didn't entirely have words for yet.

The rest of the trip passed quietly under that same weight, canyon wrens calling from ledges too high to photograph well, the dry riverbed eventually widening into a shallow pool that gave him water enough to restock without much effort, everything about the place carrying a stillness that felt less like emptiness and more like aftermath.

He rode the pull home with paintings photographed from every angle he could manage, water restocked, and a question sitting in his chest that four years of collecting animals had never quite prepared him to ask out loud.

"You're doing the quiet thing again," Buffy said, the second he walked through the door, though gentler this time, like she'd learned something from the volcano trip about how to ask.

"There were paintings. Handprints, mostly. People, once, on a world I've always just thought of as empty." He set his pack down slower than usual. "I don't know why it's hitting me this hard. I've known these worlds used to have people, theoretically. Seeing actual proof just made it real in a way I wasn't ready for."

"What do you think happened to them?"

"I don't know. I don't know if I want to know, honestly. Some part of me would rather just imagine they left somewhere better instead of finding out otherwise." He managed a small, tired smile. "Willow's gonna want the coordinates. I'll give them to her. I just — I don't know if I want her turning this one into just another data point yet. Feels like it deserves more than a chart entry."

"I think she'd understand that, if you told her." Buffy crossed the room and wrapped her arms around him, quiet and steady, not saying anything else for a long moment, just holding on.

"Yeah. Probably. I'll figure out how to say it." He looked at Buffy properly for the first time since walking in, forehead dropping to rest against hers. "Thanks for asking. For noticing there was something to ask about."

"Always," Buffy said. "That's kind of the whole point of this, right? Somebody's gotta be here when you get back, ready to actually listen instead of just wanting the highlight reel."

"Yeah," Xander said. "That's exactly the point."

***

50.5. What the Paintings Meant

He hadn't meant to see it. He'd only borrowed her laptop to check a jump schedule, and the document was already open, minimized but not closed, from whatever she'd been working on before dinner pulled her away — the canyon photos arranged in a careful grid down one side, and beside them, notes in Willow's familiar, cramped handwriting-style typing, clearly written for no audience but herself.

*These aren't random,* the note read. *Look at the placement. Every panel's got a clear sightline from the canyon floor — nothing tucked away, nothing hidden. Whoever made these wanted them seen. Not just by whoever was there at the time. By whoever came after.*

*I don't think this is a message. I think it's an invitation. People don't paint like this — this deliberately, this visibly — if they think nobody's ever coming back. You paint like this if some part of you believes somebody eventually will, and you want them to know you were here. That you mattered. That you're glad, on some level, they finally showed up to see it.*

*I haven't shown Xander this yet. I don't think he wants it turned into a data point, and he's right not to want that. But I keep thinking — maybe they didn't just vanish. Maybe they went somewhere. Somewhere else in whatever this whole tangled mess of worlds actually is. And this wasn't an ending. It was a goodbye, from people who figured somebody like him might eventually wander through and want to know they'd been real.*

*I could be wrong. I don't have proof, just a feeling and some pretty compelling placement geometry. But I don't think he found a grave out there. I think he found a letter. It just took four years and the right person walking through that canyon before anybody was actually there to read it.*

*I'll tell him when he's ready to hear it as a letter and not a chart entry. Not before.*

Xander stared at the laptop a long moment, something in his chest loosening that had been tight since the canyon, and something else utterly — warmer, quieter — settling in right behind it, the specific ache of finding out someone had already understood him this completely without ever once needing to be asked.

He closed the document gently, the way you'd close a door on a room you weren't supposed to be in yet, and found her in the kitchen twenty minutes later, and didn't say anything about what he'd seen — not that night, not for another few days, waiting instead for a quiet moment that felt like it had actually been earned.

"I think I'm ready to talk about the paintings properly," he said, eventually, once the moment arrived on its own.

Willow looked up from her notebook, something careful and hopeful crossing her face. "Yeah? Okay. I'd like that."
