"I think you already know what I'm gonna say, though. Some part of me thinks you got there before I did."

Something flickered across her expression — caught, not quite guilty, more like someone realizing a private thought had found its way into the open anyway. "Maybe. I've been sitting with some theories."

"Tell me anyway. I want to hear you say it."

So she did, careful and unhurried, the same words Xander had already read once in secret landing on him a second time with no less weight for the repetition, and by the time she finished, he'd crossed the kitchen and pulled her into a proper hug, holding on much longer than the moment strictly required.

"Thanks," he said, finally. "For not turning it into just a data point. And for understanding, before I even asked you to."

"Wouldn't dream of doing either," she said, warm against his shoulder. "Some things deserve to stay a letter, not a chart entry."

***



Chapter 51: Chapter 51



Jesse happened to be the one waiting in the driveway when Xander reappeared this time, having come by to return a video game and gotten roped into "guarding the spot" by Willow, who'd apparently decided someone needed to be there the exact second he came back.

"Okay, that's still the weirdest thing I've ever watched happen," Jesse said, as Xander blinked into existence a few feet away, duffel bag mid-swing like he'd never stopped moving. "Every time. Never gets less weird."

"Try being the one it happens to."

"I'd love to be the one it happens to. You get animals. I get nothing. Deeply unfair distribution of cosmic favors."

"You want the animals? You want the bees? The ants? The volcano that shook while I was standing on it?"

"Okay, not the volcano. I retract the request for the volcano specifically."

"Smart man." Xander dropped his bag with a groan. "This one was actually chill, for the record. Prairie world. Big open grassland, herds of something that looked like buffalo but weren't quite buffalo. No near-death experiences. Genuinely restful."

"You sound disappointed."

"A little. I've gotten used to having a story. This one's just 'saw some cool animals, had a nice time.' Doesn't have the same dramatic weight."

"I can make it dramatic if you want. I'll just add details."

"Please don't. Willow already has to fact-check your embellishments after the frostbite thing."

"That was one time."

"That was recent and memorable, is what that was."

Jesse held up the video game case like a peace offering. "Anyway, unrelated, here's your game back. I beat it, for the record. Didn't even need your tips."

"You definitely needed my tips. You called me twice."

"Those weren't tip calls, those were social calls that happened to be about the game."

"Sure, Jesse."

"They were!"

Willow appeared from around the side of the house at that exact moment, notebook already open, clearly having heard the telltale sound of Xander's return from two rooms away.

"Prairie world?" she asked, already flipping to a fresh page. "Buffalo-adjacent herd animals?"

"Word travels fast."

"I heard you say it through the window. Tell me everything. Also, did you get close enough for any actual measurements, or just photos?" Willow dropped down beside him on the porch step, close enough that their shoulders touched, and pressed a quick kiss to his cheek before flipping her notebook open, the greeting so automatic she barely paused the motion of turning the page.

"Get a room," Jesse said, fully without heat, already settling onto the step below them.

"This is the room. The room is outside. You're just also in it."

"That's not how rooms work."

"Neither is osmosis, according to Willow, so we're even on scientific accuracy tonight."

"Both," Xander continued, ignoring the interruption. "I'm learning, Willow. I'm becoming a better field scientist by osmosis."

"That's not really how osmosis works, but I appreciate the effort."

"I contain multitudes of scientific misunderstandings," Xander said, already reaching for his notebook, and the three of them settled onto the porch steps together, going through the day's findings while Jesse periodically interjected with increasingly implausible suggestions for how the story could be improved with just a little more danger added in.

***



Chapter 52: Chapter 52



Giles called with the date on a Wednesday evening, and Faith knew from the first word of his voice on the phone that she wasn't going to like the answer.

"Nine days," he said, without preamble. "The lunar alignment the text describes falls in nine days. I've checked it against three separate sources now. I'm confident in the timing."

"Nine days." Faith sat down heavily on the porch steps, phone cord stretched taut behind her. "That's not a lot of time."

"It's more than we had yesterday, when we didn't have a date at all. I'd rather have nine days of preparation than none." Giles' voice carried a steadiness he was clearly working hard to maintain. "I want you here tomorrow, and every day until then. We have notably more to figure out than just the date."

"Like what."

"Like what we're actually walking into. Whether this is a solo practitioner or something larger. Whether the entity, once summoned, can be stopped, contained, or must simply be endured until it chooses to leave on its own. I don't have those answers yet, and I won't pretend otherwise."

"But we've got nine days to find them."

"We've got nine days to find as many of them as possible, yes." A pause. "Faith, I want to say something, and I want you to actually hear it rather than deflecting the way you usually do when things get sincere."

She braced herself out of habit. "Okay."

"You found this. Weeks ago, before anyone else had any idea something was building. If you hadn't been walking that block every single night, hadn't noticed a pattern most people would have dismissed as nothing, we would have had considerably less warning than nine days. Possibly none at all." His voice was steady, certain. "Whatever happens over the next nine days, I want you to understand that the time we have is entirely because of you."

Faith didn't say anything for a moment, throat tighter than she wanted to examine too closely.

"Thanks," she managed finally. "For saying that. And for not making it weird by, like, giving me a whole speech about it."

"I believe I just gave you a whole speech about it."

"Yeah, well. A short one. I'll allow it."

"Generous of you." Something in his voice eased slightly, the tension of the day's discovery giving way, briefly, to something warmer. "Get some rest tonight if you can. Tomorrow we start working in earnest, and I suspect neither of us is sleeping particularly well between now and the ninth day regardless of how much preparation we manage."

"Speak for yourself. I sleep great during a crisis. It's the boring Tuesdays that keep me up."

"That's a deeply concerning thing to be true about you."

"Yeah, well, add it to the list." Faith let the joke fade before adding, quieter, "But we'll be ready. Whatever this is. We'll be ready."

"I believe we will," Giles said. "Goodnight, Faith."

"Night."

She sat on the porch steps a while longer after hanging up, counting nine days forward on her fingers like that might make the number feel less immediate, and found, underneath the fear that had settled in her chest the moment Giles said the word nine, something that felt almost like readiness — not confidence exactly, but the steady certainty that comes from having faced enough impossible things by now to know that impossible didn't automatically mean unbeatable.

***



Chapter 53: Chapter 53



The jungle river carried him half a mile downstream before he managed to get his footing back, which Xander decided afterward was probably the single fastest introduction any world had ever given him.

He'd materialized on what looked like solid bank, taken exactly two steps, and discovered the bank was significantly less solid than advertised, the current taking his legs out from under him before he'd even finished registering the word river.

"Okay!" he shouted, to nobody, mouth filling with water on the second syllable. "Okay, that's — that's a fast river, that's an actually fast river!"

He got to a low-hanging branch on the third grab, hauled himself up onto a muddy bank far further downstream than his actual camp gear, and lay there for a solid minute just coughing up river water and reassessing every decision that had led to this exact moment.

'Pack's still on my back,' he thought, patting himself down with hands that wouldn't quite stop shaking. 'That's something. That's genuinely something. Lost nothing, technically, just my dignity and about half a mile of dry ground.' The relief of the pack still being there did more to steady him than the actual rest did.

He spent the remainder of the first day drying out gear and nursing a healthy new respect for river currents, making camp well clear of the bank this time, and by the second morning had mostly recovered enough to actually start exploring the jungle proper instead of just recovering from his own poor first impression of it.

The butterflies found him before he found anything else worth noting — not one or two, but dozens, wings iridescent blue in a way that seemed to shift color depending on the angle he caught them at, settling on every surface including, eventually, his own arm when he held still long enough.

"Okay," he said, quietly, not wanting to startle them off. "Okay, this is really beautiful. This makes up for the river a little."

He spent an hour just sitting there, arm outstretched, more butterflies landing than he could easily count, and got photos that he suspected, even without Willow's expert opinion, were going to be some of the best he'd ever brought back.

'She's gonna frame one of these,' he thought, watching the light catch a dozen wings at once. 'I'd bet actual money on it.' It felt like a fair trade, in the end — a bad start, a good middle, and by the time the jump pulled him home, a genuine sense that the good had outweighed the bad by a comfortable margin.

He arrived home soaked through again, this time by the jump itself rather than any river, and found Faith at the kitchen table surrounded by books that hadn't been there three days ago, dark circles under her eyes that told their own story before she said a word.

"Rough few days?" he asked, dropping his gear by the door.

"Define rough." Faith didn't look up from the page she was on. "Nine-day countdown to a possible demon summoning kind of rough. That rough."

"...Right. That rough." Xander pulled up a chair across from her, setting his own concerns about rivers and butterflies quietly aside. "You want company, or is this a solo-suffering kind of research binge?"

"Company's fine. Just don't expect scintillating conversation. I've read the same paragraph about cambion binding rituals four times and none of it's sticking."

"I can't help with the research, but I can sit here and be a supportive presence while you glare at old books."

"That's honestly worth something." Faith finally looked up, managing a tired smile. "How was your trip? Anything good?"

"River almost ate me, then a few hundred butterflies decided I was furniture. Genuinely great trip, all things considered."

"That's a hell of a range for one weekend."

"Welcome to my life," Xander said, and settled in beside her, quietly present while she went back to her books.

***



Chapter 54: Chapter 54



Buffy showed up at the Magic Box on day three of the countdown without being asked, dropped a stack of her own patrol notes on the table, and didn't wait for permission before pulling up a chair.

"I'm not asking to help," she said, before Faith could object. "I'm telling you I'm helping. Six days left and Giles can't cross-reference everything alone."

"I wasn't gonna tell you no."

"Good. Because I wasn't really giving you the option." Buffy flipped open the nearest book, squinting at text that clearly wasn't in English. "Okay, I have no idea what any of this says, but I can at least hold books open while you two do the actual reading."

"That's more helpful than it sounds," Giles said, not looking up from his own stack. "Genuinely. An extra set of hands sorting relevant material from irrelevant saves considerable time."

Willow arrived twenty minutes later with her own notebook, the biome chart set firmly aside for the week, and Xander trailed in not long after that, having apparently decided his jump recovery could wait until the more pressing crisis got handled first.

"I can't read Latin," he announced, dropping into a chair, "or whatever half this stuff is written in, but I can fetch things, make coffee, and provide moral support. Consider me on standby."

"Moral support's underrated," Faith said. "I'll take it."

They worked through the afternoon in a rhythm that had become familiar to all of them over the past several years — Giles and Faith doing the actual translation work, Buffy and Willow sorting and cross-referencing, Xander running coffee and snacks and the occasional truly useful question that came from having no expertise to bias his instincts.

"What happens if we can't stop it in time?" he asked, somewhere around the third hour, the question landing heavier than the room's easy working noise had prepared anyone for.

Giles set down his pen. "Then we face whatever comes through, together, with whatever we've managed to learn by then. I won't pretend that's an ideal contingency. But it is, at minimum, a plan, rather than simply hoping the ritual fails on its own."

"That's not super reassuring."

"No," Giles admitted. "But it's honest, and I'd rather be honestly unreassuring than falsely comforting, given what's actually at stake."

Faith looked around the table — Buffy squinting at a passage she clearly couldn't parse but refused to give up on, Willow cross-referencing three books at once with the same focused intensity she brought to everything, Xander hovering with coffee refills nobody had actually asked for but everyone was quietly grateful for anyway.

"We're gonna be ready," she said, mostly to herself, though the whole table heard it. "Whatever this actually turns out to be. We've got six days and four people who won't quit until we've squeezed every answer out of these books that exists to find."

"Five people," Buffy corrected. "You're forgetting to count yourself."

"I wasn't forgetting. I just don't usually put myself first on the list."

"Start now," Buffy said. "You found this. You're the reason we've got six days instead of zero. Put yourself first on the list, at least this once."

Faith didn't have a comeback for that, which by now had become less rare than it used to be, and she went back to her book instead, the quiet warmth of the moment settling into the working noise of the room without anyone needing to comment on it further.

***



Chapter 55: Chapter 55



Xander almost didn't go on schedule, which had never happened before in four years of jumps, and he stood at the cones for a long moment before the pull started, feeling honestly torn about leaving in the middle of a six-day countdown that had turned into a five-day one overnight.

"It doesn't stop for crises," Willow had told him the night before, when he'd floated the idea of somehow skipping it. "I checked. There's no version of the mechanism that cares what else is happening. You go when you go."

So he went, same as always, and found himself standing in a meadow full of wildflowers so bright they looked almost artificial, gentle hills rolling away in every direction under a sky doing its best impression of a perfect day.

"Okay," he said, taking it in despite the weight he'd carried through the jump with him. "This is annoyingly nice, given everything happening back home."

He worked through the trip on autopilot for the first day, cataloguing a herd of small, deer-like animals with genuine but distracted attention, his mind circling back to Faith's dark-circled eyes and Giles' careful honesty about the contingency plan more often than any actual wildlife in front of him.

'She's gonna be fine,' he told himself, more than once, watching a small herd graze without much urgency in his own observation of them. 'They're all working on it. Giles wouldn't let anything actually bad happen if he could help it. Buffy's there. Willow's there. I'm just — not there, right now, and that's the part that's actually bugging me.' It was, he realized somewhere around the second day, the first time a jump had ever felt like an inconvenience rather than an adventure.

The flowers turned out to attract something worth photographing eventually — a cloud of what he could only describe as hummingbirds the size of sparrows, moving between blooms with a speed that made his eyes struggle to track them properly, colors flashing between blue and green depending on the angle of the light.

He got his photos, methodical despite his distraction, and found that focusing on the actual job helped settle some of the restlessness that had been sitting in his chest since he'd left.

'This still matters,' he thought, watching one hover impossibly still in front of a bloom. 'Even with everything else going on. This is still real, still worth doing right.' It felt like an important thing to remember, even if he couldn't have said exactly why until he tried putting it into words.

He made it home a full day earlier than the countdown clock back in Sunnydale, technically, though it never quite felt that way given how much longer three days always stretched out here, and walked straight into the Magic Box still carrying his pack, hummingbird photos already loaded and ready.

"Four days left," Faith said, without looking up, exhaustion carved plainly into her face. "How was the trip?"

"Good. Genuinely good, actually, which felt weird given everything. I kept thinking about you guys the whole time." He set his pack down and pulled up a chair beside her. "What do you need? Put me to work. I've got nothing better to do for the next four days than help however I actually can."

"Coffee," Faith said. "And maybe just sit here a while. Having people around helps more than I probably say out loud enough."

"I can do both of those. Easily. All day, if that's what it takes."

She managed a real smile at that, tired but genuine, and Xander settled in beside her, notebook of hummingbird measurements set quietly aside in favor of the more immediate job of simply being present.

***



Chapter 56: Chapter 56



Willow found the counter-ritual reference at nearly two in the morning, three days out from the deadline, and the sound she made was loud enough that Giles nearly knocked over his tea reaching for his own book in alarm.

"I found something," she said, already flipping back through the pages to confirm she hadn't misread it the first time. "A binding counter-rite. It won't stop the summoning outright, but if we can get it in place before the ritual completes, it should be able to contain whatever comes through instead of letting it loose."

Giles was at her side in seconds, reading over her shoulder with an intensity that made Faith, half-asleep in the chair across the room, sit up fully awake.

"This is genuine," Giles said, after a long moment. "This is a real counter-rite, properly documented, not some folk remedy dressed up as scholarship." He looked up at Willow with something that bordered on awe. "How did you find this? I searched this exact section twice already."

"I wasn't looking for a counter-rite specifically. I was cross-referencing symbol styles again, trying to nail down exactly which regional tradition the mural belongs to, and this popped up as a footnote in a completely different context. I almost skipped past it."

"Thank god you didn't." Faith was on her feet now, crossing to look at the page herself. "What's it need? Materials, timing, all of it?"

"That's the complicated part," Willow admitted. "It needs to be cast by someone with an actual connection to the location — not a random practitioner, someone tied to the specific ground the summoning's happening on. And it needs four supporting anchors, positioned at cardinal points, held by people willing to maintain the binding through physical contact for the duration of the ritual."

"Four people," Giles said slowly. "Holding contact points while whatever comes through tries to break loose."

"That's what it says."

The room went quiet for a moment, the weight of what that actually meant settling in.

"I'll do it," Buffy said, from the doorway, where she'd apparently been standing long enough to catch the tail end of the discussion. "One of the anchor points. Obviously."

"Me too," Xander said, immediately, not even pretending to weigh the decision first.

"You don't have any magical ability," Giles pointed out, gently.

"Doesn't matter, does it? You said held by people willing, not people with training. I'm willing. I'll hold whatever needs holding."

"He's right," Willow said, checking the text again. "It doesn't specify magical capability. Just willingness and physical presence."

"Then that's three," Faith said. "Me, Buffy, Xander. We need a fourth."

"I'll take the fourth position," Giles said. "Which leaves the actual counter-rite casting to whoever has the strongest tie to the location itself."

"Wait, are we assigning cardinal directions too, or is it just whoever grabs a corner first," Xander asked. "Because I'd like to request north. North sounds important. I want to be important north."

"It doesn't specify," Willow said. "I think any corner works."

"Then I'm claiming north preemptively. Just for the record."

"Noted," Giles said, dry enough that it almost passed for a smile, before his expression sobered again.

"That's me," Faith said, without hesitation. "I found it. I've walked that block every night for weeks. If anybody's got a tie to that ground, it's me."

Giles studied her for a long moment, clearly weighing the risk against the truth of what she'd just said. "I won't argue the logic. I will say — this puts you at the center of whatever happens, more exposed than any of us."

"I know. I'm doing it anyway." Faith met his eyes steadily. "Found this thing weeks ago because I wouldn't let it go. Not gonna let go of it now, three days out, just because the actual hard part's arrived."

Nobody argued further. The room eased into planning instead, four anchor points and a counter-rite and three days left to make sure everyone understood exactly what they'd need to do when the moment actually came.

***



Chapter 57: Chapter 57



"You just have to hold on," Giles said, for what felt like the tenth time that afternoon, demonstrating the grip pattern again on the practice circle he'd chalked onto the Magic Box floor. "Whatever happens, whatever you feel or see, the anchor point requires physical contact maintained without interruption. That's the entirety of the job."

"Sounds simple when you say it like that," Xander said, taking his position at the northern point exactly like he'd claimed. "Feels like it's gonna be much less simple when there's an actual half-demon trying to get loose in the middle of it."

"It likely will be. Which is precisely why we're practicing now, while the stakes are theoretical." Giles moved to check Buffy's stance at the western point. "The binding draws on your will as much as your physical presence. If you're frightened, that's acceptable. If you let go, even briefly, the binding weakens for everyone."

"No pressure," Buffy muttered.

"Considerable pressure, actually. I won't pretend otherwise." Giles moved to his own position at the south. "Faith, whenever you're ready."

Faith stood at the center, the practice version of the counter-rite text open on a stand in front of her, and took a slow breath before beginning the incantation — halting at first, then steadier as the words found their rhythm, the four anchor points holding their positions around her while nothing, thankfully, actually happened, since this was only a rehearsal.

"That felt weird," Xander said, once she'd finished. "Like static, almost. Right before you stopped talking."

"You felt that?" Faith looked genuinely surprised. "I wasn't sure the practice version would carry any actual charge."

"A little bit did, apparently," Giles said, considering this with visible interest despite the gravity of the day. "Which is actually useful information — it suggests the binding responds even to imperfect, unpowered casting. That bodes well for the actual attempt."

"Bodes well is a low bar today," Faith said, but there was real relief underneath the joke.

They ran through it three more times, adjusting grip and positioning each time, until even Xander — who readily admitted having no idea what he was actually supposed to be feeling — reported the same faint static building each time Faith reached the incantation's midpoint.

"I think that's as ready as rehearsal can make us," Giles said finally, checking his watch. "Two days remain. I'd suggest everyone rest properly tonight. Tomorrow will likely be difficult regardless of preparation, and exhaustion will only make it harder."

"Easier said than done," Faith admitted. "Not sure sleep's coming easy with all this hanging over tomorrow."

"No," Giles agreed. "I doubt it will for any of us. But try regardless. We'll need everyone as steady as possible."

The group dispersed slowly, Xander lingering longest, checking his own grip one more time against the chalk circle like the extra repetition might make tomorrow feel less enormous.

"You'll be great," Faith told him, catching him at it. "You're the only one of us with zero magical training and you still picked up the static on the first real try. That's gotta count for something."

"Counts for a lot, actually. Feels good to hear it out loud." He managed a real smile. "You're gonna be great too, just so that's on record somewhere."

"Working on believing that myself. Getting there."

"That's all anybody can ask, two days out."

"Yeah," Faith said. "Guess it is."

***



Chapter 58: Chapter 58



Joyce found out about the ritual the night before, which Buffy had been putting off telling her mostly because there wasn't a version of the conversation that didn't sound terrifying out loud.

"I need to tell you something," Buffy said, sitting down across from her at the kitchen table, "and I need you to let me finish before you react."

Joyce set down her coffee slowly. "Okay."

Buffy told her everything — the mural, the countdown, the counter-rite, the four anchor points — and watched her mother's face cycle through fear, then something that looked almost like pride, then back to fear again by the time she finished.

"You're one of the anchors," Joyce said finally. "You and Xander both. Neither of you have magical training."

"We don't need it. Just willingness and physical presence. Giles tested it. It works."

"That doesn't make it not terrifying to hear, Buffy."

"I know. I'm sorry. I wish I could make it sound less scary, but I don't think there's a version of tomorrow that doesn't sound scary, no matter how I say it."

Joyce was quiet for a long moment, hands wrapped around her coffee mug like she needed something solid to hold onto. "Is Faith ready? Really ready, not just saying so because it's expected of her?"

"She's ready. Scared, but ready. She's had more prep time than any of us, mentally — she's been walking toward this moment for weeks now, longer than she even fully understood at the time."

"And you trust the plan. Genuinely trust it, not just hoping it works."

"I trust Giles. I trust Willow's research. I trust that we've done everything we can to prepare." Buffy reached across the table, covering her mother's hand with her own. "I can't promise it'll be easy. I can promise we're not walking in blind, and we're not walking in alone."

Joyce nodded slowly, something settling in her expression that looked like acceptance even though it clearly cost her something to arrive at. "I want to be there. Not in the circle. Just — nearby. The way we were for the reservoir thing, whatever that turns out to mean tomorrow."

"I'd like that too, actually. Having you close by helped last time, even from a distance."

"Then I'll be there." Joyce squeezed her hand once, firm. "Whatever happens tomorrow, you're coming home. Both of you. All of you, actually, the whole ridiculous, wonderful group of you."

"We will," Buffy said, and found she believed it more than she expected to, sitting there with her mother's hand steady around her own, the kitchen quiet and ordinary around a conversation that had been anything but.

Upstairs, later, Faith found her on the landing, both of them unable to settle into actual sleep despite the day's exhaustion.

"Your mom take it okay?" Faith asked.

"About as okay as anyone takes hearing their kid's about to anchor a magical containment ritual. Which is to say, not great, but she's coming tomorrow anyway. Wants to be nearby."

"That's good. That she wants to be there." Faith leaned against the wall beside her. "You scared?"

"A little. You?"

"A lot, if I'm honest. Haven't been this scared in a long time." Faith looked over. "Glad it's you holding one of the corners tomorrow, though. Makes the scared part easier to carry."

"Same," Buffy said. "Get some sleep if you can. Big day."

"Yeah. Trying." Faith managed a small, tired smile. "See you on the other side of tomorrow, B."

"See you there," Buffy said, and meant it as a promise more than a farewell.

***



Chapter 59: Chapter 59



The mural was glowing faintly by the time they arrived, the accumulated symbols catching moonlight in a way that felt less like reflection and more like the wall itself had started producing its own light from somewhere underneath the paint.

"Positions," Giles said, quiet but firm, and the four of them moved to the cardinal points around the wall without further discussion, weeks of rehearsal taking over where nerves might otherwise have slowed them down.

"I'm at north," Xander said, mostly to hear his own voice do something other than shake. "Still north. Claimed it fair and square three weeks ago, still holding it now, under actual pressure, exactly like I said I would."

"Nobody's contesting your corner, Xander."

"I know. I'm just narrating. It's helping. Let me narrate."

Faith stood at the center, counter-rite text held steady despite her hands not being completely steady themselves, and looked around the circle one last time before beginning — Buffy at the west, solid and ready; Xander at the north, visibly pale but standing firm; Giles at the south, expression carved into the same careful calm he brought to everything that actually mattered.

"Whatever happens," she said, to all of them, "thank you. For being here. For believing this was real weeks before anybody had proof."

"Always," Buffy said. "Now do the thing, before I lose my nerve standing here being sentimental about it."

"Same, honestly," Xander added. "The sentimental window's closing fast over here."

Faith almost laughed, the tension breaking just enough to let her actually start.

The incantation felt different from rehearsal immediately — the words carrying real weight this time, the air around the mural thickening with something Faith could feel against her skin like pressure before a storm. Halfway through, the wall's glow intensified sharply, symbols flaring bright enough to cast hard shadows across all four of their faces.

"That's new," Xander said, through gritted teeth, gripping his corner of the wall like it might try to leave without him. "That's a new light situation. Nobody rehearsed the light situation."

"Hold," Giles called out, voice tight but controlled. "Whatever you feel, hold your positions."

"Holding! Very much holding! Just also narrating the holding, for my own benefit!"

The pressure built, and built, and Faith kept speaking through it, words she'd rehearsed a dozen times now feeling like they were being pulled out of her rather than simply spoken. Somewhere to her left she could hear Xander muttering a steady stream of half-panicked commentary under his breath — "okay, okay, that's a lot of pressure, that's a lot of pressure for a Tuesday" — and found, absurdly, that the sound of it actually helped, something familiar and human to hold onto in the middle of something that felt anything but.

At the ritual's peak the mural cracked open — not physically, but in some other sense, a seam of pure white light splitting down its center, something on the other side of it pressing against the opening like it could feel the barrier weakening.

Xander didn't say anything. His hand went white-knuckled against the stone, jaw locked, every scrap of narration finally failing him at the one moment that actually deserved it.

"It's working," Willow shouted, from outside the circle where she'd been monitoring with her own notes clutched tight. "The containment's forming, I can see it, keep going!"

Faith pushed through the final lines, voice cracking on the last word, and the white light snapped inward instead of outward, the pressure collapsing all at once into something considerably calmer — contained, present, but no longer straining against the edges of whatever cage the counter-rite had built around it.

Silence, for a long moment. Nobody moved.

"Is it over?" Xander asked finally, voice still shaking, hand still locked against his corner of the wall like it hadn't gotten the memo yet.

"I believe so," Giles said, though he didn't release his own position yet, watching the mural carefully for any sign of renewed pressure. "The containment appears to be holding."

"Can I let go of the wall now, or is this a trick question."

"You may let go of the wall, Xander."

"Thank god." He peeled his hand off the stone like it had personally betrayed him, flexing his fingers with visible relief. "For the record, that was the longest ten minutes of my entire life, and I say that as a man who has been chased by bees."

They stayed in position another five full minutes before Giles finally gave the word, and Faith's legs nearly gave out the moment she stepped back from the center, exhaustion crashing over her all at once now that the adrenaline had nowhere left to go.

Buffy caught her before she fully went down, both of them ending up in an undignified heap against the pavement, laughing and shaking in roughly equal measure.

"We did it," Faith said, into Buffy's shoulder, voice muffled and disbelieving. "We actually did it."

"We actually did it," Buffy confirmed. "You did it. This was yours from the start, and you saw it through to the end."

"We all did it. Team effort. Very team effort." Faith pulled back enough to look at the others — Xander still shaking out his hand like the stone had actually wronged him; Giles carefully examining the now-dormant mural with something like scholarly satisfaction underneath his obvious relief; Willow already scribbling notes about the containment's exact appearance, because apparently no crisis was too large to also be data.

"Whatever's actually in there," Giles said, straightening up from his examination, "it's contained. Properly, thoroughly contained. I'd like to study it further, at length, but not tonight. Tonight, I think we've all earned notably less academic pursuits."

"I vote food," Xander said. "A lot of food. And also, permanently, no more claiming corners of anything ever again, even for bragging rights."

"Duly noted," Giles said, and something in his exhausted, relieved expression suggested he found the narration really fond rather than exasperating now that it was safely over.

They made their way back to the cars slowly, exhausted in the loose, hollowed-out way that only comes after real fear has finally, fully passed, and found Joyce waiting exactly where she'd promised to be — close enough to see them coming, far enough to have stayed safely out of the way, already moving to meet them the moment she saw Faith walking upright and whole.

***



Chapter 60: Chapter 60



Joyce's list got its strangest entry yet the following Sunday: my daughter and her sister-in-everything-but-blood successfully contained a half-demon summoning using a ritual they learned from a library book, and I made lasagna afterward because I didn't know what else to do with my hands.

"You wrote all that?" Buffy asked, reading over her shoulder.

"I needed somewhere to put the feelings. The list seemed appropriate."

"That's the longest entry you've ever written."

"It was the longest week I've ever had," Joyce said, passing the lasagna down the table. "I'm allowed extra words for it."

Faith, significantly more rested than she'd looked all week, reached for the garlic bread with something closer to her usual energy. "For the record, Giles wants the mural studied properly starting tomorrow. Says whatever's contained in there is truly rare and he'd like to understand it better before deciding what to actually do with it long-term."

"Define 'do with it,'" Xander said, reaching over to take Buffy's hand under the table, the small gesture more about needing the contact himself than anything performative. "Because I'm hoping that doesn't mean it lives in the Magic Box basement indefinitely."

Buffy squeezed back, leaning her head briefly against his shoulder before straightening back up for dinner, neither of them making a thing of it.

"Don't know yet. That's tomorrow's problem. Tonight I'm just eating lasagna and not thinking about summoning circles for at least a few hours."

"Solid plan. I fully endorse that plan."

Willow, notebook uncharacteristically absent from the table for once, looked genuinely relaxed for the first time in over a week. "I already started writing up what I observed. Not for the chart — this needs its own separate file. This was honestly new information, magically speaking."

"Everything's data to you," Faith said, with real fondness rather than the usual teasing edge.

"It's how I process things. Also, purely as an aside, the light patterns during the containment were actually fascinating from a purely observational standpoint, completely separate from how terrified I was the whole time."

"Multitasking," Xander said. "Truly impressive multitasking, being terrified and taking mental notes simultaneously."

"Turns out I've got a talent for that too," Willow said with a grin, and the table laughed harder at that than the joke probably deserved, the kind of laughter that came easier after a week this heavy finally lifted.

Jesse, who'd shown up for dinner on the strength of having heard "there was a demon thing" and refusing to be left out of the debrief, leaned in with genuine curiosity. "Okay, but the actual scary part — what did it feel like? The pressure thing, right before it got contained?"

Faith considered the question seriously, more seriously than she usually gave anything at this table. "Like something enormous was leaning against a door I was holding shut with just my voice. That's the closest I can describe it. Scariest ten minutes of my whole life, and I've fought actual vampires hand to hand."

"That's really terrifying."

"It was. I'm allowed to say that now that it's over and everybody's okay."

"You're allowed to say it either way," Buffy said. "Before, during, after. Doesn't matter when. It was scary. That's just true."

"Yeah," Faith agreed. "It really was."

The table settled into its usual comfortable noise after that, the heaviest week in recent memory finally allowed to loosen its grip, Joyce refilling glasses, Xander already angling the conversation toward his next jump in three days like normal life had simply been waiting patiently for its turn to resume, and for the first time in over a week, nobody at that table had anything more urgent to think about than whether there was enough lasagna left for seconds.

***

60.5. Chapter 60.5

Xander went alone this time, the whole household still quietly recovering from the ritual, everyone needing a few days of ordinary distance from anything resembling a crisis before jumping back into anything else.

The world was wetland, reeds taller than his head stretching in every direction, water underfoot in every step no matter how carefully he picked his route.

"Okay," he said, already soaked to the knees. "Wet one. Very wet. Noted."

He spent the first day working through the reeds slowly, cataloguing insects in colors bright enough to seem almost artificial, and found the birds on the second morning — long-legged waders moving through the shallows with careful, deliberate steps, hunting something beneath the water's surface with a patience that put his own four years of practice to shame.

'Nice change of pace,' he thought, wading in close enough to get a proper look instead of just working the zoom. 'No crisis. No countdown. Just birds and reeds and getting soaked, same as any regular trip.' One of them let him get close enough to actually crouch beside it, entirely unbothered, more interested in the water than in him, and he held very still until it stepped near enough that scooping it up took no more effort than reaching out a hand. It felt good, settling back into the simple, uncomplicated rhythm of the actual job, after weeks of something so much bigger sitting over everything.

The rest of the trip passed quietly, wetland stretching endless and calm in every direction, and by the time the jump pulled him home, he had bird photos, waterlogged boots, one genuinely delighted new arrival for Alan's collection, and nothing more demanding behind him than patience and good aim with a camera.

Willow kissed him hello despite the mud clinging to his boots, then held him at arm's length, studying his face the way she'd been doing all week. "So?"

"Quiet. Wet, but quiet. Exactly what I needed."

"I think we all needed something like that, honestly," Willow said, and Xander found himself agreeing completely, both of them just standing there a moment in the doorway, in no hurry to go be anywhere else yet.

***



Chapter 61: Chapter 61



Willow went back through the canyon photos on a slow Tuesday evening, mostly out of habit — she'd taken to periodically re-cataloguing the older trip data whenever she had a quiet stretch, on the theory that a second look sometimes caught things a first pass missed.

She hadn't expected to find anything new. The handprints had settled, weeks ago, into something closer to a story than a data set — *a letter, not a chart entry*, the phrase she and Xander had actually agreed on out loud, and she'd mostly left it there, respected the shape of it, stopped treating the photos like a puzzle still waiting to be solved.

But something in the spacing bothered her tonight in a way it hadn't before.

She pulled up three of the clearest images side by side, frowning at the arrangement. She'd already accepted the placement was deliberate — that part wasn't in question anymore, hadn't been since the sightline geometry first convinced her someone wanted these seen. What she hadn't looked at closely enough, she realized now, was the space *between* prints. Most rock art she'd tracked down for comparison clustered organically, marks layered over each other in the casual, repetitive way people leave additions to something already there. These didn't do that. They were spaced with a regularity that looked, the longer she stared at it, less like an emotional gesture and more like counting.

'That's weird,' she thought, pulling up a ruler tool and measuring the gaps between prints, just to see if her eyes were making up a pattern that wasn't really there. They weren't. The spacing held steady across all three photos, consistent enough that it couldn't easily be coincidence.

That brought her up short for a long moment, the old, settled theory shifting underneath her in a way she hadn't braced for. A goodbye could be simple — a handprint, a shape, proof that somebody had existed. This was more than that. This looked like it was actually recording something.

'Not just "I was here,"' she thought slowly. 'Maybe "I was here, this many times." Or "this many of us." Something countable.' She thought about tally marks, about calendars, about the kinds of things people mark carefully rather than casually — the sort of record someone leaves behind on purpose, meant to be understood by someone else who came looking.

She didn't call Xander that night. It felt too early, too speculative, built on a hunch she couldn't yet back up with anything solid enough to say out loud without sounding like she was reaching — especially about something this close to a conversation they'd already closed the door on gently, once. But she saved a new file anyway, separate from the wildlife folders, separate even from the *invitation* label she'd used before, and typed a single line at the top before closing her laptop for the night:

*Not just a message. Check the spacing again.*

It sat there, unresolved, the kind of loose thread that would nag at her for days before she worked up the nerve to actually pull on it — but for tonight, it was enough just to have noticed, and to know that some part of her wasn't ready to let it go.

***



Chapter 62: Chapter 62



Xander found the file by accident, scrolling back through the shared photo folder looking for a specific shot of the canyon wrens to settle an argument with Jesse about how big they'd actually been, and the filename caught his eye before he even meant to open it.

*canyon_handprints_notjustamessage.*

He clicked it before he really thought about whether he should, and found himself looking at three familiar photos side by side, a ruler tool overlay measuring gaps between prints, and a single line of text at the top: *Not just a message. Check the spacing again.*

He read it twice, then a third time, feeling something settle uneasily in his chest that had nothing to do with wrens.

He found Willow at lunch the next day, sliding into the seat across from her before he'd even fully decided how to bring it up.

"Okay, I have a weird question," he said. "The canyon photos. There's a new file. Notjustamessage. What's that about?"

Willow went very still. "You found that?"

"By accident. I was looking for wren photos. What's the deal? I thought we already closed the book on this one."

She hesitated, clearly weighing how much of an unfinished thought she wanted to say out loud. "It's probably nothing. I didn't want to bring it up until I actually had something solid — especially not after we'd already, you know. Made peace with it, as a letter."

"You don't gotta have something solid to tell me you found something new about a wall I stood in front of for an hour. Talk to me."

Willow glanced around the table — Faith and Jesse deep in their own conversation, not paying attention — and lowered her voice anyway. "I went back through the photos and actually measured the gaps this time, instead of just eyeballing the placement like before. It's not just deliberate, Xander. It's regular. Spaced like counting. Like whoever made it wasn't just leaving a mark — they were recording something. A number. A count of something."

"Counting what, though?"

"That's what's bugging me. I don't know. Days, maybe. People. Visits. I don't have enough to say which." Willow met his eyes. "But it changes the letter a little, if I'm right. It's not just 'I was here, I hope somebody finds this.' It's closer to an actual record. Something with content, not just intention."

Xander needed a second before he could answer, the cafeteria noise suddenly feeling very far away. "So it's not just a goodbye. It's information."

"Maybe. I don't want to overclaim it — I said the same thing about the placement and turned out to be right, so I trust my gut more than I did the first time, but I still don't have proof. Just the spacing, and a strong feeling I can't fully justify yet."

"Your gut's been right about this wall every time so far. I'm inclined to trust it again." Xander pushed his tray aside, appetite suddenly gone. "What do we do with this?"

"I don't know yet. But I don't want to sit on it, now that I've actually confirmed the measurement instead of just noticing it once and letting it go." Willow pulled out her notebook, flipping past the biome chart to a fresh page. "I think it's time I actually started looking properly. Real research, not just a hunch I was scared to say out loud."

"I'm in. Whatever you need. Photos, measurements, moral support, all of it."

"I'm gonna need all three," Willow said, already writing. "This might take a while to actually figure out. But I don't think I can just file it away anymore. Not now that the spacing's held up to an actual ruler and not just a feeling."

"Then let's figure it out," Xander said, reaching across the table to squeeze her hand once, steady. "Together. Whatever it turns out to mean."

Willow squeezed back, the gesture doing more to settle her nerves than anything either of them had actually said.

***



Chapter 63: Chapter 63



Willow was buried in her own research again, quiet and distracted in a way Xander was trying hard not to read too much into, and Buffy had patrol commitments that couldn't move, which left the tide pools to Xander alone.

The tide pools were the best Xander had seen in months, a long stretch of rocky coastline exposed at low tide and packed with more color than he'd found on any single trip before — anemones in shades of purple he hadn't known existed in nature, crabs the size of dinner plates moving sideways with the unhurried confidence of creatures that had never once needed to worry about being stepped on.

"Okay, Willow's gonna need a whole day just for this stretch," he said, already crouching to get a photo of something spiny and orange he couldn't begin to guess the family of. "This is an unreasonable number of new species for one afternoon."

He spent the better part of two days just working the tide line, patient in a way he'd gotten better at over the years, cataloguing without rushing, letting the strange and the ordinary both get their fair share of his attention.

The seals showed up again on the third day, a different colony than the one he'd found before, close enough in shape to make him wonder if this was the same coastline stretching further than he'd realized, or simply another world that happened to favor the same general body plan for the same general niche.

"You guys again," he said, keeping a respectful distance. "Small world. Well — not small. Big world. You know what I mean."

He got his photos, restocked water from a clean freshwater stream running down from the cliffs, and spent his last evening just sitting on the rocks watching the sun go down over water that had never once reflected a single human-made light in the whole history of this certain Earth.

'This part never gets old,' he thought, watching the colors shift across the water. 'No matter how many worlds, no matter what else is going on back home. This part's always worth it.' It felt like an important thing to hold onto, especially lately, with everything else sitting heavier than usual in the back of his mind.

He made it home with another full memory card, mild sunburn, and the plain satisfaction of a trip that hadn't asked anything hard of him and had given plenty back anyway.

Buffy checked him over out of habit the second he walked through the door, standing on her toes to kiss him hello before he'd even fully set his bag down. "So how bad's the sunburn actually gonna look tomorrow?"

"Worth it." He kissed her back properly, then held up his camera. "Tide pools, seals, a crab the size of a dinner plate. Willow's gonna lose her whole evening to this."

"She's been a little distracted lately. Might be good for her, honestly. Something easy to get excited about."

"Distracted how?"

"Don't know exactly. She's been quieter. More in her own head than usual." Buffy shrugged. "Probably nothing. You know how she gets when she's chasing a new idea."

"Yeah," Xander said, keeping his voice carefully light. "I know how she gets."

He didn't say anything else about it, and neither did Buffy, and the evening fell into its ordinary rhythm — dinner, the usual noise, Jesse showing up uninvited but welcome as always, the crab photos making their way around the table to general delight, everything easy and warm and entirely unaware of the quiet, unfinished thing sitting just beneath the surface of two people's minds at that very table.

***



Chapter 64: Chapter 64



The Bronze on a Friday night ran at its usual volume, and Faith arrived late, sliding into the booth with the energy of someone who'd had a long patrol and an even longer week before it.

"You look wrecked," Jesse said, by way of greeting.

"Thanks. Really appreciate that observation."

"I'm just saying. Rough night?"

"Quiet night, actually. Rough week leading up to it." Faith stole a fry off the nearest tray without checking whose it was. "Nothing patrol-related. Just a lot going on."

"Cryptic," Jesse said. "I like it. Adds mystery to your whole vibe."

"There's no mystery. I'm just tired."

Buffy slid in beside her, glancing over with the quiet attentiveness she'd gotten better at over the past few months. "You wanna talk about it, or you wanna just sit here and steal everyone's fries in peace?"

"Second option. Very strongly, second option."

"Fair enough."

Xander and Willow arrived together, mid-conversation about something neither of them seemed inclined to share with the table, Xander's arm slung loosely around Willow's shoulders the whole walk from the door, which Faith noticed but didn't push, mostly because she had her own stuff going on that she wasn't exactly advertising either.

"What are you two whispering about," Jesse asked, with the nosiness of someone who considered table secrets a personal challenge, watching Xander drop a quick kiss to Willow's temple before sliding into the booth beside her. "And don't think the kissing distracts me from the question. I'm very good at multitasking suspicion."

"Wren measurements," Xander said, a little too quickly.

"That's suspiciously fast for an answer about wren measurements."

"I'm passionate about ornithology. Sue me."

"You didn't even know what ornithology meant a year ago."

"I've grown. As a person. Ornithologically. And romantically, apparently, which you'd know if you paid attention to anything besides your own gossip radar."

"My gossip radar is a finely tuned instrument, thank you, and it's currently going off about you two specifically."

Jesse let it go, mostly because the conversation shifted before he could push further, Faith steering things toward the sandwich debate, exactly the kind of redirect she always reached for when she wanted a table's attention somewhere else.

"Hot dog. Sandwich. Go."

"We're not doing this again," Willow said.

"We're always doing this. It's basically load-bearing at this point. This table would structurally collapse without the sandwich debate holding it up."

"That's not how tables work."

"It's exactly how this table works."

The argument ran its familiar course, comfortable and well-worn, and underneath the noise of it Faith caught Buffy's eye once, a small, private exchange that said more than either of them needed to say out loud — something's going on with Willow and Xander, neither of us knows what yet, and that's fine, everybody gets their secrets until they're ready to share them.

The night wound down easily after that, nobody pushing anybody past what they wanted to give, the comfortable noise of five people who trusted each other enough to let some things stay quiet until they were ready to be loud.

***



Chapter 65: Chapter 65



Willow was buried in genealogy research that week, and Buffy had pulled patrol duty covering for Faith, which left the savanna to Xander alone.

The savanna stretched gold and endless in every direction, dotted with acacia trees that looked almost too perfectly placed to be real, and Xander spent his first hour just turning in a slow circle, taking in a horizon with nothing on it but grass and sky.

"Okay, this one's a looker," he said, already reaching for the camera. "Very picturesque. Very calendar-photo energy."

The herds showed up by midmorning, more animals in one place than he'd seen on any single trip — something that read as antelope crossed with something else totally, moving in loose, unhurried groups across the grassland, occasionally lifting their heads to watch him without much concern before going back to grazing.

He spent two full days just documenting the sheer scale of it, herds blending into other herds, a whole visible ecosystem operating exactly as it should without a single human footprint to disturb the pattern.

Something strange happened late on the second day, brief enough that he almost convinced himself he'd imagined it — the sun seeming to catch at the same angle twice within an hour, the same small group of antelope crossing the same stretch of grass in what felt, for just a moment, like the exact same sequence of movements he was certain he'd already watched. He blinked, and it passed, and the herd moved on unremarkably, and he chalked it up to exhaustion and too much sun, jotting nothing about it in his notes because there was, truly, nothing sensible to write down.

'This is the kind of place Willow's chart was built for,' he thought, filling page after page with counts and rough measurements. 'This is a whole ecosystem doing exactly what it's supposed to, completely undisturbed.' It felt, more than most trips did, like genuine documentation rather than just collecting stories — a real record of something that deserved to be recorded properly.

The predators showed up on the third day, distant enough that he only caught them through the camera's zoom — something cat-shaped, moving low through the grass with the patient, coiled focus of an animal that knew exactly what it was doing. He kept his distance, respectfully, and got photos good enough that even from far away, the raw power in the animal's movement came through clearly.

"Definitely giving that one its space," he said, backing up several steps despite the safe distance already between them.

The rest of the trip passed in that same careful, appreciative rhythm — watching, documenting, never pushing his luck with anything that could reasonably kill him — and by the time the jump pulled him home, he had more usable data than any single trip had given him in months.

He found Willow already waiting when he got back, notebook out, the same quiet focus she'd been carrying for weeks now settling over her face the moment she saw his camera.

"Savanna world," he said, handing it over. "Big one. Herds for days, at least one very serious-looking predator I kept my distance from."

"This is exactly what I needed," she said, already scrolling through. "A big, stable, undisturbed ecosystem, completely intact. This is good baseline data."

"Baseline for what?"

She hesitated, just slightly, before answering. "For understanding what normal actually looks like out there. Before I go looking for anything that isn't."

He didn't push further, mostly because he understood exactly what she meant without needing her to say more, and the two of them sat together going through the photos in comfortable, quiet focus, the ordinary rhythm of cataloguing a new world holding steady even with everything else sitting unspoken just beneath it.

***



Chapter 66: Chapter 66



Willow's family tree theory had felt so promising for exactly four days, which was about how long it took her to hit the wall that made the whole approach collapse.

"It doesn't work," she told Xander, spreading printouts across her bedroom floor with visible frustration. "I traced back what I could of your family — not everything, records get thin past a certain point, but I got back a good five, six generations on a couple branches. There's no pattern. No repeating name, no single line it clusters around. Nothing."

"So it's not hereditary."

"That's the thing, I don't think that's right either." Willow rubbed her eyes, clearly exhausted from more late nights than she'd probably admit to. "I found a reference — buried, weird phrasing, the kind of thing that reads like someone was trying not to say something outright — that suggests this isn't about one family line at all. It might go back further than any family tree could ever trace. Like, before-recorded-history further."

"How much further are we talking?"

"Possibly all the way back. Like, before humans really spread out across the world at all. Which would mean it's not in your family specifically. It could be in basically anyone's family, depending on which ancient branch they descend from, and there'd be no way to trace it forward through normal genealogy because it's not really a bloodline thing anymore. It's older than bloodlines as we'd even understand them." She frowned at her own notes, tapping a particular line twice. "There's one thing that doesn't fully fit, though. Some of the dating in the source doesn't line up cleanly, like the text itself isn't sure when things actually happened relative to each other. I keep assuming that's just old, sloppy scholarship. Probably is. Still bugs me a little."

Xander was quiet for a long moment. "So this could just show up in anybody. Random. No warning, no family history, nothing."

"That's what it's looking like. Which means whoever else might have this — if anybody currently does — probably has no more idea why than you did before it happened to you."

"That's a lot bigger than I was expecting this to get."

"Yeah," Willow admitted. "Me too, honestly. I wanted a tidy answer. A specific relative, a name, something I could hand you like a solved mystery. Instead I got 'possibly older than agriculture,' which is not exactly the tidy bow I was hoping to wrap this up with."

"Are you disappointed?"

"A little. Mostly I'm just — recalibrating. The question got bigger, not smaller. That's allowed to happen in real research, even if it's annoying when it does." She managed a tired smile. "I'm not gonna stop looking. I just have to look somewhere different now."

"Where different?"

"I don't know yet. Maybe Giles has something in the Watcher archives that touches on it — if this really does go back that far, there's a chance it crosses paths with something in Slayer history, given how old that goes too. I haven't asked him yet. Wanted to have more than a hunch before I did."

"You've got more than a hunch now. You've got a honestly huge, slightly terrifying open question."

"When you put it that way, it sounds a lot more impressive than it feels sitting here on my bedroom floor at midnight." Willow gathered the printouts together, squaring the edges with more care than the moment strictly required. "I think it's time to actually ask Giles. Properly. Tomorrow."

"I'm coming with you."

"I was hoping you would."

***



Chapter 67: Chapter 67



Cordelia cornered Jesse at his locker with the determination of someone who'd finally run out of patience waiting for information to come to her.

"Okay, what's going on with Xander and Willow," she said, without preamble, arms crossed like she was owed an explanation by simple virtue of existing. "They've been weird for like two weeks. Whispering. Disappearing together. It's very suspicious, and frankly I deserve to know things before the rest of the school does."

"I actually don't know," Jesse admitted. "I've asked. They keep saying wren measurements."

"Nobody's that into wrens, Jesse. Even you're not that stupid."

"Thanks. I think. That's what I said! Nobody believed me when I said it was suspicious, and now you're saying it's suspicious, so I feel very vindicated right now."

"Don't get used to being right. It's not a look that suits you."

"It's not a dating thing, everybody already knows that," Cordelia said, waving it off like old news. "So what's the actual thing? Because 'wren measurements' isn't a thing people whisper about."

Jesse considered this with genuine seriousness. "Honestly? No idea. It's got a group project energy to it, though. Something they're working on together that they don't want the rest of us knowing about yet."

"How would you know what actual crushes look like?"

"I have extensive personal experience with unrequited ones. I'm basically an expert."

"That's not a flex, Jesse, that's genuinely one of the saddest sentences anyone's said to me all week, and I sat through Harmony's breakup story yesterday."

"I didn't say it was a flex. I said it was expertise. Two different things."

"It's one thing. It's called being pathetic."

"Ouch. Fair, but ouch."

Faith arrived partway through, catching the tail end of the conversation with visible amusement. "You two conspiracy theorists still working the wren angle?"

"It's not a conspiracy theory if it's clearly true," Cordelia said. "Do you know what they're up to?"

"Nope. And before you ask, no, I haven't pushed, because everybody's allowed secrets until they're ready to share them." Faith leaned against the lockers. "Also because I've got my own stuff going on that I'm not exactly broadcasting either, so I'm not really in a position to judge anybody else's secret-keeping."

"You have secrets too?" Cordelia looked personally offended by this development, like the universe had specifically inconvenienced her. "This whole friend group is apparently just secrets wearing normal clothes. It's exhausting, honestly, being surrounded by this much mystery when I have actual things I want to know."

"Welcome to Sunnydale," Faith said. "Everybody's got something. You get used to it."

"I don't want to get used to it, I want to know things. There's a difference, and it's called having priorities."

"That's your character flaw, not my problem."

Cordelia opened her mouth to argue, closed it, and settled for a look of pure, wordless disdain instead, directed impartially at both of them. "Fine. I'll figure it out eventually. I always do, and when I do, neither of you gets to act surprised."

"Good luck with that," Jesse said. "I've been trying for two weeks and all I've got is wren measurements."

"That's because you're bad at this. I'm good at this."

"You're not that much better at this than me."

"I'm far better at this than you, Jesse, and the fact that you'd even argue the point tells me exactly how much better."

The two of them fell into an easy, familiar bickering rhythm as they headed toward class, Faith trailing behind with a small, private smile, really enjoying the sight of ordinary friend-group nonsense continuing exactly as it always had, unbothered by anything bigger sitting quietly underneath it.

***

67.5. Chapter 67.5

Buffy and Willow both came this time, the three of them landing in a world of open plains broken by scattered rock outcroppings, wind steady and warm across grass that came up past their knees.

"Okay, this one's calm," Buffy said, scanning the horizon. "Suspiciously calm, given our track record lately."

"Don't jinx it," Willow warned, already crouching over a cluster of unfamiliar wildflowers.

The herd showed up by the second day, animals that read as bison-adjacent but leaner, moving across the plain in a loose formation that shifted and reformed with an almost choreographed grace.

"They're beautiful," Willow said, getting her photos carefully. "Look at that coordination. That's not random, that's actual social structure."

"You're gonna be writing about herd dynamics for a week, aren't you."

"Absolutely. No question."

They spent the rest of the trip working the plain's edges, cataloguing burrowing creatures that popped up at the sound of footsteps and vanished just as fast, and by the third evening had settled into the easy, comfortable rhythm that came from a trip asking nothing difficult of anyone.

"This is a good one," Buffy said, watching the sunset stretch orange across the grass, leaning into Xander while Willow's hand found his. "After everything with the mural, with Faith's whole thing — this is exactly the kind of quiet I didn't know I needed."

"Yeah," Willow agreed. "Same, honestly."

They made it home with herd photos, wildflower samples, and the sense that the trip had given all three of them exactly what they needed, without anyone having had to ask for it directly.

Faith was on the porch steps when they got back, looking considerably more rested herself after her own stretch of quiet. "You three look disgustingly relaxed."

"Bison. Wildflowers. Zero drama," Buffy said, dropping down next to her.

"I'm almost jealous. Almost."

"You should come with us sometime. Not just Xander solo — the whole group thing. I think you'd like it."

"Maybe," Faith said, something considering in her expression. "Maybe I will."

***



Chapter 68: Chapter 68



Giles listened to the whole thing without interrupting, which Willow found more unsettling than if he'd immediately dismissed it, because Giles interrupting meant he had an easy answer, and Giles staying quiet usually meant he didn't.

"Before recorded history," he said finally, removing his glasses to clean them slowly. "That's a considerable claim. I'd want to see your sourcing before accepting it outright."

"I know it sounds huge. I felt the same way when I found it." Willow pushed the relevant photocopies across the table. "But the genealogy just doesn't work otherwise. There's no family pattern. It has to be something older, something that doesn't follow normal inheritance at all."

Giles studied the pages for a long moment, and something in his expression shifted — not alarm exactly, but the stillness of a man recognizing a shape he hadn't expected to see again.

"I need to check something," he said, standing abruptly and moving toward the back of the shop, to a locked cabinet Willow had never seen him open before. He returned with a single volume, older than anything else on his usual shelves, handling it with a care that made both Willow and Xander instinctively sit up straighter.

"This is a restricted Watcher text," he said. "Not forbidden, precisely, but rarely consulted, dealing with the origins of the Slayer line itself. There's a passage — I read it years ago, during my own training, and largely dismissed it as archaic flourish rather than literal history." He flipped through carefully, searching. "Here."

He read aloud, translating as he went, voice careful: "'When the shadow men bound the demon's essence to the girl, the joining was imperfect, and something of that binding escaped into the world beyond her, scattering thin and strange through the blood of those who witnessed the rite.'"

The room went very quiet.

"Witnesses," Xander repeated. "People who were just there. Not the girl herself. Just people standing nearby when it happened."

"That would explain the absence of a traceable bloodline," Giles said slowly, working through the implication as he spoke. "If the effect scattered among witnesses rather than concentrating in a single line, it would have dispersed across an unknowable number of descendants almost immediately. Untraceable within a single generation, let alone across the many thousands since."

"So this is connected to the Slayer," Willow said. "To the actual first Slayer. Whatever happened when she was created had a side effect nobody was watching for, and it's been quietly showing up in random people ever since."

"That is, at minimum, a plausible reading of an extremely old and admittedly ambiguous text," Giles said. "I want to stress that this is one interpretation of one passage, and Watcher scholarship on the earliest era is notoriously unreliable. But it fits the shape of what you've described more comfortably than I'm utterly comfortable with."

"Comfortable how," Xander asked.

"Comfortably enough that I don't think it's coincidence," Giles admitted. "I think you may have found the actual origin of your ability, Xander. And I think it's much older, and notably stranger, than anything a normal family history could ever have prepared either of you to find."

Something about the certainty of his own words nagged at him even as he said them, a small, unexamined itch he couldn't quite place — the sense, gone as quickly as it arrived, that he was answering a smaller question than the one actually being asked. He set the feeling aside. It seemed, at the time, like nothing worth mentioning.

Willow looked at the ancient text, then at Xander, then back at the passage itself, feeling the full weight of the discovery settle into something that finally, truly felt solid instead of speculative.

"So it's real," she said quietly. "Not just a theory anymore."

"It's real," Giles confirmed. "And I suspect this is only the beginning of what we'll need to understand about it."

***



Chapter 69: Chapter 69



Buffy told Faith that evening, sitting on the porch steps where they usually ended up when something needed saying carefully rather than shouted across a room.

"So it's tied to the actual first Slayer," Faith repeated, once Buffy finished. "Whatever happened when they made her, some of it just — leaked out. Into random people who happened to be standing there."

"That's the theory. Giles thinks it's a plausible reading of the text, at least."

Faith was quiet for a while, turning that over. "Feels weird, hearing that. Like there's some cosmic family resemblance between me and Xander I never asked for."

"Is that a bad weird or a good weird?"

"Don't know yet. Little of both, maybe." Faith picked at a loose thread on her sleeve. "I spent my whole life feeling like being a Slayer was this huge, isolating thing. Only a couple people in the world ever get picked. And now it turns out the same ritual that made the first one also splashed something out into regular people, completely by accident, and it's apparently common enough that Xander just happened to get it."

"Does that make being a Slayer feel smaller?"

"No. Weirdly, it makes it feel bigger. Like the ritual that made us was so big, so much magic all at once, that some of it just spilled over into the world and kept going for thousands of years without anybody even knowing to look for it." Faith looked over. "That's a lot of power in one moment, if it's still leaking side effects this far downstream."

"Yeah," Buffy said. "That's kind of what's sitting with me too."

"You think there's more of it out there? More people like Xander, walking around with no idea why weird stuff happens to them?"

"Probably. If it scattered randomly thousands of years ago, there's no reason it'd only ever land on one person at a time. Could be dozens of people right now who have some version of this and just never had a reason to notice."

Faith considered that, the scale of it settling in slowly. "That's either really cool or really unsettling. I keep flipping between the two."

"Same. I don't think there's a wrong way to feel about it yet. It's too big to have slipped into one feeling."

They sat together for a while, the evening quiet around them, before Faith spoke again, quieter this time.

"I'll say this, though — I'm glad it's Xander. If it's gotta be somebody in our group who's connected to all this, I'm glad it's him. He's been carrying it alone for years without knowing why. At least now he's got people helping him figure it out."

"Yeah," Buffy agreed. "Me too."

"Big thing to sit on, though. An ancient accident like that, still rippling out this many years later."

"Yeah. Big thing." Buffy leaned back on her hands, looking up at a sky that felt, for a moment, significantly older and stranger than it had that morning. "We'll figure out what it means. Same as we figure out everything else. Just gonna take a while, probably."

"I can live with a while," Faith said, and Buffy nodded, both of them looking out at the quiet street, the weight of an ancient, accidental inheritance settling into something that felt, if not entirely comfortable yet, at least a little less lonely for being shared.

Faith tried the hammer again before bed that night, same as she'd taken to doing every so often without ever quite calling it a habit — quiet, private, nobody else around to watch her fail at it one more time. Nothing had changed. She hadn't really expected it to, not really. She stood there a moment after, just breathing, watching her own reflection in Buffy's dark window instead of looking at the hammer directly, and told herself, same as always, that it wasn't really about the hammer anyway. She was getting better at almost believing it.

***



Chapter 70: Chapter 70



Buffy and Willow were both still deep in the aftermath of the mural discovery, poring over texts with Giles late into most nights, which left the forest to Xander alone that week.

The forest this time was old in a way Xander could feel before he could articulate why — trees wider than cars, canopy so dense it turned midday into permanent dusk on the ground below, moss thick enough to swallow his boots up to the ankle in places.

"Okay, this one's got atmosphere," he said, voice dropping instinctively to match the hush of the place. "This one feels like it's got opinions about visitors."

He moved carefully through the first day, more out of respect for the sheer age of everything around him than any specific danger he'd identified, cataloguing fungi in colors he hadn't known existed and a woodpecker-adjacent bird whose call echoed strangely off trunks wide enough to swallow sound.

The owls found him on the second night, or rather he found them, a whole family perched in the hollow of an ancient trunk, eyes catching his flashlight beam with the flat, unblinking patience of creatures that had never once needed to fear anything walking upright on two legs.

"Hey," he said, quietly, not wanting to disturb them. "Didn't mean to interrupt. Carry on."

They didn't carry on, exactly, but they didn't flee either, watching him with mild interest until he'd gotten his photos and backed away, leaving them to whatever ancient owl business he'd briefly interrupted.

'Willow's gonna want measurements on the trees too,' he thought, running a hand along bark rough enough to catch on his palm. 'Not just the animals this time. This forest's a specimen all its own.' It felt like an important addition to make, the kind of thing that had started mattering more to him lately than it used to — not just cataloguing the strange and dangerous, but truly documenting what these worlds actually were, patiently, thoroughly, for reasons that went beyond simple collection now.

The rest of the trip passed in that same reverent quiet, the forest never quite explaining itself but never threatening him either, and by the time the jump pulled him home he had photos of trees that might well predate agriculture back on his own world, and a lingering, unshakeable sense of having stood somewhere honestly ancient.

He found the house quieter than usual when he got back, Willow and Giles' research materials still spread across the kitchen table from the night before, the weight of everything they'd found together sitting almost visibly in the air.

"Big trees," he said, setting his camera down gently among the scattered books. "Genuinely ancient-feeling place. Kind of matches the mood around here lately, honestly."

Willow looked up, managing a tired but real smile. "That's either a coincidence or the universe having a sense of humor about timing."

"I'm choosing to believe it's the universe. Feels more fun that way."
