"Fair enough." She reached for his camera, already curious despite everything else weighing on her. "Show me the owls. I need something good today."

"You've got it," Xander said, and settled in beside her, pressing a kiss to her temple before pulling up the photos, the two of them finding a small, quiet pocket of ordinary comfort in the middle of everything else that had gotten so much bigger than either of them expected.

***

70.5. Chapter 70.5

Dr. Sanjay Okafor, who'd inherited Marcus Webb's old position after his retirement, made his first and, as it turned out, only trip that autumn — a single, carefully arranged expedition that took nearly as much bureaucratic maneuvering as Webb's original had, the practice apparently not having gotten any easier to arrange in the intervening years.

"I heard about Webb's trip when I interviewed for this position," Sanjay admitted, adjusting his own gear with visible nervousness. "Never imagined I'd actually get the chance myself."

The world was coastal scrubland, and Sanjay spent the full three days in careful, meticulous documentation, filling notebooks with the same rigorous attention Webb had once brought to his own single trip.

"This is remarkable work you've been doing," Sanjay told Xander, on the final evening, reviewing his own notes with visible satisfaction. "I understand now why Webb spoke of it so highly, all those years later."

"Glad it lived up to the reputation."

"More than lived up to it." Sanjay managed a tired, genuine smile. "I don't know when logistics will allow another trip like this. But I'm deeply grateful for this one."

It would, in fact, be nearly two years before another researcher made the trip, the practice remaining rare and precious rather than routine — a fact that would only change once Elena Vasquez, considerably more insistent than either of her predecessors, took over the position and simply refused to let the opportunity lapse into occasional legend the way it had twice before.

***



Chapter 71: Chapter 71



Joyce's list got a genuinely strange entry that Sunday, one she read aloud at the table with a flatness that suggested she was still deciding how she felt about it: apparently the reason Xander can travel between worlds traces back to the actual creation of the first Slayer, thousands of years ago, and there may be other people out there with some version of the same ability who have no idea why.

"You wrote it exactly like that?" Xander asked.

"I wrote it exactly like that because that's exactly what I was told, and I didn't know how else to summarize it." Joyce passed the potatoes down the table. "I'm still working on actually processing it, if I'm honest. I made peace with vampires years ago. This is a different kind of strange."

"Welcome to the club," Faith said. "We're all still processing it too."

"How's the research going?" Buffy asked. "Anything more since Giles found the passage?"

"Slow," Willow admitted. "The text's old and vague on purpose, probably, or just because whoever wrote it originally didn't fully understand what they were describing either. Giles is going through everything else in that restricted section, but it's not exactly a fast process."

"Any leads on whether there's actually somebody else out there right now?" Jesse asked, having been let in on enough of the broad strokes over the past week to feel entitled to ask, even without every detail.

"No leads yet. It's a genuine question mark. Could be nobody currently active. Could be somebody on the other side of the world who has no idea any of this history exists." Willow pushed a piece of chicken around her plate without much appetite. "I keep going back and forth on which answer I'm hoping for."

"Why would either answer be bad?" Cordelia asked, having also been read in on a version of events, mostly because keeping her fully in the dark had proven logistically impossible once Jesse got involved.

"If there's nobody else, Xander's actually alone in this in a way that's kind of sad, honestly. If there is somebody else, we've got a whole new person to worry about, good or bad, and no idea which."

"Both options sound stressful," Cordelia said, unimpressed with the universe's choices on the matter. "I don't love either of them, and frankly I resent being told about a problem I have zero ability to fix."

"Nobody loves either of them," Faith said. "That's kind of the whole deal with big mysteries. The answer's rarely the comfortable one."

Xander, who'd been quiet through most of the discussion, finally spoke up. "Honestly, I don't feel alone in it. Not anymore. Whatever this turns out to mean, I've got all of you helping me figure it out. That's not nothing."

"That's not nothing at all," Joyce agreed, reaching over to squeeze his hand once. "Whatever you find, however big it gets — you're not carrying it by yourself. None of you are."

The table settled into a quieter, gentler noise after that, the weight of the discovery still sitting there but somehow easier to hold now that it had been said out loud, passed around the table the same way the potatoes had, shared rather than shouldered alone.

"Okay," Xander said finally, "on a lighter note, I found owls the size of toddlers this week, and I have photos, and I would like to change the subject to something far less cosmically enormous." He tugged Willow into a one-armed hug on his way to grab his bag, kissing her temple, and reached his other hand out for Buffy's across the table, needing the contact with both of them after the heaviness of the conversation.

"I'll allow it," Willow said with a grin, leaning into him. "Show me the owls."

"Finally, somebody with their priorities straight."

***



Chapter 72: Chapter 72



The vampire nest behind the old drive-in turned out to be exactly as unimpressive as Faith had expected, three fledglings barely a week old, still clumsy with their own new strength, more startled by Buffy and Faith's arrival than really dangerous.

"This is barely a warmup," Faith said, staking one before it finished standing up straight. "I've had scarier arguments about sandwiches."

"Careful what you wish for," Buffy said, catching the second one mid-lunge and finishing it just as fast. "We've got two more blocks to clear."

"I'm not wishing for anything, I'm making an observation about relative danger levels."

"That's exactly what wishing for something sounds like, dressed up as an observation."

The third fledgling made a run for it, which Faith found almost endearing in its futility, and caught up to him within half a block, the whole business over in under a minute.

"Well, that was disappointing," Faith said, dusting ash off her jacket. "I wanted at least a little bit of a workout tonight."

"You've had a rough couple weeks. Maybe an easy patrol's exactly what you needed."

"Maybe. Still would've liked a challenge, though. Something to actually think about instead of everything else currently living in my head."

They walked the rest of the route in comfortable quiet, the ordinary rhythm of patrol settling in around them, and ended up sitting on the low cemetery wall afterward, the way they usually did when the night wound down easy.

"How are you doing, actually?" Buffy asked. "With everything. The Slayer connection, all of it."

"Better than I expected, honestly. It's a lot to sit with, but it's not scary exactly. More just — big. Bigger than anything I thought I was part of." Faith looked up at the sky, stars mostly washed out by streetlight glare same as always. "Kind of makes me feel connected to something ancient in a way I never really thought about before. Being a Slayer's always felt personal, immediate. This makes it feel like part of something way older than either of us."

"Yeah. Same for me, honestly."

"You think we'll actually figure out what it all means? The other jumper thing, all of it?"

"I think Willow and Giles won't stop until they do. That's kind of their whole thing." Buffy bumped Faith's shoulder with her own. "In the meantime, we've still got a town to patrol and a whole regular life to keep living around the edges of the big mystery."

"Fair point. Speaking of regular life — you hear Cordelia's been asking around trying to figure out what Xander and Willow are up to?"

"I heard. Jesse mentioned it at lunch like six times."

"She's gonna keep pushing until somebody tells her something. She hates not knowing things."

"She'll find out eventually. Everybody always does around here." Buffy stood, stretching out a shoulder stiff from the night's work. "For now, though, I think we've earned an early night. Nest's cleared, nobody got hurt, and I've got homework I've been putting off since Thursday."

"Homework. Groundbreaking priorities."

"Somebody's gotta graduate eventually."

"Good luck with that," Faith said, grinning, and the two of them headed home together, the ordinary comfort of a quiet patrol night settling in around a mystery that, for tonight at least, could wait until morning.

***



Chapter 73: Chapter 73



He'd asked to go alone this time, honestly — after the ritual, after everything, he'd wanted one trip that was just quiet and his own to sit with. Buffy and Willow, understanding, hadn't argued.

The lakeshore stretched wide and still under an overcast sky, water flat as glass except where fish broke the surface every so often in lazy, unhurried rings, and Xander spent his first afternoon just walking the shoreline, letting the quiet of the place settle into him after a week that had felt anything but quiet back home.

"Okay, this is exactly the kind of trip I needed," he said, to nobody, watching a heron-like bird stalk the shallows with slow, deliberate patience. "Low drama. High scenery. Very restorative."

He found a family of otters on the second day, sliding down a muddy bank into the water over and over with what looked, unmistakably, like pure enjoyment, no purpose to it beyond the fun of doing it again.

"You guys have got the right idea," he told them, crouching to get a photo. "No cosmic mysteries weighing on you. Just sliding. Very enviable lifestyle."

He spent hours watching them, more than the photos strictly required, finding something truly soothing in the simplicity of it — creatures doing something purely because it felt good, with no larger question hanging over any of it.

'This is the version of the job I actually needed this week,' he thought, watching one otter climb the bank for another run. 'Something simple. Something that doesn't ask anything hard of me.' It felt like exactly the right kind of trip to land in the middle of everything else currently unfolding back home.

The rest of the visit passed in that same easy rhythm, fish counted more than measured, birds photographed more for the pleasure of it than any real scientific rigor, and by the time the jump pulled him home he felt lighter than he had in weeks, an ordinary, simple trip doing more for him than he'd expected.

"Otters," he announced, walking through the door. "A whole family of otters, just sliding down a bank for fun, no other agenda. It was exactly what I needed."

"Sounds perfect," Willow said, looking up from the research spread still covering half the kitchen table. "I could use a break like that myself, honestly."

"You wanna see the photos? Might help. Otters are pretty reliably mood-improving."

"Yeah. Actually, yeah, I really do." She pushed the books aside, making room, and leaned into him for a moment first, just breathing, before reaching for the camera, and the two of them spent the next hour just looking at otters together, no ancient mysteries, no restricted texts, just simple, uncomplicated delight in creatures that had never once needed to worry about anything bigger than their next slide down the bank.

***



Chapter 74: Chapter 74



Cordelia's patience finally ran out on a Wednesday, and she cornered Willow at her locker with the determination of someone who'd decided today was the day she got an actual answer, no more excuses accepted.

"Okay, I've been very patient," she said, "and I want it acknowledged that I've been very patient, because normally I'd have cracked this in like three days, and it's been almost three weeks, which frankly is an insult to my own skills."

"You have been patient," Willow admitted. "Genuinely, I'm impressed."

"Thank you. I know. Now tell me what's going on with you and Xander, because the wren excuse stopped working two weeks ago and everybody knows it, and frankly it was insulting to my intelligence the first time you tried it."

Willow hesitated, weighing how much to actually say. "It's not really my secret to share completely. But I can tell you the short version, if you promise not to freak out or tell the whole school."

"I would never tell the whole school something I don't even understand yet. Give me the short version, and make it good, because I've earned good at this point."

"Xander's ability to disappear on weekends — there's a reason for it. Something old. Really old, actually, tied to some pretty ancient history most people don't know about. We've been trying to figure out exactly what it means."

Cordelia stared at her, distinctly unimpressed. "That's it? That's the big secret? 'Old reason, still figuring it out'? Three weeks of mystery for a sentence that could've fit on a fortune cookie."

"That's genuinely most of what I can say right now. It's complicated, and honestly we don't have all the answers ourselves yet."

"Is it dangerous?"

"We don't think so. Not directly, anyway. It's more just — big. Bigger than any of us expected when we started looking into it."

Cordelia considered this, visibly working through whether it satisfied her or not, arms crossed with real skepticism. "Okay, that's actually more interesting than I expected, and also somehow less satisfying than I wanted. I feel like I deserve more details for three weeks of patience. This is basically emotional theft."

"I know. I'm sorry. I promise you're not being kept out of the loop to be mean, there's just honestly not more I can say yet."

"Fine. I'll allow it. For now." Cordelia crossed her arms tighter. "But when you do figure it out, whatever it actually is, I want to be told properly. Not secondhand through Jesse, who by the way is a terrible secret-keeper and folds under basically zero pressure. I once got a whole birthday surprise out of him with a single raised eyebrow."

"Noted. You'll get told properly."

"Good." Cordelia turned to leave, then paused, glancing back with something that looked almost like genuine concern underneath her usual demanding energy. "Is Xander doing okay? With whatever this is?"

Willow considered the question, a little surprised by it. "He's doing okay. Better than I expected, actually. It helps that he's not figuring it out alone."

"Good," Cordelia said again, softer this time. "Tell him I asked. Don't tell him I sounded like I actually cared, though. I have a reputation."

"Your secret's safe with me."

"See, now that's a secret I actually appreciate you keeping," Cordelia said, and swept off down the hallway, leaving Willow standing at her locker with a small, genuine smile she hadn't expected the conversation to leave her with.

***



Chapter 75: Chapter 75



Giles found the second reference on a Thursday night, alone in the Magic Box long after closing, and read it through twice more, polishing his glasses without quite noticing he'd done it, before deciding whether it was worth calling anyone about so late.

The passage was shorter than the first, tucked into a margin note rather than the main text, written in a hand different from the primary scribe's — an addition, apparently, from some later Watcher who'd read the original account and felt compelled to add something of their own.

*The scattering did not end with the rite's completion,* it read. *Those who carry the echo have, on rare occasion, found one another across great distance, drawn by some sympathy neither fully understands. Whether by accident or by the echo's own quiet design, history does not say.*

Giles read it three times, turning the implication over carefully.

Found one another. Not randomly discovered by outside research, but drawn — actively, if the margin note could be trusted at all, which was itself far from certain given how thin and uncertain the entire body of relevant scholarship remained.

He didn't call Willow that night. It felt too early, too speculative, exactly the kind of half-formed thread that deserved careful verification before he handed anyone else a hope that might not hold up under closer scrutiny.

But he copied the passage carefully into his own notes, dated it, and set it aside in a folder that was quickly becoming much thicker than he'd expected when this had all started as a simple question about a teenager's unusual habits.

'Drawn together,' he thought, closing the ancient text gently. 'If that's true, then finding another jumper may not be a matter of research at all. It may simply be a matter of time.'

He turned off the shop lights not long after, locking up carefully, and walked home under a sky that felt, for reasons he couldn't quite articulate, a little larger than it had that morning — full of old, quiet possibilities he hadn't fully appreciated until a teenager's strange weekends had cracked open a door into something notably older than any of them had bargained for.

Somewhere else entirely, on a world with no name that anyone back home would ever think to give it, the grass moved in a wind that had never once felt a human footstep disturb it, waiting, patient as it had always been, for whatever came next.

***



Chapter 76: Chapter 76



The jump dropped him into forest, which wasn't unusual by itself — dense, temperate, tall pines mixed with hardwoods he half-recognized from home, undergrowth thick with fern and fallen needles that muffled his footsteps to almost nothing, the kind of terrain that could have belonged to a dozen different worlds he'd visited before. Birdsong somewhere overhead, unfamiliar in its exact notes but recognizable in its shape, the ordinary business of an ordinary morning in an ordinary patch of woods.

What was unusual was the fence.

He nearly walked past it before his brain fully registered what he was looking at — a stretch of wire strung between weathered wooden posts, running in a straight, deliberate line through the trees, the kind of thing that occurred in nature exactly never. He stopped mid-stride, one boot still half-raised, and simply stared at it for a long moment, waiting for his brain to produce some explanation that didn't require rewriting four years of certainty.

It didn't produce one.

"Okay," he said, quietly, feeling his pulse pick up in a way four years of empty worlds had never once required. "Okay, that's not right. That's really, really not right."

He crouched by the nearest post, close enough now to see the details — the wood weathered gray by seasons of sun and rain, grain split in places, a few strands of wire gone slightly rusted where moisture had pooled at old staple points. Not new. Not recent. Whoever had built this had built it some time ago, and had built it to last.

He touched it, finally, one careful finger against the wire itself, half expecting it to somehow not be real, to dissolve at contact the way dreams sometimes did when you pressed too hard against their logic. It didn't dissolve. It was simply wire, cold and solid and undeniably, physically there.

'People,' he thought, the word landing strange and enormous in his head. 'This world's got people. This world isn't empty.' Four years of assuming the rule — no humans, ever, not here, not anywhere he'd been sent — cracked open all at once, and he actually didn't know what to do with the size of that realization, crouched in the dirt with his hand still resting against a fence post that shouldn't have existed anywhere he'd ever been sent.

He stayed there a long time, longer than made any practical sense, just processing, before he finally made himself move — following the fence line at a careful distance, cautious in a way he hadn't needed to be in years, every sense straining for any sign of movement, any sound that wasn't wind or birdsong.

The fence ran for the better part of a mile before the trees finally broke, and what waited beyond them stole whatever breath he had left to lose.

A cleared field, plowed in neat, deliberate rows, crops he half-recognized growing green and orderly against dark turned earth. Further off, past the field's edge, a building — weathered wood siding, a tin roof catching the pale afternoon light, smoke-blackened stones suggesting a chimney somewhere on its far side. A second, smaller structure beside it that might have been a shed, or a barn, or something he didn't have a category for yet.

He didn't approach. Every instinct he'd built over four years of solitary trips screamed at him to observe from a careful distance rather than walk up to a stranger's home unannounced, especially on a world where he had no idea what kind of people lived here, what they'd think of a stranger appearing out of nowhere, or what tools they might have to deal with exactly that kind of intrusion. He found a fallen log at the tree line's edge, settled low behind it, and watched.

An hour passed. Then another. The field stayed empty. Smoke rose, thin and steady, from the chimney he'd guessed at, proof of a fire tended by someone, somewhere, without that someone ever once showing themselves. A dog barked, once, distant and muffled, from somewhere behind the house, and Xander went perfectly still for a full minute afterward, heart hammering, before deciding the sound hadn't been directed at him at all.

He photographed everything he could from his hiding spot — the field, the buildings, the fence line stretching off in both directions — hands steadier now than they'd been at first contact, four years of field discipline reasserting itself even through the sheer scale of what he was actually documenting.

'Willow's gonna lose her whole mind over this,' he thought, backing carefully away from the clearing once the light began to fail, retreating deeper into the trees before making camp well clear of the fence line, further than strictly necessary, close enough only to find his way back if morning brought him the nerve to return. 'This changes everything. Every assumption we've ever made about these worlds, this breaks it.'

He barely slept that first night, mind circling the same questions over and over — who lived there, how long they'd been there, whether they were like him, whether this was the first inhabited world he'd ever found or simply the first one he'd happened to notice signs of. Somewhere well past midnight, a light flickered on in the distant house — small, warm, unmistakably a window catching lamplight — and he lay there watching it for a long time before it finally went dark again, someone's ordinary evening ending in a home he had no right to be watching.

The second day he circled wider, cautious, mapping the fence line's full extent as best he could without ever coming close enough to be seen, and found, near a bend where the wire crossed a shallow creek, boot prints in the mud — recent, purposeful, heading toward the house rather than away from it. Someone had walked this exact stretch within the last day or two. Someone real, with a real, ordinary routine, utterly unaware that anyone else had ever set foot on ground they'd probably assumed, same as Xander once had, belonged to nobody at all.

The boot prints weren't the only tracks he found that day. Farther out from the fence line, where the forest thickened back into something wilder, he crossed a game trail churned up by prints too big and too wrong to be anything he recognized — deep, canine, but larger than any wolf print had any business being, several sets together, moving as a pack. Dire wolves, he'd guess, if he had to put a name to it, though guessing was all he had to go on out here. He photographed those too, hands considerably less steady than they'd been over the boot prints, and gave the trail a wide, deliberate berth for the rest of the afternoon.

The second sighting came an hour before dusk, distant enough across a break in the trees that he almost dismissed it as a trick of the light — a shape moving through a meadow at the valley's far side, gray-brown and simply, impossibly enormous, curved tusks catching the last of the sun before it ambled back into treeline shadow and was gone. Mammoth or mastodon, he really couldn't have said which even if someone had asked; he just knew it was the kind of thing that hadn't walked anywhere on his own Earth in ten thousand years. He stood frozen for a long moment after, camera still raised, uncertain he'd actually seen what he thought he'd seen until he checked the photo and confirmed his own eyes hadn't been lying to him.

The third he only found because he nearly stepped in it — a kill site, a day or two old, what looked like deer bones scattered wide across a small clearing in a way that spoke less of scavenging and more of something big enough to drag a full carcass without much effort, drag marks leading off into brush too dense to follow safely, deep punctures in what was left of the hide that no wolf jaw, dire or otherwise, could have made. He didn't linger there long. Something about the size of those punctures left him deeply uninterested in meeting whatever had made them in person.

'Willow's REALLY gonna lose her whole mind over this,' he thought, backing away from the kill site with significantly more urgency than he'd shown backing away from the farmhouse the night before.

He didn't get close enough to learn anything more before the jump pulled him home on the third evening, the whole trip passing without him ever working up the nerve to actually make contact, and he came back through with photos of a fence, a field, a distant building, a light in a window, boot prints in creek mud, wolf tracks too large to be wolves, something enormous and tusked at the edge of a meadow, and a kill site he still wasn't sure he wanted the full details on — evidence, undeniable and world-shaking, that somewhere out there, on at least one of these countless empty Earths, he was not, and perhaps had never truly been, the only person who'd ever set foot, and that whoever lived there shared their world with far more than farmland.

"You're pale," Buffy said, the second he walked through the door, none of her usual joking making it into her voice at the sight of his face. "What happened?"

"Nothing happened. That's not — nothing bad happened." He set his camera down with hands that weren't quite steady. "I need to call Willow. I need to call Willow right now."

"Xander, what did you find?"

He looked at her, the weight of it still settling, four years of certainty about how these worlds worked cracking open into something he didn't yet have words solid enough to hand her.

"People," he said finally. "I found people, Buffy. Or evidence of them, anyway. There's a fence. There's a farm. There's somebody living out there, and I have no idea who."

***



Chapter 77: Chapter 77



Willow made him tell the story three times before she'd let herself fully process it, each retelling picking up small details he hadn't thought to mention the first time — the exact weathering on the fence posts, the crop rows, the angle of the tin roof catching light.

"A farm," she said, for what felt like the tenth time, staring at the photos spread across the kitchen table. "An actual, functioning farm. On a world that's supposed to be completely untouched."

"Not completely untouched, apparently. Just untouched by me, this whole time, because I've never happened to land anywhere close to wherever they are."

"Do you think it's just the one homestead? Or a whole community out there?"

"No idea. I didn't get close enough to find out. Didn't feel right, showing up unannounced on somebody's land without knowing anything about who they are or how they'd react to a stranger appearing out of nowhere."

"That was smart," Giles said, from across the table, having arrived within twenty minutes of Willow's call the night before and clearly not having slept much since. "Genuinely smart, Xander. Approaching an unknown human population without any context could have gone very badly, regardless of how curious the discovery made you."

"I wanted to go say hi. Every part of me wanted to just walk up and knock on the door. I just — didn't trust that it was actually the safe move."

"Your instincts served you well." Giles studied the photos again, expression carefully neutral in the way it got when he was working through something he hadn't fully articulated yet. "This does raise a rather significant question, though."

"Which is?"

"Whether this population arrived there naturally, through some means we haven't considered, or whether they arrived the same way you did." Giles met his eyes. "If it's the latter, this may be much more than a chance discovery."

The room went quiet.

"You think it's them," Willow said slowly. "Whoever the margin note was talking about. The other people who carry this."

"I think it's a possibility worth taking seriously. I don't think we should leap to that conclusion without more evidence. But a farm, built by someone, on a world otherwise totally devoid of human presence — that is not the sort of thing that happens by accident."

Xander went quiet, the size of it settling slowly. "So I might go back there and actually meet somebody like me. Somebody who's been doing this too."

"You might," Giles said. "Though I'd urge patience before rushing back. If this is what it appears to be, the stakes of getting the first contact wrong are considerably higher than any previous trip you've taken."

"I wasn't planning on rushing back tomorrow," Xander admitted. "Honestly, I don't know if I'm even ready. Four years of assuming I was the only one, and now there's a real chance I'm not, and I don't — I don't know how to feel about that yet. Excited. Terrified. Both, probably."

"That's an utterly reasonable response," Giles said. "Take whatever time you need before deciding how to proceed. This isn't a discovery that requires an immediate answer."

Willow had gone quiet partway through, still flipping back through the photos, and stopped cold on the last three — the wolf tracks, the distant tusked shape, the kill site.

"Xander," she said, voice climbing about half an octave. "Xander. What. Are these."

"Oh. Yeah. That was also a whole thing." He rubbed the back of his neck. "Wolf tracks, but bigger than they should be. Some kind of elephant-adjacent thing with tusks, way off in a meadow. And a kill site I didn't stick around long enough to properly document, because the puncture wounds on it made some very compelling arguments about not doing that."

"This is — okay, this is genuinely one of the biggest things you've ever found, and I say that fully aware you found an actual farm on the same trip." Willow was already reaching for a fresh notebook page. "Dire wolves. Actual dire wolves, or something close enough to matter. And mammoths, or mastodons, truly could be either, we'd need way better photos to even guess at the species — this world isn't just inhabited, Xander, it's got a whole surviving Ice Age ecosystem running on top of it."

"Which is either the coolest thing I've ever heard or extremely concerning, depending on how close any of that lives to the farm."

"Both. It's allowed to be both." Willow's pen was already moving, fast and sure. "Worth noting, though — most large predators tend to avoid humans by default when they've got other options. That's true on our Earth, and there's no obvious reason it wouldn't hold here too. You probably weren't in nearly as much danger as it felt like in the moment." She paused over the kill-site photo. "And honestly, if this world's got dire wolves and mammoths running around, the smart money's already on sabertooth for whatever left that kill site. It's kind of the obvious third member of the set."

"Yeah, that tracked before you even said it. Ice Age's greatest hits, apparently."

"Tell that to the kill site."

"The kill site had deer bones. You had a pulse and forward momentum. Statistically different categories of encounter."

"Comforting. Somewhat." Xander managed a tired smile. "So what, we just... sit on this? A whole possible sabertooth on top of the wolves and the giant tusked things, and we just don't do anything about it?"

"Not sit on it forever. Sit on it responsibly." Giles removed his glasses, cleaning them in the way that meant he was thinking carefully. "Should you wish to document — or, in time, attempt to bring back — any of what you found, it will require notably more preparation than the sloth did. An actual animal behaviorist's input on dosage, rather than your own careful guesswork. Alan's usual trailer arrangement is unlikely to scale to something the size of what you photographed. None of that happens on the next trip. It happens after proper planning, if it happens at all."

"After proper planning," Xander agreed. "Not today. Not this week. But — yeah. Eventually. I want to actually see one of them up close. Properly, not through a camera zoom from behind a bush."

"Then we'll plan properly," Willow said, already sketching a rough outline at the bottom of her notes — dosage question marks, a column for "bigger trailer," a column for "ask Alan who to even call about mammoths." "This is gonna take a while to get right. I'm okay with that. I'd rather get it right."

"Yeah," Xander agreed, looking at the photos once more — the fence, the field, the distant roof, all of it sitting there patiently, waiting for whenever he worked up the nerve to actually find out who'd built it. "Yeah. I think I need a minute. Maybe more than a minute."

"Take as long as you need," Willow said, reaching over to squeeze his hand under the table, steady. "We'll be here whenever you're ready."

***



Chapter 78: Chapter 78



Buffy and Willow both had commitments that weekend, which meant this one was solo, same as plenty still were even now.

The same restless pull Xander had felt during the countdown week settled back over him before the next jump, wanting badly enough to just stay this time that he caught himself hunting for some loophole in a rule Willow had already confirmed didn't bend. It was only the second time that particular impulse had ever come close to winning, and knowing that did nothing to make it feel smaller. Willow talked him down anyway, same as she had the first time, same reminder as always: wanting to stay didn't change what the pull actually let him do.

"It doesn't wait for you to be ready," she reminded him. "You go when you go."

So he went, and found himself standing in scrubland so ordinary, so thoroughly unremarkable, that some small part of him actually relaxed at the sight of it — no fences, no fields, nothing but scattered brush and rock stretching toward a horizon with nothing man-made anywhere on it.

"Okay," he said, and for once the word carried real relief instead of bracing for whatever came next. "Okay, this one's just normal. Just an empty world doing empty world things. I can work with normal."

He spent the trip in a grateful, uncomplicated rhythm, cataloguing lizards and scrub birds without any of the weight that had followed him through the last several days, letting the ordinary strangeness of the job be exactly that — ordinary, familiar, uncomplicated by anything bigger than the animals directly in front of him.

'This is good,' he thought, photographing a lizard sunning itself on a flat rock, unbothered by his presence in the same unlearned way every creature on every world had always been. 'This is exactly the kind of trip I needed. Just animals. Just the regular version of this job.' It felt, after everything else sitting heavy in his chest lately, like being handed a genuine breath of air.

He made it home with lizard photos and significantly lighter shoulders than he'd carried in weeks, and found the house in its usual evening rhythm — Joyce cooking, Buffy doing homework at the kitchen table, everything so thoroughly normal that it took him a moment to remember there was anything else going on at all.

"Good trip?" Buffy asked, not looking up from her textbook.

"Really good, actually. Boring, even. In the best possible way."

"Boring's allowed to be good sometimes."

"Boring's underrated, honestly. I've developed a whole new appreciation for it lately."

She looked up at that, something knowing in her expression. "You needed a break from the big stuff."

"Yeah. Kind of desperately, actually." He dropped into the seat across from her. "Doesn't mean I'm not thinking about it. Just meant I needed one trip where I didn't have to."

"That's allowed. Nobody said you had to solve the whole mystery of human existence on your very next jump."

"Feels like I should be more urgent about it, somehow. Like I'm supposed to be racing back there."

"You're allowed to take your time, Xander. This isn't a countdown. There's no deadline on when you decide you're ready to go back."

"I know. Doesn't always feel like I know it, though."

"That's normal too," Buffy said, closing her textbook to give him her full attention. "Big, scary things don't come with a schedule for when you're supposed to feel ready. You go when you go, same as the jumps. Not before."

He managed a real smile at that, the first one in days that didn't feel forced into place. "That's actually a good way to put it."

"I have my moments."

"Rare ones."

"Don't push your luck."

***



Chapter 79: Chapter 79



Boot print. Not Dad's.

I crouched down and checked anyway, out of habit, matching it against the tread I'd memorized without meaning to. Not his. Not Mom's. Not mine.

Fresh. A day old, maybe less.

I followed it a hundred yards. It stopped in open ground. No reason to stop there. No trail out.

Which meant one thing.

I radioed Dad that evening. Kept it short, the way he taught me to keep everything short on the radio, just in case.

"Found tracks. East perimeter. Not ours. They just end."

Pause. Longer than the news should've needed.

"You're sure they're not old?"

"I'm sure."

Another pause, and I could hear something underneath it this time, something I didn't have a name for yet but recognized anyway, because I'd heard it in his voice exactly twice before in my life. "Well." A beat. "That's new."

"That's what I thought too."

"You want the lecture about not jumping to conclusions, or should I skip it?"

"Skip it. I already jumped to it. Sorry."

"Cent."

"I'm serious, though. There's somebody else."

He didn't say anything for a second, and I let him have it. I'd had a whole afternoon to get my head around it. He'd had exactly forty seconds. I knew what forty seconds could do to Dad when something touched the old wound. Growing up, I'd watched him check locks twice on nights he never explained. Watched Mom go quiet the same way, somewhere else in the house, once he told her.

"We always figured it was possible," he said finally. "Statistically. Doesn't mean I expected to actually hear it."

"What do we do?"

"Nothing yet. Watch. Don't approach if they come back. We don't know anything about them."

"They don't know anything about us either."

"Cuts both ways," he agreed. I heard the same carefulness in it he always brought to anything that could circle back around to somebody using one of us against the other. He didn't say Cox's name. He almost never does. Not out of fear, I don't think. More like a debt he never got to finish paying — the man died settling accounts that weren't really his to settle. That kind of math sticks with a person. He didn't have to say the name for me to hear it anyway.

I looked at the print again after we hung up. Somebody had stood exactly there, and then hadn't. No car. No trail. No reason for the ground to just stop explaining itself like that, unless they'd done exactly what I'd have done.

Somebody else could do this.

I didn't sleep great that night, and it wasn't the bad kind of not sleeping. Mostly. Some of it was the bad kind too, the kind that comes from growing up knowing exactly what it can cost, loving somebody who can do what we do.

***

79.5. Cent: "Does Dad know?"

Mom caught me on the stairs before I'd even put the notebook away.

"You've got your thinking face on."

"Found a boot print out east. Not ours. Told Dad already, over the radio."

She didn't ask if I was sure, which I appreciated more than I said. Just: "Does Dad know what you think it means?"

"I think he's being careful about saying it out loud. I wasn't."

"What'd you say?"

"That there's somebody else."

Mom sat down on the step below me, which put her eye level with my knees, an old trick of hers from back when I was small enough that eye level actually mattered for these conversations. Still worked, honestly.

"How do you feel about that?"

"Scared. Excited. Both at once, which is a stupid combination of things to feel at the same time."

"It's not stupid. It's just big." She reached up, tucked a piece of hair behind my ear, which I would've swatted away from anyone else. "Your father's not being cagey to worry you less. He's being cagey because he doesn't know what to feel yet either, and he processes by going quiet."

"You process by asking a hundred questions."

"I process by asking exactly the right number of questions. There's a difference."

I almost laughed. "That's such a therapist thing to say."

"I am a therapist. Occupational hazard." She was quiet a second, something crossing her face I didn't have a word for yet. Not fear exactly. Just old, and still a little tender when it got poked. "For what it's worth — the government once used me as leverage against your father, back before you were born. Very politely, by their account. Lawyers, a judge, nobody raised their voice. Didn't make it less coercive, being handled politely. I don't want that memory running this conversation before we even know who this person is."

I hadn't expected her to say it plain like that. "You think Dad's scared of that. Specifically that."

"I think it's the shape every fear takes for him now, whether it fits or not. Doesn't mean he's wrong to be careful. Just means we have to be careful not to let one bad memory decide everything in advance."

She stood, hand on my shoulder for a second on her way past. "Keep documenting. Don't chase it. And Cent—it's okay to be excited. You don't have to apologize for that part just because it's also scary."

I sat there a while after she'd gone, notebook back open on my knees, and let myself actually feel the excited part without immediately talking myself out of it.

***



Chapter 80: Chapter 80



Nothing for two weeks. Long enough I started thinking I'd imagined the whole thing.

Then the tracks came back. Same spot. Foil wrapper this time, the kind off a ration bar. I didn't touch it. Photographed it instead, like Dad taught me.

"Twice now," I told him. "Same boots. Same spot."

"Could be his range just overlaps ours. Random."

"Or he noticed something's here and he's checking it out. Same as I would."

He turned that over, and I watched something tighten in his jaw before he spoke again. "What do you want to do?"

"Leave something. Not scary. Just—something that says we noticed too."

"That could go either way, Cent." He said it quiet, not sharp, but I heard the weight underneath it. "I've trusted the wrong people about this ability before. It cost me weeks I still don't talk about, and it put your mother through hell trying to get me back."

"I know, Dad. I know what it cost. I'm not being careless about it."

"I know you're not. I just need to say it out loud once, so it's sitting in the room with us instead of just in my head."

"I think it's worth the risk anyway. If it were me, I'd rather know somebody already saw me than wonder forever if I imagined the whole thing."

He was quiet a second. "Your mother would say the same thing."

"So we're doing it?"

"We're doing it." He said it like a decision he was making on purpose, not one he was just letting happen.

We carved a marker. One word. Welcome. Didn't overthink it past that, because overthinking it felt like the wrong instinct fully—this wasn't supposed to be complicated, it was supposed to be simple enough that a total stranger could read it and understand.

I set it myself. Stood back and looked at it a long minute.

Hope you're not scared of this, I thought at it, which is a stupid thing to think at a piece of wood, but there it was.

Checked every day for a week. Nothing. By day six I was standing there kicking dirt going "come on, come on," and by day seven I just said "fuck it" out loud to an empty field, which is a honestly stupid thing to do, but I was tired of being patient and having nothing to show for it.

Day eight, the marker had moved. Not damaged. Turned. Angled toward the trail, like somebody had picked it up, actually read it, and set it back down meaning something by it.

Next to it, scratched in the dirt with a stick:

Hi.

I stood there a while, laughing and not-quite-crying at the same time, which is an annoying combination of things to be doing at once, and then I ran the whole way home to tell Dad the door had opened.

***

80.5. Cent: "You should've seen my face."

Mom was still up when I got back, reading in the good chair by the fire, and I must have looked like something had happened, because she put the book down before I'd said a word.

"What."

"He answered. Actually answered. Not just moving the marker—there's writing this time."

She sat up straighter. "What'd it say?"

"Hi. Just that. One word, scratched in the dirt with a stick."

"That's it?"

"That's everything, Mom. You should've seen my face when I found it. I don't even know what my face was doing. Some combination of laughing and crying that I don't think has an actual name."

Mom smiled, the slow kind she does when she's actually moved and trying not to make a whole thing of it. "Where's your father? Does he know yet?"

"Told him on the way in. He's still processing. Doing the quiet thing."

"He'll come around to properly excited by breakfast. Give him a night." She patted the cushion beside her, and I sat, still buzzing too much to actually settle. "I'm glad it's real, is the thing. I know some part of you was scared it wouldn't be."

"Some part of me still can't fully believe it is."

"That's allowed too. Belief takes a minute to catch up to good news sometimes." She squeezed my hand once, and then, quieter, "I spent a long stretch of my life waiting for word that your father was still alive. I know what it does to a person, not knowing. I'm glad this one's got an easier answer than that."

I hadn't known what to say to that the first few times she'd said something like it, years ago, before I understood what she meant. I still didn't have a perfect answer now. "I'm glad too. For both of you."

"Get some sleep if you can. I have a feeling tomorrow's going to want you well-rested."

I didn't sleep much anyway. Didn't really mind.

***



Chapter 81: Chapter 81



It took Xander eleven days to decide he was ready, and even then, he wasn't sure ready was the right word for it — more that the not-knowing had finally gotten heavier than the fear of finding out.

"You don't even know if you'll end up anywhere near it," Willow reminded him, the morning of, watching him check and recheck his pack with more care than usual. "It found you once. That's not the same as it finding you twice."

"I know. Doesn't change anything, though. If the jump puts me somewhere else entirely, fine, I'll go do whatever that world needs from me instead. But if it puts me back there again, I don't want to spend three days telling myself I'm still not ready to go look. I've done enough of that already."

"And if it does put you back? You still don't have to make contact."

"I know. Same plan as last time, if I even get the chance to use it. Observe first. Decide the rest once I'm actually standing there."

The jump dropped him a short distance from where he remembered the fence line, and he approached carefully, the same caution he'd carried the first time settling back over him the moment the tree line came into view.

The fence was still there, exactly as he'd left it in his memory, and he followed it slower this time, more deliberate, giving himself permission to actually look rather than simply confirm what he already knew was real.

It was the marker that stopped him cold.

A small wooden post, driven into the ground a short distance off the fence line, carved with a single word he read three times before he trusted it: *Welcome.*

"Okay," he said, quietly, feeling something enormous shift in his chest. "Okay, that's not — that's not something a farm just has lying around. That's for me. That's specifically for me."

He stood there a long time, hand hovering near the marker without quite touching it, running through every possible interpretation before settling on the one that actually made sense: somebody knew. Somebody had found evidence of him, the same way he'd found evidence of them, and instead of fear or aggression, they'd left him an invitation.

'They know,' he thought, staring at the word until it stopped looking like just letters and started looking like an actual hand reaching out. 'They found my tracks or something, and they know, and instead of being scared, they left this.' The relief of it hit harder than he expected, four years of solitary uncertainty cracking open into something that finally, genuinely felt less lonely.

He didn't approach the homestead itself, still not ready for that step, but he picked up the marker, turned it over carefully in his hands, feeling the care in the carving, and set it back down exactly where he'd found it, angled slightly toward the trail he'd taken in — an answer, of sorts, without yet being ready to give a fuller one.

Then, crouching down beside it, he found a stick and scratched two letters into the dirt, simple and small and completely inadequate for the size of what he actually wanted to say.

*Hi.*

He stayed close by for another hour, watching from a careful distance, some part of him hoping whoever had left the marker might appear so he could see them at last, but nobody came, and eventually the day wore toward evening and he retreated to make camp well clear of the fence line, mind racing too fast for much actual sleep.

'Somebody out there knows I exist,' he thought, staring up at unfamiliar stars. 'For the first time in four years, somebody actually knows.' It felt like the biggest thing that had ever happened to him, bigger even than the ability itself, and he lay awake a long while just holding the size of it, unsure yet whether he was ready for whatever came next, but no longer alone in wondering.

***



Chapter 82: Chapter 82



"They left a marker," Xander said, spreading the photos across the kitchen table for the second time in as many weeks, though this batch was different — the carved word, the angle of it, his own scratched response beside it. "A real one. Carved. Welcome. And when I answered, they didn't just leave it there. They read it."

Willow leaned in, studying the photo of the dirt-scratched "Hi" like it might reveal more if she looked hard enough. "This is real contact. Actual, two-way contact."

"I know. I keep looking at it and it still doesn't feel real."

"What happens now?" Buffy asked.

"I don't know. I want to go back. I also have no idea what the actual next step looks like. Do I leave another note? Do I try to actually show myself? What if I do that and they're not ready, or I'm not ready, or—" He stopped, running a hand through his hair. "There's no manual for this. Nobody's ever had to figure out how to introduce themselves to the only other person who might understand what they are."

Giles, who'd been quiet through most of this, finally spoke. "May I make a suggestion?"

"Please."

"Let them lead, if they're willing to. They left the first marker. They may have thoughts on how they'd like this to unfold that are better informed than any guess we might make from here." Giles adjusted his glasses. "You could leave a note asking exactly that — how they'd like to proceed. It puts the next move in their hands rather than forcing you to guess blind."

"That's actually really smart," Xander admitted. "Less pressure on me to get it right if I'm just asking instead of deciding."

"It also signals something important," Giles added. "That you're not simply pushing forward on your own timeline. That you're willing to move at a pace that works for both of you."

Faith, who'd been listening from the counter, finally spoke up. "Take it from someone who's spent the last few months learning that big scary things go better with somebody else setting some of the terms — do that. Let them help steer. It's not weakness. It's just smart."

"Since when do you dispense wisdom about big scary things," Xander said, though there wasn't any real teasing in it.

"Since I've had some practice lately," Faith said, with a small, tired smile. "Learned some stuff the hard way. Figured I'd pass it along cheap."

Xander considered the whole table — Willow already sketching out draft wording for the note, Giles making careful notes of his own about protocol and pacing, Buffy watching him with the quiet attentiveness she always carried when something actually mattered, Faith leaning against the counter, at ease in a way that came from having really been where he currently stood.

"Okay," he said finally. "I'll ask them how they want to do this. No assumptions. Just — an actual question, and then I'll wait for the answer."

"That's exactly the right call," Giles said.

"Feels like the right call," Xander agreed. "Doesn't make it feel less enormous, though."

"It's allowed to be both," Buffy said, reaching over to squeeze his hand. "Enormous and right aren't opposites."

He managed a real smile at that, and the table eased into the quiet, focused work of drafting a message meant to travel further than any of them had ever really considered possible before this whole thing started.

***

82.5. Chapter 82.5

Willow came along this time, both of them needing a break from the weight of everything currently unfolding with the marker and the note and the fence line waiting on another world entirely.

The jump dropped them somewhere unexpectedly cold, snow dusting a pine forest that stretched further than either of them could see.

"Okay, didn't pack for this," Xander said, already layering up. "Willow, please tell me you packed for this."

"I packed for everything. Always. It's kind of my whole thing." Willow pulled out extra layers, then a squat little propane heater that folded out from something not much bigger than a paperback, with visible satisfaction. "You're welcome."

They staked the shed out before doing anything else, both of them having learned that specific lesson the hard way on colder trips than this one, and spent the first day working through the snow-dusted trees, cataloguing tracks from something neither of them could identify, ducking back to camp every so often to thaw out properly by the heater before pushing back into the cold again, and found the animal itself on the second morning — a fox-like creature with fur so thick it looked almost spherical, entirely unbothered by the open-air cold that still had both of them shivering the moment they stepped more than a few yards from camp.

"That's so unfair," Xander said, watching it saunter past. "It's got a full winter coat and I'm out here regretting every life choice that led to this exact moment."

"Evolution's not fair. That's kind of the whole point of evolution."

"I know. Doesn't make it less annoying to watch in person." He crouched down slowly, and the creature approached him first, curious rather than wary, sniffing at his offered glove before allowing an actual scratch behind one round ear. "Also, hi, hello, you're coming home with us, aren't you. Alan's gonna lose his mind over the coat alone."

It settled into the shed with them that night easily enough, more interested in the warmth than in escaping it, curling up near the door like it had simply decided this was the better deal.

They spent the rest of the trip documenting the forest's quiet winter rhythm, and by the third evening had fell into an easy, companionable silence outside the shed around their campfire, Willow tucked against his side under a shared blanket, the weight of everything waiting back home feeling, for a little while, truly distant, before the real cold set back in and sent them both retreating inside for the night.

"This is helping," Xander admitted, staring into the flames, pressing a kiss to the top of her head. "Having something to focus on that isn't just waiting for the next note under a rock."

"Yeah. I figured it might. That's part of why I wanted to come this time." Willow tipped her face up for a proper kiss instead, unhurried, firelight catching against both of them.

"Thanks. Genuinely."

"Anytime. That's what I'm here for," she said, settling back against his shoulder.

They made it home with the strange, round fox curled contentedly in Xander's arms and a full pack of firewood samples Willow insisted on bringing back for reasons she promised would make sense later — exactly the break both of them had needed, and neither of them in a hurry to call it anything fancier than that.

Buffy opened the door already talking, then stopped dead at the sight of the fox creature blinking sleepily up at her from Xander's arms. "Okay, and also, we have a fox now. A weird round fox. Just — that's happening, that's a thing that's happening in this house now."

"Cold, but good. Exactly what I needed before whatever happens next." Xander adjusted his grip as the fox creature yawned, totally unbothered by its new surroundings. "And yeah. We have a fox now. Alan's gonna love this one, it's basically a very dense, very round marshmallow."

Buffy reached out to scratch behind one round ear despite herself, already halfway to losing the argument with her own resolve not to get attached before Alan even saw it.

***



Chapter 83: Chapter 83



Dad wanted to wait. Mom wanted to wait longer. I wanted to know things now, which is apparently a family trait, because Mom laughed when I said it and didn't argue the point.

"He's being careful," Dad said, third time he'd said it that dinner. "That's good. Means he's not reckless."

"I know it's good. I'm not saying rush him. I'm saying I want to actually talk to him. Words, not sticks and dirt."

"You could go back with a note. Ask how he wants to do this."

"I could. Feels weird, though. Like—hey, stranger, want to have the biggest conversation of both our lives via handwriting in the mud."

Mom snorted into her water glass. "Better than the alternative. You want to just show up unannounced?"

"No." I did, a little. Didn't say it.

Dad caught it anyway. He's annoyingly good at that. "You do want to."

"A little. I'm not gonna, though. I'm not an idiot."

"Never said you were."

"You were thinking it."

"I was thinking you're seventeen and impatient, which is different from idiot, but overlaps sometimes."

I threw a roll at him. He caught it one-handed without looking, because of course he did, twenty-some years of catching things people throw at him.

"Okay. Note it is." I pulled out paper right there at the table, because waiting felt worse than writing something imperfect. "What do I even say?"

"Something simple," Mom said. "You're not writing a treaty. You're writing hello."

I wrote: *Whenever you're ready. We'll wait. No rush.* Read it back twice. Added: *Also, curious what your name is, if you want to share it.*

Dad read over my shoulder. "Good. Not too much. Leaves it open." He paused, still looking at it. "Don't mention the reserve by name. Don't mention the lodge. If he's exactly who he seems, it won't matter. If he's not, it will."

"I wasn't going to."

"I know. Habit, saying it out loud anyway."

"You think he'll answer fast?"

"No idea. Man's been doing this alone for years, from what the note implies. Might take him a minute to trust it's real."

I thought about that—alone, for years, no family who understood, no marker on a wall telling him he wasn't imagining things. Made my own version of this look easy by comparison, and I hadn't exactly been finding it easy.

"I hope he's okay," I said, mostly to myself.

"Yeah," Dad said, quiet. "Me too."

I carried the note out myself the next morning. Set it under a rock by the marker, same spot, and stood there a minute before heading back.

Whoever you are, I thought. Hope the alone part's almost over.

***



Chapter 84: Chapter 84



Willow was still knee-deep in Cent's family history questions and Buffy had a Slayer training session she couldn't move, which left the tundra to Xander alone.

The tundra stretched flat and pale under a sky so wide it made Xander feel smaller than he had in months, low scrub and lichen-covered rock running to a horizon broken only by distant, snow-capped peaks.

"Okay," he said, breath fogging in air cold enough to bite. "Cold again. Very cold. Willow's chart's gonna have a field day with how many cold ones I've had lately."

The caribou herd appeared on the second day, hundreds of them moving across the tundra in a loose, patient migration, antlers catching the pale sun, entirely unbothered by the stranger who'd walked right into the middle of the slow-moving mass without a single one so much as breaking stride. He let one graze close enough to rest a hand against its flank for a moment, the same casual, easy contact he'd have offered any calm animal back home, before it wandered on with the rest of the herd, utterly unbothered by the whole encounter.

"You guys are a lot," he said, honestly awed despite the cold. "Like, an unreasonable amount of caribou for one horizon."

He thought about the tranq pistol exactly once, weighing a real tissue sample against the fact that the herd was already calm enough to touch without one — decided a light dart on an animal this docile felt like using a hammer on something that just wanted to be left alone, and settled for what he could get by hand instead: a few shed antler fragments off the ground, a tuft of loose winter coat caught on scrub brush, close enough to actual science that Willow probably wouldn't complain too hard about the missing blood work.

He spent the rest of the trip documenting the herd's movement, the way they grazed and shifted as a single organism rather than individuals, and found himself thinking, more than once, about the marker back on the other world, about the note he'd left, about whether an answer was waiting even now while he stood here freezing in the name of good science.

'Focus,' he told himself, more than once, forcing his attention back to the caribou. 'This trip's for this. That one's for later.' It was harder than it should have been, four years of single-minded cataloguing suddenly competing with a question that mattered more than any animal he'd ever photographed.

He made it home two days later, half-frozen and thoroughly documented, and found himself standing in his own kitchen doorway for a long moment before he could bring himself to ask the question actually sitting in his chest.

"Any word?" he asked Willow, who'd taken to checking in on his behalf whenever he was gone, an arrangement neither of them had ever formally agreed to but both understood completely.

"Not that I know of. You'd be the one to check, though. That's your trip to make."

"Right. Yeah. Obviously." He set his caribou photos down without really looking at them. "I think I need to go back there next. Not this cycle. The one after."

"Whenever you're ready."

"Everybody keeps saying that. Whenever I'm ready. Feels like I should be more ready by now, given how many people are being patient with me about it."

"Being ready isn't a thing you can rush just because people are patient about it," Willow said gently. "It happens when it happens."

"That's annoyingly wise."

"I have my moments."

"That's Faith's line."

"I'm allowed to borrow it. She borrowed 'contain multitudes' from you first."

He laughed at that, surprising himself with how easy it came, and the ordinary, easy comfort of the kitchen settled back around him, caribou photos still waiting to be properly catalogued, the bigger question still waiting too, patient in its own way as anything else out there on the tundra.

***



Chapter 85: Chapter 85



The rock had a note under it this time, folded twice, edges damp from a rain that had come through sometime in the days since it was left.

Xander read it standing right there, not trusting himself to wait until he'd found somewhere to sit.

*Whenever you're ready. We'll wait. No rush. Also, curious what your name is, if you want to share it.*

"Okay," he said, out loud, to the fence and the field and nobody in particular. "Okay, that's — that's a real question. That's somebody actually wanting to know me."

He sat down right there in the grass, pulled out the small notebook he'd started carrying specifically for this, and thought for a long moment about how to answer something that felt, despite being only four words long, like it deserved more weight than a quick scrawl.

*Xander,* he wrote finally. *That's my name. Been doing this four years, and until your marker showed up I figured I was the only one this had ever happened to. Whatever pace works for you works for me. Not in a rush either, just really glad I'm not imagining you.*

He read it back twice, decided it was honest even if it wasn't especially polished, and folded it carefully before tucking it under the same rock, weighted down against wind and weather.

'Four years,' he thought, sitting back on his heels, looking out at the field beyond the fence line. 'Four years thinking I was the only person this had ever happened to, and I finally get to say that out loud to somebody who actually understands what that number means.' Buffy and Willow had made the weekends bearable, made the danger shareable, made the whole thing feel less like a burden — but neither of them had ever once felt the pull itself, and there was a certain kind of alone in that difference he'd never quite had words for until right now, setting it down for the first time.

He stayed a while longer, watching the field, half hoping someone might appear despite knowing that wasn't the arrangement yet, and eventually made his way back to camp as the light started to fail, mind turning over the exchange the whole way.

He told the group everything when he got home, reading his own note out loud like it needed a witness to feel real.

"You told them your actual name," Buffy said, something soft in her voice.

"Felt right. They asked. Didn't feel like a question worth dodging."

"How do you feel?" Willow asked.

"Good. Genuinely good. Scared, still, a little. But good." He looked around the table at all of them, four years of assuming he was the only one who'd ever felt this particular pull suddenly feeling like they'd been leading somewhere he hadn't seen coming until now. "I think this is actually happening. I think I'm actually gonna meet them."

"When you're ready," Giles said.

"Yeah," Xander agreed, and for the first time, the word ready didn't feel quite so far away as it had every other time he'd said it.

***



Chapter 86: Chapter 86



Xander.

I said it out loud a few times, just to hear how it sounded attached to an actual person instead of a boot print and a foil wrapper.

Four years alone. He'd written it plain, no drama around it, which somehow made it worse than if he'd tried to make it sound tragic. Just a fact. Four years, alone, and then a piece of carved wood changed that.

I brought the note to dinner. Read it out loud once, then let Dad read it himself, because some things land different in somebody else's voice.

"Four years," Dad said, setting it down carefully. "That's a long time to carry something like this by yourself."

"Longer than I've even had it."

"You had us to figure it out with, though, the second it happened. He didn't have anybody."

That sat with me a minute. Getting the ability wasn't the hard part, not for me—it happened, it was terrifying for about a day, and then Mom and Dad were right there walking me through it. It was the not-having-anyone-who-understood part I'd never actually had to face. He'd had both, alone, for four straight years.

"I want to meet him," I said. "Properly. Not notes under a rock forever."

"I know you do."

"You don't sound thrilled."

"I'm not not thrilled. I'm careful. There's a difference, and you know the difference, because you're the one always saying it to me about your own stunts."

Fair. I let him have that one.

"When, though," I said. "Not today. Not this week. But eventually there's gotta be an actual when."

"I'd like more than a name and four years of loneliness before we set anything," Dad said. "Not because I don't believe him. Because believing him and verifying him aren't the same thing, and I've paid for skipping that step before."

Mom finally spoke up from across the table. "Let him set it. He's the one who's been alone longer. He gets to decide when he's ready to not be. Verifying and rushing aren't the same thing either, Davy."

I didn't love waiting on somebody else's timeline. Never have. But I turned it over and had to admit she was right—easier for me to be patient with somebody else's fear than it would've been in his position, four years deep into believing there was nobody else out there at all.

I wrote back that night. Kept it short.

*Xander. Good name. I'm Cent. Whenever you're ready, we'll be here. That's not a line, I mean it.*

I almost added more. Almost asked a dozen questions I'd been sitting on for weeks. Didn't. Some things earn patience, even from me.

Set it under the rock the next morning. Walked home without checking whether he'd already come and gone. For once, I just let myself trust that he would, in his own time. That trusting felt like its own kind of progress.

***

86.5. Cent: "How do you feel?"

I read Dad the note out loud a second time, mostly because saying Xander's name felt like it needed saying twice to become real.

"He has a name," I said. "Xander."

Dad chewed slower. Thinking face. "Good name."

"That's what I thought."

Mom looked up from her book. "Did you write back?"

"Already did. Told him mine too."

"How do you feel?"

I had to actually think about that one. "Like something's been sitting loose in my chest for weeks and it just clicked into place."

Dad reached over, squeezed my shoulder once, didn't say anything else, which from him is its own kind of a lot.

***



Chapter 87: Chapter 87



The symbol research had gone quiet for a stretch, which Faith had learned by now didn't mean nothing was happening, just that Giles was deep in a phase of reading that didn't produce anything worth reporting yet.

"Anything?" she asked, dropping into her usual chair at the Magic Box.

"Slower going than I'd like," Giles admitted, not looking up from his book. "The margin note about jumpers finding one another is the most concrete thing I've found in weeks. Everything else is fragments, half-references, scholars two centuries removed from the actual event making guesses same as we are."

"So basically nothing new."

"Basically nothing new," he agreed. "Though I don't consider that fully discouraging. Some mysteries take far longer than a few months to properly unravel."

"Feels weird, this part being slow. The mural thing moved so fast. Nine days and then it was just — over."

"This is a different kind of mystery. The mural had a deadline forcing our hand. This one doesn't, which means it can afford to be patient, even if that patience is occasionally frustrating."

Faith considered that, turning it over. "Guess I've gotten used to things happening fast around here. Forgot slow's allowed to be its own thing."

"Slow is very often the more honest pace," Giles said. "Fast tends to mean someone's in danger. Slow tends to mean we actually have the luxury of getting it right."

"When you put it that way, slow doesn't sound so bad."

"It rarely does, in retrospect." Giles finally looked up. "How are you finding the pace of things generally? Beyond the research."

"Good, actually. Really good. First time in a while nothing's actively on fire." Faith stretched, working out a knot from patrol the night before. "Feels weird not having a countdown hanging over me. Gonna take some getting used to."

"You're allowed to enjoy the quiet stretches. They don't come around as often as anyone would like in this town."

"Yeah. Trying to actually enjoy it instead of waiting for the other shoe." She glanced at the research table, currently quiet, no urgent countdown ticking beneath it. "Guess I'll let you get back to your fragments and guesses."

"I appreciate the company regardless of the pace," Giles said, and meant it, and Faith left the Magic Box that evening with the strange, unfamiliar comfort of a mystery that could simply wait, and a life around it that, for once, didn't need to rush toward anything at all.

***

87.5. Chapter 87.5

Willow and Buffy both came this time, the research on Faith's symbol having gone quiet enough that all three of them could afford a few days away without guilt.

The world was savanna again, though wetter than most Xander had visited, a river cutting through the grassland thick with wildlife along both banks.

"Okay, this one's busy," Buffy said, watching a crocodile-adjacent creature slide into the water. "Very busy. Very much a lot going on at once."

"Willow's gonna need a week just for the reptiles," Xander said. "Let alone everything else."

"I've made peace with needing a week for most of these trips at this point," Willow said, already crouched by the water's edge, delighted.

They spent three full days working the riverbank, cataloguing more species in one trip than most worlds offered in a month, and by the final evening had filled nearly every page of Willow's notebook with careful, excited observations.

"This might be the best one yet," Willow said, looking over her notes with visible satisfaction. "Genuinely might be."

"High bar, given everything else we've seen," Buffy said. "But yeah. I'll agree with that assessment."

They made it home exhausted in the good way, cameras full, notebooks bursting, all three of them reminded exactly why this strange, impossible job was worth every complication that came bundled with it.

Faith looked up from the couch as they piled in, still buzzing. "That good, huh? You're all doing the glow thing."

"Best one in a while," Xander confirmed. "You should've come. Genuinely."

"Next time," Faith said, something considering crossing her face again. "Maybe next time."

***



Chapter 88: Chapter 88



"New topic," Jesse announced, sliding into the booth with the energy of a man who'd been saving something up all day. "Hypothetically. If you could have one animal from Xander's collection as a pet, which one?"

"Gerald," Faith said, without hesitation. "Already established. Gerald's the correct answer to every version of this question."

"You can't keep picking Gerald, that's not in the spirit of the hypothetical."

"It's exactly in the spirit of the hypothetical. Gerald's objectively the best answer."

"Gerald bit somebody once."

"Gerald bit somebody who deserved it."

Willow, arriving mid-argument, sat down with her usual notebook conspicuously absent for once. "Are we still doing the Gerald debate? I feel like we resolved this months ago."

"We resolved it in Gerald's favor, and Jesse's still salty about it."

"I'm not salty. I'm advocating for variety. There's a whole zoo of options and everybody just says Gerald."

"Because Gerald's correct," Faith said. "I don't make the rules of correctness, I just recognize them when I see them."

"That's not how correctness works."

"It's exactly how correctness works."

Buffy slid in last, catching the tail end of it with visible amusement. "Still the Gerald thing?"

"Still the Gerald thing," Willow confirmed.

"I have a hypothetical of my own," Buffy said. "If the sloth ever actually got caught, who has to be the one to explain that to Animal Control?"

The table went briefly, genuinely quiet, considering the sheer scale of that specific hypothetical disaster.

"Not it," Jesse said immediately.

"Not it," Faith echoed.

"I feel like it should be Xander, since it's his sloth," Willow said.

"It's not my sloth," Xander said, arriving in time to catch the accusation, dropping into the seat beside Buffy and stealing a quick kiss hello before he'd even fully sat down, the gesture automatic enough that nobody at the table so much as glanced up. "I've never even touched the sloth. I photographed it from a respectful, non-custody-implying distance."

"You'd still be the one explaining it."

"I would be, and I've already started drafting the explanation in my head, just in case, because apparently that's a genuine possibility I now have to prepare for."

"What's the explanation?" Jesse asked, delighted.

"Currently just 'no comment' repeated with increasing confidence until they give up."

"That's not a strategy."

"It's the only strategy I've got. I'm open to suggestions."

"Bribery," Jesse offered. "Works on everybody. I bribed the vice principal with donuts for two years and he's never once written me up."

"That's not the flex you think it is, Jesse."

"It's absolutely the flex I think it is. I have zero disciplinary record and a suspiciously friendly relationship with the front office. That's a legacy."

"That's a bribery scandal waiting to happen."

"Only if somebody talks, and nobody's gonna talk, because everybody who knows is also implicated." Jesse grinned, completely unbothered. "Mutually assured destruction. It's a beautiful system."

Willow made a face. "I did not need to know that our vice principal takes bribes from a sixteen-year-old."

"He doesn't take bribes. He accepts gifts. There's a legal distinction, my dad explained it to me once, very thoroughly, after a similar incident with a different authority figure."

"That sentence should concern me more than it does."

The table dissolved into genuine, easy laughter after that, workshopping increasingly absurd cover stories for a hypothetical sloth-related legal crisis that would, statistically, almost certainly never happen, and for one evening at least, nobody at the table needed to think about anything bigger than that.

***



Chapter 89: Chapter 89



It happened on an ordinary Tuesday, which Xander would later find almost funny — no dramatic buildup, no sudden clarity, just him sitting at the kitchen table doing homework when he realized, quietly and without fanfare, that he was ready.

"I want to go meet them," he said, to Willow, who was sitting across from him working through her own assignment. "Not next cycle. This one. I think I'm actually ready."

Willow looked up. "You sound sure."

"I am sure. I don't know when that happened, exactly. I think it just kind of arrived while I wasn't looking for it."

"That tracks with how most big things actually work, honestly. You don't usually feel it building. You just wake up one day already there."
