The room went quiet for a moment, three people independently arriving at the same low-grade unease without quite wanting to name it out loud first, the kind of silence that settled over people who'd each individually decided, without discussing it, that a pattern was worth taking seriously.

"A week," Steve said finally, decisive in the way he always got once he'd actually made up his mind about something. "If he's not back or in contact within a week, we start actually looking. Properly. Not just noting it and hoping it resolves itself."

"A week," Natasha agreed, and made a note on the tablet, the number sitting there glowing quietly, an unremarkable little countdown that none of them yet understood the actual shape of, or where, precisely, it might eventually lead them looking — a small, ordinary Tuesday morning conversation that would turn out, in hindsight, to be the first thread of something considerably larger than any of them currently had reason to suspect.

"For what it's worth," Bruce added, already halfway back into his own thoughts, "if it does turn out to be something, I'd rather know sooner than later. I don't love surprises these days. Had enough of those for one lifetime."

"None of us love surprises," Natasha said. "That's basically the whole job description, though. Loving them was never part of the deal."

Steve managed something close to a tired smile at that, and the three of them settled back into their separate work, the countdown quietly ticking forward in the background of an otherwise unremarkable morning.

***



Chapter 101: Chapter 101



The man appeared in the middle of Main Street on a Tuesday afternoon, which under normal Sunnydale circumstances might have drawn notably more panic than it actually did, mostly because he looked less like a threat and more like someone who'd wandered off the set of a Renaissance festival and gotten seriously, spectacularly lost.

He was enormous, for one thing — well over six feet, built like he split logs recreationally, dressed in something that might generously be called armor if armor came in "vaguely medieval cosplay" instead of anything a blacksmith had actually made. He stood in the middle of the street, turning in a slow circle, expression somewhere between confusion and offended dignity, like a man used to being recognized on sight.

"This is not Asgard," he announced, to no one in particular, in a voice that carried significantly further than the empty street should have allowed.

A woman walking her dog gave him a wide, nervous berth and kept walking, muttering something that might have been "renaissance fair got weird this year" under her breath.

"Nor does it appear to be Midgard proper, in any capacity I recognize," he continued, undeterred by the lack of audience. "Curious."

He tried a small storefront next, peering through the glass at a display of discount candles with genuine scholarly interest, and moved on to studying a parked sedan with the same intensity, running one hand along its hood as if expecting it to reveal some hidden mechanism.

He was still standing there, turning slowly, scanning rooftops like a man tracking something specific, when Xander happened to walk by on his way back from the Magic Box, took one look, and stopped dead.

"Okay," Xander said, mostly to himself. "Okay, that's a whole Renaissance-festival situation happening in the middle of the street, and normally I'd just keep walking, but something about that specifically feels like it's gonna become my problem regardless of what I do."

The man turned, spotted him, and brightened far.

"You! Small mortal! I require information."

"I'm being called small by a man who looks like he benches trucks recreationally, but sure, go ahead."

"I seek an object. A hammer, forged in Asgard, sent from my realm some time ago by a trusted friend, for reasons I am still working to fully understand." He said it plainly, expecting the sentence to make perfect sense to whoever heard it. "My senses tell me it rests somewhere in this vicinity. Have you encountered it?"

Xander opened his mouth, closed it, and took a moment to fully process the sentence he'd just heard.

"Okay, weirdly, yes, actually, I think I might have. Big guy, dresses like you, name's Thor?"

The man's whole posture shifted, delight breaking across his face like weather clearing. "You know of me!"

"I know of you from a comic book, mostly, and also apparently now from you standing in the middle of my actual street, which is a truly strange sentence to be saying out loud right now." Xander rubbed his face. "Okay. Yeah. I think I know exactly where your hammer is. Might wanna sit down for the explanation, though. It's a whole thing."

"I do not require sitting. I require my hammer."

"Fair. Very direct. I respect it." Xander gestured vaguely toward the rest of town. "C'mon. Follow me. I know a guy who's gonna have a lot of feelings about this conversation."

Thor fell into step beside him, considerably more graceful than his size should have allowed, still scanning the surrounding buildings with quiet, focused intensity.

"This realm feels smaller than I expected," he observed. "Also much more beige."

"Yeah, that's Sunnydale. Small and beige, with occasional demons. You get used to it."

"Demons," Thor repeated, mildly interested rather than alarmed, like this was a Tuesday rather than a revelation. "I have some experience there."

"Somehow I believe you."

They passed a hardware store, a laundromat, a coffee shop with a hand-lettered sign advertising a seasonal latte, Thor taking in each with the same open, cataloguing curiosity, occasionally pausing to examine something — a fire hydrant, a mailbox, a bicycle chained to a lamppost — determined to understand every strange new piece of this small, unremarkable town.

"What is this vehicle's purpose," he asked, stopping beside the bicycle. "It appears to require considerable personal effort for such modest speed."

"It's a bike. Exercise, mostly, or getting around without a car. It's very normal, please keep walking."

"I find it charming. Inefficient, but charming."

"You're gonna find a lot of stuff here charming-but-inefficient. Just a heads up."

"I look forward to it," Thor said, utterly sincere, and Xander found himself, despite the sheer absurdity of the afternoon, unable to entirely suppress a small, disbelieving laugh.

"You're taking this whole thing remarkably well, by the way. The finding-out-you're-in-the-wrong-reality thing."

"I have found, over considerable years, that alarm rarely improves a difficult situation. Better to gather information calmly and act once the shape of things is clear." Thor glanced at him sideways. "You seem remarkably calm yourself, for someone who's just been informed a god is walking through his town."

"Four years of finding car-sized sloths and getting kidnapped by penguins has given me a pretty generous tolerance for weird, honestly. You're just today's weird. Ask me again in a week, might have a different answer."

"A reasonable coping strategy."

"It's the only one I've got."

They reached the Magic Box a few minutes later, Xander already dreading, with real specificity, exactly how this conversation with Giles was about to go, and paused at the door a moment before pushing it open.

"Fair warning," Xander said. "The guy inside's gonna have a lot of questions, and he's gonna want to write most of your answers down."

"I have no objection to being documented. I find scholars, generally, easier to work with than soldiers."

"That's a good attitude to have here, honestly. We've got a lot of scholars. Fewer soldiers than you'd expect, given everything."

"Then perhaps," Thor said, something almost hopeful in his voice, "this search has finally led me somewhere reasonable."

"Define reasonable," Xander said, and pushed the door open, bell chiming overhead, Giles looking up from his research with an expression that would, within the next thirty seconds, undergo several rapid and dramatic changes.

***

101.5. Heimdall: What the Eye Sees

Heimdall watched Thor arrive in Sunnydale from the observatory, same as he watched everything eventually, standing where he always stood, golden eyes fixed on a realm most of Asgard would never think to wonder about at all. He permitted himself something close to a smile at the look on the prince's face upon realizing this, of all realms, was where his search had finally led.

Small. Beige, by Thor's own assessment, relayed later and repeated since with visible bemusement. A realm that should, by any reasonable measure, have held nothing worth a god's attention. And yet here it sat, at the exact center of a disturbance vast enough to have reached even his own ancient, patient sight.

He had watched countless realms across countless centuries, had seen empires rise and fall in the time it took other beings to blink, and still, occasionally, a place like this one caught his attention in ways he couldn't fully explain even to himself — small, ordinary, unremarkable by every measure that usually mattered, and yet somehow, quietly, becoming the fulcrum on which something notably larger balanced.

Heimdall had not chosen where the hammer landed, not truly — Mjolnir's own will had carried it there, same as it always did when he set it loose during a crisis, trusting its judgment more than his own in matters of where worthiness might be found. But he had watched, in the intervening time, patiently, the way anyone who understood that worthiness rarely announced itself loudly learned to watch.

He had seen the golden-haired girl lift it without thought, the weight yielding easily to hands that carried burdens Heimdall recognized immediately, having watched significantly more than one champion across considerably more than one realm — a readiness that came not from strength alone but from a settled, steady willingness to carry weight on behalf of others without needing to be asked twice.

He had seen the other girl try, and fail, and the flicker of old hurt crossing her face before she buried it beneath practiced nonchalance — and he had recognized that too, the old ache of measuring your own worth by comparison rather than by any true accounting of what you actually carried. He had watched her walk away from that failure carrying it quietly, the way so many carried such things, unspoken and unresolved, waiting for a moment she didn't yet know was coming.

He had watched, later, when that same girl returned alone, and lifted it after all, and felt something that might have been satisfaction settle in his old, patient chest — proof, once again, that the hammer's judgment ran deeper and slower than most who encountered it ever fully appreciated.

'Interesting,' he thought, watching both moments replay in his memory with the effortless clarity of his sight. 'The hammer made its assessment of one quickly. The other required time to properly become what the hammer was already waiting for.'

He said none of this to Thor, who remained convinced, as princes often did, that his search was purely about retrieval — the simple matter of finding an object and bringing it home. Heimdall had long since learned that princes, even good ones, even ones he loved as he loved Thor, rarely appreciated having their simple errands revealed as something far more layered than they'd assumed.

Heimdall knew better, or suspected he did, watching the small, strange realm continue its small, strange business fully unaware of how closely it was being observed — a town full of ordinary people carrying, without fully realizing it, the weight of something that reached far beyond their own understanding of the stakes.

'Let him learn slowly,' he thought, turning his attention, briefly, to other realms requiring his sight before returning it, as he always eventually did, to the quiet, beige town that had somehow become the center of much more attention than its size would ever suggest it deserved. 'Some lessons land better without being rushed.'

He watched the girl called Faith a while longer, curious despite himself about what shape her eventual readiness might take, and found himself, not for the first time in his long watching, honestly uncertain how the story would resolve — a rare and not unwelcome sensation, after so many centuries of seeing patterns repeat themselves across realm after realm.

He watched a while longer, patient as ever, and said nothing to anyone, keeping his own counsel the way he always did — the same way, he reflected, that Giles the Watcher was currently keeping his, an ocean and a universe apart, both of them equally certain that some truths were better held quietly than spoken too soon, both of them, in their own separate ways, guardians of secrets they'd chosen to carry alone rather than risk mishandling by sharing too early.

Somewhere far beyond even his considerable sight, the disturbance that had first drawn his attention to this small realm continued its slow, patient work, and Heimdall, watching it all unfold from a vantage point no other being in the Nine Realms could claim, found himself hoping, quietly, that whatever came of it would prove worth the long, careful watching he'd already invested.

***

101.7. Chapter 101.7

Giles took the news of Thor's identity with notably more composure than Xander had expected, mostly by way of removing his glasses, cleaning them with unusual thoroughness, and asking exactly one clarifying question before proceeding as though gods showing up in his shop was simply Tuesday. Buffy, who'd arrived partway through the introduction, had slipped into a chair wide-eyed and quiet, doing careful math on how many impossible things one lifetime could reasonably be expected to hold.

"You said the hammer was sent away during an 'incursion.' Might I ask what that actually entailed?"

Thor considered the question with more gravity than the casual phrasing probably warranted, settling into a chair that creaked audibly under weight it clearly hadn't been designed for. "That is somewhat complicated to explain simply, given even our own scholars argue the particulars."

"I have some tolerance for complicated."

"Some years ago, by Midgardian reckoning, several of my allies undertook an effort involving considerable interference with the flow of time itself. Retrieving certain objects from various points in history, in service of preventing a catastrophe that had already occurred." Thor said this the way a man discusses weather he's long since stopped finding remarkable. "The undertaking succeeded, broadly. It also, by every account I've heard since, fractured something considerably larger than any of us fully understood at the time."

"Fractured how?" Willow asked, leaning in despite herself, notebook already appearing in her hands with the reflexive speed of a habit learned the hard way, across many previous impossible conversations.

"Reality does not enjoy being altered at a single point without consequence. Removing an object, restoring it, moving between moments — each such action apparently sends disturbance outward from the point of change, not merely forward through time but backward as well, weakening whatever separates one reality from its neighbors, at every point along the way simultaneously."

"That doesn't sound like it should be possible," Xander said. "Cause is supposed to come before effect."

"I did not say it was sensible. I said it happened." Thor shrugged, an oddly human gesture on someone his size. "Time, once sufficiently disturbed, appears significantly less linear than any of us would prefer. Our own philosophers have produced entire libraries arguing the mechanics without reaching consensus. I confess I stopped attempting to fully understand it myself some while ago."

"That's either deeply reassuring or the opposite," Buffy said, finally finding her voice. "I actually can't tell which."

"Both, typically," Thor said. "I've found the two rarely travel far apart, in matters this large."

Giles set his glasses back on, very still, rapidly reorganizing several long-held assumptions at once. "You're suggesting this event — whenever it actually occurred — could be responsible for instability reaching far further back than its own point of origin."

"That is my understanding, yes. Imperfect as it is."

"That would explain a great deal," Giles said, quietly, almost to himself, already reaching for a nearby book before catching himself and setting it back down, apparently deciding this conversation deserved his full attention before research could resume.

"Such as?" Thor asked.

Giles glanced at Xander, a silent question passing between them before he answered. "Such as why a boy in this town has been able to reach other realities for the better part of four years, when nothing in any text I've studied suggested such a thing should be possible at all, let alone this consistently."

Thor considered that with genuine interest. "You believe the same fracture responsible for my hammer's journey might also explain his."

"I believe it's the first explanation I've encountered that doesn't require ignoring several inconvenient facts to make sense of the rest," Giles said. "Which, in my experience, tends to mean it's worth taking seriously, however strange the source."

"I thought we already had an answer, though," Willow said slowly, working through it out loud, pen tapping against her notebook. "The Slayer-ritual thing. The scattering. That was supposed to be why Xander could do this in the first place."

Giles was quiet a moment, something uncomfortable crossing his face. "I called it the actual origin, at the time. I remember saying so, with rather more confidence than the evidence probably warranted."

"You weren't wrong, though," Xander said. "Just — not finished being right yet."

"I don't think either explanation replaces the other," Giles said. "The ritual, whatever exactly transpired during it, may well be the mechanism — the reason it manifested in Xander, and presumably in others, as this certain ability, tied to witnessing something ancient and powerful enough to leave a lasting mark." He removed his glasses again, turning them over thoughtfully. "But a mechanism still requires a reality capable of allowing it to function as more than a curious anomaly. If Thor is correct, the fracture may be what made that scattering, ages ago, actually take hold as something real and lasting, rather than fading into nothing the way such things typically would have."

"So the ritual's the how," Xander said slowly, "and the fracture's the why any of it was even possible."

"Approximately, yes. Layered rather than contradictory, if I'm reading this correctly." Giles glanced at Thor. "Assuming your account of the mechanics holds any water at all, which I confess I have no way yet to properly verify."

"Few do," Thor said. "I've long since stopped expecting a tidy answer to satisfy everyone at once. Reality, it seems, prefers its explanations layered rather than simple."

Buffy shifted in her seat. "So what happens now? Now that we've got at least part of an actual explanation?"

"Now," Giles said, "we determine what, if anything, can be done about instability of this scale — assuming anything can be done at all, which I confess remains an open question."

"Strange sources are, in my experience, often the only sources willing to admit how little anyone actually understands," Giles murmured, and something in the easy way Thor had said it made the whole impossible conversation feel, for a moment, almost completely reasonable, four people sitting in a small magic shop quietly reckoning with a truth much larger than any of them had expected to face when the day started.

***



Chapter 102: Chapter 102



Thor took the news that his hammer was, at present, propped against a teenage girl's closet door with rather more composure than Xander expected, all things considered.

"You are certain."

"Very certain. I've seen it. It showed up in Buffy's backyard a while back, nobody knows how, and it's been at the house ever since — she and Faith kept it. Neither of them's got any idea what it actually is, not really, not the whole story." Xander glanced at Giles, who'd been quietly cataloguing his own reaction throughout the whole conversation, giving away exactly as much as he'd decided to and not one detail more. "We can take you there. Tonight, if you want."

"I would like that very much." Thor's hands flexed once, an old, habitual motion, the muscle memory of a lifetime spent carrying something now sitting empty in his grip. "Four years is a considerable time to be parted from something so central to who I am."

"Try four years of doing something without any instructions at all," Xander said. "I've got some sympathy for the wait."

Giles cleared his throat, glancing between the two of them, visibly hesitant about complicating an already complicated conversation. "Before we proceed — I feel I should mention, in the interest of full disclosure, that the hammer has already been tested, in a sense. Twice, in fact."

Thor's attention sharpened immediately, whatever ease had settled into his posture vanishing at once. "Tested how?"

"Two of the young women in our circle attempted to lift it. One succeeded without apparent effort. The other found it entirely immovable."

"That is..." Thor considered this information with visible weight, sitting back in his chair as though the news itself carried physical presence. "That is significant. More significant, perhaps, than you realize."

"I gathered as much. I confess I don't fully understand the mechanism."

"Few do, myself included, and I've lived considerably longer with the question than you have." Thor's expression turned thoughtful, distant, eyes fixed on some middle point that suggested he was seeing something notably further away than the Magic Box's cluttered shelves. "Might I meet them? Both of them. I would like to understand what the hammer has already decided, before I simply take it back and disrupt whatever judgment it's already made."

Xander exchanged a look with Giles, the same silent question passing between them that had been passing back and forth all afternoon. "Yeah. I think that's doable. Faith and Buffy are actually both around tonight."

"Faith and Buffy," Thor repeated, testing the names like he was committing them to some permanent internal record. "I look forward to it."

They found Buffy first, at the house, halfway through explaining the entire situation to Joyce — patient about it, like it was the two-hundredth impossible thing her mother had learned to accept without blinking.

"So there's a Norse god in the living room," Joyce said, mostly to herself, setting down a stack of mail without so much as glancing up twice. "That's — yes. Okay. That tracks with everything else, honestly."

"I appreciate your composure," Thor said, with genuine warmth, ducking slightly under the doorframe on his way into the kitchen. "Most mortals require rather more convincing."

"I stopped requiring convincing somewhere around the third apocalypse," Joyce said, already reaching for the good glasses, the ones reserved for guests she'd decided deserved a little extra ceremony. "Can I get you anything? Coffee? Something stronger?"

"I would not say no to something stronger."

Buffy, for her part, studied Thor with a frank, appraising look — someone who'd fought significantly stranger things and come out the other side mostly unbothered, arms crossed, weight shifted onto one hip in a posture Xander recognized immediately as her fighting-stance-adjacent default. "So you're here for the hammer. The one I picked up without thinking about it."

"I am told you lifted it without effort."

"I mean, I guess? It just felt normal. Lighter than it looked, honestly."

Thor's expression did something complicated — pleased and searching at once, studying her the way a scholar studies a particularly interesting specimen. "That is not a small thing, what you're describing. I would like, at some point, to understand you better. Not tonight, necessarily. But eventually."

"That's either really flattering or really ominous," Buffy said. "I can't decide which."

"Both, likely," Thor said, totally serious. "Worthiness rarely announces itself as one or the other cleanly."

Buffy considered that, something in her expression settling into cautious curiosity rather than alarm. "Okay. I can work with both."

Across the room, quiet in a way that didn't utterly escape Xander's notice, Faith said nothing at all, leaning against the kitchen counter with her arms folded, watching the whole exchange with a stillness that felt far heavier than her usual easy presence.

Thor turned to her eventually, the room's attention shifting with him. "You would be Faith."

"That's me."

"The one who could not lift it."

Faith held his gaze, chin tilted slightly, refusing whatever flinch the old wound might have once produced. "Yeah. That's me too."

"I would like to speak with you properly, when there's time for it. Not tonight, given everything else demanding attention. But soon."

"Sure," Faith said, giving nothing away, though something in her shoulders eased fractionally at the offer — an acknowledgment, however small, that whatever conversation waited might actually matter to her in ways she wasn't quite ready to name out loud yet.

***



Chapter 103: Chapter 103



Xander wanted badly enough to just stay, given everything currently happening with Thor, that he caught himself hunting for a loophole again — a third close call in four years that neither of them was prepared to start treating as ordinary. Willow talked him down anyway, patient and firm, the same as she had both times before.

"It doesn't wait for gods to show up either," she reminded him. "You go when you go."

So he went, Buffy and Willow both along this time, and for one disorienting second the pull didn't feel like the ordinary lottery it always had — no scattering uncertainty beforehand, no held breath waiting to find out which world he'd open his eyes into. Just a single, sure certainty settling into his chest a half-beat before his boots actually hit ground, the same unmistakable rightness he remembered from every other time he'd landed at the fence line.

"Okay," Willow said, blinking, taking in the familiar field, the distant roofline, the fence itself stretching off toward the horizon exactly where it always was. "That's — that's not random, right? Landing somewhere you actually recognize has never happened before."

"Never once. Four years of nothing but random, and then—" He looked around, something settling and unsettling both moving through him at once. "This."

Buffy caught his hand, steadying more than asking. "You okay?"

"Yeah. Yeah, I think I actually am." He thought of the margin note Giles had mentioned once, almost in passing — jumpers finding one another, drawn by some sympathy nobody fully understood. "I think this might be that thing Giles found in the archive. The 'drawn together' thing. Didn't think it'd ever actually happen to me specifically. Didn't think it'd happen at all, honestly, given how thin he said the source was."

"You think the pull did this on purpose?" Willow asked, already reaching for her notebook despite the shock still visible on her face, old habit winning out even now. "Chose this, instead of just landing wherever?"

"I don't know if 'purpose' is even the right word for whatever this actually is. But it's the first time in four years it hasn't felt random, and that's gotta mean something."

Cent found them before they'd made it halfway to the house, Millie a few steps behind her, both of them already smiling at the sight of three people instead of the usual one.

"You brought company," Cent said, delighted, taking in Buffy and Willow both with open, curious warmth. "Finally. I was starting to think I invented you two."

"They're real," Xander confirmed. "Very real. Also apparently the reason the jump did something it's never done before, if my theory's right."

Millie's eyebrows went up at that, sharp interest replacing the easy welcome. "It's never landed you here on purpose before? Not once, in however many times you've made it back?"

"Not once. Every time before this was luck. This time it just — knew. Like something decided the odds didn't need to apply today."

"Huh." Millie considered that a long moment, something thoughtful moving behind her eyes. "That's worth writing down properly, if you're willing. We've got our own old stories about the door choosing, here and there, though nobody in this family's ever had solid proof of it happening in front of witnesses before."

"Willow's already halfway to a full page of notes," Buffy said, fond. "Give her ten more minutes and she'll have a working theory."

"Give me five," Willow said, without looking up.

They stayed the full three days properly this time, Cent walking Buffy through the parts of the farm Xander usually skipped past in favor of the fence line and the fields beyond it, Willow and Millie disappearing for hours into exactly the kind of technical conversation both of them had apparently been waiting years to have with someone who actually understood the questions.

"Okay, this is stupidly beautiful," Buffy said, on the second evening, all three of them sitting in the flattened grass beyond the fence watching the sky go orange, her head resting against Xander's shoulder while Willow leaned into his other side, the three of them tangled together loosely the way they usually ended up by the end of a long day. "After the last few weeks, I'll take stupidly beautiful."

"Can we talk about the whole Thor thing for a second," Buffy said, "Because I keep waiting for it to feel less insane and it's just not happening."

"Same," Willow admitted. "A literal god. In Sunnydale. Looking for a hammer that apparently decides who gets to swing it based on some cosmic worthiness test."

"And he wants to understand you better," Xander added, looking at Buffy. "Which, side note, still a weird sentence to have said out loud about my girlfriend."

"I know. I don't really know what to do with it either." Buffy picked at the grass, thoughtful. "Faith's been weird about the whole thing too. Quieter than usual."

"You noticed that too?"

"Hard not to. She usually can't resist a good comment. She's been sitting there just... not saying anything."

Willow looked between them, working through something. "You think it's about the hammer specifically? The whole not-being-able-to-lift-it thing?"

"Maybe. I don't want to push, though. Feels like the kind of thing she'll bring up when she's ready, not before."

"That's probably right," Xander said. "Faith's earned the right to process things on her own timeline. We've all seen where rushing her gets people."

They let the conversation settle into quieter things after that — the wildflowers, the strange, gentle wind, the easy comfort of three people who'd been through considerably more together than any of them had expected when this whole thing started.

By the time the jump pulled them home, they had a full catalogue of an fully new flower species, a memory card of really stunning photos, and a real calm that came from a trip that had, for once, been exactly as peaceful as it looked.

Joyce checked all three of them over out of habit, more out of relief than worry these days. "How's Cent's family?"

"Good. Really good," Buffy said. "No drama. Just flowers and a lot of talking."

"I'll take flowers and talking over penguins and bees any day," Joyce said with a grin, already turning back toward the kitchen, Thor's continuing presence in the living room sitting alongside everything else as just one more strange, manageable piece of an evening that didn't need to make a bigger deal of itself than that.

***



Chapter 104: Chapter 104



Faith found Thor on the porch steps, alone for once, the rest of the group having drifted inside for food and normalcy after a long evening of impossible conversation. The night had cooled much since sundown, crickets working steadily somewhere in Joyce's hedges, the kind of ordinary Sunnydale evening that felt, after everything discussed inside, almost defiantly unremarkable.

"Didn't peg you for a porch-sitter," she said, mostly to have something to say, settling onto the step below him.

"I find porches remarkably restful. Asgard has balconies. Not quite the same thing." Thor studied her with the same open, unhurried attention he'd given everyone else that night. "You are Faith."

"Guilty."

"You are also the one who could not lift the hammer."

She'd expected that to sting more than it did, hearing it said so plainly, without any cruelty behind the words at all — just fact, delivered the same way he'd probably deliver the weather. "Yeah. That's me. Big fail, right in front of Buffy."

"I would not call it a failure."

"Kinda hard to call it anything else, given the hammer didn't budge an inch and Buffy lifted it like it was nothing."

Thor considered this, unhurried, the way he seemed to consider most things, turning a small stone over in one hand he'd apparently picked up off the porch steps without her noticing. "The hammer's judgment is not always simple. It does not measure strength, or goodness in any tidy sense. It measures readiness — a kind of readiness that shifts and changes across a person's life, sometimes across a single year, sometimes across a single day."

"So I'm just not ready yet."

"Perhaps. Or perhaps the hammer sensed something in you it did not yet have an answer for. Those are different things, Faith, and I would encourage you not to conflate them." He said it simply, without any performance of comfort behind it. "I was not always worthy myself. There was a time the hammer judged me completely unready, and left me without it for what felt, at the time, like an unbearable length of time."

Faith huffed something close to a laugh, mostly at herself. "So I'm cookie dough. Not done baking yet." Buffy's line, actually, stolen fair and square, but it fit too well right now not to use it.

"I do not fully grasp the reference," Thor said, with the particular gravity of a man giving a strange metaphor his complete, genuine consideration anyway, "but I suspect it is accurate."

That got her attention properly, enough that she actually turned to look at him. "You? The actual guy who's supposed to be worthy by default?"

"Nobody is worthy by default. That is rather the entire point of the mechanism." Thor tossed the small stone gently, catching it again without looking. "I was arrogant. Cruel, in ways I am not proud to recall. The hammer knew this before I did, and took itself away until I'd become someone worth trusting with it again."

"What'd you actually do? If you don't mind me asking."

"I started a war I had no business starting, out of pride and boredom rather than any genuine cause." Thor's voice carried no self-pity, just plain, settled fact, the kind of admission that had clearly been worn smooth by many previous tellings. "Cost lives that should never have been risked over something so small as my own wounded ego. I did not become a better person quickly. It took genuine loss, and genuine time, before I understood what had actually been asked of me."

"Damn." Faith went quiet a beat, chewing on it. "So it's not permanent. The no."

"I have never known it to be permanent, no. Only ever a pause, waiting for something to catch up to itself."

"That's either really comforting or really annoying, depending on how patient I'm feeling."

"Most true things are," Thor said, something almost like a smile at the corner of his mouth. "I did not say it to comfort you specifically. I said it because it is true, and because I suspect you would see through comfort offered simply to make you feel better."

"Yeah. I would've." Faith allowed herself something closer to an actual smile. "Appreciate you not doing that, then. Most people can't help themselves."

"I have had considerable practice not doing what most people can't help themselves doing. It is, I have found, one of the more useful skills a very long life eventually teaches you."

"That's either wisdom or you're just old. Hard to tell which with you."

"I contain both in generous supply," Thor said, entirely straight-faced, and Faith actually laughed, short and surprised, the sound cutting cleanly through the evening quiet.

"Okay, that one's actually funny."

"I have my moments."

"That's my brand of comeback, not yours."

"Perhaps it belongs to whoever needs it in the moment," Thor said, echoing something he'd apparently say again, months later, in circumstances neither of them could have predicted from this quiet porch. "I find good lines rarely stay owned by just one person for long."

Faith shook her head, still smiling, and let the moment settle back into something quieter. "I will say this much, Faith — whatever the hammer is waiting for, I do not believe it will wait forever," Thor continued, tone shifting back toward the gravity that had started the conversation. "Nothing worth becoming ever announces exactly when it arrives. It simply arrives, one day, and you look back and realize you've already become it."

She didn't have a ready comeback for that, which had become less rare around people who actually meant what they said to her, and the two of them sat together in the quiet a while longer, the porch settling into comfortable silence, an old, patient god and a girl still catching up to whatever she was becoming, both content, for the moment, to let the quiet hold rather than rush toward an answer neither of them could force into arriving any sooner than it meant to.

"Thanks," Faith said, eventually, quiet. "For not treating it like a big deal. Or, I guess, for treating it like a big deal that's also not a crisis. There's a difference."

"There is," Thor agreed. "I am glad you noticed I was attempting the distinction."

"Hard not to notice, with you. You're pretty obvious, for a god."

"I have been told that before, actually. I've decided to take it as a compliment."

"Good instinct," Faith said, and the crickets kept singing, and neither of them moved to go back inside for a good while longer.

She tried the hammer again that night, same as always, alone in the dark once the house had gone quiet — except this time she found herself almost hoping less for the thing to move than for the trying itself to mean something, the way Thor had made it sound like it might. It still didn't move. She let go slow this time instead of dropping her hand fast the way she usually did, and stayed crouched there a moment longer than she needed to, one palm still resting flat against the head of it, something in her chest loosening instead of sinking for once. She still went to bed after, steadier about it than she'd expected to feel.

***



Chapter 105: Chapter 105



Thor's arrival had, predictably, become the only topic of conversation at the lunch table for the better part of a week, and Cordelia had opinions about all of it, delivered with her usual complete confidence.

"Okay, but is he single," she said, apropos of nothing, sliding into her seat. "Because if there's an actual god walking around this town, I feel like that's relevant information the group chat has been criminally slow to provide."

"He's not really available for that kind of question," Xander said. "Also, unrelated, please don't try to date the Norse god currently staying at my house."

"I wasn't going to. I was just establishing baseline facts."

"Sure you were."

Jesse, who'd been uncharacteristically quiet through most of this, finally spoke up. "For the record, I have several extremely reasonable questions about hammer mechanics that nobody's letting me ask. Like, does size correlate to anything. Purely scientific curiosity."

"Nobody's stopping you from asking, Jesse, we're just collectively choosing not to encourage it."

"That's basically the same thing."

"It's really not, and also, ew."

"I said nothing incriminating!"

"You implied plenty, and your face is doing a thing."

Cordelia studied Jesse for a moment, something calculating crossing her face. "You've been weirdly normal about the god thing. I expected way more chaos out of you specifically, honestly, given your baseline."

"I'm processing internally. It's very mature of me."

"That's new."

"I contain depths, Cordelia. Untapped depths."

"You contain a used napkin collection under your bed, is what I've heard."

"That's — okay, that's a specific and unfair thing to know about me."

"I have sources."

"Your sources are wrong. It's a very small collection." Jesse caught himself, visibly regretting the sentence the second it left his mouth, and the table dissolved into laughter loud enough that Faith, arriving late, had to ask what she'd missed.

"Napkin collection," Willow reported, still laughing. "Jesse just accidentally confirmed a napkin collection."

"That's the best thing I've heard all week," Faith said, sliding in beside them, something lighter in her posture than she'd carried the past several days. "Better than the god thing, honestly."

"Everything's better than the god thing at this point," Xander said. "We've had a full week of nothing but Thor discourse. I'd welcome literally any other topic."

"Napkins it is, then," Cordelia said. "Jesse, elaborate. For the group's benefit."

"Absolutely not."

"Come on. You opened the door."

"I did not open the door, the door opened itself against my will."

"That's not how doors work, Jesse."

"It's exactly how this door worked!"

The table dissolved further into laughter, Thor's continuing presence in town receding, for one blessedly ordinary lunch period, behind the far more pressing matter of Jesse's napkin collection and his increasingly desperate efforts to reclaim some shred of dignity from the wreckage of his own sentence.

***



Chapter 106: Chapter 106



Buffy and Willow were both roped into helping Giles catalogue Thor's account of Asgardian history for the Watcher archive, which left the next jump to Xander alone, and he found himself almost grateful for the quiet of it after a week thick with gods and revelations.

The world this time was rocky coastline, waves crashing hard against black stone cliffs, spray carrying salt far enough inland to taste it on the wind from his very first breath.

"Okay," he said, taking it in. "This one's got attitude. Respect."

He spent the first day just working his way along the cliff base, cataloguing tide pools tucked into crevices too narrow for anything larger than his hand, strange anemones pulsing in colors that shifted depending on the angle of the spray hitting them.

'Quiet trip,' he thought, more than once, working through his notes with an ease that had been in short supply lately. 'No gods. No worthiness tests. Just cliffs and water and whatever's hiding in these pools.' It felt good, settling back into the familiar rhythm of a job that had, for four years now, mostly just been his — a private, ordinary strangeness that belonged to him in a way none of the recent chaos quite did.

The birds showed up on the second day, nesting in impossible numbers along ledges that looked totally unclimbable, diving for fish in the churning water below with a precision that made his own attempts at photography feel clumsy by comparison.

"You're all very good at your jobs," he told them, getting his shots from a careful distance. "Wish I had that kind of confidence about literally anything right now."

He watched the birds work a while, turning over everything the past week had actually meant — Faith's quiet processing, Thor's genuine kindness underneath all the formality, the strange, steadying comfort of watching his own found family absorb one more impossible thing without breaking stride.

'Guess that's the job too,' he thought, eventually. 'Not just the animals. Figuring out how to hold all the weird stuff at once and still function.' It felt like a truer description of the last four years than any single trip report had ever quite captured.

He spent the last day simply resting, letting the ordinary rhythm of the coastline wash over him, and let the pull take him home with a full memory card, salt-crusted clothes, and a quiet satisfaction that came easy after a trip that had asked nothing more of him than to notice things properly.

Willow leaned in the kitchen doorway, arms crossed, giving him the same once-over she always did. "Survive it?"

"More than survived. Quiet. No drama. Exactly what I needed after this week."

"I don't blame you. It's been a lot."

"It's been a whole lot." He set his gear down, feeling more settled than he had in days. "How's the archive project going?"

"Giles is thrilled. I think he's been waiting his whole career for source material this reliable." Willow grinned. "Also Thor tells truly great stories. Did you know he once lost a drinking contest to a dwarf and had to wear the losing hat for a month?"

"I did not know that, and I need every detail immediately."

"Get comfortable," Willow said. "It's a whole saga."

***

107. Cent: "Wait, a WHAT showed up?"

Xander showed up instead of leaving a note, which told me before he even said a word that whatever happened this week wasn't going to fit under a rock.

"Okay, so, a god showed up in my town."

I sat down right there in the grass, still catching up to the fact that he was actually standing in front of me on a day nobody could have planned for. "I'm sorry, a what?"

"A god. Thor. Like, actual mythology Thor, looking for his actual hammer, which turned out to be sitting in Buffy's bedroom this whole time."

"You're messing with me."

"I promise I am not messing with you. I have never been less capable of making something like this up."

I processed that for a solid ten seconds. "Okay. Explain. All of it. Don't skip anything."

He didn't skip anything. Took almost forty minutes. The hammer. The worthy-hands thing. Faith's reaction. Thor's whole search across a dozen realities before landing in his backyard. Somewhere in the middle of it I caught myself just grinning at him like an idiot. Of course this was his life. Of course a literal god showing up was just Tuesday for him now.

"So there's a whole other layer to this thing we've got," I said, once he finished. "Not just us. Whatever's letting people cross between realities, it's apparently connected to something way bigger. Gods. Time travel. The whole works."

"Yeah. Giles thinks it goes back further than either of us even realized. Like, actually explains why the ability exists at all, not just why it shows up randomly."

"That's a lot to process."

"Tell me about it. I've had a week."

I told Dad about it that night, the short version, and watched his whole face do the thing it does when a new piece of the puzzle actually fits somewhere unexpected.

"A time fracture," he said slowly. "That's — actually, that tracks with some things I've wondered about for years. Never had a good reason before."

"You think it connects to us? The whole scattered-across-generations thing?"

"Maybe. Possibly." He considered it, actually considered it, the way he does when something matters enough to think through properly instead of just reacting. "I'd like to talk to this Giles person eventually. Compare notes."

"I'll ask Xander. I don't think that's a weird request, all things considered."

"Nothing about any of this is weird anymore, by comparison." Dad managed a tired, genuine smile. "Guess we've got even more company in this than we thought."

"Guess we do," I said. Turned the whole impossible week over one more time. Didn't mind the company growing bigger than expected. Not even a little.

***



Chapter 108: Chapter 108



Thor had, over the course of two weeks, eased into Sunnydale life with a speed that continued to surprise everyone except, apparently, Thor himself, who seemed to regard the whole adjustment as a matter of simple logic rather than anything requiring specific effort.

"I do not understand the appeal of this 'television' fully," he announced, from Joyce's living room couch, remote in hand like a foreign artifact he was determined to master through sheer persistence, having already accidentally changed the channel four times in the last ten minutes without seeming to notice, "but I confess I find the concept of professional wrestling deeply resonant."

"Of course you do," Xander said, dropping onto the other end of the couch, a bowl of chips balanced on his knee. "Of course that's the one that clicked for you."

"The theatricality. The clear heroes and villains. The dramatic entrances." Thor gestured at the screen with genuine enthusiasm, leaning forward as a wrestler in gold spandex made his way toward the ring amid pyrotechnics and a thumping bass line. "It reminds me somewhat of home, if home were notably more concerned with pyrotechnics."

"I'm not gonna argue with that assessment."

"Also, I find the commentary honestly informative. This man appears to be explaining the narrative stakes to the audience in real time, which I appreciate. Asgardian storytelling could learn something from the practice."

"Please don't tell Giles wrestling counts as narrative craft. He'll have opinions."

"I welcome opinions. I find them, generally, more interesting than agreement."

Joyce wandered through with a laundry basket, pausing at the sight of a Norse god fully absorbed in Saturday afternoon programming, one boot propped on her coffee table in a way she'd apparently decided, weeks ago, wasn't worth the fight. "Are you doing all right, Thor? Settling in okay?"

"Remarkably well, thank you, Joyce. Your hospitality has been considerable." He said it with genuine warmth, the same easy sincerity he brought to most things, pausing the wrestling utterly to give her his full attention, which she waved off with a small, pleased laugh. "Though I confess I am unaccustomed to towns of this size. Everything here happens so close together."

"That's Sunnydale for you. Small and cozy, minus the occasional apocalypse."

"I have grown rather fond of the occasional apocalypse, actually. Gives the week a certain shape."

"That's a very concerning thing to say with that much cheerfulness."

"I contain multitudes," Thor said, entirely straight-faced, and Xander nearly choked on his own drink.

"Okay, where did you even hear that phrase."

"You use it constantly. I assumed it was some manner of local greeting."

"It's not a greeting, it's — okay, it's a whole thing, but not a greeting."

"My apologies. I shall use it more selectively going forward." Thor didn't look remotely apologetic, settling further into the couch cushions with the contentment of a man who'd found, against all expectation, something resembling peace in the least likely place imaginable.

Joyce set the laundry basket down long enough to steal one of Xander's chips, fully unbothered by the whole exchange. "You're welcome to say it as often as you like, Thor. Keeps things interesting around here."

"Coming from someone who's clearly seen a great deal of interesting already, I'll take that as a compliment."

"You have no idea," Joyce said, and disappeared toward the laundry room with the basket, leaving the two of them to the wrestling.

Buffy arrived partway through, dropping her patrol bag by the door with the ease of someone who'd stopped questioning the sight of a god watching wrestling on her mother's couch days ago, stakes still visible poking out from the bag's open top. "How's the search going? Any leads on the whole 'why is this happening here specifically' question?"

"Giles and I have made some progress," Thor said, muting the television without being asked, a habit Xander had noticed him picking up in just the past few days, small courtesies absorbed the same way he seemed to absorb everything else. "Slow progress, admittedly. The mechanics of the fracture remain significantly harder to trace than the fracture's existence."

"Story of my life lately," Buffy said, dropping onto the arm of the couch, kicking her boots off with a small, tired sigh. "Knowing something's real and actually understanding it are apparently two completely different jobs."

"An astute observation. I've made peace with the gap myself, over considerably more centuries than I'd like to admit required for the lesson to stick."

"Well, when you put it that way, I feel better about only needing a few months."

"You should. Patience with the unknown is, in my experience, one of the harder skills a person can develop, and you appear to be developing it rather ahead of schedule."

Buffy smiled at that, something genuine and pleased in it, settling more comfortably into the arm of the couch, close enough now that all three of them fit into the same warm patch of afternoon light coming through the window.

"You gonna finish watching this with us, or is patrol calling?" Xander asked.

"Patrol can wait twenty minutes. I want to see if gold-spandex-guy wins."

"He's not gonna win. Look at his face. That's a losing face."

"That is absolutely a winning face, you don't know wrestling faces."

"I know exactly enough wrestling faces to know that's a losing face."

Thor watched the two of them argue with visible delight, unmuting the television again to settle the debate properly, and the three of them settled into the easy, ordinary comfort of a Saturday afternoon, wrestling blaring in the background, a god relaxing on the couch like he'd always belonged there, Sunnydale continuing its long, quiet habit of absorbing the impossible into something that felt, improbably, like home.

***



Chapter 109: Chapter 109



Faith asked to come along on the next jump, which still caught Xander a little off guard, though not completely — she'd been circling the idea out loud for a while now, ever since that one trip a while back and the "maybe next time" she'd left hanging after the last one.

"Yeah? You sure? Been a good stretch since you tried it that one time."

"Yeah, it has been. Just — think I need a change of scenery for a few days. Something that isn't Sunnydale, isn't patrol, isn't any of the stuff currently living in my head." Faith shrugged, a little more vulnerable about the admission than her usual easy confidence let on. "Figured a few days somewhere with zero cosmic significance sounded pretty good right about now."

"I can absolutely offer zero cosmic significance. That's basically my brand."

"Perfect. Let's do that, then."

The world they landed in turned out to be exactly the kind of unremarkable Xander had been hoping for on her behalf — rolling hills, scattered pine forest, a river cutting through the middle of it all clean and cold and entirely uneventful.

"This is nice," Faith said, turning slowly to take it in. "Genuinely nice. No immediate threats, no ancient mysteries, just trees and a river."

"I do occasionally get one of these. Not as often as I'd like, but they happen."

They spent the first day just walking the river's edge, Faith more relaxed than Xander had seen her in weeks, throwing rocks into the current with the loose energy of someone who'd finally set something heavy down for a while.

"Can I ask you something," she said, on the second evening, both of them sitting by a small fire as the light faded. "About the whole god thing. Thor."

"Sure."

"You believe him? About the whole worthiness-isn't-permanent thing he told me?"

Xander considered the question seriously. "Yeah. I think I do, actually. Guy's had way more time to figure out what worthy even means than any of us. Feels like the kind of thing he'd know."

"Yeah. That's what I keep landing on too." Faith poked at the fire, thoughtful. "Still weird, though. Having an actual god tell you you're not done becoming whatever you're supposed to become yet."

"Sounds like a good weird, though. Not a bad one."

"Mostly good. Little bit annoying, not gonna lie. I like knowing things now, not later."

"Yeah, that tracks. You've always been more of a right-now person."

"Guilty as charged." She managed a real smile, firelight catching it warm. "Appreciate you letting me tag along on this one, though. Didn't realize how much I needed a trip with actual zero stakes."

"Anytime. Seriously. You ever need an excuse to disappear from Sunnydale for a few days, I'm apparently available for that now."

"Might take you up on that more than once."

"I'd be into that, honestly. Good to have company that isn't running from bees or ants for once."

"Low bar, but I'll take being better than bees."

They spent the last day simply enjoying the quiet, fishing badly, talking about nothing important, and by the time the jump pulled them home, Faith looked lighter than she had in weeks, the ordinary strangeness of an uneventful world having done exactly the work she'd needed it to do.

Buffy greeted Xander with a quick kiss at the door, then turned her attention properly to Faith, something searching in the look. "You look different. Good different."

"Turns out sometimes the best cure for cosmic significance is a whole weekend of absolutely none," Faith said.

"I'll keep that prescription in mind," Buffy said, and bumped her shoulder against Faith's on the way past, easy, the kind of touch that didn't need to be anything more than exactly what it was.

***



Chapter 110: Chapter 110



Joyce's list hit its own quiet milestone that Sunday, though nobody realized it until she read the entry out loud with a small, satisfied smile: for the first time since I started keeping this list, an entire week passed where nothing on it required immediate follow-up. I'm choosing to consider this progress.

"That's genuinely impressive," Xander said. "A whole week. I don't think we've had one of those since before the whole Thor thing started."

"I know. I almost didn't trust it enough to write it down." Joyce passed the potatoes down the table. "Felt like tempting fate, honestly."

"Don't say that word around this table," Faith said. "We've got a whole tradition of things going sideways the second somebody jinxes it."

"I'm aware. I decided the risk was worth it, for once."

Thor, seated at his now-customary spot at the table, looked actually delighted by the whole exchange. "This ritual of shared meals continues to be one of my favorite discoveries here. Asgard has feasts, certainly, but rarely this... comfortable."

"Comfortable's kind of our whole thing," Buffy said, resting her hand on Xander's knee under the table without really thinking about it. "Low stakes, home cooking, everybody yelling about sandwiches occasionally."

"The sandwich debate," Thor said, with genuine reverence. "I have been informed of its significance. I confess I find myself unable to resolve it either, having considered the matter at some length."

"See, this is why we like him," Jesse said. "Actual god, still can't crack the sandwich question. Validates the whole debate."

"It is a really difficult philosophical question," Thor said, totally serious. "I do not think less of any of you for failing to resolve it."

"High praise," Willow said, grinning.

The table fell into its usual comfortable noise after that — Faith stealing rolls on principle, Jesse angling for another embarrassing story now that the napkin incident had faded into legend, Cordelia interrogating Thor about Asgardian fashion with genuine scholarly interest, Joyce refilling glasses, long since unsurprised by the exact shape her table's chaos took each week.

"I think that's everything," Buffy said, eventually, the way she always did.

"For now," Joyce echoed, fond as ever, reaching for dessert.

Later, on the porch, Xander found himself sitting beside Faith, both of them looking out at a street that felt, for once, utterly peaceful.

"Good week," Faith said.

"Good week," he agreed. "Feels weird to say that out loud, given everything currently going on. Feels like it should sound more complicated than it does."

"Maybe complicated things are allowed to feel good sometimes. Not every big thing's gotta be a struggle the whole way through."

"That's annoyingly wise. You've been spending too much time around Thor."

"Guilty. He's got some truly decent takes, once you get past all the formal Asgardian phrasing."

They sat together a while longer, the evening settling into comfortable quiet, one more ordinary night added to a life that had, against every reasonable expectation, found a way to hold gods and mysteries and penguins and heartbreak and hope all at once, and somehow, most weeks, still come out the other side feeling like exactly what it was: home.

***



Chapter 111: Chapter 111



Buffy and Willow both had exams that couldn't move, which left the next jump to Xander alone, and he found the world this time thick with fog, visibility dropping to a few feet in every direction the second he materialized.

"Okay," he said, squinting into the gray. "That's a new one. Never had a fog this thick before."

He made camp close to his landing point rather than risk exploring blind, and spent the first day mostly listening — strange calls echoing through the mist, something large moving at a distance he couldn't judge, the whole world reduced to sound and guesswork.

'Willow's gonna want to know what made those calls,' he thought, cataloguing them as best he could by ear alone. 'I'm gonna have approximately zero visual confirmation, and she's gonna be so mad about it.' It felt like an oddly restful kind of frustration, the fog forcing a patience on him that four years of jumping rarely allowed.

The fog broke on the second afternoon, all at once, revealing a coastline he hadn't expected — cliffs dropping to churning water, and along those cliffs, nesting in numbers that rivaled anything he'd seen before, birds with wingspans wider than he was tall.

"Okay, that's a lot of bird," he said, already reaching for his camera. "That's an unreasonable amount of wingspan happening on that cliff."

He spent the rest of the trip documenting them, patient and careful, the fog rolling back in periodically to remind him how close he'd come to missing the whole thing entirely, and by the time the jump pulled him home, he had photos of birds Willow would spend a week trying to properly classify, and a renewed appreciation for patience as its own kind of field skill.

"Big ones this time," he told her, handing over the camera, pausing to kiss her hello properly before either of them got distracted by the photos. "Wingspan you would not believe."

"Oh, I'm going to believe it," Willow said, already scrolling through, delighted. "I'm going to believe it so hard."

"Good. That's the spirit."

***

112. Cent: "Dad wants to meet your Watcher guy."

Dad brought it up at breakfast, which meant he'd been sitting on it a while, turning it over before deciding it was worth actually saying out loud.

"I want to talk to this Giles person. Compare notes on the fracture thing."

"You said that last week. You want me to actually set it up?"

"If Xander's willing to broker it. I don't love the idea of just showing up somewhere new uninvited. Learned that lesson a long time ago."

Mom looked up from her book. "You think it'll actually help? Combining what you both know?"

"Maybe. Giles has apparently got old texts touching on all this. I've got the practical side, decades of living with the ability without any of the old-book context." Dad turned his coffee cup slowly. "Between the two of us, might actually get somewhere neither of us could alone."

I told Xander the next time he came through. He said yes almost before I finished asking, which didn't surprise me, since Giles has been chasing this thing with actual hunger lately.

"He'll be thrilled," Xander said. "I mean that. Actually thrilled. Giles doesn't get real collaborators on this stuff often."

"Neither does Dad. He's spent his whole life being the only source anybody could ask."

"That's rough. Feels like the theme of, like, everyone connected to this whole mess. Everybody's spent years being the only one."

"Yeah." I thought about that a second. "Guess that's what's actually changing, then. Not just answers. Just... not being the only one anymore."

"Yeah. Exactly that."

We set the meeting for the following week, neutral ground again, same diner Willow and Mom had used. I wasn't sure I'd be there for it — felt like Dad's thing to have, the same way Willow and Mom's first meeting had been theirs — but he asked me to come along anyway.

"Why me?" I asked. "This is your world of expertise, not mine."

"Because you're the one who actually found this whole thing," Dad said. "Every piece of it traces back to that boot print you found where it had no business being. Feels wrong, doing this without you in the room."

I didn't have a great answer for that, so I just said okay. Spent the next week almost as curious about what these two very different men might find in common as I'd been about anything else this whole strange year.

***



Chapter 113: Chapter 113



Giles arrived at the diner with three books he probably didn't need, visibly nervous about meeting a colleague he genuinely respected before ever having spoken to him.

Davy arrived far more relaxed, though Cent recognized the careful, assessing look he gave the room before sitting down — old habit, the kind that never fully switched off no matter how safe a place turned out to be.

"Mr. Rice," Giles said, extending a hand. "Rupert Giles. Thank you for agreeing to this."

"Davy's fine. And thank you for reaching out first. Made this a lot less awkward than it could've been." Davy shook his hand, then glanced at the books already stacked on the table. "You brought homework."

"Force of habit. I wasn't certain what would be useful, so I erred toward comprehensive."

"I respect the instinct. I've got much less paper and notably more lived experience to bring to the table, myself."

"That may well prove more valuable than my books, honestly." Giles removed his glasses, cleaning them the way Cent had come to recognize, even secondhand through Xander's descriptions, as his tell for genuine uncertainty. "I confess I've spent months chasing partial theories with no one to actually test them against. This is rather a relief."

"Same, on my end. Decades of partial theories, if we're counting." Davy leaned back. "So. Where do you want to start?"

They started with the basics — Davy's own manifestation, the crisis-trigger pattern, decades of careful, empirical observation about how the ability actually worked in practice. Giles listened closely, finally getting primary source material instead of secondhand fragments, occasionally cross-referencing something against his books, occasionally simply nodding, absorbing.

"The crisis trigger," Giles said, at one point. "You're certain it's universal? Every manifestation you're aware of followed that pattern?"

"Every one I've directly confirmed, yes. Extreme stress, sudden and severe. Never gradual, never chosen." Davy considered something. "Your boy's case fits that pattern too, doesn't it? Xander's first jump."

"It does, as it happens. A moment of genuine danger, if I recall the account correctly."

"That's consistent, then. Whatever else is going on with the ritual scattering, the fracture, all of it — the actual trigger mechanism seems to hold steady across every case I know of."

Giles made a note, something satisfied in the gesture. "That's useful. Genuinely useful. A consistent variable, in a question that's otherwise been maddeningly short of them."

Cent watched the two of them fall into an easy, technical rhythm, trading pieces of a puzzle neither of them had ever had enough pieces to properly see before, and felt something warm settle in her chest at the sight of it — her father, who'd spent so many years being the only expert in a field of one, finally getting to actually collaborate with someone who took the whole impossible subject as seriously as he did.

"This is good," she said quietly, to nobody in particular, and meant it more than the small sentence probably conveyed.

"Very good," Giles agreed, catching her comment, glancing up from his notes with something like genuine gratitude. "I don't believe I've properly thanked you yet, Cent, for making any of this possible in the first place."

"Wasn't just me. Xander found the fence line before I ever found the footprint."

"Fair enough. Between the two of you, then." Giles allowed himself a small, real smile. "I suspect this is only the beginning of what we'll manage to piece together, working at it properly instead of alone."

"Damn right it is," Davy said, and reached for another book, already three questions ahead of where the conversation currently stood.

***



Chapter 114: Chapter 114



Thor's communication device — some ungainly hybrid Tony had cobbled together specifically so Asgardian tech could actually talk to SHIELD's network, a project Thor still didn't fully understand the mechanics of and Tony still complained about at length whenever it came up — had sat largely ignored since his arrival, given how little use he'd had for it, but chimed for the first time in weeks while he was in the middle of explaining the concept of nachos to Willow with genuine, focused enthusiasm, three separate cheese varieties laid out on the kitchen counter like evidence in some elaborate culinary trial.

"The key," he'd been saying, holding up a chip with real reverence, "is achieving even distribution. Too many toppings pooled in one location, and the remainder suffers for it. This is, I have come to understand, a genuine culinary science, and I intend to master it properly."

"You're overthinking nachos."

"I do not believe one can overthink nachos. I believe one can only under-invest in nachos, and I refuse to be that person."

The device chimed again, insistent, and he frowned at it, surprised, chip still held aloft. "That would be Natasha."

"Should you get that?" Willow asked.

"Probably wise, given how she responds to being ignored." Thor set the chip down with visible reluctance and answered it, and Xander, close enough to catch fragments, watched his expression shift from casual to honestly sheepish within about four seconds, the transformation almost comedic in how completely it overtook him.

"...yes, I am aware it has been some while... no, I did not intend to leave without word... it's rather a long story, Natasha... yes, I understand that 'rather a long story' is not an acceptable excuse for eleven days of silence..."

Willow mouthed 'eleven days?' at Xander, who shrugged, equally curious, both of them now watching the conversation with the rapt attention usually reserved for particularly good television.

"I am well. Genuinely well, in fact. I found the hammer." A pause, Thor visibly holding the phone slightly away from his ear. "Yes. Yes, it required some searching. Twelve realms, as it turned out." Another pause, longer this time, Thor's expression shifting again, something more careful entering it. "That is... considerably more complicated to explain over this connection. Might I call properly, once I've had a chance to gather my thoughts?"

Whatever Natasha said in response made Thor wince slightly, actually pulling the phone an inch further from his ear.

"Yes. I understand. I'll expect the full debrief, and I promise not to make you extract it from me one reluctant sentence at a time." He glanced at Xander, covering the receiver briefly, expression somewhere between amused and actually nervous. "She wants to come see for herself. Apparently my track record for 'I'm fine, just a long story' does not inspire confidence."

"That's... probably fair, given the eleven days thing."

"Entirely fair," Thor agreed, uncovering the receiver. "Yes. Yes, that would be —"

A second voice cut in on Natasha's end before he could finish, sharp enough that Xander and Willow both went still. Tony's, unmistakably, but stripped of every ounce of the easy, talk-show energy either of them would have expected from the name alone — flat, cold, precise in the specific way anger gets when it's actually fear wearing a harder face.

"When, Nat." Not a question. "Because if the answer's anything other than right fucking now, I want you to say that to my face, not down a phone line, and explain to me exactly which part of eleven days of nothing made 'sometime this week' sound like the responsible call."

Somewhere behind him, faint but audible, Steve started to say something that began with "Lang—" and simply stopped there, apparently deciding, correctly, that this was not that moment.

Thor's expression went very still, the last of the sheepishness draining out of it entirely.

"Tony—" Natasha, muffled, clearly trying to reclaim the conversation.

"No. I already did the math once on how many of these end without a phone call at the end of it. I'm not doing it again on this one. I'm coming tonight. Ride with me or meet me there, I don't care which, but I am not spending one more night not knowing for certain." A pause, the edge in his voice cracking just slightly at the end of it. "Just — let me see him. Please."

Nobody in the kitchen said anything. Willow's hand had gone still on her notebook.

A long moment of quiet on the line, and then Natasha again, quieter now herself. "...Tonight, then. Steve and Bruce are apparently coming too, so brace yourself."

"Understood," Thor said, and it came out smaller than anything he'd said yet, something old and grateful and faintly ashamed moving behind his eyes. "I'll let the people here know to expect all of you."

He ended the call and set the phone down with unusual care, not looking at anyone for a moment.

"So," he said, eventually, something quieter under the usual warmth. "Natasha will be coming tonight. Tony as well, and it seems Steve and Bruce along with him. I did not entirely appreciate, until just now, how much that silence actually cost the people who care about me. I owe him considerably more than an apology when he arrives."

Xander didn't say anything right away, just reached over and set a hand briefly on Thor's shoulder, the gesture doing the work words probably wouldn't have done as well.

"You're allowed to have scared people," Xander said finally. "Doesn't make you a bad friend. Just means you're worth being scared for."

Thor managed something close to a real smile at that, quiet and grateful, and the moment eased, gradually, back into something Willow could safely reach past.

"That's still a very reasonable thing for a friend to want to confirm," she said, gentler than her earlier excitement, though it crept back in anyway around the edges. "Also, no offense, but actual Avengers. Plural. Coming here. To Sunnydale."

"I did warn you my associates tend toward the dramatic once really worried."

"I'm not complaining," Willow said. "I'm the opposite of complaining. I have so many questions already forming. Does she actually use the Widow's Bite in the field or is that mostly cinematic embellishment, I've always wondered—"

"I would ask that you keep the questions to a reasonable number," Thor said, weary but fond, clearly having had this exact conversation with enthusiastic strangers more than once before. "Natasha does not always appreciate enthusiasm at first meeting. It tends to grow on her slowly."

"I can be reasonable," Willow said, in a tone that suggested she absolutely could not.

"You always say that," Xander pointed out, "right before you ask somebody four questions about clearance levels in the first five minutes."

"That was different. Those were good questions."

"They were extremely good questions. Also extremely not reasonable, by your own definition."

"I contain multitudes," Willow said, grinning, and both Xander and Thor groaned in unison, the reflex now so well established between the household that it barely required conscious thought anymore.

"That's still not your line," Xander said.

"I'm aware. I'm workshopping alternatives. This one felt right in the moment."

Xander just laughed, already bracing himself, with real fondness, for whatever chaos actual Avengers showing up in Sunnydale were about to add to an already thoroughly unpredictable month, and reached for one of Thor's carefully constructed nachos, earning an immediate, wounded protest about proper cheese distribution ratios that carried them both, laughing, back into the kitchen.

***

Getting three people and a minivan across the actual boundary between realities turned out to need considerably more hardware than a phone call ever had.

Tony had it half-built already, it turned out — a paranoid, just-in-case project he'd shelved months back and never quite stopped tinkering with in the margins of everything else, and it took him and a borrowed SHIELD storage unit outside the city under four hours to finish it properly, adrenaline apparently doing what deadlines usually did for him. What it amounted to, in the end, was a rough ring of squat black cylinders arranged on bare concrete, cables running to a case not much bigger than a suitcase, the whole rig looking considerably less impressive than the single most expensive interdimensional engineering project any of them would ever personally witness had any right to look.

"This is definitely gonna work," Tony said, not looking up from the tablet in his hands. "Probably. Ninety percent. Ninety-two if I'm feeling generous with myself."

"Those are not confidence-inspiring numbers," Steve said, from the minivan's driver seat, both hands already on the wheel like it might matter yet.

"They're honest numbers, which is different and better. I built a listening device. I did not build a door. What I've got now is that same listening device with delusions of grandeur, wired to enough output that it might, possibly, punch a hole exactly where Thor's own signature's already worn the wall thin for us. Might being the operative word. Try not to dwell on the word might."

"I'm dwelling on the word might extremely hard right now," Bruce said, from the passenger seat, hands folded very deliberately in his lap.

"Don't. Counterproductive." Tony made one last adjustment, and the cylinders hummed up into a low, climbing pitch that raised the hair on the back of Steve's neck. "Whichever of you prays, now's the time."

"I don't," Steve said.

"I sort of do, complicatedly," Bruce said. "Now seems reasonable to start."

The air between the cylinders didn't so much open as it stopped being air — a seam of gold-edged dark, ragged at first, then smoothing, widening, until it stood there clean and impossible, a doorway shaped like nothing that had ever needed a door before.

"There it is." Something fierce and relieved cut through Tony's deadpan for just a second. "Drive slow. Steve, I mean you specifically, I've seen how you take corners."

"That was one time."

"One time is exactly the number of times it needs to happen for me to remember it forever."

Steve eased the minivan forward, and for one held breath the headlights speared straight into gold instead of asphalt, and then the gold was behind them instead of ahead, and the road under the tires had gone quiet and dark and unfamiliar, a sleepy small town's back road standing in for the storage lot they'd just left entirely.

"Well," Bruce said, faint. "That happened."

"That happened," Tony agreed, already grinning, adrenaline burning off into something closer to triumph. "Somebody find me a street sign. I want to know exactly which impossible town we just broke several laws of physics to reach."

***
