Tony showed up that same night anyway, because "talked down" and "actually stayed home" had never once, in the whole history of knowing him, meant the same thing — and he hadn't come alone.

He didn't bring a jet. He brought a rented minivan, sunglasses, zero apology, and, trailing in behind him with the specific weary patience of men who'd tried arguing and lost, Steve and Bruce.

"He wouldn't take no for an answer," Steve said, by way of greeting, before anyone could ask. "We tried. Repeatedly. It didn't take."

"I don't take no for an answer from people who are wrong," Tony said, already walking into the kitchen like he'd been invited weeks ago instead of explicitly asked not to come. "Natasha said don't fly out. I didn't fly. I drove. Very different thing."

"Natasha said not to come at all," Xander said, staring at all three of them.

"Natasha said a billionaire's jet would draw attention. I rented a minivan. Extremely stealth." Tony set a case down on the table and popped it open, revealing something that looked like it had been built out of half a satellite and half a toaster. "Also, I built the thing letting you people talk to a literal Asgardian across a reality boundary, and I wasn't about to leave that completely unsupervised in a town that apparently also has a hellmouth. Non-negotiable. Be annoyed about it later. Steve and Bruce came because I told them it'd be interesting, and because somebody responsible needed to make sure I actually behaved myself. Ongoing project, that one."

"I'm managing expectations," Bruce said, mild, already eyeing the half-satellite-half-toaster device with open scientific curiosity he clearly couldn't fully suppress. "Low expectations. But managing them."

"How does it even work?" Willow asked, already leaning in, notebook forgotten in favor of the actual hardware. "The cross-reality part, I mean. Not the translation layer, I get the translation layer. The part where the signal crosses at all."

"Smartest question anyone's asked me all week." Tony looked, for a second, genuinely pleased. "Short version: I didn't invent a way to punch a signal through the wall between universes, because that's insane and also not how any of this works. What I did notice is that Thor already opens a door every time he moves between realms, and that door doesn't slam shut the instant he's through it — there's residual bleed, a signature, for a while after. I built something that listens for that signature and rides it back, piggybacking a signal on a door somebody else already opened. I'm not bridging realities. I'm eavesdropping on somebody who already can."

"That's—" Willow stared at the device like it had personally impressed her. "That's actually elegant."

"Thank you. Somebody around here appreciates good engineering." Tony glanced at Thor. "You, meanwhile, still owe me eleven days of silence and a much longer explanation than 'long story,' and I'm not leaving until I get it."

Thor sighed, the particular sigh of a man who'd seen this exact standoff coming from a great distance. "I rather suspected as much."

He didn't get away with the short version, and didn't try particularly hard to.

They sat in the Summers' kitchen well past midnight, Joyce working through a pot of coffee that eventually gave way to tea nobody quite finished, while Thor worked through all of it — the hammer's disappearance, the four-year search across twelve realms, Xander, the fracture, the mural, the counter-rite Faith and the others had faced down without ever fully understanding what they were up against. Bruce took notes without seeming to notice he was doing it, the old scientific habit surfacing the moment the conversation touched anything resembling data. Steve listened the way he always did, quiet and total, saving his questions for the moments they'd actually matter. Tony interrupted constantly, which turned out, once Xander adjusted to the rhythm of it, to be its own kind of careful listening — every interruption a specific, sharp question aimed at whichever part of the story didn't yet make sense.

"Back up," Tony said, somewhere past one in the morning, when Thor reached the part about the fracture reaching backward through time as well as forward. "You're telling me the reason reality's got a crack in it traces back to something we did. Us. The team."

"That is my current understanding, yes. Imperfectly held."

"That's — okay, that's a whole different problem than 'my friend's hammer went missing.'" Tony rubbed at his eyes, some of the earlier fury finally burning down into something closer to exhausted focus. "That's a problem I actually need to be in the room for, professionally, not just personally."

"I rather suspected you'd say that," Thor said, something almost fond under the weariness.

"He's not wrong to say it," Steve said, quiet. "If this actually traces back to us, Fury needs the full picture. Today, not eventually."

"I know," Thor admitted. "I have been slow reaching that conclusion, out of some combination of pride and simply wanting to understand it myself before asking anyone else to carry it. I recognize, now, that the delay itself was a mistake."

"Wasn't a mistake," Bruce said, unexpectedly, looking up from whatever calculation he'd been running in the margin of a napkin. "You wanted to bring us something real instead of half-formed panic. I get that instinct. Doesn't mean it was the right call, but it wasn't nothing, either."

Thor considered that a long moment, something in his shoulders easing slightly. "Thank you. I had not thought of it in those terms."

By the time the sky outside the kitchen window started going grey, the four of them had, between them, something closer to an actual working theory than Thor had managed alone in months — messier, more contested, considerably more caffeinated, but real. Tony left with a list of questions he promised to keep to a "reasonable" number once he brought it to Fury, which nobody in the room believed for a second. Steve left with the particular settled look of a man who'd gotten the information he'd actually come for. Bruce left still scribbling, three tangents deep into a theory about the fracture's actual physics he promised, absently, to explain properly once he'd tested it.

"That went considerably better than I feared," Thor said, once the minivan's taillights had disappeared down the street, gold flaring briefly at the edge of sight as they found their way back through wherever Tony had left the doorway standing open for the return trip.

"They love you," Xander said, simply. "Scary amounts, apparently. Kinda nice to see, honestly, given how much of my own life runs on 'never let anyone see you sweat.'"

"They do," Thor agreed, something quiet and grateful settling into his face as the first real light of morning found its way over Sunnydale's rooftops. "I am still learning not to take that for granted."

***



Chapter 115: Chapter 115



"Another one," Faith repeated, at lunch, flatly disbelieving, like she'd genuinely thought last night had already used up the month's quota of impossible visitors. "We just had three. How many Avengers does one god actually know?"

"That's what Thor said," Xander confirmed. "Natasha somebody. Apparently doesn't love being kept in the dark, and apparently doesn't move as fast as Tony, so at least we get actual advance notice on this one."

"Understandable, given the whole eleven-days-radio-silence thing," Buffy said. "I'd want to check on my friend too."

"Advance notice," Cordelia repeated, already visibly recalculating her entire week's plans. "Finally. Last night gave me zero warning to plan an outfit, and I'm not making that mistake twice."

"You don't need to impress a superhero, Cordelia," Willow said.

"I don't need to. I want to. There's a difference."

"That's not really a difference, that's just you wanting to be extra."

"I contain multitudes," Cordelia said, and the table groaned in near-unison.

"That's not your line," Xander said. "That's very much not your line."

"I'm workshopping a new signature phrase. Give me feedback."

"The feedback is no."

Jesse, who'd been unusually quiet through the whole exchange, finally spoke up. "Does she, like, fight people? Actual dangerous fighting? Also, and I say this with complete respect for her professional accomplishments, is she single, or is that a question I get murdered for asking."

"You get murdered for asking," Faith said. "Immediately. No trial."

"From what I understand, yes," Xander said. "Very dangerous fighting. Very good at it. And I'm not answering the second question on principle."

"Cool. Very cool. Totally normal thing to be casually informed about over lunch."

"Welcome to the last several months of my life," Faith said. "Gods, Avengers, ancient hammers. Nothing's normal anymore, we've just gotten really good at pretending it is."

"Fair point," Jesse admitted. "I've gotten weirdly used to it, actually. Feels concerning how used to it I've gotten."

"That's basically the whole Sunnydale experience," Buffy said. "You either leave, or you adjust until absurd feels ordinary."

"I choose adjust," Jesse said. "Absurd's got better stories anyway."

The table settled into its usual noise after that, the sandwich debate resurfacing briefly before everyone silently agreed to let it rest for the day, genuine excitement buzzing underneath the ordinary lunchtime chaos at the prospect of one more impossible thing about to walk, however briefly, into their thoroughly impossible lives.

"Can I just say," Willow said, quieter, mostly to Xander, leaning into his side, "this is truly the best month of research access I've ever had. Jumpers, gods, now actual Avengers. I don't think I'm going to sleep properly until she's come and gone."

"I believe you." He pressed a kiss to the top of her head, brief and easy. "Try to sleep at least a little, though. Can't have you collapsing from excitement before she even gets here."

"No promises," Willow said, grinning, already reaching for her notebook, a fresh page waiting, hungry and ready, for whatever came next.

***



Chapter 116: Chapter 116



Buffy came along this time, the two of them landing in a world of red-rock canyons that reminded Xander, briefly and uncomfortably, of the canyon with the handprints, before he registered the actual differences — no paintings here, just endless striated stone and a river cutting far below, silver-bright in the sun.

"Okay, this one's a looker too," Buffy said, peering over the canyon edge with more caution than her tone suggested. "Very grand-canyon energy."

"Very grand-canyon energy, minus the gift shop," Xander agreed. "Watch your footing, though. Loose rock everywhere."

They spent the first day working their way along the rim, cataloguing a flock of pale, crested birds that nested directly into the cliff face itself, diving for insects with a precision that made Buffy audibly gasp the first time she watched one drop nearly a hundred feet in a single controlled fall.

"That's honestly terrifying," she said. "In a cool way. Terrifying-cool."

"Willow's gonna want measurements on the dive angle. She's gotten weirdly specific about aerodynamics lately."

"She's gotten weirdly specific about everything lately. I love it, honestly."

They found a way down into the canyon itself on the second day, a narrow switchback trail worn into the rock by nothing but weather and time, and spent hours at the river's edge, cataloguing fish species neither of them could begin to classify, the water running cold and clear enough to see straight to a rocky bottom. Xander filled the collapsible tank with a few of the more striking ones before he'd even fully thought it through, muscle memory from three years of Alan's increasingly specific requests taking over before his conscious brain caught up.

Buffy waded in up to her knees at one point, jeans rolled up, hair coming loose from its ponytail in the humidity, and Xander caught himself staring for a solid three seconds longer than any actual fish-cataloguing purpose required before he managed to redirect his own brain back toward the notebook in his hands. 'Okay, focus,' he thought, mortified at himself the way only a teenage boy caught mid-stare by his own internal monologue could manage. 'She's talking about fish gill structure. Fish. Gills. Not — whatever your brain's currently doing. Get it together.'

"You're staring," Buffy said, not even looking up from the water.

"I'm cataloguing. There's a difference."

"Cataloguing my knees?"

"...The knees are part of the ecosystem now. Context matters."

"That's the worst save I've ever heard."

"I'm workshopping it. Give it time."

"This is actually one of the best ones," Buffy said, sitting with her feet in the water, watching light play off the canyon walls above them, entirely unbothered by his flustered recovery. "I get why you kept doing this alone for so long, before any of us started coming along. There's something about it. Being somewhere nobody's ever been."

"Yeah. That part never really wore off, even on the bad trips." Xander sat beside her, quiet a moment. "Glad I get to share it now, though. Doesn't make it less special. Just makes it less lonely."

"Good trade."

"Really good trade."

The third day brought trouble, mild but real — a rockslide, distant enough not to threaten them directly, close enough that the sound alone sent both of them scrambling for solid ground, hearts pounding long after the actual danger had passed.

"Okay, that was a lot," Buffy said, once they'd caught their breath. "That was a genuinely a lot of rock, moving very fast, very far away from us specifically, thank god."

"Canyon's earned its keep today. Very dramatic, very much a reminder that we're guests here."

"Noted. Staying further from the walls from now on."

They made camp well clear of the canyon edge after that, and spent their last evening simply watching the sky change color over red rock, comfortable and quiet, the kind of ordinary wonder that had, over four years, become the actual heart of the whole strange job.

By the time the jump pulled them home, they had photos of birds, a tank full of fish nobody back home had ever seen, a story about a rockslide that would get retold at least a dozen times over the following weeks, and the certain, settled satisfaction of two people who'd shared something really beautiful and come home from it together.

Willow was cross-legged on the kitchen counter when they came in, mid-snack, and nearly fell off it sitting up so fast. "Tell me there's fish. Please tell me there's fish."

"There's fish. Also a canyon, some birds, and one truly terrifying rockslide," Buffy said. "Ten out of ten, would recommend."

"I want all the details. All of them. Don't skip the rockslide."

"Wouldn't dream of it," Xander said, already pulling out his notebook, and the three of them slipped into the easy, familiar ritual of cataloguing one more impossible world, primary premise intact, exactly the way it had always been.

***



Chapter 117: Chapter 117



Willow and Faith both came this time, the three of them making an unusual trio that turned out, within the first hour, to work far better than any of them expected — Willow's careful observation balancing nicely against Faith's blunt, practical instincts about which way looked survivable and which didn't.

The world was jungle again, though a different one than any of them had seen before, canopy so dense overhead that the ground stayed in permanent green-tinted twilight even at midday.

"Okay, this is atmospheric," Faith said, peering up at the canopy. "Very haunted-forest energy."

"Very haunted-forest energy that's also apparently home to about six species nobody's ever documented," Willow said, already crouched over something small and iridescent crawling along a fallen branch. "I need to catalogue every single thing in this place before we leave."

"That's gonna take a while."

"I'm aware. I've made peace with it."

Xander left the two of them to it, splitting off toward the tree line to check the wider perimeter the way four years of habit had taught him to on any new world, glancing back every so often with the easy, fond amusement of a man watching two people he loved get along better than he'd necessarily expected them to.

They found the frogs on the second day, dozens of them clustered around a slow-moving stream, skin patterned in colors bright enough to look almost artificial against the muted greens surrounding them.

"Those are definitely poisonous," Faith said, keeping a healthy distance. "Nothing looks that colorful for fun."

Willow had gone very still at first sight of them, the particular stillness of someone actively arguing with an old, deeply irrational reflex. "Okay. They're just frogs. Extremely numerous, extremely bright frogs, and I'm a grown woman with a notebook, and I can absolutely do this."

"You good?" Faith asked, actually looking over now.

"I have a whole thing about frogs. It's very stupid. I'm aware it's stupid. Doesn't make it less real." Willow took a slow breath and crouched down anyway, camera steady despite the faint tremor in her hands, getting her photos from well outside arm's reach. "You're probably right about the poison, for what it's worth. Bright coloring's usually a warning system in nature. Advertise the danger instead of hiding it."

"You're doing the science-brain thing to talk yourself through it."

"Extremely accurate diagnosis. Let me have it, though. It's working."

"Fair enough." Faith drifted a half-step closer anyway, easy, unspoken backup. "See, I know things too. I'm not just muscle."

"Never said you were just muscle."

"You were thinking it."

"I was thinking several things, none of which were that specifically."

They spent the rest of that day working through the underbrush carefully, Faith's patrol-honed instincts catching hazards Willow's scientific focus occasionally missed — a rotten branch that wouldn't hold weight, a nest of something with an aggressive-sounding buzz neither of them wanted to investigate directly.

"This is actually a really good combo," Willow admitted, on the third evening. "You keeping us from dying, me actually knowing what we're looking at."

"Damn right it's a good combo. I should come on more of these."

"You really should. I like having you along."

"Yeah," Faith said, something genuine and pleased under the usual easy confidence. "Yeah, me too, actually."

The pull took them home with a full catalogue of frogs, insects, and one heart-stopping near-miss with what Faith firmly identified as "a spider the size of my actual hand, no thank you," and despite the danger, none of them would have called the trip anything but honestly good, start to finish.

"Nobody's bleeding, so I'm calling that a win already," Joyce said, taking in the state of them at the door.

"Excellent," Willow said, once she'd recovered. "Terrifying spiders, gorgeous frogs, and Faith turns out to be a genuinely great field partner."

"Don't sound so surprised," Faith said, grinning. "I contain multitudes."

"That's still not your line," Xander said, and the whole kitchen dissolved into laughter, one more strange, ordinary evening settling into place around a job that had, four years running, never once stopped finding new ways to be worth doing.

***



Chapter 118: Chapter 118



Everyone had commitments this time — Buffy with patrol, Willow buried in cross-referencing Davy's notes against Giles's texts, Faith covering a shift she'd promised weeks ago — which left the jump to Xander alone, and he found himself almost welcoming the solitude after a stretch that had felt, lately, thick with other people's schedules and other people's crises.

The world was high desert, mesas rising in the distance under a sky so clear it looked painted, the air thin and dry enough to crack his lips within the first hour.

"Okay," he said, already reaching for water. "Dry one. Very dry. Noted."

He spent the first day working his way toward the nearest mesa, cataloguing scrub vegetation that had adapted into shapes he'd never seen before, spines and waxy leaves clearly built for a world that rarely offered much water and never offered it gently.

'Four years of this,' he thought, pausing to sketch a particularly strange, barrel-shaped plant. 'Still finding new stuff every single trip. Still not tired of it, somehow.' It felt like a small, private kind of luck, that the wonder hadn't worn thin yet, even with everything else going on lately.

The lizards showed up in numbers on the second day, dozens of them sunning themselves along sun-warmed rock faces, scattering and resettling in loose, shifting patterns as he made his way past.

"You guys are everywhere," he told them, getting his photos carefully. "Very efficient use of a rough environment. Respect."

One of them, bolder than the rest or maybe just thirstier, lingered near his camp long enough that he cracked his water reservoir and left a shallow cap of it out on a flat rock, more out of curiosity than any real expectation — and, if he was honest, his own cracked lips weren't exactly unsympathetic to the impulse either. It came back for the water twice more before the day was out, unbothered by his presence in the same unlearned way every calm thing on every world always was, and by the second refill it was drinking close enough to his hand that scooping it up for the trip home felt less like a capture and more like a formality neither of them had bothered to argue about.

He climbed partway up the nearest mesa that afternoon, slow and careful given the loose scree underfoot, and found a view at the top worth every scraped knuckle it cost him — mesas rolling out in every direction, shadows stretching long and purple as the light started to fail, a silence so complete he could hear his own heartbeat.

'This is the part nobody back home really gets,' he thought, staying still a long while. 'Not the animals. This. Just being somewhere completely untouched, completely quiet, and getting to just exist in it for a minute.' It felt like the truest, simplest reward the job had ever offered him, four years running.

He made camp near the mesa's base that night, and spent the last day simply savoring the solitude, unhurried, in a way he hadn't quite let himself in weeks, waiting for the pull to take him home on its own schedule.

By the time the jump pulled him home, he had photos of lizards and strange desert plants, one much less patient lizard tucked into the crook of his arm, sunburn along the back of his neck, and a quiet, restored sense of why he'd loved this job in the first place, long before gods and Avengers and ancient hammers had ever entered the picture.

Joyce was mid-way through the dishes when he wandered into the kitchen, and she got exactly one word out — "So—" — before she noticed the lizard tucked against his side. "Oh. Hello. We've got a new addition, I see."

"Really good one. Quiet. Just me, some very resilient plant life, and apparently a new roommate for Alan." Xander held the lizard up slightly, and it blinked at Joyce with the same unbothered patience it had shown the entire walk home. "He followed me for water and then just sort of... didn't stop following me. I didn't really fight it."

"Sounds like exactly what you needed." Joyce reached out, giving the lizard a careful, curious scratch along its back before it could decide whether to be alarmed by the gesture. "Both the quiet and, apparently, the company."

"Yeah," Xander said, meaning it fully. "Yeah, it really was."

***



Chapter 119: Chapter 119



Willow and Buffy both came this time, the three of them landing in a world of towering sequoia-like trees, trunks wide enough that all three of them together couldn't have circled one with linked arms.

"Okay, these are enormous," Buffy said, craning her neck up toward a canopy lost somewhere far overhead. "Genuinely enormous. Very small-human-being energy, standing here."

"I need measurements," Willow said, already pulling out her tape measure with visible glee. "So many measurements."

They spent the first two days simply working through the grove, cataloguing a forest floor thick with ferns and fungi in colors none of them had names for, and found the actual wildlife on the third morning — something owl-like but far larger, perched in the lower branches with unblinking, patient eyes that tracked their movement without any apparent concern.

"That's a big owl," Xander said, keeping his voice low. "That's an actually unreasonable owl."

"Everything out here is really unreasonable," Buffy said. "I think that's just the whole theme of these worlds at this point."

They got their photos carefully, respectful of the distance the creature seemed to prefer, and spent the rest of the trip in the easy, comfortable rhythm that came from three people who'd long since figured out how to work well together — Willow cataloguing, Buffy watching for hazards, Xander keeping them all moving at a pace that balanced curiosity against actually getting anything done.

"This is a good one," Willow said, on the last evening, looking over pages of notes with visible satisfaction, leaning back against Xander's chest while Buffy stretched out with her head in his lap. "Genuinely one of the best groves I've ever seen."

"High bar, considering the competition," Xander said. "But yeah. I'll agree."

They made it home with photos of the massive owl and measurements of trees that would take Willow weeks to properly catalogue, reminded all over again why the job stayed worth doing, gods and Avengers and ancient hammers notwithstanding.

"Giant trees, giant owl, zero complications," Buffy announced before Joyce could even get the door all the way open. "I'll take it."

"I think we've all earned a few zero-complication trips lately," Joyce said, already stepping back to let them in.

***



Chapter 120: Chapter 120



Natasha arrived on a Thursday, exactly the "sometime this week" Thor had relayed, her entrance carrying none of the chaos Tony's had. SHIELD had spent the week since turning his one-off, ninety-percent-confidence rig into something considerably more controlled — a proper crossing point, manned and monitored, built out in the same storage unit outside the city — and Natasha came through it on foot, alone, whatever vehicle she'd used left well out of earshot on the other side. She made her presence known by simply appearing in the Summers' kitchen doorway while Joyce was making coffee, duffel bag over one shoulder, an expression that suggested she'd already cataloged three exits and two potential threats before saying a single word.

Joyce, to her considerable credit, only jumped slightly, coffee pot steady in her hand despite the surprise. "You must be Natasha."

"I must be. Sorry for the entrance. Force of habit." Natasha's eyes did a quick, assessing sweep of the kitchen, the house, presumably the whole situation, before settling into something closer to relaxed, shoulders dropping a half-inch that Xander wouldn't have caught if he hadn't already spent weeks learning to read Thor's own subtle tells. "Where's Thor?"

"Living room. Wrestling's on."

"Of course it is." Natasha allowed herself something almost like a smile, setting her bag down by the door, keeping her hands free out of old habit. "That tracks. Tony give you all any warning before he descended, or did he just show up?"

"No warning at all," Joyce confirmed. "Just arrived, drove straight through to nearly one in the morning at my kitchen table, and I made enough coffee to float a boat while your friend explained, at some length, why the universe apparently owes all of you an apology. Very polite about it, though. Left the kitchen cleaner than he found it, which I genuinely wasn't expecting."

"That's Steve's influence, not his. Tony wouldn't think to wipe a counter if his life depended on it." Natasha shook her head, something fond under the exasperation. "Glad he got his fix in early. Means I get to actually do this properly instead of racing him to it."

She found Thor exactly where Joyce said, boots still up on the coffee table, and the two of them fell into an easy, familiar rhythm that told Xander, watching from the doorway, more about their actual friendship than eleven days of worried silence ever could have — the kind of comfort that only came from years of trusting someone enough to actually let your guard down around them.

"You look unharmed," Natasha said, dropping onto the couch arm without ceremony. "Annoyingly unharmed, actually, given how worried you made everyone."

"I am unharmed. Genuinely." Thor met her eyes, sincere despite the teasing edge in her voice. "I apologize for the silence. It was not my intention to worry anyone."

"I know. Doesn't make the eleven days less annoying." She studied him a moment longer, something in her posture easing further, the last of the tension she'd walked in with finally draining out now that she'd confirmed with her own eyes what she'd apparently already half-believed from his voice on the phone. "So. Long story?"

"Extremely long story."

"Give me the short version first. I'll get the rest later. I always do."

Thor obliged, working through the hammer, the search, the fracture, Xander's own connection to it all, and Natasha listened with the same careful, assessing attention she'd brought to the whole room initially, occasionally glancing at Xander or Willow like she was filing away details for later, expression giving away almost nothing except the very slight tightening around her eyes whenever the scope of the situation expanded beyond what she'd apparently been braced for.

"So there's a whole multiversal instability situation," she said, once he finished, "caused by us, apparently, and it's been quietly reshaping reality for years without anyone noticing."

"That is, broadly, the current theory, yes."

"Great. Wonderful. Exactly the kind of thing I love hearing about on a Thursday." Natasha rubbed her temples, though there was real humor underneath the exasperation. "Does Fury know?"

"Some of it. Tony has what I gave him and Steve that first night, which Steve made rather clear would be reaching Fury regardless of my own preferences on the timing. But that was before Giles and I had worked through the mural, the counter-rite, or how any of it actually connects back to the ability itself. What Fury has is the rough shape of it. Not the full picture."

"He's gonna want the full picture. Sooner rather than later, given the scale you're describing. Trust me on this one, Thor. The longer this sits half-explained, the worse the conversation gets when he finally has all of it."

"I agree. That's rather why I wanted you to have it properly, before anyone tries assembling a report out of half-finished pieces."

"Fair. Just don't wait too long. I've had that conversation with him before, on your behalf, more than once. It doesn't get easier with practice."

Natasha considered the room again — Xander, Willow, Giles' books stacked on the coffee table, a half-eaten plate of Thor's nachos still sitting forgotten on the counter, the whole strange, domestic scene of a god settled comfortably into small-town life. "This is a truly bizarre way to spend a Thursday."

"Welcome to Sunnydale," Xander said. "We've got a whole thing going on. You get used to it."

"I'm gonna need considerably more context before I get used to anything," Natasha said, though something in her tone suggested she was already, despite herself, finding the whole situation more interesting than alarming, the professional wariness gradually giving way to genuine curiosity. "Start from the beginning. All of it. I've got time."

"You've got time?" Xander repeated, a little disbelieving. "Don't you have, like, an actual job? Missions, or whatever they're called?"

"I've got a few days before anyone comes looking for me specifically. Fury's good about giving me room to actually investigate something properly instead of rushing through it." She settled more fully into the couch cushions, the last of her guarded posture finally easing into something almost comfortable. "Also, honestly? This is more interesting than most of what's currently on my desk."

"Buckle in," Willow said, grinning, already pulling out her notebook, determined not to miss a single detail. "This one's got layers."

***



Chapter 121: Chapter 121



Everyone was tied up with Natasha's visit, which left the next jump to Xander alone, and he found himself almost grateful for the excuse to step away from a house currently hosting both a god and a spy.

The world was river delta, channels braiding through mudflats in a pattern that looked almost deliberate from a distance, wading birds working the shallows in numbers he hadn't seen since the wetland trip weeks back.

"Okay," he said, picking his way carefully along a drier ridge between channels. "Muddy one. Very muddy. At least it's not cold this time."

He spent the first day documenting the birds, herons and something crane-like moving through the shallows with careful, deliberate steps, and found a den on the second morning tucked into the ridge itself — otters, or something close enough to the concept that his brain filed them there immediately, sliding in and out of the water with the same loose, playful energy he remembered from an otter trip long before any of this Thor business started.

"You guys are a good reminder," he told them, crouching close enough that one investigated his boot with idle curiosity before losing interest and sliding back into the water. "Not everything's gotta be complicated. Sometimes it's just sliding into water because it's fun." One of the pups, notably braver than the rest, climbed halfway into his lap on its own initiative, completely unbothered by the whole encounter. "Okay, that's — yeah, you're coming home. Alan's gonna name you something ridiculous, I can already tell."

He spent the rest of the trip in that same easy rhythm, and by the time the jump pulled him home, he had bird and otter photos both, mud up to his knees, one very small and very pleased new addition tucked into the makeshift carrier, and nothing complicated behind him for once, in a stretch where everything else definitely had been.

Willow caught him at the door, Natasha visible in the background still deep in conversation with Thor, and kissed him hello despite the mud before she even asked. "Good one?"

"Really good one. Otters. Birds. Very grounding, honestly, after everything else going on."

"I bet. How was the mud?"

"Extensive. Deeply extensive." Xander glanced toward the living room. "How's the whole superhero situation going?"

"Surprisingly well. She's actually really easy to talk to, once you get past the whole assessing-everyone-immediately thing."

"Good. Glad it's going well." He headed for the stairs, mud-caked boots already coming off. "Gonna go get cleaned up, then I want the full debrief on whatever I missed."

"Deal," Willow said, and let him go track mud up the stairs, the god and the spy still deep in conversation in the next room, entirely unbothered by any of it.

***

122. Cent: "An actual Avenger?"

Xander told me about it the next time he made it back to the fence line, still visibly buzzing about the whole thing.

"So. Natasha's here."

"The actual Avenger."

"The actual Avenger. Spent most of last week camped out in my living room, actually, talking shop with Thor about the fracture thing."

That took me a second to absorb. "That's a lot of cosmic significance for one house."

"Tell me about it. I keep waiting for somebody to also mention aliens, just to round it out."

"Don't jinx it."

"Too late. Already thought it out loud."

I laughed. Good laugh, the real kind. "How's she taking all of it? The whole multiverse situation?"

"Better than I expected. Way more composed than I would've been, hearing my organization accidentally broke reality."

"That tracks. Probably takes a lot to rattle somebody like that."

"Yeah. Guessing so."

I told Dad about it at dinner. Watched him go quiet the way he does when something's genuinely landed.

"An Avenger," he said slowly. "That's — that's a whole different level of exposure than anything we've dealt with before."

"Exposure for Xander, maybe. Not really for us, though, if you actually work the distance out. Her whole organization's on a completely different Earth from the one Xander lives on, and his is a completely different Earth from ours. Two separate doors between anything they'd ever learn and anything that actually touches this family."

Dad considered that, something in his shoulders easing slightly. "That's... a fair point. Hadn't thought it through in those terms."

"Xander trusts her, too. Says she's careful."

"Careful people still work for organizations. Organizations still have their own agendas." But he said it without the edge it would've carried a minute earlier, already turning the actual math of it over instead of just the fear. "I'd still like to meet her myself, eventually. Get my own read. Not out of worry, particularly. Just good practice, knowing who knows how much."

"I'll ask."

"Not yet, though. Let this settle first."

"Yeah. Fair."

Mom looked between us, something thoughtful crossing her face. "This whole thing keeps getting bigger, doesn't it. Started with a boot print."

"Started with a boot print," I agreed. "Ended up gods and spies and time travel."

"World's a strange place," Dad said.

"Always was," Mom said. "We just didn't used to notice it this much."

That stuck with me a while after dinner, turning the whole strange year over one more time. Boot print. Marker. Note. Xander. Thor. Now an actual Avenger, sitting in a living room two thousand miles away, calmly discussing the end of predictable reality like it was just another Thursday.

Big year, I thought. Getting bigger.

Didn't mind it, though. Not even a little.

***



Chapter 123: Chapter 123



Faith came along this time, easy about it now in a way that hadn't been true a few months back, and Buffy joined as well, the three of them landing in a world of scattered lakes dotting a landscape of low, rolling hills.

"Okay, this one's calm," Faith said, taking in the scenery. "Very calm. I could get used to calm."

"Careful," Buffy warned, grinning. "Every time somebody says that, something happens."

"I'm allowed to hope."

Nothing happened, as it turned out, which all three of them noted with real relief — three quiet days spent working the lake edges, cataloguing waterfowl and a family of beaver-like creatures busy reinforcing a dam that looked, by Willow's later assessment secondhand, more sophisticated than any beaver dam back home had any right to be.

"This is exactly the kind of trip I needed," Faith said, on the second evening, watching the sunset paint the lake orange. "After everything with Natasha, with the fracture thing, with just — all of it. Needed something simple."

"Yeah," Xander agreed. "Same, honestly. Feels like we've all earned a few simple ones lately."

"You've earned way more than a few," Buffy said, close enough to Xander that their shoulders touched, easy and unthinking the way it always was between them. "You're the one holding half this stuff together, even when you don't realize you're doing it."

Faith watched that small, ordinary closeness a beat longer than she meant to, something quiet and unexpected settling in her chest at the sight of it. 'Huh,' she thought, turning the feeling over carefully, unwilling to name it too fast. 'That's new.' Not jealousy, exactly — she'd half expected jealousy, if this ever came up, and it wasn't that. Something more like wanting, plain and simple, the kind she hadn't let herself feel about much of anything in longer than she could easily place. She filed the thought away without deciding what to do with it yet, the way she'd learned to file most things that mattered too much to rush.

"I don't know about holding it together. Mostly just trying to keep up."

"That counts too."

They spent the rest of the trip in that same easy, appreciative quiet, and by the time the jump pulled them home, all three of them felt lighter than they had in weeks — Faith especially, something in her posture looser than it had been since the whole hammer business started.

The house was quiet when they got back, Joyce already asleep and Willow curled up on the couch over a book, blinking awake as the door creaked open. "Hey — didn't hear you land. Good one?"

"No drama," Faith said. "Just lakes and beavers and good company."

"I'm glad," Willow said, and meant it, already scooting over to make room on the couch for whichever of them wanted it.

***



Chapter 124: Chapter 124



Buffy came along this time, both of them landing in a world of scattered volcanic islands connected by shallow, warm water, steam rising faintly from distant peaks that looked dormant enough not to worry about.

"Okay, this one's got personality," Buffy said, taking in the steam. "Very moody-landscape energy."

"Moody but hopefully not actively dangerous," Xander said, checking the nearest peak with a practiced eye. "Learned my lesson about active volcanoes a while back."

They spent the first day wading between islands, cataloguing fish in colors bright enough to look almost tropical, and found the actual highlight on the second morning — a colony of crab-like creatures with shells patterned in intricate, almost decorative designs, scuttling across volcanic rock in numbers that turned the whole shoreline into a shifting, clicking mass.

"That's honestly beautiful," Buffy said, crouching to get a closer look. "In a slightly unsettling way. Beautiful-unsettling."

"Willow's gonna lose her whole mind over the shell patterns. I already know it."

"She's gonna want us to catch one, isn't she."

"Absolutely. Already dreading that conversation."

They spent the rest of the trip working the shoreline, careful of loose volcanic rock, and by the third evening had settled into the easy, appreciative quiet that came from a trip asking nothing more of either of them than patience and good footing.

"This is nice," Buffy said, watching steam drift against a darkening sky, leaning back into Xander's chest, his arms coming around her without either of them making a thing of it. "After everything with Natasha and the whole fracture reveal, this is exactly the kind of normal I needed."

"Yeah. Same, honestly. Feels like these quiet ones matter more lately, somehow."

"They do. I think we both know why."

They made it home with crab photos, warm water still clinging to their clothes, glad all over again that the ordinary parts of this life mattered just as much as the extraordinary ones.

Willow met them at the door already reaching. "Please tell me you got photos of whatever it was. I can see it on your faces, you found something."

"Volcanic islands, decorative crabs, zero danger," Buffy confirmed. "Ten out of ten."

"I need every single crab photo immediately."

"Called it," Xander said, already handing over the camera.

Faith was in the kitchen when they came through, quieter than usual, watching the easy way Buffy leaned into Xander while she talked with a look Buffy couldn't quite place — not unkind, just thoughtful in a way that felt, for reasons Buffy didn't examine too closely, like it was about more than just the crab photos.

***



Chapter 125: Chapter 125



Natasha stayed three days, significantly longer than Xander expected given how she'd initially framed the visit as a quick check-in, and by the second evening she'd embedded herself into the household's rhythm with the same quiet efficiency she seemed to bring to everything — coffee made without being asked, a knife found and sharpened in Joyce's kitchen that nobody had realized needed sharpening, small competencies deployed so smoothly they barely registered as remarkable until you stopped to actually notice them.

"You're better at this than I expected," Giles told her, over books spread across the Magic Box counter, both of them cross-referencing Thor's account against what fragments of Watcher lore touched on extra-dimensional matters, the two of them having eased into a working rhythm that felt, by the second day, like it had existed far longer than it actually had. "Most people find the scope of this rather overwhelming."

"I've spent a career finding the scope of things overwhelming and doing the job anyway," Natasha said, not looking up from the page she was cross-referencing against a printout of Thor's own hand-drawn diagram. "You adjust. Or you don't, and you find different work."

"A pragmatic philosophy."

"The only kind that's kept me alive this long." She glanced up, something almost like genuine warmth in the look. "You're not bad at this yourself, for a librarian."

"I am not, strictly speaking, a librarian."

"Could've fooled me, given the shelving system."

"The shelving system is a matter of considerable personal pride, thank you."

Giles seemed to consider that an actually reasonable exchange, and the two of them fell back into their research with the easy rhythm of two people who'd both spent considerable careers being the only expert in rooms that didn't understand what they actually did — a recognition that apparently required very little time to establish once both parties actually met someone who shared it.

Xander found Thor on the porch that same evening, Natasha's visit clearly weighing on him in ways he hadn't quite let show around her directly, sitting with his elbows on his knees and his gaze fixed somewhere past the ordinary suburban street.

"You okay?" Xander asked, sitting down beside him.

"I am. Genuinely. It's simply strange, having someone from my other life here, in this life. I hadn't quite prepared for how that would feel." Thor turned the observation over visibly, working through something that clearly hadn't fully settled yet. "Natasha knows a version of me I have not been, totally, since arriving here. Warrior. Avenger. Someone who solves problems with considerably more force than conversation. I find I have grown somewhat fond of the version of myself this town has allowed me to become instead."

"Yeah, I get that. Kind of got my own version of that lately, with Cent's family. Different Xander shows up depending which world I'm standing in."

"How do you manage it? Holding both lives at once."

Xander considered the question seriously, turning it over the way Thor himself often did before answering something that mattered. "Honestly? Badly, some days. Mostly you just let each side know the other exists and hope they don't collide too hard. Seems to be working out okay so far."

"That is, I confess, not a terribly reassuring strategy."

"Yeah, well, it's the only one I've got. Four years in, still winging most of it."

Thor managed something like a laugh at that, genuine despite the weight still sitting behind his eyes. "I appreciate the honesty, if not the reassurance."

"That's basically my whole brand at this point."

"It suits you. Better than false confidence would, I suspect."

They sat together a while in comfortable quiet, the ordinary evening settling around them, before Xander finally asked the question that had been sitting with him since Natasha's arrival. "You gonna tell her everything? Even the parts that make this whole thing sound really scary?"

"Yes. She has earned honesty, same as anyone who cares enough to search eleven realms' worth of worry looking for me." Thor's expression turned thoughtful. "I have learned, this past year, that the people who deserve careful truth rarely benefit from being protected from it instead."

Natasha commandeered Thor's device the following morning to reach Fury directly, an ordinary phone being useless for the job, Xander catching only fragments through the wall, but enough to understand the conversation was serious, careful, and much longer than either of them had probably expected going in, her voice dropping into a register Xander hadn't heard from her before — precise, economical, the voice of someone delivering intelligence rather than simply making conversation.

"He wants a full report," she told Thor afterward, expression giving away little, though something in the set of her shoulders suggested the conversation had cost her more focus than she was letting show. "Written. Detailed. He's taking this seriously."

"As he should."

"Yeah. As he should." She studied Thor a moment longer, something assessing in the look that softened, gradually, into something closer to genuine affection. "You did good, finding this. Even if it took you eleven realms and eleven days of worrying everyone half to death to get here."

"I regret the worry. I do not regret the finding."

"Fair trade, probably." Natasha allowed herself something almost like warmth. "Keep me posted. Both of you. This isn't the kind of thing that stays small."

"Understood," Xander said, and meant it, already bracing, with a strange mix of dread and genuine curiosity, for whatever came next now that the wider world had officially, formally noticed what had been quietly unfolding in Sunnydale all along.

***



Chapter 126: Chapter 126



Willow came along this time, both of them landing in a world of chalk cliffs overlooking a churning gray sea, gulls wheeling overhead in numbers loud enough to hear from well before they crested the final rise.

"Okay, this one's dramatic," Willow said, peering over the cliff edge with appropriate caution. "Very dramatic coastline energy."

"Very dramatic coastline energy that's apparently also a bird highway," Xander said, watching the wheeling flocks. "This is a lot of gulls."

They spent the first day documenting the cliff colony, careful of loose chalk underfoot, and found something genuinely new on the second morning — a species of seal hauled out on a narrow beach below, spotted in a pattern unlike anything either of them had catalogued before.

"Those spots are incredible," Willow said, already sketching furiously. "I need every angle. Every single angle."

"You've got time. They don't look like they're going anywhere fast."

They spent hours documenting the colony, careful of the steep, unstable path down to the beach, and by the third evening had filled nearly every page of Willow's current notebook with careful, excited observation.

"This might truly be one of my favorites," Willow said, looking over her notes with visible satisfaction. "The spot pattern alone is gonna keep me busy for weeks."

"Glad it's a good one. You've earned a good one, honestly, with everything else you've had going on lately."

"Same to you. Feels like we've both been running pretty hard lately." Willow leaned her head against his shoulder, notebook finally set aside for the evening.

"Yeah. This helps, though. Always does." Xander pressed a kiss to her hair, and they sat like that a while, chalk dust and salt air and the easy, uncomplicated comfort of just being together with nothing urgent demanding either of their attention.

They made it home with seal photos and chalk dust in every crease of their clothes, carrying exactly the grounding both of them had needed.

Buffy greeted Willow with a kiss hello before turning to do the same to Xander. "You look like you've been living in a chalk quarry. Good one?"

"Spotted seals, dramatic cliffs, honestly excellent data," Willow confirmed, once she'd caught her breath from the kiss. "I'm thrilled."

"I can tell," Buffy said, grinning at the state of her.

"Same colony's probably good for an actual capture attempt someday," Willow added, almost as an afterthought, flipping back through her notes. "Not this trip. This trip was for us. But that spot pattern's actually new-to-science — Alan's gonna want one properly documented in person eventually, not just sketched from a cliff."

"Someday," Xander agreed. "Today was for chalk dust and no urgency. I'm not ruining that by planning logistics in the doorway."

***

127. Cent: "Fury wants a report?"

Xander told me about the phone call the next time he made it back to the fence line. I could hear it was weighing on him before he even got the words out.

"Natasha called her boss. Fury. Wants a full written report on the whole fracture thing."

That landed. "That's... a lot bigger than a private theory now."

"Yeah. That's what I keep thinking too."

"You worried?"

"A little. Mostly just — this was our thing, you know? Small. Manageable. Now it's apparently a government-agency thing too."

I understood that better than he probably realized. "Welcome to my whole childhood."

"Fair point. Guess I'm getting a crash course."

"Dad's gonna want to know everything, once I tell him. He's got a lot of opinions about what happens when agencies start taking interest in things like this."

"Good opinions or bad ones?"

"Earned ones. Not the same thing as good, necessarily, but definitely earned."

Xander was quiet a second. "You think this could actually go bad? Like, bad-bad?"

"I don't know. Could. Doesn't have to. Depends who's actually reading the report, what they decide to do with it." I thought about Dad, about Cox, about all the ways careful information could turn into something else in the wrong hands. "Just — be careful what gets written down. That's the one piece of advice I've actually got."

"Noted. Passing that along to Giles."

"Good. He seems like the type who'd actually listen."

"He is. Genuinely is."

I told Dad that night. Watched the careful, familiar stillness cross his face — the one that means he's already three steps into worst-case scenarios before he lets himself say a word out loud.

"A written report," he said slowly. "Going up an actual chain of command."

"That's what Xander said."

"I don't love that."

"I know. Neither do I, honestly. But it's already happening. Nothing to do but hope it lands somewhere reasonable."

He turned that over a long moment. "I'd like to see that report, if there's any way to arrange it. Not to interfere. Just to know."

"I'll ask Xander. Can't promise anything."

"Understood. Just ask."

I carried the whole conversation with me a while after, turning it over the way I always do when something matters enough. Bigger and bigger, this whole thing. Boot print to god to Avenger to actual government paperwork.

Hoped it landed somewhere good. Didn't have much more control over it than that.

Chapter 128: Chapter 128



"So wait, an actual government agency knows about all this now?" Jesse asked, at lunch, visibly working through the implications with more seriousness than his usual lunch-table energy.

"Apparently," Xander said. "Natasha's boss wants a full report. Giles is working on it with her right now."

"Is that bad? That feels like it could be bad."

"Honestly not sure yet. Could go either way."

Cordelia, who'd been listening with unusual focus, finally spoke up. "Okay, but does this mean we're all, like, government business now? Because I did not sign up to have a file somewhere."

"You probably already have a file somewhere, Cordelia," Willow said. "Given everything that's happened in this town over the years."

"That's deeply unsettling information and I need you to take it back."

"Can't take back a probably-true fact."

"You can try."

Faith, arriving mid-conversation, dropped into her usual seat. "Still on the Avenger thing? Feels like we've talked about nothing else all week."

"It's a genuinely big development," Willow said. "I feel like it deserves the conversation time."

"Fair. Just feels weird, our lunch table being where world-altering information gets casually discussed."

"Welcome to Sunnydale," Xander said. "We've had worse lunch table topics."

"That's true, actually," Jesse admitted. "Remember the werewolf thing?"

"Please don't remind me of the werewolf thing."

"I'm just saying, at least this time nobody's currently trying to eat anyone."

"Genuinely valid point," Faith said. "Low bar, but valid."

The table settled into its usual noise after that, the government-agency revelation slowly giving way to more ordinary concerns — the sandwich debate resurfacing briefly, and Cordelia rounding on Jesse with visible relish.

"So are you ever going to give up, or is this a lifelong project," she said. "I'm asking for planning purposes."

"Give up on what?"

"Don't play dumb, it doesn't suit you. Well, it suits you, but not in a way that's working right now."

"I have no idea what you're referring to."

"You've been not-so-subtly trying to get my attention since the ninth grade, Jesse. I'm simply asking if there's an end date, or if I should just budget for this as a permanent background feature of my life."

"I would never. That's a completely baseless accusation."

"Your face is doing the thing it does when I catch you."

"My face does not have a thing."

"Your face has several things, and I've catalogued all of them."

The whole comfortable chaos of a lunch table that had, by now, absorbed enough impossible things to treat even the newest one with something close to routine acceptance.

"I think it's gonna be okay," Willow said, quieter, mostly to Xander, leaning her head against his shoulder. "Giles is careful. Natasha seems careful too. Feels like the report's in good hands."

"Yeah. I think so too, honestly. Just weird, having it be this big now." He pressed a kiss to the top of her head, the gesture as automatic as breathing by now.

Faith caught the moment from across the table, same as she had a handful of times over the past couple weeks now, and found herself, not for the first time lately, quietly cataloguing exactly how easy it looked, wondering, without quite letting the thought finish itself, what it might feel like to be part of that easy instead of just watching it from a few seats over.

"Big's allowed sometimes. Doesn't mean it's bad."

"No," Xander agreed. "Guess it doesn't."

***



Chapter 129: Chapter 129



Faith and Buffy both came this time, the three of them landing in a world of high alpine meadows, snow-capped peaks ringing the horizon in every direction under a sky so clear it looked unreal.

"Okay, this one's stunning," Faith said, turning slowly to take it all in. "Genuinely stunning. Different kind of stunning than the lake trip."

"Every trip's got its own kind, apparently," Buffy said. "I've stopped being surprised by how many kinds there are."

They spent the first day working through the meadow, cataloguing wildflowers in colors bright enough to rival anything from previous trips, and found the actual wildlife on the second morning — a herd of long-haired, goat-like creatures grazing at the meadow's edge, utterly unbothered as all three of them walked right up into the middle of the herd without a single animal so much as looking up from the grass.

"They're kind of majestic," Faith said, really charmed, reaching out to run a hand through one's shaggy coat while it kept right on grazing. "Didn't expect majestic. Expected weird, mostly."

"Most of it's weird and majestic at the same time, honestly," Xander said. "That's kind of the whole theme." He glanced at the nearest kid, small and curious, nosing at his sleeve without a trace of the herd's usual wariness. "Also, hey, baby one's friendly. Alan's gonna cry actual tears over this."

They spent the rest of the trip in easy, appreciative quiet, and by the third evening had fell into the kind of comfortable rhythm that came only from real trust between people who'd been through notably more together than any of them had expected when this whole strange life started, one new addition to the zoo's collection curled up contentedly in the makeshift carrier Willow had insisted they start bringing along for exactly this kind of trip.

"This is a good one," Buffy said, watching the last light fade off the peaks. "After everything with Fury, with the report, with all of it — this is exactly the reset I needed."

"Same," Faith agreed. "Feels good, having this be normal now. Having somewhere to just be quiet together."

"Yeah," Xander said, meaning it fully. "Feels like the actual point of all this, honestly. Not just the animals. This part too."

They made it home with goat photos, wildflower samples, and a fresh reminder for all three of them why this strange, complicated life was worth every bit of trouble that came bundled with it.

Willow was waiting up, curled in the doorway with a blanket around her shoulders. "Well?"

"Best one in a while," Buffy confirmed. "Mountains, goats, genuine peace. Ten out of ten."

"I'm so glad," Willow said, and pulled them both in before either had even finished setting their bags down.

***

Buffy caught up with Faith on patrol that same week, the two of them falling into step together along the cemetery's back row, comfortable in the kind of quiet that came from years of trusting someone at your back.

"Can I say something," Buffy said, eventually, "and you tell me if I'm wrong, and we never bring it up again if I am?"

Faith's steps didn't change, but something in her shoulders went carefully still. "Sure."

"That lake trip. You watched me and Xander for a second longer than you needed to, and it wasn't jealous. It was something else." Buffy kept her eyes forward, giving Faith room not to have to look at her while she answered. "I'm not saying that to put you on the spot. I'm saying it because if I'm right, I don't want you sitting on it alone, wondering if it's even safe to say out loud. It is. Whatever you end up deciding to do with it."

Faith was quiet a long moment, a stake turning over slowly in her hand. "You're not wrong."

"Okay."

"That's it? Just 'okay'?"

"For now, yeah. You don't owe me the rest of it tonight. I just wanted you to know I noticed, and that noticing isn't the same thing as it being weird." Buffy bumped her shoulder against Faith's, easy, familiar. "Take whatever time you need. We're not going anywhere."

Faith let out a breath she hadn't quite realized she'd been holding. "Yeah. Okay. Might take me a minute."

"Take your minute. I've got nowhere else to be."

They finished the patrol in comfortable quiet after that, nothing else needing saying just yet, but something had shifted regardless — a door left open rather than closed, waiting for whenever Faith was actually ready to walk through it.

129.5. Chapter 129.5

Faith waited until the gear was unpacked and the evening had settled into its ordinary quiet before she said anything, which in itself told Xander something was coming — Faith rarely waited for a good moment when a blunt one would do just as well, and the fact that she'd clearly picked this one meant she'd been carrying it a while.

"So," she said, dropping onto the couch across from where the three of them had settled, "I've been thinking about something for a few weeks now, and I'd rather just say it than keep circling it."

"Okay," Buffy said, carefully neutral, the way she got when she could tell something mattered before knowing what it was.

"You three. This thing you've got." Faith gestured vaguely between them, not unkindly. "I've been watching it for a while now. Longer than you probably realized. The way you are with each other. It's not subtle, for the record, in case anybody was under the impression it was subtle."

"We know it's not subtle," Willow said, a little wary.

"I'm not bringing it up to be weird about it. I'm bringing it up because I've been sitting with something since that lake trip, and every week since it's just gotten harder to keep sitting on it instead of saying it out loud." Faith held Xander's eyes for a second, then Buffy's, then Willow's, steady despite the vulnerability underneath it. "I want in. Not — I'm not saying that to be dramatic, or because I think I'm owed anything. I'm saying it because I've thought about it a long time now, and I'd rather be honest and get told no than keep sitting on it and wondering."

The room went quiet for a moment, nobody rushing to fill it.

"Can I ask why now?" Xander said, gently, not a rejection, just genuine curiosity.

"Honestly? The hammer thing helped. Figuring out I was worth something on my own terms, not just compared to somebody else." Faith shrugged, though the gesture cost her more than she let show. "And that lake trip, weeks back. Watching you three just be easy with each other, the way you are, and realizing I wanted that. Not instead of anything. Just — also. If that's something you'd even want. I've caught myself watching it happen more times than I probably should've since then, honestly."

"I noticed you noticing," Buffy admitted. "Wasn't sure what it meant, exactly. Didn't want to assume."

"I've thought about it too," Willow said, surprising herself a little with how easily it came out. "Not, like, actively planning anything. Just — noticed. That you fit, somehow. In a way that made sense before I ever tried to name it."

"Same," Buffy said, quiet. "I didn't want to say anything, because I didn't want to make it weird if it was just me reading into something."

"It wasn't just you," Xander said, something easing in his own chest at finally admitting it out loud. "I've thought about it too. Didn't know how to bring it up, though, given everything else you've had going on lately."

Faith let out a breath she'd clearly been holding since she sat down. "Okay. That's — truly more of a yes than I let myself expect, walking in here."

"It's not a yes to anything specific yet," Buffy said, careful but warm. "It's a yes to actually talking about it properly. Figuring out what it'd even look like, together, instead of just deciding it in one conversation on a couch."

"I can work with that. I wasn't expecting one conversation to solve it anyway." Faith managed a real smile, some of the tension finally leaving her shoulders. "Just needed to say it. Sitting on it for weeks was getting old."

"For what it's worth," Willow said, "I'm glad you said it. All of it. The wanting-in part and the honest-about-why part both."

"Yeah," Xander agreed. "Me too."

They talked well into the night after that — careful, unhurried, nobody rushing toward an answer bigger than the conversation could actually hold in one sitting, and by the time Faith finally headed home, something had shifted in the room that none of them fully had words for yet, but all of them recognized as real.

Chapter 130: Chapter 130



Xander went alone this time, everyone else tied up finishing the report for Fury, and found the world thick with bioluminescent fungi covering every fallen log and shaded hollow, glowing faint blue-green even in daylight.

"Okay," he said, crouching to get a closer look. "This is honestly beautiful. Didn't expect glowing mushrooms today."

He spent the first day documenting the fungi, and found the actual wildlife after dark on the second night — small, moth-like creatures drawn to the glow, wings patterned to mimic the fungal light so closely he nearly missed them entirely against the forest floor.

"That's some genuinely impressive camouflage," he told them, getting his photos carefully by the dim, natural light. "Willow's gonna have a field day with this one."

He spent the rest of the trip documenting the strange, glowing ecosystem, and by the time the jump pulled him home, he had photos unlike anything he'd brought back before, and a fresh reminder of why solitary jumps still mattered even now that company had become the norm.

Willow caught him at the door, papers from the Fury report still scattered across the kitchen table behind her, and kissed him hello before either of them said another word. "Okay. Now tell me."

"Really good one. Glowing mushrooms, camouflaged moths. Genuinely one of the coolest ones yet." He glanced past her toward the table. "Faith still buried in this too?"

"She left an hour ago. Said she needed air. I think the whole 'talking through what this actually looks like' conversation is still settling for her, same as it is for the rest of us."

"Yeah. Same, honestly. Good settling, though. Just a lot to hold at once."

"I need every photo. Immediately." Willow pushed the report papers aside, visibly grateful for the distraction. "How was it, being out there alone again? After all the group trips lately?"

Xander considered the question seriously. "Good, actually. Different good. Reminded me it's still my thing too, even with everyone else along most of the time now."

"That makes sense. Glad you got that reminder."

"Yeah. Me too." He handed over the camera, already reaching for a chair. "How's the report coming?"

"Slowly. Giles keeps rewriting sections. Wants it exactly right before it goes anywhere."

"Good instinct, probably, given who's reading it."

"That's what I keep telling him," Willow said, already scrolling through the glowing mushroom photos with visible delight, momentarily entirely absorbed by something that had nothing to do with Fury or reports or any of the rest of it.

***

Xander brought Davy the finished report on his next trip through, same as he'd promised Cent he would, spreading the pages across the kitchen table while Millie poured coffee nobody had asked for and Davy read in careful, methodical silence, the same unhurried thoroughness he brought to preflight checklists and grading decisions alike.

He read it twice before saying anything.

"This is good," he said finally, setting the last page down. "Careful. Doesn't overstate what you don't actually know, doesn't understate what you do. I've read worse reports from people with a lot more training than whoever wrote this."

"Giles, mostly. With Natasha's editing."

"Tell them both they did right by you." Davy tapped the page laying out the fracture theory, thoughtful. "One thing. This part, about where the ability actually comes from — you didn't mention us at all. Our side of it."

"Didn't think that was mine to include, without asking first."

Davy considered that a long moment, something like real respect settling into his expression. "Good instinct. Appreciate you thinking of it, actually asking instead of just deciding for us." He slid the pages back across the table. "Send it as it is. And Xander — thank you for bringing it. You didn't have to. Means something that you did."

"Cent said you'd want to see it. Wasn't gonna not do that."

"She's got good instincts too. Wonder where she gets that." Davy's mouth twitched, almost a smile. "Go on. Send your report. We'll be right here regardless of what comes back."

Chapter 131: Chapter 131



Fury's response came back faster than anyone expected, and considerably more measured than Xander had braced for, given everything he'd heard about the man's reputation secondhand — the eyepatch, the sense that he'd seen too much to be surprised by anything, the way even Natasha occasionally chose her words a little more carefully whenever his name came up in conversation.

They gathered in the Magic Box's back room to hear it, the same table that had hosted every major planning session of the past several months now bearing the weight of one more, Giles pouring tea nobody had asked for out of the same nervous habit he always fell into when a conversation mattered more than he wanted to admit out loud.

"He wants a meeting," Natasha said, reading from her tablet, having stayed an extra day specifically to deliver whatever answer came back rather than relay it over an insecure line. "Not an extraction. Not a lockdown. A meeting. Small group, controlled setting, his choice of location."

"That's... actually better than I expected," Giles admitted, setting the teapot down with visible relief.

"He's not reckless. Whatever reputation's reached you, the actual man's more careful than that." Natasha set the tablet down, screen still glowing with a message Xander could see but not quite parse from his angle. "He wants Thor there. Wants me there. Says he'd like you there too, Giles, given your research. And Xander, since it's his ability at the center of all this."

"He wants me to fly to wherever this meeting happens?" Xander asked, already running through logistics in his head — school, the jump schedule, whether Joyce would need advance warning about hosting a federal agency inside her own living room. "Because that seems like a logistics problem, given everything else going on."

"He's coming here, actually. Says Sunnydale's already proven it can keep a secret this size. Doesn't see a reason to relocate the conversation."

Giles removed his glasses, cleaning them slowly — the tell Xander had learned to read as genuine uncertainty rather than mere thoughtfulness. "That's either remarkably reasonable or remarkably concerning, depending on how one chooses to read it."

"Probably both," Natasha said. "That's usually how it goes with him. He doesn't do anything without a reason, and the reasons aren't always the ones you'd guess at first."

Thor, who'd been quiet through most of this, weighing the conversation carefully before contributing to it, finally spoke. "I trust your assessment of him, Natasha. If you believe this is a genuine attempt at understanding rather than something more forceful, I am inclined to believe it too."

"I do believe that. He's not perfect, but he takes real threats seriously and real discoveries seriously. This qualifies as both, from everything the report actually laid out."

"What did you end up adding, at the end? The note." Xander asked, a small, anxious edge creeping into the question despite himself. "Never actually saw that part. Wasn't sure it was mine to read before you sent it."

"Enough to make him take it seriously. Not enough to make him panic. There's a balance to that kind of writing." Natasha's expression softened slightly, reading the anxiety underneath his question. "I put my own name behind Giles's assessment, plainly, in my own words. Said I'd vouch for it personally — that you're exactly what the report says you are, a kid who stumbled into something enormous and has spent four years handling it better than most trained professionals would have. Giles did the real work, three weeks of rewrites and all. I just made sure the ending carried enough weight that Fury couldn't read it and walk away unconvinced."

That landed somewhere Xander hadn't quite expected it to, and he found himself quiet a moment before responding. "Thanks. Genuinely. Both of you."

"It's the truth. Giles doesn't waste good writing on flattery, and neither do I."

Xander needed a long moment to take in the size of it, turning the whole conversation over. "So this is really happening. An actual government official, crossing over from an entirely different reality, coming here, to talk about all this directly. In my town. Probably in this actual room, knowing how things have been going lately."

"It's really happening," Natasha confirmed. "Next week, most likely. I'll stay to help coordinate the logistics — security, timing, making sure nothing catches anyone off guard on either side."

"That's... a lot."

"It is. You've handled a lot already, though. I think you'll handle this too."

Xander appreciated the vote of confidence more than he let on, and the room slipped into a thoughtful quiet, everyone processing the size of what was coming, Thor's hand finding Xander's shoulder briefly, steadying, the same easy warmth he seemed to bring to most things even in moments carrying real weight.

"Whatever happens," Thor said, "you will not face it alone. That much I can promise you."

"Yeah," Xander said, feeling something settle in his chest that had been unsteady since the conversation started. "Yeah, I'm starting to actually believe that part."

Giles, still holding his glasses, finally put them back on, something resolved in his expression. "Then I suppose we have a week to prepare properly, rather than simply react to whatever arrives. I'd rather we used it well."

"Agreed," Natasha said, already pulling up a fresh document on her tablet, planning already underway before anyone else in the room had fully finished processing the news at all.

***



Chapter 132: Chapter 132



Buffy and Faith both came this time, all three of them wanting something ordinary before whatever Fury's visit turned out to bring the following week.

The world was prairie, endless grass rolling toward a horizon broken only by distant, scattered trees, wind carrying the smell of earth and growing things.

"Okay, this one's simple," Faith said, taking it in. "Simple's good right about now."

"Simple's great right about now," Buffy agreed. "I could use a few days of nothing complicated."

They spent the first two days working the grassland, cataloguing a herd of long-horned creatures that grazed without much concern for their presence, and found a den of small, burrowing animals on the third morning, popping up and vanishing at the sound of footsteps with a speed that had all three of them laughing within minutes.

"They're so fast," Faith said, delighted despite herself. "Didn't expect to be charmed by burrowing rodents today, but here we are."

"Life's full of surprises," Xander said. "That's basically the whole job description."

They spent the rest of the trip in easy, appreciative quiet, and by the time the jump pulled them home, all three of them felt lighter, steadier, better prepared for whatever the coming week actually held.

Faith caught Xander's hand at one point, the two of them just walking, tentative about it in a way that didn't match her usual easy confidence, glancing at Buffy like she was still checking whether this was actually allowed.

"You can, you know," Buffy said, catching the look, taking Faith's other hand herself without making a thing of it. "We talked about it. This is allowed."

"Still feels new. Gonna take me a minute to stop double-checking."

"Take all the minutes you need," Xander said, squeezing her hand once. "Nobody's in a hurry here."

Joyce was up marking something in her list when they came in, still holding hands, none of them quite ready to let go of it yet. She looked up, taking in all three of them at once — the held hands included — and didn't comment on it. "Good one?"

"Really good one," Buffy confirmed. "Prairie, funny rodents, zero drama. Exactly what we all needed before next week."

"I'm glad," Joyce said, and went back to her list, leaving them the doorway and whatever they wanted to do with it.
