Miriam climbed down first, older than Xander expected somehow, though he couldn't have said why — something in the way she carried herself, perhaps, the weight of a person stepping back into a role she'd believed permanently closed to her — and right behind her came Cent herself — having insisted, apparently, on making the trip alongside her rather than waiting anxiously at home for secondhand updates — followed by three strangers from Home: a woman with sharp, assessing eyes who introduced herself as Ilsa, a younger man who kept glancing at everything with open, barely contained wonder, and a third, quieter, clearly still working through the emotional weight of the crossing itself.

Xander and Cent looked at each other a beat too long before either of them managed to say anything, months of careful notes under rocks and stolen, too-short visits crowding into a single, relieved breath at the sight of her actually here, now, on her own terms instead of whatever narrow window his jump happened to allow.

"Hi," Cent said, first, breaking the moment with a small, crooked smile. "Figured I wasn't sitting this one out."

"Hi," Xander managed back, something warm cutting through the fear currently sitting in his chest. "Glad you're here. Genuinely."

"Xander," Miriam said, extending a hand, giving them both a moment before continuing, her grip firm despite the visible fatigue of the crossing. "Cent's told us so much about you. I'm sorry it took this long to actually meet properly."

"Yeah, this has been a whole journey for everybody, apparently." He shook her hand, glancing at the others. "You must be the team from Home."

"Ilsa Kovač," the sharp-eyed woman confirmed. "This is Petra, Dov." She studied the tree line briefly, then Xander himself, with the same careful, assessing focus Xander had seen in Natasha weeks earlier, her posture suggesting someone who'd walked into more unknown situations than she'd probably ever counted. "Your world feels different than I expected. Louder, somehow, even standing in the middle of the woods."

"I get that a lot from visitors, apparently."

Petra, older and more composed, offered a brief nod of greeting before her attention drifted, almost immediately, to the surrounding vegetation, professional interest visibly warring with the urgency of the moment. Dov, the younger of the two, hung back a moment longer than the others, taking in the tree line, the ordinary suburban rooftops visible through the branches, with an expression closer to wonder than assessment.

"This is the first time I've properly set foot on a tame side we didn't already have our own gate to," he admitted, to no one in particular. "Every other one I've studied was through proper records — its people catalogued and watched over from a distance, each one with its own wildside standing by, quietly, in case they ever needed somewhere to go. This one's just — Miriam's old reports and whatever we can see standing here. Smaller than I expected. And louder, exactly as Ilsa said."

"Welcome to Sunnydale," Xander said. "Small and loud, with occasional demons. You get used to it."

"I intend to document as much of it as I possibly can, given how rare this opportunity actually is," Dov said, already producing a small recording device, delighted like a linguist encountering an totally new dialect for the first time. "I hope that won't prove disruptive."

"Honestly? Least strange thing happening in this town this week."

Getting everyone home turned out to be less complicated than it should have been, and considerably stranger. There was no quick way back through Colorado and onward from there, not on any timeline that made sense against a crisis already measured in weeks — which left exactly one real option, one Xander had never actually tested: wait out the three days until the pull came for him again, and bring everyone through with it.

"I don't actually know if this works with this many people," he admitted, the morning it came due, all five of them gathered loosely in the yard, hands finding wrists and shoulders and each other in whatever configuration let everyone stay in contact at once. "Never tried more than two before."

"Then we're about to find out together," Miriam said, steady, her hand closing around his forearm.

The pull came at its usual hour, and for one held, uncertain second nothing happened at all, five people quietly bracing against a disappointment nobody said out loud — and then the fence line was gone, and Sunnydale's familiar kitchen stood around all of them instead, Joyce halfway through pouring coffee and very nearly dropping the pot at the sudden addition of four new people to her morning.

By the time everyone had settled, the whole household had folded into the same careful rhythm of introduction and debrief that had, by now, become almost routine — Giles walking them through everything, Ilsa listening with an intensity that made even Strange's earlier focus look casual by comparison, occasionally pausing him mid-sentence to clarify a detail with a precision that suggested she was already, mentally, cross-referencing everything against her own instruments.

"Your instruments," Giles said, once introductions settled, "are much more refined than anything we've had access to. I understand you've been tracking this instability independently."

"We have," Ilsa confirmed, already pulling out equipment that looked, to Xander's untrained eye, both ancient and impossibly advanced at once — brass housing wrapped around components that pulsed faintly with an internal light he couldn't begin to explain. "And I'm afraid what we've found is not encouraging."

The room went quiet.

"How not encouraging," Fury asked, having arrived that morning specifically to be present for exactly this exchange.

Ilsa laid out her readings carefully, translating figures Xander couldn't begin to follow into plain, careful language, her instruments displaying graphs that climbed in a curve steep enough to look, even to an untrained eye, actually alarming. "The rate of acceleration isn't linear. It's exponential, and it's been accelerating faster over the past month than in the entire period before it. Whatever this Convergence actually is, it's approaching its critical threshold notably faster than any of our earlier models suggested."

"How much faster," Strange asked, sharp.

"Weeks, not months. Possibly less, if the current rate holds." Ilsa met the room's stunned silence steadily, not flinching from the weight of what she'd just delivered. "I wish I had better news. I don't."

Miriam's face had gone pale, old fear surfacing behind her eyes, her hand finding the edge of the table as though she needed something solid to hold onto. "Twenty million," she said quietly, mostly to herself. "That's what it cost us, last time reality nearly came apart at the seams."

"This isn't the same event," Ilsa said, gently, reaching out to briefly touch Miriam's arm, a small gesture of shared understanding between two people who'd both, in their own ways, carried the weight of that history. "But I understand the fear it's stirring up. I share it."

The room settled into a heavy, focused silence, the full weight of the timeline finally, properly understood by everyone present at once, the abstract urgency of the past weeks suddenly, concretely real.

"Then we don't have the luxury of slow, careful planning anymore," Fury said, finally, voice flat in the way of a man who'd made countless difficult calls before and recognized another one arriving. "We need a working theory of what actually happens at the threshold, and we need a plan to prevent it, and we need both significantly faster than any of us were expecting to produce them."

"Agreed," Strange said. "Which means everyone in this room needs to start working together properly, not just comparing notes politely from separate corners."

Nobody argued the point, and the room shifted, almost visibly, into something more urgent, more focused — four separate parties, four separate reasons for being here, finally united by the same stark, undeniable deadline now sitting over all of them at once, chairs pulled closer together, books and instruments spreading across every available surface, the actual work of survival finally, properly beginning.

***



Chapter 147: Chapter 147



They worked through the night, and the next, and the one after that, the Magic Box slowly transforming into something closer to a war room than a magic shop — books and instruments and half-finished theories covering every available surface, four separate bodies of knowledge grinding slowly, painfully, toward something like a unified understanding. Joyce kept everyone fed with a steady rotation of sandwiches and coffee that nobody had time to properly thank her for, and Willow's carefully color-coded whiteboards had multiplied into an entire wall's worth of overlapping diagrams that only she and, increasingly, Ilsa seemed able to fully parse.

"The epicenter," Strange said, on the third night, staring at a projection of overlapping reality-signatures that made Xander's head hurt just looking at it, light patterns shifting and rippling across the shop's back wall like something between a star chart and a heat map. "Every reading we've gathered, from every source, points back to roughly the same point."

"Which is where, exactly?" Fury asked.

"Here," Ilsa said, quiet, setting down the instrument she'd been calibrating for the past hour. "Not metaphorically. Literally here. This town. Possibly this exact location."

The room went very still.

"That would explain a great deal," Giles said, slowly, removing his glasses in the gesture Xander had long since learned to read as genuine unease rather than mere thoughtfulness. "Why every disturbance, every anomaly, every arriving party has converged on Sunnydale specifically, rather than simply on this general reality."

"It would," Strange agreed. "Which means whatever's actually happening at the threshold isn't going to happen somewhere abstract, somewhere we can study from a safe distance. It's going to happen here. Possibly very close to where we're currently standing."

Xander felt something cold settle in his stomach, glancing instinctively toward the shop's front window, toward the ordinary street outside that had no idea what was about to potentially unfold around it. "So what do we actually do about it?"

Strange and Ilsa exchanged a long look, the kind that suggested they'd already been circling this exact question separately, arriving now at something they'd each hoped the other might have already solved.

"Containment," Ilsa said finally. "Not prevention — I don't believe prevention is possible at this stage, given how far the acceleration has already progressed. Containment. A localized reinforcement of the boundary, strong enough to hold even as the wider instability continues to build."

"Home's gate technology was built for controlled crossings," Miriam said, quiet, fatigue visible in the set of her shoulders after days of near-continuous work. "Not for reinforcing failing boundaries. I don't know if it can be adapted for this."

"It can, potentially, if paired with something else," Strange said. "Sorcery can reinforce boundaries directly, but not at this scale, not alone. We'd need an anchor. Something with genuine physical presence in multiple senses of the word — mystical, structural, and symbolic all at once."

Silence settled over the room as everyone turned the problem over privately, searching for whatever piece might complete it, the only sound the faint hum of Ilsa's instruments and the distant creak of the shop settling around them.

"The hammer," Giles said, quietly, into the silence.

Every eye in the room turned to him.

"Forged in a realm considerably more attuned to questions of worthiness and structural integrity than either sorcery or gate technology alone," Giles continued, working through the logic even as he spoke it aloud, gathering confidence with each sentence. "An object that already exists, quite literally, as a bridge between realities, having been sent here deliberately during a previous crisis. If anything in this room qualifies as an anchor capable of holding multiple systems together at once, I suspect it's sitting in Buffy's bedroom."

Thor, who'd been quiet through most of the discussion, sat up straighter, something shifting in his expression as the logic fell into place. "The hammer has held realities together before. Not at this scale, admittedly. But the principle is not unfamiliar to it. I have felt it stabilize far smaller tears, back on Asgard, during far lesser crises."

"And it would need someone to actually wield it," Strange said, "during whatever working we ultimately design. Someone the hammer has already judged worthy, ideally, given how little margin for error this leaves us."

Faith, who'd been sitting quietly at the edge of the room through most of the debrief, finally spoke, her voice steady despite the weight of every gaze suddenly turning toward her.

"I can do that."

Every eye turned to her this time.

"I lifted it," she said, simply. "A few weeks back. Didn't tell anybody outside the house, because it felt like mine to sit with first. But I can do it. If that's what this actually needs."

Thor studied her a long moment, something like genuine pride crossing his face, mixed with a father's worry he didn't quite bother concealing. "Then perhaps this crisis has already been preparing its own answer, longer than any of us realized."

"You sure about this?" Buffy asked, quiet, concern plain in her voice.

"No," Faith admitted, honest despite the fear it cost her to say it out loud. "But I'm sure enough to try. That's gotta count for something, given the timeline we're working with."

"It counts for everything," Ilsa said, gently. "Certainty is a luxury none of us have right now. Willingness matters much more."

Willow, quiet through most of this, finally spoke up, notebook already covered in fresh diagrams. "If we're pairing the hammer with the gate technology and Strange's sorcery, we need a working theory of how they actually interface. Three completely different systems, forced to function as one, in real time, under active strain."

"That is, admittedly, the piece I'm least confident about," Strange said. "I've never attempted a working quite like this."

"None of us have," Ilsa said. "That's rather the nature of an unprecedented crisis."

"Comforting," Xander muttered.

"I never promised comfort," Ilsa said, something almost gentle beneath the bluntness. "Only honesty, and the best plan we can actually assemble in the time we've got."

The room settled into a new kind of focus after that — not despair exactly, but the clear-eyed, determined energy of people who'd finally found the shape of an actual plan, however enormous the task ahead of them still remained, papers and instruments shifting once more as the real work of preparation properly began.

***



Chapter 148: Chapter 148



The urge to just stay came nearer than it ever had, given everything, a fourth close call in four years and by far the nearest that particular impulse had ever come to winning, but Willow insisted, quiet and firm, that normal still mattered even now, maybe especially now, and Xander found himself grateful for her insistence the moment the pull actually took him.

Buffy and Willow both came, all three of them needing, more than any previous trip, a genuine reminder of why any of this had ever been worth doing in the first place.

The world was ordinary grassland, unremarkable in the best possible way, small herds grazing peacefully under a sky doing nothing more dramatic than being blue.

"Okay," Xander said, feeling something in his chest loosen for the first time in days. "This one's just nice. No catch. I need no catch right now."

"No catch," Willow agreed, already documenting a small, harmless bird with real, uncomplicated delight. "This one's just for us."

They spent the full three days in easy, appreciative quiet, cataloguing without urgency, resting without guilt, and by the final evening had slipped into something that felt, despite everything looming back home, really peaceful.

"I needed this," Buffy admitted, watching the sunset paint the grassland gold. "Needed to remember there's still an ordinary version of this life underneath everything else happening."

"Yeah," Xander agreed. "Same. Feels important, actually. Like — whatever happens with the Convergence, this part's still real. Still worth protecting."

"That's exactly it," Willow said, quiet. "That's exactly why we're doing any of this."

They made it home steadier than they'd felt in a week, the ordinary comfort of the trip settling into something that would carry them through whatever came next — a genuine reminder, right when they needed it most, of precisely what all the enormous, frightening stakes were actually protecting.

Joyce had the door open before they'd even reached the porch, worry still etched faintly around her eyes despite the visible calm all three of them carried back with them, Faith visible just behind her in the kitchen doorway, having clearly been waiting too. "Good one?" It came out lighter than her face looked, like she was working to make it sound like an ordinary question.

"Really good one," Xander confirmed. "Exactly what we needed before everything else." He caught Faith's eye over Joyce's shoulder, and she managed a small, grateful smile back, the kind that still felt a little new on both their parts but no less real for it.

"I'm glad," Joyce said, and hugged him longer than usual, neither of them needing to say out loud what they both understood — that whatever the coming days actually held, this ordinary, quiet love, all four of them now folded into it, was worth fighting for, whatever the cost turned out to be.

***



Chapter 149: Chapter 149



The working came together over the following days with the frantic, focused energy of people racing a clock none of them could actually see, only feel, ticking down somewhere just out of view, sleep becoming increasingly optional for everyone involved, the whole household running on coffee and adrenaline and a determination that came from truly understanding what was actually at stake.

Strange handled the sorcery, weaving protective wards through diagrams that looked, to Xander's untrained eye, like nothing so much as an enormously complicated piece of abstract art, gold light tracing patterns across the shop's back room late into the night, the Cloak occasionally shifting to catch and redirect stray sparks before they could scorch anything nearby. Ilsa and Petra worked the gate technology, adapting Home's careful, controlled crossing mechanisms into something that could, hopefully, function as reinforcement rather than passage, their brass instruments spread across an entire table, components disassembled and reassembled a dozen different ways as they tested configuration after configuration. Fury coordinated logistics with quiet, efficient competence — someone who'd clearly run notably more dangerous operations than this one, on paper at least, arranging perimeter security and evacuation contingencies with the same calm he'd have applied to any operation, regardless of scale.

Buffy went to get it herself, given she was the one who'd actually proven she could carry it without strain, jogging upstairs to her own room where it had leaned against the closet door for months now, faithful and unremarkable as a coat rack right up until it mattered.

Buffy carried it home herself that evening, and it sat in her room overnight, propped against her closet door like an oddly serious houseguest, before she brought it over to Faith the next morning.

"Feels weird, handing this off," Buffy admitted, setting it down on Faith's bed. "Like I should say something meaningful. I don't actually have anything meaningful to say. Mostly just — good luck. You've got this."

"That's meaningful enough," Faith said, and took it up, the weight settling into her grip like it had simply been waiting for an actual destination all along.

Faith trained with the hammer every evening after that, Thor beside her, walking her through weight and balance and the discipline of wielding something that responded to more than muscle alone, the two of them working late into the dark behind the Summers house, the hammer's arcs catching moonlight with each controlled swing.

"You're doing well," Thor told her, on the fourth evening, watching her complete another controlled swing, sweat beading at her temples despite the cool night air. "Better than I expected, given how little time we've had to prepare properly."

"Feels like I've been preparing for this longer than a week, honestly. Just didn't know what for."

"Perhaps you have been. The hammer judged you ready some time before this crisis ever demanded proof of it."

Faith considered that, turning it over, resting the hammer's head against the ground for a moment's breather. "Guess that tracks. Feels like everything's been building toward something, this whole year. Just didn't expect it to be this."

"Few of us ever expect the actual shape our preparation was building toward," Thor said. "That rarely makes the preparation any less real, once the moment finally arrives."

"You nervous? For me, I mean. Not for yourself."

Thor considered the question with real seriousness before answering. "Yes. Considerably. But nervousness and confidence are not opposites, Faith. I am nervous because this matters. I am confident because I have watched you become exactly the person capable of meeting it."

"That's a genuinely good answer. Wasn't expecting a honestly good answer."

"I contain multitudes," Thor said, and Faith actually laughed despite the exhaustion, the sound cutting welcome through the tense evening air.

Xander found his own role considerably less clear than everyone else's — no sorcery, no gate technology, no ancient weapon judging his worthiness, just an ability that had started all of this without ever fully explaining itself, and he found himself, more than once during those frantic days, hovering at the edges of conversations he didn't fully understand, feeling useful mostly in the smallest, most unglamorous ways — fetching supplies, keeping everyone fed alongside Joyce, listening when Faith needed to talk through her nerves without anyone actually solving anything.

"You're the variable nobody can quite account for," Giles told him, during one of their planning sessions, not unkindly, setting down the book he'd been cross-referencing for the past hour. "Your ability predates the Convergence, connects to it, yet doesn't behave according to any of the systems the rest of us understand. That may prove more valuable than any of us currently realize, or it may prove entirely incidental. I actually don't know which."

"Reassuring."

"I did warn you honesty wouldn't always be comfortable." Giles managed a tired, genuine smile. "I want you to hear this part clearly, Xander — whatever role you end up playing in this, the fact that you exist at all, that your ability manifested the way it did, may itself be part of why any of us are equipped to face this at all. Don't discount your own significance simply because it doesn't fit neatly into anyone else's framework."

"I'll try to remember that when everything's actually happening."

"Do. I suspect you'll need the reminder."

Cent found him on the porch that same night, both of them taking a rare, quiet break from the frenetic preparation happening inside, and sat beside him without saying anything for a long moment, just letting the silence hold whatever needed holding.

"You okay?" she asked finally.

"Getting there. Feels weird, watching everyone else have a clear job while I'm just kind of... around."

"Being around counts for more than you think. Trust me. I've watched my parents handle enough crises to know the person keeping everyone fed and steady matters just as much as the person wielding the actual weapon."

"Yeah. Guess I know that too, somewhere. Just hard to remember in the moment."

"That's what I'm here for, then. Reminding you." She bumped his shoulder gently. "Whatever happens, you already did the hardest part. You found this. You brought everyone together who's actually capable of fixing it. That's not nothing, Xander. That's the whole reason any of us are even here."

The preparations continued, day into night into day again, everyone working toward a deadline that grew steadily, uncomfortably closer, until finally, on the seventh morning, Ilsa's instruments registered a spike significantly sharper than anything they'd tracked before, an alarm chirping insistently from her equipment loud enough to bring the whole household running.

"It's accelerating faster than the model predicted," she said, voice tight, reading figures that made even Strange's composed expression falter slightly. "We don't have weeks anymore. We might not even have days."

The room went very still, the theoretical crisis they'd been racing toward suddenly, undeniably, about to become real.

***



Chapter 150: Chapter 150



The first tear opened over the Sunnydale water tower at 2 a.m., a jagged seam of white light that hung in the air like something had reached in and simply pulled reality apart at the stitching, static crackling audibly across every radio and television within a half-mile radius, and by the time Willow's instruments screamed loud enough to wake the whole household, two more had already opened across town.

"Okay!" Xander shouted, already pulling on boots, adrenaline making his hands clumsy on the laces. "Okay, that's — that's happening, that's actually happening now, we're doing this now, apparently, no warm-up, just straight into it—"

'This is fine,' he thought, not believing a single word of it. 'This is a completely normal Tuesday night activity. Lace the boots. Don't think about the tear in reality currently visible from your bedroom window. Lace the boots, Harris, you can panic properly once your shoes are on, there's a whole system to this.'

"You're narrating," Faith said, already moving past him toward the door, hammer in hand, weirdly steady given everything.

"I narrate when I'm terrified, this is a documented pattern, you've all seen it—"

"Then narrate faster and walk at the same time."

They converged on the epicenter site within twenty minutes — a stretch of woods behind the old drive-in, unremarkable until you accounted for every reading from every instrument all pointing to this exact patch of ground — headlights cutting through predawn dark as three separate vehicles arrived within moments of each other, everyone spilling out already moving, already working, months of preparation collapsing into pure, focused motion. They found the seam already visible even from a distance, a vertical tear in the air itself, edges rippling like heat distortion except far more wrong, a low, subsonic hum pressing against Xander's teeth the closer they got.

"That's the one," Ilsa confirmed, instruments already out, voice tight but controlled. "That's the epicenter tear. If we can reinforce this one, the others should stabilize in response."

"Should," Strange repeated.

"Should," Ilsa agreed. "I won't pretend to certainty we don't actually have."

"Comforting," Xander muttered.

"I told you honesty wouldn't always feel good," Giles said, arriving with an armful of materials for Strange's wards.

They set up quickly, positions falling into the rehearsed pattern — Faith at the center with the counter-working, Strange and Ilsa flanking with sorcery and gate-tech respectively, Thor and Fury holding the perimeter against whatever might come through before containment took hold, Xander positioned at Giles' insistence directly behind Faith, "for reasons I confess I can't fully articulate, beyond a persistent instinct that your presence matters here." Cent took up a position beside Petra, helping monitor the secondary readings with a steadiness that surprised even her, given everything, while Dov worked the perimeter's edge alongside Thor, translating shouted instructions between Ilsa's technical readings and Giles' half-remembered Watcher terminology fast enough that nobody lost a critical second to confusion.

"Really specific instructions, Giles."

"I did warn you my confidence in this plan wasn't absolute."

The tear pulsed, once, hard enough that all of them staggered, and something on the other side of it — vast, formless, more felt than seen — pressed against the opening with a weight that made Xander's teeth ache.

"Now!" Ilsa shouted.

Faith began the working, voice steady despite everything, hammer held ready, and Strange's wards flared to life around her in patterns of gold light, Ilsa's gate technology humming an answering frequency beneath it all.

"I can feel it pushing," Faith gritted out, hand tightening on the hammer's grip. "It's not like the ritual. It's bigger. So much bigger."

"Hold," Thor called, from the perimeter, voice carrying easy authority even now. "You are holding it. Trust that."

"Easy for you to say, you're not the one—"

"I absolutely am the one, I have held much larger things than this, and I promise you it is fully a matter of belief as much as strength."

"That's not helpful right now, Thor!"

"It was meant to be encouraging!"

"Bad time for encouragement, really bad time—"

"Then WHEN is a good time, if not during the actual crisis it's meant to address?"

"NEVER, Thor, that's when, or, or afterward, at the earliest, over drinks, when I'm not personally holding back the apocalypse with my ARMS—"

"You're not holding it with your arms, you're holding it with the hammer—"

"That is NOT the point I'm making right now—"

"I merely wished to be precise, given the circumstances—"

"Your precision is NOT what I need from you at this exact moment, your precision can WAIT—"

"Noted!" Thor called back, sounding, despite everything, faintly wounded. "I shall save my precision for a more convenient occasion!"

"THANK you!"

"You are most welcome!"

Even Ilsa, mid-crisis, monitoring readings that could still end everything, spared half a second to glance sideways at the exchange with an expression that suggested she genuinely could not decide whether to be impressed or alarmed.

"Is this normal?" Petra asked her, quietly, from the perimeter.

"I have no idea," Ilsa said. "I've stopped asking that question about this town completely."

Xander, despite the terror, felt something in his chest that was almost laughter, the sheer, absurd humanity of an actual Norse god getting scolded mid-crisis by a teenager holding back the end of reality with a magic hammer.

The seam widened, briefly, dangerously, and Ilsa's gate mechanism screamed with strain.

'Okay, this is the part where I die,' Xander thought, with the strange, detached clarity that apparently arrived right before actual disaster, some unhelpful corner of his brain already drafting an obituary that would definitely mention the sloth. 'Died the way he lived: narrating, mildly unprepared, standing near a hole in reality he had no actual business standing near. Good use of a Tuesday.'

"We're losing containment!" Petra shouted, from her position monitoring the readings. "Fifteen seconds, maybe less!"

"Xander!" Giles shouted, sudden and urgent. "Your hand. On Faith's shoulder. Now, whatever instinct told me to place you there—"

Xander didn't question it, reaching out and gripping Faith's shoulder the same way he'd have gripped anyone's right before a jump, and the moment his hand made contact, something shifted — the ability that had carried him across a hundred worlds suddenly, inexplicably, threading itself into the working, adding its own strange resonance to the mix.

The seam stopped widening.

Then, slowly, agonizingly, it began to close.

"That's it!" Ilsa shouted, disbelief and triumph tangled together in her voice. "Whatever that was, keep doing it!"

"I don't know what I'm doing!" Xander shouted back, hand still locked on Faith's shoulder.

"Then don't stop not knowing!" Faith gritted out, and despite everything, despite the terror and the strain and the vast, formless pressure still pushing from the other side, Xander found himself laughing, short and disbelieving, the sheer absurdity of the instruction landing perfectly even now.

The seam closed fully thirty seconds later, the light collapsing in on itself with a sound like a held breath finally released, and the silence that followed felt, for a long moment, more overwhelming than the crisis itself had been.

***



Chapter 151: Chapter 151



The other tears closed within minutes of the epicenter's collapse, Ilsa's instruments confirming it with a kind of disbelieving relief that spread through the whole group like something physical.

"It held," she said, staring at her readings like they might change if she looked away and back. "It actually held. The reinforcement radiated outward, stabilizing every secondary point almost simultaneously."

"Just like that?" Fury asked, lowering the weapon he'd been holding ready this entire time, expression somewhere between relief and professional suspicion of anything resolving this cleanly.

"Not 'just like that,'" Strange said, dryly. "Considerable expertise, several converging systems working in tandem, and one extremely lucky variable nobody fully understood contributing at exactly the right moment." He glanced at Xander. "You'll want to figure out what you actually did there, eventually. For future reference, if nothing else."

"I truly have no idea," Xander admitted, hand finally releasing Faith's shoulder, both of them shaking slightly now that the adrenaline had nowhere left to go. "I just did the thing that felt right. Wasn't exactly following a manual."

"Story of your entire life, from what I understand," Ilsa said, something almost like a smile crossing her face for the first time since arriving.

Faith sat down hard on the ground, hammer resting across her knees, breathing like she'd run a marathon. "That was the scariest ten minutes of my entire life. And I include the last crisis in that ranking."

"You held it," Thor said, kneeling beside her, genuine pride in every word. "Completely. From the very first moment to the last."

"Didn't feel like holding anything. Felt like barely hanging on."

"That is, in my experience, precisely what holding something enormous actually feels like from the inside. It rarely feels as steady as it looks from the outside."

Faith managed a tired, real laugh at that. "Comforting. In a weird way."

Willow, still catching her breath from the walk back, couldn't resist. "For the record, Thor, 'never, or over drinks afterward' is honestly the best crisis-management advice I've heard all week."

"I have decided to take that as a compliment," Thor said, with as much dignity as a man freshly scolded mid-catastrophe could reasonably muster.

"You should. It was delivered with real precision."

"Do not," Thor said, "start with the precision."

"He got told off by a teenager holding back the end of reality," Fury said, arriving in time to catch the tail end of it, one eyebrow raised. "Mid-crisis. I heard the whole thing over comms. I'm not going to pretend that wasn't the highlight of my week."

"I was attempting to be encouraging."

"You were attempting to be precise while somebody was actively fighting off an apocalypse," Fury said. "Different thing."

"That is precisely what she said."

"Then maybe," Faith said, grinning despite the exhaustion, "you should've listened the first time."

"I contain multitudes of comforting weirdness," Thor said, entirely straight-faced, and the whole group, despite everything, despite the exhaustion and the fear still working its way out of their systems, dissolved into genuine, disbelieving laughter.

"That's still not your line," Xander managed, between laughs.

"I am beginning to suspect it belongs to whoever needs it most in any given moment," Thor said, and nobody had a good argument against that.

Cent found Xander a little apart from the group, still catching his breath, and didn't say anything at first, just leaned into his shoulder, both of them letting the adrenaline finish draining out in silence.

"You okay?" she asked finally.

"Getting there. You?"

"Getting there too." She managed a small, tired laugh. "Hell of a way to finally meet all your friends in person."

"Yeah, this wasn't exactly how I pictured introducing everybody."

"Could've been worse. Nobody died. Reality's still mostly intact. I'd call that a win, given the bar we were working with."

"Fair bar to clear," Xander agreed, and for a moment, despite the exhaustion, despite everything still humming through both their bodies, it felt like exactly the right kind of quiet to finally just breathe in together.

They stayed at the site another hour, confirming readings, making certain the containment held steady rather than merely appearing to, and by the time they finally made their way back into town, dawn was breaking pale and ordinary over Sunnydale, the whole impossible night settling, slowly, into something that felt almost like it could be believed.

"Is it over?" Willow asked, quiet, once they were all finally back at the house, the adrenaline crash hitting everyone in earnest now.

"The immediate crisis, yes," Ilsa said. "The wider Convergence — the instability across every reality currently affected — that will take longer to fully understand and address. But the epicenter's contained. The acceleration should slow considerably now that its source point is stabilized."

"Should," Xander repeated, echoing Ilsa's own earlier hedge.

"Should," she agreed, managing a tired smile of her own. "I'm learning to live with that word notably better than I expected to, this past week."

Miriam sat quietly at the kitchen table, watching the whole exhausted, relieved scene unfold with an expression Xander couldn't quite read until she finally spoke. "We did it. Actually did it. I wasn't sure, going in, that we would."

"None of us were," Petra said, dropping into the chair beside her, equipment finally set aside for the first time in days.

"I know. That's what makes it feel real, somehow. The uncertainty. If we'd been certain going in, I don't think I'd trust the outcome nearly as much." Miriam looked at Cent, something fiercely proud crossing her face. "You did good, coming here. Both of you."

"Learned it from my own mother," Cent said, quiet, and Miriam's eyes went a little bright at that anyway, something in Cent's steadiness clearly calling up an old ache of her own — all the ordinary mother-daughter moments her own years away from Home had cost her. She said nothing further, just reached over and squeezed Cent's hand once, hard, something instinctively warm in the gesture despite how recently the two of them had actually met.

The group settled into the exhausted, grateful quiet of people who'd faced something actually enormous and, against real uncertainty, actually come through it — not unscathed, not without real fear still working its way out of their systems, but together, and, for now, totally, gratefully alive.

***



Chapter 152: Chapter 152



The departures happened gradually, over the following week, each one carrying its own specific weight of goodbye, the Magic Box slowly emptying of instruments and books and half-finished diagrams as the household eased, day by day, back toward something resembling its ordinary rhythm.

Fury left first, with characteristically little ceremony, his unremarkable sedan waiting exactly where he'd left it a week earlier. "I'll be in touch," he told Giles, in the doorway of the Magic Box, tone giving away nothing and everything at once. "This isn't the kind of thing that stays quiet forever, even contained. But you people handled this better than most professionals I've worked with. That's not nothing."

"High praise, coming from you," Giles said.

"Don't get used to it," Fury said, though something almost warm flickered behind the flat delivery. He paused at the door, something more careful crossing his expression. "One more thing. That report's classified at the highest level I've got access to grant. Doesn't mean it stays that way forever. Keep your guard up, even with this one behind you. I've learned the hard way that the people who shouldn't know things have a way of finding out anyway."

"Understood," Giles said, something in his tone suggesting he'd take the warning seriously rather than as mere parting caution.

Fury nodded once, and left without further sentiment, exactly as he'd arrived.

Strange and Wong lingered a day longer, Strange spending most of it in quiet conversation with Giles about the deeper implications of what they'd all just witnessed, the two of them cross-referencing notes late into their final evening with the easy rhythm of colleagues who'd genuinely enjoyed the collaboration despite its terrifying circumstances.

"The Ancient One would have wanted to know this resolved as well as it did," Strange said, on their last evening. "I intend to make sure her note gets properly annotated, for whoever reads it next. A warning that led, eventually, to an actual answer."

"I'd be glad to contribute what we've learned here," Giles offered. "If it would help future scholars facing something similar."

"It would. Considerably." Strange extended a hand. "You did good work, Giles. All of you did."

"We should come back sometime," Wong added, unexpectedly. "Under significantly less dire circumstances, preferably. I'd be curious to see this shop when it isn't functioning as a war room."

"You're welcome anytime," Giles said, and meant it.

They left through a portal far less startling than their arrival had been, everyone having grown accustomed enough to golden light appearing out of nowhere that barely anyone flinched this time, Willow waving with genuine enthusiasm right up until the light sealed shut behind them.

Home's team stayed longest, Ilsa insisting on thorough final readings before she'd fully trust the containment, days of careful, methodical checking that left her visibly, finally satisfied by the end of it. Miriam lingered even after that, wanting time with Cent that the crisis had, understandably, crowded out, the two of them spending long hours together that Xander mostly gave them privacy for, catching only fragments through open windows — laughter, once, and later, something quieter that sounded like relief, two people who'd found unexpected common ground finally, properly catching up.

"I'm proud of you," she told Cent, the night before Xander took her back through the jump, both of them sitting together on the porch steps, stars just beginning to show overhead. "Of all of you. This could have gone so much worse, and it didn't, largely because people who'd never have found each other otherwise chose to trust one another instead of standing apart."

"You okay? Going back? After everything?"

"I will be. It's strange, reconnecting with Home after so many years believing that part of my life was over. But I think it's a good strange. I think I needed this, in ways I hadn't let myself admit."

"Will you keep in touch? With everyone here?"

"I intend to. This connection matters too much now to let it lapse again." Miriam hugged her tight, holding on a beat longer than usual. "Tell Xander I'm grateful. For all of it. For finding your family in the first place, all those months ago, without which none of this would have come together the way it did."

"I will."

Ilsa and her team left the following morning, the gate closing behind them with a quiet finality that felt, to everyone watching, considerably less dramatic than the crisis that had first brought them here, Petra and Dov both offering warm, genuine goodbyes before stepping through, Dov's recording device still clutched in one hand as though reluctant to stop documenting even in this final moment.

"Reach out if anything changes," Ilsa told Xander, in her last moments before crossing back. "We'll be watching more closely now. Properly, this time, not from decades of disconnected silence."

"I will. Thank you. Genuinely. For coming, for the team, for everything."

"Thank you for being worth coming for," she said, and stepped through, the gate sealing quiet behind her, the woods settling back into ordinary stillness as though nothing extraordinary had ever passed through them at all.

Thor stayed the longest of anyone, though even his own departure, when it finally came, felt less like an ending and more like the natural next step of a story that had found its resting point.

"I must return to my own responsibilities eventually," he told the assembled household, over one last dinner, the table crowded with everyone who'd become, over these strange months, something close to his own found family. "Natasha's already covered for my absence longer than any of us should reasonably expect. But I intend to visit. Often, if you'll have me."

"Always," Joyce said, and meant it completely.

"Good," Thor said, something really moved in his expression. "Because I find, rather unexpectedly, that this strange, small, remarkable town has become something much more than a place I once searched for a lost hammer. It has become, in its own way, a second home."

"We contain multitudes," Xander said, and this time, nobody bothered correcting him, the whole table dissolving into warm, easy laughter instead, one more strange, wonderful chapter closing gently into whatever ordinary, extraordinary life came next.

***

152.5. Cent: Coming Home

We came back through the jump the morning after that last conversation, Home's team already gone ahead of us, and I slept almost the entire first day home. The kind of sleep that isn't really rest so much as your body finally admitting how much it's been holding. Woke up disoriented twice, not sure which world I was actually in.

Dad was waiting when I woke up for good. Sat on the edge of my bed, didn't say anything at first, just looked at me a long moment. Same look he gets checking gear before a jump. Assessing whether everything's actually intact.

"You scared me," he said finally. "Going through like that. Being right there when it happened."

"I know. I'm sorry."

"Don't be sorry. I'd have done the same thing at your age. Doesn't mean it didn't scare me."

"You'd have done the same thing at any age, Dad. Don't pretend that's a young-person thing."

That got a real laugh out of him, tired but genuine. "Fair. Guess some things don't change with age."

He reached over, squeezed my hand once. Same gesture he's used my whole life for the stuff too big for words. Didn't say anything else. Didn't need to.

Mom came in a while later, sat on the other side of the bed, and for a minute the three of us just sat there together, not saying much, the kind of quiet that only works when everybody in the room actually trusts everybody else.

"Tell me the parts you didn't put in what you told us," Mom said eventually. "The real parts. Not the version you gave us worried about scaring us further."

So I told her. The actual fear, standing at the perimeter, watching Faith hold something none of us fully understood while reality itself tried to come apart. The moment Xander's hand found her shoulder and something shifted, unexplainable, exactly enough. The silence after, more overwhelming than the crisis itself had been.

"That's a lot to carry," Mom said, quiet.

"Yeah. Glad I wasn't carrying it alone, though."

"None of us were, this time," Dad said. "That's new. Worth noticing."

I thought about that — how different this whole thing had felt from every other crisis I'd grown up hearing about, secondhand, from a family that used to face everything in careful, isolated silence. This one, we'd faced together. Out loud. With people who weren't even family a year ago, who were something like family now anyway.

"You think Home's gonna want us more involved going forward?" I asked. "Given everything?"

"Probably," Mom said. "I'm not sure how I feel about that yet. Good thing, mostly, I think. Still getting used to the idea of being reconnected at all, let alone what it's gonna actually mean long-term."

"We'll figure it out," Dad said. "Same way we figure out everything else. Slowly, and mostly by accident, and somehow it works out anyway."

"Comforting life philosophy," I said.

"It's the only one I've got. Seems to keep working, though."

I laughed at that, the real kind, and felt something ease in my chest that had been sitting there since the moment the gate first opened in Colorado and started all of this.

Xander made it back to the fence line the following weekend, checking in properly now that the adrenaline had fully drained out of both of us.

"You doing okay?" he asked.

"Yeah. Slept like a rock most of today. You?"

"Same, actually. Feels good, being able to just rest now. Feels earned, for once."

"It is earned. Don't undersell it."

"Noted." A pause. "Glad you came. For the actual crisis, I mean. Not just — glad you're in my life generally, obviously. But glad you were actually there."

"Me too. Wouldn't have missed it. Even the scary parts."

"Especially the scary parts, probably. Given how it turned out."

"Yeah," I said. "Especially those."

We talked a while longer, nothing urgent, just the easy, ordinary comfort of two people who'd been through something enormous together and come out the other side still finding each other worth talking to about nothing in particular. He told me about Fury's exit line. I told him about Dad's hand squeeze. Small things. The kind that actually stick.

I mentioned Fury's line to Dad in passing the next morning, more as a funny aside than anything else — the whole "people who shouldn't know things have a way of finding out anyway" bit. He didn't laugh. Went quiet a second — the kind of quiet he gets when something old resurfaces without him meaning it to — then just nodded and said "smart man" and changed the subject. Didn't think much of it at the time. Filed it away without really filing it away, if that makes sense.

Big year, I thought, once he'd finally had to go. Turned into a good one, somehow, despite everything. Started with a boot print. Ended with reality itself nearly coming apart, and somehow, all of us still standing on the other side of it.

Wouldn't have traded it. Not even the scary parts.

***

152.6. Charlie: What Came Back Through

Mom came back through the gate looking older than when she'd left, which didn't make sense given she'd only been gone a handful of days, but grief and relief both age a person in ways that don't always wait for actual time to pass. The gate sealed shut behind her with a low hum, the barn settling back into its ordinary quiet, hay dust drifting in the afternoon light like nothing extraordinary had just happened at all.

I was waiting in the barn when she stepped through, the same wary posture I'd carried around her ever since the whole gate business first came out, though something in my own chest had softened over the past weeks, wariness gradually giving way to something closer to cautious trust.

"It's done," Mom said, before I could even ask. "The immediate crisis. Contained. I'll explain everything properly, but I wanted you to know that first."

"Contained," I repeated. "That's a very careful word for whatever actually happened."

"It's the accurate word. I won't pretend it wasn't close. It was closer than anything I've lived through since we lost almost everything, all those years ago." Mom set down the small case she'd carried through, hands not quite steady. "But it held. People I'd never have expected to find common ground actually found it, and it held."

I took that in for a long moment. "You okay? Really okay, not just saying it?"

"Getting there." She managed a tired smile. "It's strange, being properly reconnected to Home again after so long believing that part of my life was simply over. Strange, but not bad. I think I needed it more than I let myself admit, these past years."

"Are they gonna want you going back? For more of this?"

"Probably, at some point. Not permanently. I made that clear, and they respected it." She looked at me steadily. "This is my home now. You're my home now. Whatever Home needs from me going forward, it's a piece of my life, not the whole of it, the way it once was."

Dad came in partway through, having heard voices from the house, and Mom crossed to him without a word, the two of them holding onto each other a long moment in a way that said more than either of them probably could have managed out loud right then.

"Welcome home," Dad said, finally.

"Good to be home," Mom said, and meant it completely.

We had dinner together that night, the three of us, the ordinary rhythm of the table holding steady around everything that had just happened — not ignoring it, exactly, just letting it settle into the shape of an actual life instead of staying a crisis suspended forever. Dad had made his usual Tuesday chili, unaware or unbothered that this certain Tuesday had nearly seen reality itself come apart at the seams somewhere far outside our own quiet corner of it.

"So there's a whole other family out there now," I said, partway through the meal, spoon pausing halfway to my mouth. "This Xander person. His friends. All of them, connected to us because of some ancient hammer and a fracture in reality itself."

"Apparently," Mom said. "Strange as it sounds."

"Everything about our family's strange, Mom. That one barely cracks the top ten."

That got a real laugh out of both of them, the first one that felt utterly unforced since Mom had left, Dad nearly choking on his own chili trying to laugh and eat at the same time.

"I'd like to meet them properly, someday," I said. "Not through a crisis. Just — meet them. See what it's actually like, having that kind of connection."

"I think that could be arranged," Mom said. "Once things settle further. I'd like that too, honestly."

"I want to bring the Five, when we do. Clara especially wants to meet the girl who lifted a magic hammer. Says that sounds like exactly the kind of person she'd get along with."

"I don't doubt that for a second," Mom said, something warm and amused crossing her face.

We finished dinner slowly, nobody in a hurry to leave the table, the ordinary comfort of being together outweighing, for once, every enormous, complicated thing sitting just underneath the surface of our lives.

Later, lying in bed, I thought about the wildside — the empty grass, the buffalo, the whole vast, untouched world that had started all of this years ago — and found myself wondering, for the first time, what Xander's worlds actually looked like. Whether they held their own quiet wonders, their own strange, undiscovered corners.

Someday, I thought. Someday I'd like to see one myself. Bring Clara along too, probably, once things settled properly. She'd never forgive me if I didn't.

For now, it was enough just knowing they existed, and that whatever thin, strange threads connected our family to his, they'd held, when it mattered most, exactly the way threads like that were supposed to.

***



Chapter 153: Chapter 153



Buffy and Willow both came this time, the first jump since the crisis, and all three of them felt the difference immediately — not fear exactly, but a new, quiet awareness of how much bigger the whole enterprise had turned out to be than any of them had understood when it started.

The world was ordinary meadow, wildflowers and gentle hills, nothing dramatic, nothing world-altering, just an empty world doing what empty worlds did.

"Okay," Xander said, breathing it in properly for the first time in what felt like months. "This one's just nice. Genuinely, uncomplicatedly nice."

"I don't think I've ever appreciated nice this much," Willow admitted, already cataloguing wildflowers with real, uncomplicated joy. "After everything, this feels like exactly the right kind of return."

They spent three easy days in the meadow, cataloguing without urgency, resting without guilt, and by the final evening had eased into something that felt, despite everything they now knew about the wider stakes involved, truly, wonderfully ordinary.

"You think it's really over?" Buffy asked, watching the sunset with real peace for the first time in weeks, leaning back against Xander while Willow's head rested on his shoulder, all three of them tangled together in the easy way that had become second nature by now. "The Convergence, all of it?"

"The immediate part, yeah," Xander said. "Ilsa said the wider instability will take longer to fully settle. But the actual crisis, the part that could've ended everything? That part's done."

"Good," Buffy said, simply. "I like knowing that. Even if the bigger picture's still complicated."

"Same," Willow agreed. "Feels good, having room to just be normal again. Even knowing normal's got a much bigger context now than it used to."

They made it home steadier than they'd felt in weeks, the ordinary comfort of the trip settling into something that would carry them forward — proof, gentle and undeniable, that whatever else this strange, expanded life now included, the actual heart of it remained exactly what it had always been.

"Good one?" Joyce asked, catching them at the door — the exact same three words she'd said a hundred times before this, back when it was just a habit and not, like now, something that came out sounding almost unbearably relieved.

"Really good one," Xander confirmed. "Genuinely, wonderfully ordinary."

"I'm so glad," Joyce said, and meant it completely, pulling all three of them into a hug that lasted a beat longer than any of her usual ones did, nobody in the doorway in any hurry to point that out.

***



Chapter 154: Chapter 154



Joyce's list marked its own quiet milestone that Sunday, the entry she read aloud carrying more weight than any single line had in longer than any of them could remember: We faced something that could have ended notably more than just our own small corner of existence, and we came through it together, stranger and more connected than we ever expected to become. I'm choosing to call that a good outcome, however it got here.

"That's a big entry," Xander said.

"It's been a big month," Joyce said, passing the potatoes down a table significantly fuller than usual — Faith, Buffy, Willow, Jesse, Cordelia, Giles, Thor, all crowded around in the comfortable chaos that had, by now, become simply how Sunday dinner worked, chairs pulled from three different rooms to accommodate everyone, the whole scene warm with the noise of a family considerably larger than the one it had started as.

"Speaking of big," Faith said, "I still can't get over how casual everyone's being about the whole 'we prevented reality from unraveling' thing. Feels like that should be a bigger deal at this table specifically."

"We process big things by making them ordinary," Willow said. "It's basically the whole Sunnydale coping strategy."

"Deeply validating information about my adopted hometown," Thor said, entirely serious, and the table dissolved into the easy laughter that had become, over the past several months, its own kind of ritual.

Jesse, who'd spent most of dinner quietly working through his own version of processing, finally spoke up. "Can we talk about the fact that I've now met an actual Avenger, an actual sorcerer, and people from an fully different reality, and somehow none of that beats the napkin thing as my most embarrassing Sunnydale memory?"

"Nothing beats the napkin thing," Cordelia said, delighted. "That's just objectively true."

"I stand by my earlier defense. It was a small collection."

"It's never gonna stop being funny, Jesse. Make your peace with that."

Cordelia, who'd been folded into enough of the actual details by now to follow most of the conversation, finally spoke up herself. "Okay, but for real, is it actually over? Or is this just, like, round one?"

"Ilsa said the immediate crisis is resolved," Giles said. "The wider instability across affected realities will take far longer to fully settle, and may require ongoing monitoring for years. But the acute danger, the part that threatened genuine catastrophe — that's passed."

"So we might have to do this again someday."

"Possibly. Though hopefully with much more warning next time, and notably less immediate peril."

"I'll take that trade," Faith said, and the table murmured general agreement.

"For what it's worth," Buffy added, "I think we've earned a honestly long stretch of boring after this one. Universe owes us that much, at minimum."

"I would not count on the universe honoring debts," Thor said, "but I appreciate the optimism regardless."

Later, on the porch, Xander found himself sitting beside Faith, her hand loosely laced with his, both of them looking out at a street that felt, for the first time in longer than either could easily place, completely, wonderfully ordinary — porch lights flickering on up and down the block, a dog barking somewhere distant, the whole unremarkable rhythm of a town that had no idea how close it had come to something it would never have understood even if it had. Through the window behind them, he could still hear Buffy and Willow's laughter mixing with the general noise of dinner winding down, and something in his chest settled at the sound of it, complete in a way he hadn't quite let himself name out loud yet.

"Good month," Faith said, eventually.

"Good month," he agreed. "Feels weird to say that, given everything. But yeah. Good month." He pressed a kiss to her temple, the gesture long since settled into something easy and unremarkable after months of practice.

"Maybe complicated things are allowed to be good, even when they're also enormous and scary."

"That's annoyingly wise."

"I have my moments."

"That's still your line, though, right? Not somebody borrowing it?"

"Damn right it's mine. You people can borrow the multitudes thing. This one's staying with me."

They sat together a while longer, the evening settling into comfortable quiet, and Xander found himself turning over, one more time, everything the past year had actually held — four years of solitary jumps becoming something shared, a mystery that had turned into a family, a god who'd become a friend, a crisis that had, against every reasonable fear, resolved into something survivable.

'Big life,' he thought, watching the ordinary street settle into evening dark. 'Bigger than I ever expected, back when this was just me and a random world every weekend.' It felt, sitting here now, less like an ending and more like exactly what it actually was — one more quiet chapter, closing gently, in a story that would, he suspected, keep finding new and impossible ways to grow.

"Same time next weekend?" Faith asked, nodding toward the horizon, toward whatever ordinary, extraordinary world waited next.

"Same time next weekend," Xander confirmed, and meant it completely, ready, as he always somehow managed to be, for whatever came next.



Bonus Chapter: The Honest Trailer



*The screen showed Xander Harris standing behind Sunnydale High’s football field inside a square marked by traffic cones.*

*“In a world where most teenagers spend the weekend sleeping late, avoiding homework, and making regrettable social decisions, one teenager has a standing appointment with an uncontrollable interdimensional wilderness commute.”*

The cones vanished. So did Sunnydale.

*“Same time next weekend.”*

This is *Working for the Weekend: Bridge Over Troubled Water*.

Watch Xander get pulled once a week into a random, uninhabited version of Earth for three subjective days, with no control over the destination, no guarantee of ever returning, and a backpack whose inventory suggests several previous weekends ended with highly specific new rules.

Water purifier. Machete. Collapsible fish tank. Tranquilizer pistol. Bear mace. Air horn. Portable shed.

*“That isn’t camping gear,”* the narrator said. *“That’s a patch history.”*

Follow him across untouched grasslands, forests, deserts, and coastlines while he catalogs extinct animals, retrieves impossible specimens, and learns that “random world every week” is less a superpower than an extremely aggressive continuing-education program in field biology.

Watch a giant ground sloth arrive behind the football field and become several adults’ problem with no advance notice.

Meet Buffy and Willow, who eventually join the jumps, turning Xander’s solitary exile into a three-person relationship, expedition team, and recurring reminder that the phrase “family camping trip” can include prehistoric megafauna.

Meet Joyce, whose contribution to interdimensional exploration consists of hugs, food, spare rooms, and the firm conviction that every apocalypse is easier to manage after everybody has eaten.

Meet Faith in a highway motel after Diana’s death, where Joyce offers her a room without a clipboard, Council terms, or a demand for gratitude.

The narrator paused on the doorway.

*“The story contains multiversal collapse, gods, portals, alien technology, and a hammer. One of its most important acts is still a woman asking a frightened girl whether she wants somewhere safer to sleep. We’re not making that smaller.”*

Then watch the crossover radius expand.

Meet Davy, Millie, and Cent Rice on the one wildside world Xander unexpectedly reaches twice. Meet Charlie and the fixed wooden gate. Meet Miriam and the civilization called Home. Meet Thor, Natasha, Fury, Tony, Steve, Bruce, Strange, Wong, and enough different Earths that the word “home” requires capitalization rules.

The door in Colorado is literal wood with hinges and a padlock. The emergency Gate is a luminous tear in reality. Tony’s relay piggybacks on residual dimensional signatures. Xander’s weekly pull does whatever it wants.

*“Please consult the travel appendix,”* the narrator said. *“No, not that door. The other door.”*

Watch Tony Stark respond to a cross-reality communication problem by building a device that rides the wake of someone else’s portal, then scale it up enough to drive a minivan through.

Ninety percent confidence.

*“The other ten percent was apparently ‘we become a continuity error.’”*

Then face the Convergence: realities destabilizing, Home reconnecting, the whole expanding family discovering the wildside mechanism is part of something much larger, older, and less accidental than a weird weekend curse.

Miriam’s team crosses. Xander carries more people through his personal jump than he has ever tried. Strange helps keep existence from becoming an optional setting. Everyone survives, then immediately schedules another ordinary trip because this series refuses to let cosmic scale take the ordinary heart away from it.

## The *Bridge Over Troubled Water* Drinking Game

Take a drink every time Xander returns with an animal no local veterinarian was trained to treat.

Take two whenever somebody says “long story” and is forced to provide the long version before dawn.

Drink whenever a new world requires clarification about which side is tame, wild, Home, or MCU.

Take a sip whenever Joyce asks whether it was a good trip.

Finish your drink when Tony drives a minivan between realities on ninety-percent confidence.

Do not drink every time a chapter number contains a decimal. The interstitial POV structure is deliberate; your hospitalization would not be.

## Starring

Xander Harris as **Wildside Gig Economy Employee of the Month**.

Buffy and Willow as **Two Girls, One Random Dimensional Pull**.

Joyce Summers as **Bed, Breakfast, and Apocalypse Management**.

Faith as **No Clipboard, No Council, One Spare Room**.

Davy, Millie, and Cent as **The Other Teleporting Family You Meet While Teleporting**.

Charlie as **The Boy Behind the Correct Wooden Door**.

Tony Stark as **Interdimensional Minivan Engineer**.

Thor as **Residual Signature Donor**.

Doctor Strange as **Continuity Repair Wizard**.

And Xander’s backpack as **Mary Poppins’ Tactical Field Kit**.

Alternate titles appeared:

*Into the Wildside-Verse.*

*The Weekend Has No Cancellation Policy.*

*One Hundred Fifty-Four Chapters and a Very Full Kitchen.*

The narrator looked at the title already printed on the manuscript.

*“Simon and Garfunkel did the naming work for us.”*

The card appeared.

# BRIDGE OVER TROUBLED WATER

*Directed by: a weekly schedule with no regard for emergencies.*

*Written by: every crossover door somebody forgot to close.*

*Field support by: a backpack containing the exact consequence of last week’s mistake.*

*Special thanks to: Joyce’s front door, which stayed open even when reality did not.*

The last screen showed Xander, Buffy, and Willow in an uncomplicated meadow after the crisis.

*“Good one?”* Joyce asked when they returned.

*“Really good one.”*

The narrator let the ordinary answer stand.

*“Same time next weekend.”*
